Tomoki Nakanishi
March, 2025, Nagoya

Cluster Algebras

202x

\name
Tomoki Nakanishi

\InsertVolumeTitle

This is a reasonably self-contained exposition of
the fascinating interplay between
cluster algebras and the dilogarithm in the past two decades.

The theme of the monograph is the dilogarithm identity associated with each period in a cluster pattern of cluster algebra theory.
They provide a vast generalization of Euler’s and Abel’s identities. We present several proofs, variations, and generalizations of them
with various methods and techniques.
The quantum dilogarithm identities are also treated from the unified point of view with the classical ones.

Let us give a brief overview of the subject.
The dilogarithm
is defined by a power series or an integral expression as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{x^{n}}{n^{2}}}=-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\log(1-y)}{y}\,dy,

and it was studied first by Euler
[
Eul68
]
.
The function has had a rich history since then and is related to various fields of mathematics and physics
[
Lew81
,
Kir95
,
Zag07
]
.
One of the main features of the dilogarithm is that it satisfies a wide variety of functional equations.
We call them
dilogarithm identities
(DIs).

In the 1980s, it was discovered
by Bazhanov, Kirillov, and Reshetikhin
[
KR86
,
BR89
]

that the dilogarithm plays a key role
in studying some mathematical physics models known as the
integrable lattice models
.
Meanwhile,
Zamolodchikov
[
Zam91b
]
introduced
systems of functional/algebraic equations called the
Y
-systems
(of type ADE)
in the study of the
factorizable
S
-matrix models
.
Also,
the periodicity of the solutions of the
Y
-systmes was conjectured
[
Zam91a
]
.
Then, the
Y
-systems were generalized by Kuniba-Nakanishi
[
KN92
]
and Ravanini-Tateo-Valleriani
[
RTV93
]

based on root systems.
Following these developments, a family of DIs associated with these
Y
-systems
was conjectured by Gliozzi-Tateo
[
GT95
]
.
Remarkably, these conjectural DIs provide a vast generalization of the celebrated Euler’s and Abel’s identities
based on root systems,
where the latter identities correspond to the simplest case of types
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
,
respectively.
This conjecture got the immediate attention by several experts at that time.
However,
the only special case of type
A
were proved by Gliozzi-Tateo
[
GT96
]
and Frenkel-Szenes,

[
FS95
]
,
and the rest were left as mysteries.
This was a situation in B.C. (= Before Cluster algebra).

The scene changed
after the introduction of
cluster algebras
(in “A.D.”) by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ02
]
around 2000.
Cluster algebras are a certain class of commutative algebras.
The original motivation was
to study the structure of the coordinate rings of certain algebraic varieties
in Lie theory (e.g., the Grassmannian, the double Bruhat cells).
Remarkably, the above
Y
-systems were recognized as
a part of the cluster algebra structure.
Based on this observation, the periodicity of the
Y
-systems of type
ADE

was proved by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ03b
]
.
Then, using this result, the DIs for the
Y
-systems of type
ADE
were proved
by Chapoton
[
Cha05
]
.
This was the first important step to connect the DIs
and cluster algebras.
However, to prove the DIs for the
Y
-systems in full generality,
it was necessary to wait for further technical development of cluster algebra theory,
especially the separation formula
[
FZ07
]
and the categorification results
[
Ami09
,
Pla11
]
.
Then, based on these new results, the periodicity and DIs for
Y
-systems
were proved in full generality by
Keller
[
Kel13b
]
and Nakanishi
[
Nak11a
]
, respectively.
Similar DIs for variants of
Y
-systems that had been conjectured in B.C.
were also proved by the same method
[
IIK+13a
,
IIK+13b
,
NT10
]
.
After these developments, it was recognized by
[
Nak11b
]

that

a DI is associated with each period in a cluster pattern
,
and all above DIs are special instances of such DIs.
These DIs play the role of the “leitmotif” throughout the entire text.

Next, we describe the contents of the text.

Part I is the foundation of the whole text and presents the above results in detail.
In Chapter 1, we introduce the dilogarithm and its variations.
Then, we present Euler’s and Abel’s identities, which are the two most basic DIs of all.
Following
[
FK94
]
, we also refer to Abel’s identity as the
pentagon identity
.
In Chapter 2, a quick course on cluster algebras
is given.
In Chapter 3,
by reversing the history in an updated point of view,
we first formulate and prove
the
DI associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern
(or a cluster pattern) (Theorem
3.5
).
This is the leitmotif throughout the entire text, as already mentioned.
Then, in Chapter 4, we formulate the
Y
-systems in terms of
Y
-patterns
in cluster algebra theory.
We also observe the similarity between the
Y
-systems and the actions of the
Coxeter elements on the root systems.
Then, combining them with the results in Chapters 2 and 3,
the DIs for
Y
-systems are proved.
In the proof, we use the
tropicalization
as a key technique.

In the rest, consisting of three parts, we present further developments of the interrelation between
cluster algebras and DIs from various points of view,
which are closely connected with each other.

In Part II, the classical mechanical method is presented.
In Chapter 5
each mutation of
y
-variables in a
Y
-pattern is decomposed into two parts,
the tropical and the nontropical parts
(the Fock-Goncharov decomposition).
After introducing the mutation-compatible Poisson bracket,
the Euler dilogarithm appears as
the Hamiltonian of a mutation.
This gives a more intrinsic and direct connection between cluster algebras and the dilogarithm.
In Chapter 6
the canonical coordinates (in the sense of classical mechanics)
are introduced.
Then, by the Legendre transformation, the modified Rogers dilogarithm
appears as the Lagrangian.
Based on this picture, we give an alternative proof of Theorem
3.5
.
The DIs are now interpreted as the invariance of the action integral under the periodicity.
Moreover, this statement is regarded as a version of Noether’s theorem in classical mechanics.

In Part III, the cluster scattering diagram method is presented.
This is based on the recent development of cluster algebra theory
by the scattering diagram method by Gross-Hacking-Keel-Kontsevich

[
GHKK18
]
.
In Chapter 7 an algebraic formulation of the dilogarithm is given.
For a given skew-symmetric matrix, a certain group
G
is defined.
Then, a family of elements in
G
called the
dilogarithm elements

are introduced. They satisfy the
pentagon relation
in the group
G
, which is regarded
as an algebraic version of the pentagon identity of the dilogarithm.
In Chapter 8 a quick course on cluster scattering diagrams is given.
In Chapter 9
we extend Theorem
3.5

to the
DI associated with a loop in a cluster scattering diagram (CSD)
.
We give two proofs. The first one is the extension of the method in Part II.
The second one is solely based on the pentagon relation.
This also gives yet another proof of Theorem
3.5
; moreover,
it shows the
infinite reducibility
of these DIs.

In Part IV, the quantum dilogarithm identities (QDIs) are presented.
In Chapter 10 we introduce the
quantum dilogarithm

and show the pentagon relation
following Faddeev, Kashaev, and Volkov
[
FV93
,
FK94
]
.
In Chapter 11 the quantum mutation of
y
-variables is given
following Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09a
]
.
The quantum dilogarithm naturally appears as the Hamiltonian
of the quantum mutation.
Thus, it is indeed the quantization of the classical mechanical
picture in Part II.
At the same time, the algebraic picture in Part III also has the
quantization. Namely, we have the
quantum dilogarithm elements

and the pentagon relation among them.
In Chapter 12 we present the QDI associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern,
which is completely parallel to Theorem
3.5
.
Also, we have the quantum version of the
DI associated with a loop in a CSD in Part III.
However, not much is known about the structure of quantum cluster scattering diagrams (QCSDs).
We present some examples of QDIs and open problems.

In the beginning, we mentioned that the exposition is
“reasonably self-contained”.
What we mean is as follows.

•

The text is aimed at researchers and graduate students interested in the interrelation between cluster algebras and the dilogarithm.
We expect the reader is already familiar with one of the two subjects to some extent, but not necessarily both.
Naturally, we assume the reader is a newcomer to both.

•

To make the text in reasonable length,
we skip the proofs of the standard facts on cluster algebras
and cluster scattering diagrams, which are collected in Chapters
2

and
8
, respectively.
The proofs and extensive explanations are found
in the monograph by the author
[
Nak23
]
, which we regard as the companion
of this text.

•

Other than the above, we give the proofs for
most
facts
unless they are too complicated or too far from the context.

In
[
Zag07
]
Zagier impressively described the nature of the dilogarithm as follows:
“
Almost all of its appearances in mathematics, and almost all the formulas relating to it, have something of the fantastical in them as if this function alone among all others possessed a sense of humor.
”
I completely agree with it and wish to add words:
“
The function is so shy that it hides its algebraic nature under the mask of the integral.
”
I hope that the reader may share this feeling through this exposition.

I thank
Michael Gekhtman,
Rei Inoue,
Osamu Iyama,
Rinat Kashaev,
Bernhard Keller,
Atuso Kuniba,
Dylan Rupel,
Salvatore Stella,
Junji Suzuki,
Roberto Tateo,
and
Andrei Zelevinsky
for fruitful collaboration in various works related to this fascinating subject.
I thank Ivan Ip for useful communications.
This work is supported in part by JSPS grant No. JP22H01114.

Part I
Dilogarithm, cluster algebras, and
Y
-systems

Chapter 1
Prologue: Mysterious dilogarithm identities

Cluster algebras were introduced around 2000 by Fomin and Zelevinsky.
Our story began in B.C. (= Before Cluster algebra).
After the series of works by
Bazhanov, Kirillov, and Reshetikhin in the study of some mathematical physics problems in the 1980s,
mysterious dilogarithm identities
(the DIs for
Y
-systems)
were conjectured
by Gliozzi and Tateo in the 1990s.
This introductory chapter presents this historical background.

1.1
Euler Dilogarithm

To begin with,
we present some basic facts about the dilogarithm,
its variants, and dilogarithm identities.
The subject has a long and rich history started by Euler
[
Eul68
]
, and it is related to various fields of mathematics and physics.
We will concentrate on the material we are going to treat here.
We recommend the references by Lewin
[
Lew81
]
, Kirillov
[
Kir95
]
, and Zagier
[
Zag07
]
for further information.

First of all, the
dilogarithm
is defined as a power series

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{x^{n}}{n^{2}}},

(1.1)

where the notation
\mathrm{Li}
stands for the
logarithmic integral
by the reason we see very soon.
Here, we also call it the
Euler dilogarithm
to distinguish it from its variants.
The power series (
1.1
) has the convergent radius 1, so it defines a complex analytic function
on the disk
|x|<1
.
More generally, for any positive integer
k
,
the
polylogarithm of order
k
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{k}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{x^{n}}{n^{k}}}.

(1.2)

By (
1.1
), we have

\displaystyle x\frac{d}{dx}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\frac{x^{n}}{n}}=\mathrm{Li}_{1}(x)=-\log(1-x)\quad(|x|<1).

(1.3)

Thus, we obtain an integral expression

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)=-\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\log(1-y)}{y}\,dy,

(1.4)

by which
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
is analytically continued to the whole complex plain
\mathbb{C}
.
We have to be careful about the branches, though.
There is obvious multivaluedness around
x=1
by the branches of
\log(1-x)
in the integrand.
On the other hand, the function is smooth at
x=0
as a convergent power series.
Nevertheless, if the integration contour starts from
x=0
, goes around
x=1
, say,
counterclockwise
,
and comes back to
x=0
, then the integration (
1.4
) picks up an
additional term

2\pi\sqrt{-1}/y
in the integral. Since it has a simple pole at
y=0
, the function
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
now has multivaluedness also around
x=0
.
For our purpose, however, it is enough to concentrate on the interval
(-\infty,1]
in the real line
\mathbb{R}
,
where the expression (
1.4
) is regarded as an ordinary real integral.
Then, there is no such issue of multivaluedness.
From now on, we consider
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
only on the interval
(-\infty,1]
.

The following two special values are important:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(0)=0,\quad\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1)=\zeta(2)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}=1.6449\dots,

(1.5)

where
\zeta(s)
is the Riemann zeta function.
The first one is obvious, while the second one is the famous answer to
the Basel problem by Euler.

Figure 1.1:
The graph of the Euler dilogarithm
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
.

The function
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
is monotonically increasing on
(-\infty,1]
,
because its derivative
-\log(1-x)/x
is positive on
(-\infty,1)
.
The graph is presented in Figure
1.1
,
where we see nothing spectacular.
However, the function is known to have one very specific feature
that is not expected from its definition.
Namely,
it satisfies various functional identities.

Here, we vaguely call them
dilogarithm identities
(DIs).

Let us present the two most basic examples of DIs.
The first one is due to Euler: (
Euler’s identity
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li_{2}}(x)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1-x)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-\log x\log(1-x)\quad(0\leq x\leq 1).

(1.6)

Here, we understand that,
at
x=0
and
1
,

\displaystyle\log 0\log 1:=\lim_{x\rightarrow 0^{+}}\log x\log(1-x)=0.

(1.7)

The second one is regarded as a two-variable extension of (
1.6
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{Li_{2}}(x)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(y)+\mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{1-x}{1-xy}\biggr)+\mathrm{Li_{2}}(1-xy)+\mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{1-y}{1-xy}\biggr)\\
=\ &\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}-\log x\log(1-x)-\log y\log(1-y)\\
&\qquad-\log\biggl(\frac{1-x}{1-xy}\biggr)\log\biggl(\frac{1-y}{1-xy}\biggr)\quad(0\leq x,y<1).\end{split}

(1.8)

This identity was discovered independently by Spence (1809), Abel (1826, published in 1881), Hill (1830), Kummer (1840), and Schaeffer (1846)
in various equivalent forms
[
Lew81
,
Pir21
]
.
It is usually called
Abel’s identity
, or the
five-term relation
.
Recently, it is also called the
pentagon identity
(e.g.,
[
FK94
]
),
due to a hidden relation to the geometry of a pentagon.
To see some clue, let us set the variables appearing in the left-hand side (LHS) of (
1.8
) as

\displaystyle X_{1}=x,\quad X_{2}=y,\quad X_{3}=\frac{1-x}{1-xy},\quad X_{4}={1-xy},\quad X_{5}=\frac{1-y}{1-xy},\quad

(1.9)

and also
X_{i+5}=X_{i}
.
Then, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following recurrence relation holds:

\displaystyle X_{i+2}=\frac{1-X_{i}}{1-X_{i}X_{i+1}}.

(1.10)

Moreover, the identity (
1.8
) is written in the cyclic form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{5}\mathrm{Li_{2}}(X_{i})&=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}-\log X_{1}\log X_{3}-\log X_{2}\log X_{4}-\log X_{3}\log X_{5}\\
&\qquad-\log X_{4}\log X_{1}-\log X_{5}\log X_{2}.\end{split}

(1.11)

Thus, there is a cyclic symmetry of order 5 behind the identity (
1.8
).

We will prove the identities (
1.6
) and (
1.8
) very soon in some alternative forms.
Before that, let us play a little with them.
First, by using (
1.6
), one can rewrite (
1.8
) as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \mathrm{Li_{2}}(x)+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(y)-\mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{x(1-y)}{1-xy}\biggr)-\mathrm{Li_{2}}(xy)-\mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{y(1-x)}{1-xy}\biggr)\\
&=\log\biggl(\frac{1-x}{1-xy}\biggr)\log\biggl(\frac{1-y}{1-xy}\biggr),\end{split}

(1.12)

where the constant term
\pi^{2}/2
disappeared.
Next, we set

\displaystyle X=\frac{x(1-y)}{1-xy},\quad Y=\frac{y(1-x)}{1-xy}.

(1.13)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{X}{1-Y}=x,\quad\frac{Y}{1-X}=y,\quad\frac{XY}{(1-X)(1-Y)}=xy.

(1.14)

Putting them into (
1.12
), we obtain another alternative
expression of the pentagon identity.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{X}{1-Y}\biggr)+\mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{Y}{1-X}\biggr)-\mathrm{Li_{2}}(X)-\mathrm{Li_{2}}\biggl(\frac{XY}{(1-X)(1-Y)}\biggr)-\mathrm{Li_{2}}(Y)\\
&=\log(1-X)\log(1-Y).\end{split}

(1.15)

This is the expression obtained by Abel
[
Lew81
]
.

1.2
Rogers dilogarithm

The following variant or the symmetrization of the Euler dilogarithm was
introduced by Rogers
[
Rog07
]
:

\displaystyle L(x):=

\displaystyle\ \mathrm{Li_{2}}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\log x\log(1-x)

(1.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{x}\biggl\{\frac{\log(1-y)}{y}+\frac{\log y}{1-y}\biggr\}\,dy.

(1.17)

It is called the
Rogers dilogarithm
(also called
Rogers’
L
-function
).
We concentrate on the interval
[0,1]
, so that there is no
issue of multivaluedness again.
By (
1.5
), we have the following important special values:

\displaystyle L(0)=0,\quad L(1)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.18)

The function
L(x)
is monotonically increasing on
[0,1]
.
The graph of
L(x)
is presented in Figure
1.2
.
We observe that it is close to the linear function
y=(\pi^{2}/6)x
.

Figure 1.2:
The graph of the Rogers dilogarithm
L(x)
.

A special feature of the function
L(x)
is
that
it cleans up some DIs
, and it is the main reason why the function was introduced by Rogers.
For examples, the identities (
1.6
) and (
1.8
)
are rewritten in the following form:

\displaystyle L(x)+L(1-x)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\quad(0\leq x\leq 1),

(1.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}L(x)+L(y)+L\biggl(\frac{1-x}{1-xy}\biggr)+L(1-xy)+L\biggl(\frac{1-y}{1-xy}\biggr)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\\
\quad(0\leq x,y<1).\end{split}

(1.20)

Observe that the log terms in the RHSs disappear in both identities.
Using (
1.19
), the pentagon identity (
1.20
) is also written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}L(x)+L(y)-L\biggl(\frac{x(1-y)}{1-xy}\biggr)-L(xy)-L\biggl(\frac{y(1-x)}{1-xy}\biggr)=0\\
\quad(0\leq x,y<1),\end{split}

(1.21)

where the constant term also disappeared.
This is the counterpart of the form (
1.12
).

Now we prove Euler’s identity in the form (
1.19
).
Of course, it is quickly proved by the integration by parts.
However, we prefer the following method. (The reason will be clear very soon.)
The proof consists of two steps.

•

Step 1 (Constancy)
.
Verify the constancy of the LHS
.
Namely,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}(\mathrm{LHS})=0.

(1.22)

This is true due to
the symmetry of the integrand of (
1.17
) between
y
and
1-y
.

•

Step 2 (Constant term)
.

Determine the constant value of the LHS
. To do it, we simply set
x=0

in (
1.19
).
Then, by (
1.18
), we obtain
\mathrm{LHS}=L(0)+L(1)=\pi^{2}/6
.
This is why we stressed the importance of (
1.18
).

We apply the same method to prove the pentagon identity in the form (
1.20
).
First, we prove the constancy
(good exercise for undergraduate calculus!)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(\mathrm{LHS})=\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(\mathrm{LHS})=0.

(1.23)

Next, we set
x=y=0
.
Then, we have
\mathrm{LHS}=2L(0)+3L(1)=3\pi^{2}/6=\pi^{2}/2
.
This also clarifies the meaning of the constant
\pi^{2}/2
.

The point of the method is that we do not carry out
any integration for the integral (
1.17
); rather,
we differentiate it
and do some algebraic manipulation for its
integrand.
In other words, the structure behind these DIs are
algebraic

rather than
analytic
.
So, we call the above method the
algebraic method
.
We are going to apply it to prove various much more complicated DIs.

The readers may already start enjoying
the “sense of humor” of the dilogarithm
in the words of Zagier, quoted in the preface.

1.3
Modified Rogers dilogarithm

Here, we introduce yet another variant of the dilogarithm,
which especially fits our problem.
Let

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(x):=

\displaystyle\ L\biggl(\frac{x}{1+x}\biggr)\quad(0\leq x)

(1.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{x}\biggl\{\frac{\log(1+y)}{y}-\frac{\log y}{1+y}\biggr\}\,dy

(1.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ {}-\mathrm{Li_{2}}(-x)-\frac{1}{2}\log x\log(1+x).

(1.26)

The importance of the function
\tilde{L}(x)
has been gradually recognized
since the 1990s
through the study of DIs, as we explain very soon.
A little surprisingly,
the expressions (
1.25
) and (
1.26
) seem
less well-known in the literature
until recently (e.g.,
[
Nak11b
,
Nak18
]
);
they are
quickly and
transparently
proved by the algebraic method in the previous section.
The function
\tilde{L}(x)
does not have a name in the literature.
Since this is inconvenient, here we call it the
modified Rogers dilogarithm
.

Again, by (
1.18
), we have the following important special values:

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(0)=0,\quad\tilde{L}(\infty):=\lim_{x\rightarrow\infty}\tilde{L}(x)=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.27)

The function is monotonically increasing on
[0,\infty)
,
and its graph is presented in Figure
1.3
.

Figure 1.3:
The graph of the modified Rogers dilogarithm
\tilde{L}(x)
.

Using the identity

\displaystyle\frac{x}{1+x}+\frac{x^{-1}}{1+x^{-1}}=\frac{x}{1+x}+\frac{1}{1+x}=1,

(1.28)

one can rewrite Euler’s identity (
1.19
)
as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(y)+\tilde{L}(y^{-1})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}\quad(0\leq y),

(1.29)

where we use the variable
y
instead of
x
to fit the context appearing later.

To rewrite the pentagon identity (
1.20
) with
\tilde{L}(x)
,
we introduce independent variables
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
, and we set

\displaystyle\begin{split}&Y_{1}=y_{1},\quad Y_{2}=y_{2}(1+y_{1}),\quad Y_{3}=y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2}),\\
&Y_{4}=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+y_{2}),\quad Y_{5}=y_{2}^{-1},\end{split}

(1.30)

and
Y_{i+5}=Y_{i}
(
i\in\mathbb{Z}
).
They satisfy the recurrence relation

\displaystyle Y_{i+2}=Y_{i}^{-1}(1+Y_{i+1}).

(1.31)

Moreover, if we set

\displaystyle X_{i}=\frac{Y_{i}}{1+Y_{i}}\quad(i\in\mathbb{Z}),

(1.32)

we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1-X_{i}}{1-X_{i}X_{i+1}}&=\frac{1+Y_{i+1}}{(1+Y_{i})(1+Y_{i+1})-Y_{i}Y_{i+1}}\\
&=\frac{Y_{i}^{-1}(1+Y_{i+1})}{Y_{i}^{-1}(1+Y_{i}+Y_{i+1})}=\frac{Y_{i+2}}{1+Y_{i+2}}=X_{i+2}.\end{split}

(1.33)

This is the recurrence relation in (
1.10
).
Thus, with variables
Y_{1}
, …,
Y_{5}
,
and
y_{1}
, …,
y_{5}
, the pentagon identity (
1.20
)
is rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{5}\tilde{L}(Y_{i})&=\tilde{L}(y_{1})+\tilde{L}(y_{2}(1+y_{1}))+\tilde{L}(y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2}))\\
&\qquad+\tilde{L}(y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+y_{2}))+\tilde{L}(y_{2}^{-1})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}\quad(0<y_{1},\,y_{2}).\end{split}

(1.34)

Note that in the limit
y_{1},\,y_{2}\rightarrow 0
,
we have

Y_{1},\,Y_{2}\rightarrow 0
and
Y_{3},\,Y_{4},\,Y_{5}\rightarrow\infty
.
This is consistent with the constant
\pi^{2}/2=3\pi^{2}/6

in the RHS.
Thanks to Euler’s identity (
1.29
),
the pentagon identity (
1.34
) is also written without a constant term as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \tilde{L}(Y_{1})+\tilde{L}(Y_{2})-\tilde{L}(Y_{3})-\tilde{L}(Y_{4})-\tilde{L}(Y_{5})\\
=&\ \tilde{L}(y_{1})+\tilde{L}(y_{2}(1+y_{1}))-\tilde{L}(y_{1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2})^{-1})\\
&\qquad-\tilde{L}(y_{1}y_{2}(1+y_{2})^{-1})-\tilde{L}(y_{2})=0.\end{split}

(1.35)

This is the counterpart of the form (
1.21
).

Though
we do not see why the variables
Y_{i}
in (
1.30
) work well at this moment,
they further reveals the algebraic structure (
1.31
),
which is simpler than (
1.10
), behind the
pentagon identity.

The identities (
1.29
), (
1.34
), and (
1.35
) in these forms
are the prototypical examples of DIs we are going to study.

1.4
Periodicities and DIs for
Y
-systems

So far, we have seen only two DIs, namely,
Euler’s identity and the pentagon (Abel’s) identity.
Here, we introduce a family of infinitely many DIs,
which we call the
DIs for
Y
-systems
.
It turns out that
they are a vast generalization of the above two identities
in view of
root systems
.

In the series of works

[
KR86
,
BR89
,
KR90
,
Kir89
,
BR90
]
,
Bazhanov, Kirillov, and Reshetikhin studied
some mathematical physics problems on
certain
statistical models on the two-dimensional lattice
by the
thermodynamic Bethe ansatz (TBA) method
.
The
Yang-Baxter equation

[
TF79
,
KRS81
]
and
the
quantum groups

[
Dri85
,
Jim85
,
CP95
]

are behind the scenes as
their algebraic background.
As a part of the results, they naturally
reached a remarkable conjecture on a family of (non-functional) dilogarithm identities.
Let us present the conjecture without explaining its mathematical physics meaning
because we are interested in the mathematical aspect of the identities in this text.

1
2
r-1
r
A_{r}
1
2
r-2
r
r-1
D_{r}
1
2
3
4
5
6
E_{6}
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
8
E_{8}
1
2
3
4
5
6
7
E_{7}

Figure 1.4:
Simply-laced Dynkin diagrams of finite type.

Let
X
be a simply-laced (connected) Dynkin diagram of finite type

[
Bou02
,
Kac90
]
,
namely,
X=A_{r}
(
r\geq 1
),
D_{r}
(
r\geq 4
),
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
, or
E_{8}

as in Figure
1.4
.
The number
r
of the vertices of
X
is called the
rank
.
Let
I=\{1,\,\dots,\,r\}
be the index set for the vertices of
X

as in the figure.
Let
\ell\geq 2
be any integer,
which we call the
level
.
For any pair
(X,\ell)
,
there is an integrable lattice model in two dimensions
called the
restricted solid-on-solid (RSOS) model of type
X
with level
\ell

[
JMO88
,
BR89
,
BR90
]
.
Let
\{y_{m}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\mid a\in I;\,m=1,\,\dots,\,\ell-1\}

be a positive real
solution of the following system of algebraic relations:

\displaystyle(y_{m}^{(a)})^{2}=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}(1+y_{m}^{(b)})}{(1+y_{m-1}^{(a)}{}^{-1})(1+y_{m+1}^{(a)}{}^{-1})},

(1.36)

where
b\sim a
means that
b
is adjacent to
a
in the Dynkin diagram
X
.
For example, for
X=E_{6}
, we have
3\sim 2
, 4,
6
.
Also, we set
y_{0}^{(a)}{}^{-1}=y_{\ell}^{(a)}{}^{-1}=0
if they appear
in the denominator of the RHS.

Remarkably, the special value of the modified Rogers dilogarithm
\tilde{L}(y_{m}^{(a)})
emerges in the calculation
of a certain thermodynamic quantity for the RSOS model.
Motivated by this background, the following summation formula was conjectured
by Kirillov, Bazhanov, and Reshetikhin:

Conjecture 1.1

(
[
Kir89
,
BR90
]
)
.

For the above
y_{m}^{(a)}
’s,
the following equality holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{a=1}^{r}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell-1}\tilde{L}(y_{m}^{(a)})=\frac{r(\ell-1)h}{h+\ell}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6},

(1.37)

where
h=h(X)
is the Coxeter number of
X
, namely,

h=r+1
for
A_{r}
,
2r-2
for
D_{r}
, and 12, 18, 30 for
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
.

Remark 1.2
.

(1).
To be precise, the original conjecture was given in terms of the
solution of another system of algebraic relations called the
Q
-systems
,
and the equality is given by the Rogers dilogarithm
L(x)
.
It was translated as above by Kuniba-Nakanishi-Suzuki
[
KNS94
]
.

(2).
Later, it was shown by Nahm-Keegan
[
NK09
]
that there exists a unique positive real solution of (
1.36
).

Remarkably,
for
X=A_{r}
and any
\ell
, Kirillov
[
Kir89
]
gave the explicit expression of
y_{m}^{(a)}

and proved the equality based on it.
Unfortunately, this approach does not work for the other cases,
and they were left as conjectures in B.C.

After Conjecture
1.1
,
a functional extension (affinization) of the system of the
algebraic relation (
1.36
)
called the
Y
-system

was introduced by Zamolodchikov
[
Zam91a
]
for
\ell=2
,
and by Kuniba-Nakanishi
[
KN92
]
and Ravanini-Tateo-Valleriani
[
RTV93
]
for general
\ell
.
Here, we present the version in
[
RTV93
]
, where the symmetry
of the numerator and denominator in (
1.36
) is manifest.

Definition 1.3

(
Y
-system)
.

Let
(X,X^{\prime})
be a pair of simply-laced Dynkin diagrams of finite type with
index sets
I=\{1,\,\dots,\,r\}
and
I^{\prime}=\{1,\,\dots,\,r^{\prime}\}
for their vertices, respectively.
We introduce variables
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)
for
(a,a^{\prime})\in I\times I^{\prime}

and
u\in\mathbb{Z}
,
and consider the following system of functional/algebraic relations
(the
Y
-system of type
(X,X^{\prime})
):

\displaystyle Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u+1)Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u-1)=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}(1+Y_{b,a^{\prime}}(u))}{\displaystyle\prod_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}}(1+Y_{a,b^{\prime}}(u)^{-1})},

(1.38)

where the symbol
\sim
denotes the adjacency
in
X
in the numerator
and in
X^{\prime}
in the denominator.
The product is 1 if it is empty.

In view of the
Y
system (
1.38
),
we identify the former system (
1.36
)
with the
constant
Y
-system

of type
(X,A_{\ell-1})
.
Namely, it is satisfied by the
constant solution

y_{m}^{(a)}=Y_{a,m}(u)
of the
Y
-system
with respect to
u
.
In the original context in mathematical physics,
u
is a
complex
parameter.
However, for our purpose, we may restrict
u
as an
integer parameter
.
Then, the relation (
1.38
)
is regarded as a discrete dynamical system with
the discrete-time
u
and a given initial condition at
u=0
and
1
.

Observe that

Y
-systems of types
(X,X^{\prime})
and
(X^{\prime},X)

are equivalent
under the correspondence
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)^{-1}\leftrightarrow Y_{a^{\prime},a}(u)
.
We call it the
level-rank duality

in view of the original context of the constant
Y
-system (
1.36
).

The following conjecture was given by Zamolodchikov, Ravanini-Tateo-Valleriani,
and Gliozzi-Tateo, again motivated by the underlying mathematical physics
models:

Conjecture 1.4
.

Let
\{Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\mid(a,a^{\prime})\in I\times I^{\prime},u\in\mathbb{Z}\}
be any positive real
solution of the
Y
-system of type
(X,X^{\prime})
.
Let
r,\,r^{\prime}
and
h,\,h^{\prime}
be the ranks and the Coxeter numbers of
X
and
X^{\prime}
, respectively.

(1) Periodicity
[
RTV93
]
(
[
Zam91a
]
for
X^{\prime}=A_{1}
).

\displaystyle Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u+2(h+h^{\prime}))=Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u).

(1.39)

(2) Dilogarithm identity
[
GT95
]
.

\displaystyle\sum_{u=0}^{2(h+h^{\prime})-1}\sum_{(a,a^{\prime})\in I\times I^{\prime}}\tilde{L}(Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u))

\displaystyle=2{rr^{\prime}h}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.40)

Let us call the conjectural identity (
1.40
) the
DI for the
Y
-system
of type
(X,X^{\prime})
.
Some remarks are in order.

1.

The sum in (
1.40
) is
over the fundamental domain of the periodicity in (
1.39
).

2.

The general solution of the
Y
-system (
1.38
)
is expressed as subtraction-free rational functions in the initial variables
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)
at

u=0
and
1
.
Thus, the equality (
1.40
) is regarded as a functional
identity with respect to the initial variables
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)
and
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(1)

that take values in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

3.

Alternatively, the initial variables
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)
can be taken at

u=-1
and
0
.

4.

By the level-rank duality,
the identity (
1.40
) is equivalent to
the identity

\displaystyle\sum_{u=0}^{2(h+h^{\prime})-1}\sum_{(a,a^{\prime})\in I\times I^{\prime}}\tilde{L}(Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)^{-1})

\displaystyle=2{rr^{\prime}h^{\prime}}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.41)

Alternatively,
two identities (
1.40
) and (
1.41
) are shown to be equivalent
by Euler’s identity (
1.29
)
because
there are
2rr^{\prime}(h+h^{\prime})
terms in
the sums of (
1.40
) and (
1.41
), respectively.

5.

The original conjectural (non-functional) identity (
1.37
) follows from the DI (
1.40
).
Indeed,
\{y_{m}^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\}
satisfying (
1.36
) is
a constant solution
y_{m}^{(a)}=Y_{a,m}(u)
of the
Y
-system (
1.38
)
of type
(X,A_{\ell-1})
as mentioned above.
We have
r^{\prime}=\ell-1
and
h^{\prime}=\ell
therein.
Then, in the LHS of (
1.40
),
the sum over
u
is replaced with
the multiplication by
2(h+\ell)
.
This is exactly the equality (
1.37
).

Let us see the two simplest examples for Conjecture
1.4
.

Example 1.5
.

(X,X^{\prime})=(A_{1},A_{1})
.
We have
r=r^{\prime}=1
and
h=h^{\prime}=2
.
We set
Y(u):=Y_{1,1}(u)
.
Then,
the
Y
-system (
1.38
) looks as

\displaystyle Y(u+1)Y(u-1)=1.

(1.42)

Thus, we have the periodicity

\displaystyle Y(u+4)=Y(u+2)^{-1}=Y(u).

(1.43)

Meanwhile,
(
1.39
) claims the periodicity

\displaystyle Y(u+8)=Y(u).

(1.44)

Thus, the periodicity (
1.39
) holds.
Next, the identity (
1.40
) claims that

\displaystyle\sum_{u=0}^{7}\tilde{L}(Y(u))=4\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.45)

By (
1.43
),
this is reduced to

\displaystyle 2\{\tilde{L}(Y(0))+\tilde{L}(Y(0)^{-1})+\tilde{L}(Y(1))+\tilde{L}(Y(1)^{-1})\}=2\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.46)

Moreover,
since
Y(0)
and
Y(1)
are independent variables,
the identity (
1.46
) decomposes into two identical identities

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(Y(0))+\tilde{L}(Y(0)^{-1})=\tilde{L}(Y(1))+\tilde{L}(Y(1))^{-1})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.47)

This is Euler’s identity in the form (
1.29
).

Example 1.6
.

(X,X^{\prime})=(A_{2},A_{1})
.
We have
r=2
,
r^{\prime}=1
and
h=3
,
h^{\prime}=2
.
We set
Y_{1}(u):=Y_{1,1}(u)
and
Y_{2}(u):=Y_{2,1}(u)
.
Then,
the
Y
-system (
1.38
) looks as

\displaystyle Y_{1}(u+1)Y_{1}(u-1)

\displaystyle=1+Y_{2}(u),

(1.48)

\displaystyle Y_{2}(u+1)Y_{2}(u-1)

\displaystyle=1+Y_{1}(u).

(1.49)

The system decouples into two independent systems,
one for
Y_{a}(u)
’s with even
a+u
(the
even sector
),
and the other for
Y_{a}(u)
’s with odd
a+u
(the
odd sector
).
Let us concentrate on the even sector,
and we set

\displaystyle Y(u):=\begin{cases}Y_{1}(u)&\text{$u$ is odd,}\\
Y_{2}(u)&\text{$u$ is even.}\end{cases}

(1.50)

Then, the
Y
-system on the even sector is unified into a single relation

\displaystyle Y(u+1)Y(u-1)=1+Y(u).

(1.51)

We have already encountered this recurrence relation in (
1.31
),
where we identify
Y_{i}
therein with
Y(i-1)
.
Moreover, a general solution was already given in (
1.30
),
which yields the periodicity

\displaystyle Y(u+5)=Y(u).

(1.52)

Meanwhile,
(
1.39
) claims the periodicity

\displaystyle Y(u+10)=Y(u).

(1.53)

Thus, the periodicity (
1.39
) holds.
Next, the identity (
1.40
) restricting to the even sector claims
that

\displaystyle\sum_{u=0}^{9}\tilde{L}(Y(u))=6\frac{\pi^{2}}{6},

(1.54)

where the RHS of (
1.40
) was divided by 2.
By the periodicity (
1.52
),
it is reduced to

\displaystyle\sum_{u=0}^{4}\tilde{L}(Y(u))=3\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(1.55)

This is the pentagon identity in the form (
1.34
).

Thus, we saw that the two simplest cases of the conjectural DIs (
1.40
)
are Euler’s and the pentagon (Abel’s) identities.
In other words, DIs (
1.40
)
yield a vast generalization of these classic identities
in view of root systems.
This was indeed a remarkable and novel discovery in the long history of the dilogarithm.

Of course, we need to prove Conjecture
1.4
.
The conjecture consists of two parts.
The first part (
1.39
)
seems easier.
Indeed, for very low ranks, it is
proved by explicitly solving the
Y
-system.
Also,
for higher ranks,
it is possible to test and confirm the periodicity

numerically

with randomly chosen initial values.
In general, however, the problem turned out to be not easy.
It was proved only in the case
(X,X^{\prime})=(A_{r},A_{1})
by Gliozzi-Tateo
[
GT96
]

and Frenkel-Szenes
[
FS95
]
by giving the explicit solution (with different expressions to each other) of the
Y
-system.
Similarly, the second part (
1.39
) was proved only
in the same case by them
[
GT96
,
FS95
]

using their explicit solutions.
This was the situation in B.C.

The scene changed after the introduction of cluster algebras (in “A.D.”).
It was recognized that there is a cluster algebraic structure behind
the
Y
-system (
1.38
).
Using the structure, Conjecture
1.4

and their variants for other
Y
-systems were proved in full generality.
So, we may say that
it is exactly the cluster algebra structure
that is responsible for Conjecture
1.4
.
We are going to reveal this hidden and exciting relationship in the rest of Part I.

1.5
Reduction problem

Let us conclude this introductory chapter by presenting the following important result
by Wojtkowiak
[
Woj96
]
.

Theorem 1.7

(
[
Woj96
, Theorem 4.4]
)
.

Any functional identity of the dilogarithm with rational functions of one variable
as arguments is reduced to the trivial one by the successive application of
the pentagon identity.

Based on this fact, there is a conjecture that (or a question if) the same is true for
the DIs with
rational functions of
more than one variable

as arguments (e.g.,
[
Zag07
, §2.A]
).
Let us call it the
reduction problem
.
There are several case studies on the problem (e.g.,
[
Kir95
]
).
No counterexample is known,
but there is also no systematic study of the problem, as far as we know.
For
the DIs for
Y
-systems (and all DIs considered in this text),
we will answer the question (in a little unexpected way) later in Section
9.3
.

Notes

Many DIs
are presented in
[
Lew81
]
and
[
Kir95
]
.
Most of them seem to be of a different kind from the one we consider in this text.
We do not know how wide the cluster algebraic structure covers general DIs.

There is
another class of mathematical physics models called the
factorizable
S
-matrix models
(
[
KM90
,
Zam91b
,
RTV93
]
),
which are also based on the Yang-Baxter equation.
The dilogarithm sums (
1.37
) and the corresponding
Y
-systems appeared
also in the study of their thermodynamic property.

As for
Y
-systems,
see the review
[
KNS10
]
for the background in mathematical physics
and further information.
There are several variants of
Y
-systems other than the
one presented here,
and the conjectures on their periodicities and DIs for them were also given in B.C.,
for example,
for the nonsimply-laced
Y
-systems
[
Kun93
,
KN92
,
KNS94
]

and the sine-Gordon
Y
-systems
[
Tat95
,
GT96
]
.

Chapter 2
Quick course on cluster algebras

Let us return in A.D. (= after cluster algebra).
Cluster algebras introduced by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ02
]

are a class of commutative algebras
originated in Lie theory.
In this chapter, we present several basic notions and fundamental results on cluster algebras.
All contents are well-known in cluster algebra theory.
We skip proofs
because the readers can apply these results to the forthcoming problems
without knowing the proofs.
Proofs of all results in this chapter except for Theorem
2.13

are conveniently found in the companion monograph

[
Nak23
]
.
More specific instruction is found in Notes of the chapter.

2.1
Semifield

Let us begin with the notion of a semifield following
[
FZ02
]
.

Definition 2.1

(Semifield)
.

A multiplicative abelian group
\mathbb{P}

equipped with
a binary operation

\oplus

is called a
semifield
if
the following properties hold:
For any
a,\,b,\,c\in\mathbb{P}
,

\displaystyle a\oplus b

\displaystyle=b\oplus a,

(2.1)

\displaystyle(a\oplus b)\oplus c

\displaystyle=a\oplus(b\oplus c),

(2.2)

\displaystyle(a\oplus b)c

\displaystyle=ac\oplus bc.

(2.3)

The operation
\oplus
is called the
addition
in
\mathbb{P}
.
Note that there is no
subtraction
in
\mathbb{P}
.

For example, the set of positive real numbers
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a semifield
by the usual multiplication and addition.

The following two examples are especially important in cluster algebra theory.

Example 2.2
.

(a)
Universal semifield

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of
variables.
Let
\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{u})
be the rational function field of
\mathbf{u}

over
\mathbb{Q}
.
We say that a
rational function
f(\mathbf{u})\in\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{u})

has a
subtraction-free expression

if it is expressed as

f(\mathbf{u})=p(\mathbf{u})/q(\mathbf{u})
, where both
p(\mathbf{u})
and
q(\mathbf{u})

are
nonzero
polynomials in
\mathbf{u}
whose coefficients
are
nonnegative
integers.
For example,
f(\mathbf{u})=u_{1}^{2}-u_{1}+1=(u_{1}^{3}+1)/(u_{1}+1)
has a subtraction-free expression.
Let
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})

be the set of all rational functions in
\mathbf{u}

having subtraction-free expressions.
Then,
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{u})

is a semifield by the usual
multiplication and addition in
\mathbb{Q}(\mathbf{u})
.

(b)
Tropical semifield

\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
.
Let
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of
variables.
Let
\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
be the set of all
Laurent monomials in
\mathbf{u}
with coefficient 1,
which is a multiplicative abelian group by the usual
multiplication.
We define the addition
\oplus
by

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{a_{i}}\oplus\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{b_{i}}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}^{\min(a_{i},b_{i})}.

(2.4)

Then,
\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{u})
becomes a semifield.
The addition
\oplus
is called the
tropical sum
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
FZ03a
]
)
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
be the group algebra of
\mathbb{P}
(as a multiplicative group).
Then,
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
is a domain, i.e., there is no zero divisor other than 0.

Thanks to the lemma, we have the fraction field
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{P}
of
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
.

2.2
Seeds and mutations

Here, we introduce the most fundamental notions in cluster algebras,
namely,
seed
and
mutation
.

Definition 2.4

(Skew-symmetrizable matrix)
.

An integer square matrix
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}

is said to be
skew-symmetrizable

if there is a diagonal matrix
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})

whose diagonal entries
d_{i}
are positive rational numbers
such that
DB
is skew-symmetric,
i.e,

\displaystyle d_{i}b_{ij}=-d_{j}b_{ji}.

(2.5)

The above matrix
D
is called a
(left) skew-symmetrizer

of
B
,
which is not unique to
B
.

For example, any skew-symmetric matrix is skew-symmetrizable with
a skew-symmetrizer
D=I
.
For any skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
,
b_{ii}=0
holds by
(
2.5
).

Definition 2.5

(Seed/
Y
-seed)
.

Let
n
be any positive integer,
and let
\mathbb{P}
be any semifield.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a field that is isomorphic to
the rational function field of
n
-variables
with coefficients in the field
\mathbb{Q}\mathbb{P}
.

•

A
(labeled) seed
with coefficients in
\mathbb{P}

(or a seed in
\mathcal{F}
)

is a triplet

\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)

such that

\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is an

n
-tuple of algebraically independent and generating elements
in
\mathcal{F}
,

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
is an

n
-tuple of any elements
in
\mathbb{P}
,
and
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}

is an
n\times n
skew-symmetrizable (integer) matrix.

•

In the above, a pair
(\mathbf{y},B)
is called
a
Y
-seed in
\mathbb{P}
.

•

We call
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
, and
B

the
cluster
,
the
coefficient tuple
,
and the
exchange matrix

of
\Sigma
, respectively.
The variables
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are called
a
cluster variable

and a
coefficient
, respectively.
In this text, we mainly call them
x
-
and
y
-variables
, respectively.

•

We call
n
,
\mathbb{P}
, and
\mathcal{F}
the
rank
,
the
coefficient semifield
,
and the
ambient field

of a seed
\Sigma
, respectively,
and also of the forthcoming cluster patterns, cluster algebras, etc.

For any seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
,
we attach an
n
-tuple

\hat{\mathbf{y}}=(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})

of elements in the ambient field
\mathcal{F}
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{i}=y_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}}.

(2.6)

They are called
\hat{y}
-variables

and play an important role in cluster algebra theory.

For any integer
a
,
we define

\displaystyle[a]_{+}:=\max(a,0).

(2.7)

We have a useful equality

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=[a]_{+}-[-a]_{+}.

(2.8)

Definition 2.6

(Seed mutation)
.

For any seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)

and
k=1,\,\dots,\,n
,
we define a new seed

\Sigma^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},B^{\prime})

of the same kind
by the following rule:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{[-b_{jk}]_{+}}\Biggr)\frac{1+\hat{y}_{k}}{1\oplus y_{k}}&i=k,\\
x_{i}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(2.9)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i}y_{k}^{[b_{ki}]_{+}}(1\oplus y_{k})^{-b_{ki}}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(2.10)

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-b_{ij}&\text{$i=k$ or $j=k$,}\\
b_{ij}+b_{ik}[b_{kj}]_{+}+[-b_{ik}]_{+}b_{kj}&i,\,j\neq k,\end{cases}

(2.11)

where
\hat{y}_{k}
in (
2.9
) is a
\hat{y}
-variable defined in (
2.6
).
The seed
\Sigma^{\prime}
is called the
mutation of
\Sigma
in direction
k
,
and denoted by
\mu_{k}(\Sigma)=\mu_{k}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
.

One can easily check the following facts.

1.

Any skew-symmetrizer
D
of
B
is also a skew-symmetrizer of
B^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
B^{\prime}
is also skew-symmetrizable.

2.

We have
\Sigma=\mu_{k}(\Sigma^{\prime})
. Namely, each mutation
\mu_{k}
is involutive.

3.

x^{\prime}_{1}
, …,
x^{\prime}_{n}
are algebraically independent and generating elements
in
\mathcal{F}
. Thus,
\Sigma^{\prime}
is indeed a seed.

4.

We have
\det B^{\prime}=\det B
. In particular,
B^{\prime}
is nonsingular if and only if
B
is so.

5.

We say that a square matrix is
indecomposable
if it is not a direct sum of two square matrices
up to the simultaneous change of the row and column indices.
Then,
B^{\prime}
is indecomposable if and only if
B
is indecomposable.

The following fact exhibits a certain duality between
x
- and
y
-variables.

Proposition 2.7

(
[
FZ07
, Prop. 3.9]
)
.

The
\hat{y}
-variables in
(
2.6
) mutate as
y
-variables in
\mathcal{F}
. Namely, we have

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\hat{y}_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
\hat{y}_{i}\hat{y}_{k}^{[b_{ki}]_{+}}(1+\hat{y}_{k})^{-b_{ki}}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(2.12)

For example, for
i\neq k
,
by using the fact
b_{kk}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{y}^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=y^{\prime}_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x^{\prime}_{j}{}^{b^{\prime}_{ji}}

\displaystyle=y_{i}y_{k}^{[b_{ki}]_{+}}(1\oplus y_{k})^{-b_{ki}}\Biggl(\prod_{\scriptstyle j=1\atop\scriptstyle j\neq k}^{n}x_{j}^{b_{ji}+b_{jk}[b_{ki}]_{+}+[-b_{jk}]_{+}b_{ki}}\Biggr)

\displaystyle\quad\ \times\Biggl(x_{k}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{[-b_{jk}]_{+}}\Biggr)\frac{1+\hat{y}_{k}}{1\oplus y_{k}}\Biggr)^{-b_{ki}}

\displaystyle=\hat{y}_{i}\hat{y}_{k}^{[b_{ki}]_{+}}(1+\hat{y}_{k})^{-b_{ki}}.

We define the action of the symmetric group
S_{n}
on seeds.

Definition 2.8

(
S_{n}
-action)
.

For a seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
and a permutation
\nu
of
\{1,\,\dots,\,n\}
,
we define the action of
\nu
on
\Sigma
by

\displaystyle\nu\Sigma=(\nu\mathbf{x},\nu\mathbf{y},\nu B),

(2.13)

where

\nu\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
,

\nu\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
,

\nu B=B^{\prime}
are defined by

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}=x_{\nu^{-1}(i)},\quad y^{\prime}_{i}=y_{\nu^{-1}(i)},\quad b^{\prime}_{ij}=b_{\nu^{-1}(i)\nu^{-1}(j)}.\quad

(2.14)

This is a left action of the symmetric group
S_{n}
of degree
n
; namely,
we have
\tau(\nu\Sigma)=\tau\nu(\Sigma)

for
\nu,\,\tau\in S_{n}
.

One can easily confirm that
the action is compatible with mutations in the following sense:

\displaystyle\mu_{\nu(k)}(\nu\Sigma_{t})

\displaystyle=\nu(\mu_{k}(\Sigma_{t})).

(2.15)

2.3
Cluster patterns and
Y
-patterns

Let
\mathbb{T}_{n}
be the
n
-regular tree
graph;
Namely, it is a connected tree graph such that exactly
n
edges are attached to each vertex of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
Moreover, edges are labeled by
1
, …,
n

so that the edges attached to each vertex
have different labels.
By abusing the notation,
the set of vertices of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
is also denoted by

\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
See Figure
2.1
.
We say that a pair of vertices in
t
and
t^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}

are
k
-
adjacent
, or
t^{\prime}
is
k
-
adjacent to
t
,
if they are connected with an edge labeled by
k
.

2
1
2
1
n=2
1
n=1
1
2
3
2
3
1
3
1
2
n=3

Figure 2.1:
The
n
-regular trees
\mathbb{T}_{n}

for
n=1
,
2
,
3
.

Definition 2.9

(Cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern/
B
-pattern)
.

Fix the rank
n
,
the coefficient semifield
\mathbb{P}
,
and the ambient field
\mathcal{F}
for seeds.

•

A collection of seeds
\mathbf{\Sigma}=\{\Sigma_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

indexed by
\mathbb{T}_{n}

is called a
cluster pattern
with coefficients in
\mathbb{P}

if,
for any vertices
t,\,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
that are
k
-adjacent,
the equality
\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}=\mu_{k}(\Sigma_{t})
holds.

•

The collection of
Y
-seeds

\mathbf{\Upsilon}=\{\Upsilon_{t}=(\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
extracted from a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is called
a
Y
-pattern
in
\mathbb{P}
,
or the
Y
-pattern

of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.

•

The collection of exchange matrices

\mathbf{B}=\{B_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
extracted from a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is called
a
B
-pattern
,
or the
B
-pattern
of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.

We arbitrarily choose a distinguished
vertex (the
initial vertex
)
t_{0}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.
Any cluster pattern

\mathbf{\Sigma}=\{\Sigma_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
is uniquely determined from
the
initial seed

\Sigma_{t_{0}}
at the initial vertex
t_{0}

by repeating mutations along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
.

In the above, if
B_{t}
is skew-symmetric for some
t
, it is skew-symmetric for any
t
.
Such a cluster pattern,
Y
-pattern, or a
B
-pattern is said to be
skew-symmetric
.
Similarly, if
B_{t}
is nonsingular for some
t
, it is nonsingular for any
t
.
Such a cluster pattern,
Y
-pattern, or a
B
-pattern is said to be
nonsingular
.

For a seed
\Sigma_{t}=(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})

in a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
we use the notation

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t}=(x_{1;t},\dots,x_{n;t}),\quad\mathbf{y}_{t}=(y_{1;t},\dots,y_{n;t}),\quad B_{t}=(b_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}.

(2.16)

For the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}=(\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}},\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}},B_{t_{0}})
, we often drop the indices
t_{0}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}),\quad\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n}),\quad B_{t_{0}}=B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n},

(2.17)

if there is no confusion.
We also use similar notations for
\hat{y}
-variables
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=(\hat{y}_{1;t},\dots,\hat{y}_{n;t})

and the initial

\hat{y}
-variables
\hat{\mathbf{y}}_{t_{0}}=\hat{\mathbf{y}}=(\hat{y}_{1},\dots,\hat{y}_{n})
.

Every
x
-variable
x_{i;t}
is regarded as
a rational function of the initial

x
-variables
\mathbf{x}
by repeating the mutations (
2.9
).
The following stronger fact served as the guiding principle for the formulation of cluster algebras by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ02
]
.

Theorem 2.10

(Laurent phenomenon
[
FZ02
, Thm. 3.1]
)
.

Every
x
-variable
x_{i;t}
is expressed as a Laurent polynomial in
\mathbf{x}
with
coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
.

Now, we give the main notion in cluster algebra theory.

Definition 2.11

(Cluster algebra)
.

For a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
,
the associated
cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}(\mathbf{\Sigma})

is the
\mathbb{Z}\mathbb{P}
-subalgebra of the ambient field
\mathcal{F}

generated by all
x
-variables
x_{i,t}
(
i=1,\,\dots,\,n;\,t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
).

We vaguely refer to the totality of the objects and notions related to cluster algebras and/or cluster patterns as

cluster algebraic structure
.
In this text, we focus on cluster patterns rather than cluster algebras.
Moreover, we mainly work with
Y
-patterns.
In fact, for the most part, we do not use
x
-variables.
Nevertheless, we include
x
-variables in the presentation here,
because
it provides a better perspective on how cluster algebra theory
is related to the dilogarithm.

Definition 2.12
.

We say that a cluster pattern is
of finite type

if there are only finitely many distinct seeds.
We say that a
Y
-pattern is
of finite type

if it is the
Y
-pattern of a cluster pattern of finite type.

1
2
r-1
r
B_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}
1
2
3
4
F_{4}
1
2
r-1
r
C_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}
1
2
G_{2}

Figure 2.2:
Nonsimply-laced Dynkin diagrams of finite type

For any skew-symmetrizable integer matrix
B=(b_{ij})
,
we define a symmetrizable integer matrix
A(B)=(a_{ij})
of the same size
by

\displaystyle a_{ij}=\begin{cases}2&i=j,\\
-|b_{ij}|&i\neq j.\end{cases}

(2.18)

The matrix
A(B)
is called the
Cartan counterpart
of
B
.
It is well known that the (indecomposable) Cartan matrices of finite type
are classified by the Dynkin diagrams of types
A_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}
,
B_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}
,
C_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}
,
D_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r}
,

E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
,
F_{4}
, and
G_{2}
.
The simply-laced ones (types
ADE
) already appeared in Figure
1.4
.
So, we only present the nonsimply-laced ones in Figure
2.2
.
The following theorem shows a strong similarity
between cluster algebras and root systems.

Theorem 2.13

(Finite type classification
[
FZ03a
, Thm. 1.4]
)
.

Suppose that the exchange matrices of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}

are indecomposable.
Then,
a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is of finite type
if and only if it contains an exchange matrix
B_{t}
such that
its Cartan counterpart
A(B_{t})
is a Cartan matrix of finite type.
Moreover,
the Dynkin type of
A(B_{t})
is uniquely determined from the underlying
B
-pattern.

2.4
Examples: Cluster patterns of types
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

Let us present the two simplest examples of cluster patterns.

Example 2.14

(Type
A_{1}
)
.

Consider the simplest example with
n=1
.
The initial seed is given by
\Sigma_{t_{0}}=\Sigma=(x_{1},y_{1},B=(0))
.
The Cartan counterpart
A(B)=(2)
of
B
is the Cartan matrix of type
A_{1}
.
So, the corresponding cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is called a cluster pattern of type
A_{1}
.
The only seed in
\mathbf{\Sigma}
other than
\Sigma
is
\Sigma^{\prime}=\mu_{1}(\Sigma)=(x^{\prime}_{1},y^{\prime}_{1},B^{\prime}=(0))
,
where
\hat{y}_{1}=y_{1}
, and

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{1}=\frac{1+y_{1}}{1\oplus y_{1}}x_{1}^{-1},\quad y^{\prime}_{1}=y_{1}^{-1}.

(2.19)

Example 2.15

(Type
A_{2}
)
.

Consider the next simplest example with
n=2
.
We consider the following arrangement of
a cluster pattern on
\mathbb{T}_{2}
.

\displaystyle\cdots\ \!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\scriptstyle{2}\\[-9.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\\[-7.22743pt]
\end{array}\Sigma_{t_{-2}}\ \!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\scriptstyle{1}\\[-9.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\\[-7.22743pt]
\end{array}\Sigma_{t_{-1}}\ \!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\scriptstyle{2}\\[-9.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\\[-7.22743pt]
\end{array}\Sigma_{t_{0}}\ \!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\scriptstyle{1}\\[-9.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\\[-7.22743pt]
\end{array}\Sigma_{t_{1}}\ \!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\scriptstyle{2}\\[-9.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\\[-7.22743pt]
\end{array}\Sigma_{t_{2}}\ \!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\scriptstyle{1}\\[-9.0pt]
-\!\!\!-\\[-7.22743pt]
\end{array}\cdots.

Let
t_{0}
be the initial vertex. Below we use simplified notations such as
\Sigma_{t_{s}}=\Sigma_{s}
,
B_{t_{s}}=B_{s}
,
\mathbf{x}_{t_{s}}=\mathbf{x}_{s}
,
\mathbf{y}_{t_{s}}=\mathbf{y}_{s}
, etc.
We consider the
initial seed
\Sigma_{0}=\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
with

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad A(B)=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\\
-1&2\end{pmatrix}.

(2.38)

The matrix
A(B)
is the Cartan matrix of type
A_{2}
.
Thus, the corresponding cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
is called a cluster pattern of type
A_{2}
.
The initial
\hat{y}
-variables
(
2.6
) are given by

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{1}=y_{1}x_{2},\quad\hat{y}_{2}=y_{2}x_{1}^{-1}.

(2.39)

By the mutations (
2.11
),
the exchange matrices are given by

\displaystyle B_{s}=\begin{cases}B&\mbox{$s$: even},\\
-B&\mbox{$s$: odd}.\\
\end{cases}

(2.40)

Accordingly, we have,
for even
s
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{1;{s+1}}=x_{1;s}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{1;s}}{1\oplus y_{1;s}},\\
x_{2;{s+1}}=x_{s},\end{cases}\quad\begin{cases}y_{1;{s+1}}=y_{1;s}^{-1},\\
y_{2;{s+1}}=y_{2;s}(1\oplus y_{1;s}),\end{cases}

(2.41)

and,
for odd
s
,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}x_{1;{s+1}}=x_{1;s},\\
\displaystyle x_{2;{s+1}}=x_{2;s}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{2;s}}{1\oplus y_{2;s}},\end{cases}\quad\begin{cases}y_{1;{s+1}}=y_{1;s}(1\oplus y_{2;s}),\\
y_{2;{s+1}}=y_{2;s}^{-1}.\end{cases}

(2.42)

Let us calculate the mutations of
x
- and
y
-variables.
To make the effort minimal,
we first calculate
y
-variables,
then calculate
x
-variables
with the help of (
2.12
) and (
2.39
).
Then,
we have the following result: (If you have never calculated it by yourself before,
we strongly recommend doing it now!)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{1;1}=x_{1}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{1}}{1\oplus y_{1}},\\
x_{2;1}=x_{2},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{1;1}=y_{1}^{-1},\\
y_{2;1}=y_{2}(1\oplus y_{1}),\end{cases}

(2.43)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{1;2}=x_{1}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{1}}{1\oplus y_{1}},\\
\displaystyle x_{2;2}=x_{2}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{2}+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}}{1\oplus y_{2}\oplus y_{1}y_{2}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{1;2}=y_{1}^{-1}(1\oplus y_{2}\oplus y_{1}y_{2}),\\
y_{2;2}=y_{2}^{-1}(1\oplus y_{1})^{-1},\end{cases}

(2.44)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{1;3}=x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{2}}{1\oplus y_{2}},\\
\displaystyle x_{2;3}=x_{2}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{2}+\hat{y}_{1}\hat{y}_{2}}{1\oplus y_{2}\oplus y_{1}y_{2}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{1;3}=y_{1}(1\oplus y_{2}\oplus y_{1}y_{2})^{-1},\\
y_{2;3}=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1\oplus y_{2}),\end{cases}

(2.45)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{1;4}=x_{1}x_{2}^{-1}\frac{1+\hat{y}_{2}}{1\oplus y_{2}},\\
\displaystyle x_{2;4}=x_{1},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{1;4}=y_{2}^{-1},\\
y_{2;4}=y_{1}y_{2}(1\oplus y_{2})^{-1},\end{cases}

(2.46)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{1;5}=x_{2},\\
\displaystyle x_{2;5}=x_{1},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\begin{cases}y_{1;5}=y_{2},\\
y_{2;5}=y_{1}.\end{cases}

(2.47)

We observe the following properties in the above.

•

(Laurent phenomenon).
As claimed in Theorem
2.10
,
all
x
-variables are expressed as Laurent polynomials in the initial
x
-variables
\mathbf{x}

due to the systematic reduction of rational functions starting at
s=3
.

•

(Periodicity/Synchronicity).
Even though
x
- and
y
-variables
mutate differently, they look closely related.
In particular,
they enjoy the same (half) periodicity at
s=5
.
Moreover, together with the result (
2.40
),
we have the following (half) periodicity of seeds

\displaystyle\Sigma_{5}=\tau_{12}\Sigma_{0},

(2.48)

where
\tau_{12}
is the transposition of 1 and 2.
This periodicity is interpreted as the periodicity of the triangulations of a pentagon
under the alternating flips of diagonals
[
FZ03a
, §12.3]
.
Thus, we call it the
pentagon periodicity
of the cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.

2.5
Separation formulas and tropicalization

In
[
FZ07
]
it was clarified that
there is an important structure in seeds that is compatible with the tropicalization.

2.5.1
Separation formulas

We first introduce related notions
called
C
-matrices,
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials.
Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be any cluster pattern,
and let
\mathbf{B}
be the
B
-pattern of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
.
Let
t_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be a given initial vertex.

We introduce a collection of
matrices
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}=\{C_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

called a
C
-pattern
,
which is uniquely determined from
\mathbf{B}
and
t_{0}
, therefore, eventually only from
t_{0}
and
B_{t_{0}}
.

Definition 2.16

(
C
-matrices)
.

The
C
-matrices
C_{t}=(c_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}

(t\in\mathbb{T}_{n})

of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with an initial vertex
t_{0}

are
n\times n

integer matrices
uniquely determined
by the following initial condition
and the mutation rule:

\displaystyle C_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=I,

(2.49)

\displaystyle c_{ij;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-c_{ik;t}&j=k,\\
c_{ij;t}+c_{ik;t}[b_{kj;t}]_{+}+[-c_{ik;t}]_{+}b_{kj;t}&j\neq k,\end{cases}

(2.50)

where
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent.
Each column vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}=(c_{ji;t})_{j=1}^{n}
of a matrix
C_{t}
is called
a
c
-vector
.

The mutation (
2.50
), in particular, implies

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{k;t^{\prime}}=-\mathbf{c}_{k;t}.

(2.51)

One can easily check that the mutation (
2.50
) is involutive,
using (
2.8
), (
2.11
), and (
2.51
).

Under the same assumption of
C
-matrices,
we introduce another collection of
matrices
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}=\{G_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

called a
G
-pattern
,
which is uniquely determined from
\mathbf{B}
,
t_{0}
,
and
\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}
defined above,
therefore, eventually only from
t_{0}
and
B_{t_{0}}
.

Definition 2.17

(
G
-matrices)
.

The
G
-matrices
G_{t}=(g_{ij;t})_{i,j=1}^{n}

(t\in\mathbb{T}_{n})

of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with an initial vertex
t_{0}

are
n\times n

integer matrices uniquely determined
by the following initial condition
and the mutation rule:

\displaystyle G_{t_{0}}

\displaystyle=I,

(2.52)

\displaystyle g_{ij;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-g_{ik;t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}g_{i\ell;t}[-b_{\ell k;t}]_{+}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}b_{i\ell;t_{0}}[-c_{\ell k;t}]_{+}&j=k,\\
g_{ij;t}&j\neq k,\end{cases}

(2.53)

where
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent.
Each column vector
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}=(g_{ji;t})_{j=1}^{n}
of a matrix
G_{t}
is called
a
g
-vector
.

The following relation
between
C
- and
G
-matrices
can be proved by
the induction on
t
along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
from
t_{0}
:
(
the first duality
)

\displaystyle G_{t}B_{t}=B_{t_{0}}C_{t}.

(2.54)

Using this relation,
one can show that the mutation (
2.53
) is involutive.

Let

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of variables.
(They are just formal variables at this moment,
but they will be related with the initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}

of
\mathbf{\Sigma}
soon. So, the notation is compatible.)
Under the same assumption of
C
-matrices,
we introduce a collection of
rational functions in
\mathbf{y}
having subtraction-free expressions

\mathbf{F}=\mathbf{F}^{t_{0}}=\{F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})\}_{i=1,\,\dots,\,n;\,t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

called an
F
-pattern
,
which is uniquely determined from
\mathbf{B}
,
t_{0}
,
and
\mathbf{C}^{t_{0}}
, therefore, eventually only from
t_{0}
and
B_{t_{0}}
.

Definition 2.18

(
F
-polynomials)
.

The
F
-polynomials
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})

(i=1,\,\dots,\,n;\,t\in\mathbb{T}_{n})

of a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with an initial vertex
t_{0}

are uniquely determined
by the following initial condition
and the mutation rule:

\displaystyle F_{i;t_{0}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=1,

(2.55)

\displaystyle F_{i;t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\frac{\displaystyle M_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})}{\displaystyle F_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})}&i=k,\\
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(2.56)

where
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
k
-adjacent,
and
M_{k,t}(\mathbf{y})
is a rational function of
\mathbf{y}
(actually a polynomial in
\mathbf{y}
as we see below)
defined by

\displaystyle M_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{[c_{jk;t}]_{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{[b_{jk;t}]_{+}}+\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{[-c_{jk;t}]_{+}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}(\mathbf{y})^{[-b_{jk;t}]_{+}}.

(2.57)

We have

\displaystyle M_{k;t}(\mathbf{y})=M_{k;t^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}).

(2.58)

Therefore,
the mutation
(
2.56
) is involutive.

As the name suggests, rational functions
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})

are reduced to polynomials in
\mathbf{y}
.
This is essentially a restatement of the Laurent phenomenon in
Theorem
2.10
.

Proposition 2.19

(
[
FZ07
, Prop. 3.6]
)
.

Any
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
is a polynomial in
\mathbf{y}

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}
.

We stress that
C
- and
G
-matrices and
F
-polynomials
depend not only on
t
but also on the choice of the initial vertex
t_{0}
.

Now, we are ready to present key formulas in cluster algebra theory.

Theorem 2.20

(Separation formulas
[
FZ07
, Prop. 3.15, Cor. 6.13]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
,
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
be the
initial variables at
t_{0}
.
Then, the following formulas hold.

\displaystyle x_{i;t}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{g_{ji;t}}\biggr)\frac{F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})}{F_{i;t}{|_{\mathbb{P}}}({\mathbf{y}})},

(2.59)

\displaystyle y_{i;t}

\displaystyle=\biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{c_{ji;t}}\biggr)\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}{|_{\mathbb{P}}}(\mathbf{y})^{b_{ji;t}},

(2.60)

where
F_{i;t}{|_{\mathbb{P}}}({\mathbf{y}})\in\mathbb{P}

is obtained from the
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})

by replacing
+
in
a subtraction-free expression of

F_{i;t}({\mathbf{y}})
with
\oplus
for
\mathbb{P}
.

Thanks to the above formulas, the study of cluster patterns is reduced to
the study of
C
- and
G
-matrices, and
F
-polynomials.

Example 2.21

(Type
A_{2}
)
.

Let us consider the cluster pattern of type
A_{2}

in Example
2.15
.
By comparing the result therein with the separation formulas
(
2.59
) and (
2.60
),
one can quickly read off
C
- and
G
-matrices and
F
-polynomials
as follows:

\displaystyle C_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle G_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1;0}(\mathbf{y})=1,\\
F_{2;0}(\mathbf{y})=1,\\
\end{cases}

(2.61)

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle G_{1}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1;1}(\mathbf{y})=1+y_{1},\\
F_{2;1}(\mathbf{y})=1,\\
\end{cases}

(2.62)

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle G_{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1;2}(\mathbf{y})=1+y_{1},\\
F_{2;2}(\mathbf{y})=1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2},\end{cases}

(2.63)

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle G_{3}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1;3}(\mathbf{y})=1+y_{2},\\
F_{2;3}(\mathbf{y})=1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2},\\
\end{cases}

(2.64)

\displaystyle C_{4}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle G_{4}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1;4}(\mathbf{y})=1+y_{2},\\
F_{2;4}(\mathbf{y})=1,\\
\end{cases}

(2.65)

\displaystyle C_{5}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle G_{5}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\

\displaystyle\begin{cases}F_{1;5}(\mathbf{y})=1,\\
F_{2;5}(\mathbf{y})=1.\\
\end{cases}

(2.66)

One can observe the following nontrivial properties in the above results.

1.

(Unit constant property). Every
F
-polynomial has
a constant term 1.

2.

(Laurent positivity). Every
F
-polynomial has
no negative coefficient.

3.

(Sign-coherence of
c
-vectors).
Every
c
-vector is a nonzero vector,
and its nonzero components have the same sign.

4.

(Second duality).
The following relation between
C
- and
G
-matrices holds:

\displaystyle G_{t}^{T}C_{t}=I,

(2.67)

where
M^{T}
is the transpose of a matrix
M
.
(The general form is given later in (
2.74
).)

2.5.2
Tropicalization

The

tropicalization
is a new and common idea in recent mathematics.
See
[
SS09
]
for the background of the notion and the terminology.
Remarkably, the tropicalization is naturally built into cluster algebra theory.

Let us
consider the semifields
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
and

\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{y})
with
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
as common variables.
We have a unique semifield homomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}:\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathrm{Trop}(\mathbf{y})

(2.68)

such that
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}(y_{i})=y_{i}
for any
i=1
, …,
n
.
We call it the
tropicalization (homomorphism)
.
For example, for
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})
,

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}\left(\frac{3y_{1}y_{2}^{2}y_{3}^{2}+2y_{1}^{2}y_{2}y_{3}}{3y_{2}^{2}+y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{2}+y_{1}y_{2}^{3}y_{3}}\right)=\frac{y_{1}y_{2}y_{3}}{y_{2}^{2}}=y_{1}y_{2}^{-1}y_{3}.

(2.69)

Roughly speaking, it extracts the “leading monomial” of

f(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
.

To apply the tropicalization,
we especially consider a cluster pattern whose coefficient semifield
is
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
,

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
,
and its initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}

coincide with
\mathbf{y}
.
We call such coefficients
free coefficients

or
free
y
-variables
at
t_{0}
.
We also call its
Y
-pattern a
free
Y
-pattern
.
(This terminology is not common, and it is used in
[
Nak23
]
.)
Each free
y
-variable
y_{i;t}
is an
element of
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
.
Thus, we may apply the tropicalization

\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}
in (
2.68
) to
y_{i;t}
.
Then, we have the following result.

Proposition 2.22

(
[
FZ07
, Prop. 5.2]
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}(y_{i;t})

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y^{\mathbf{c}_{i;t}}:=}\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{c_{ji;t}},

(2.70)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}(F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y}))

\displaystyle=1.

(2.71)

Namely, in the separation formula (
2.60
),
the monomial with powers given by the
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
is
the
tropical part
of the free
y
-variable
y_{i;t}
,
while the product of
F
-polynomials is the
nontropical part
.
In other words, the tropicalization
is the operation of “forgetting
F
-polynomials” in (
2.60
).

For a cluster pattern
whose coefficient semifield
is
\mathbb{P}=\rm{Trop}(\mathbf{y})
and
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}
,
the coefficients are called
principal coefficients
at
t_{0}
.
As we have seen, the principal coefficients at
t_{0}
are obtained by applying
the tropicalization
to the free coefficients at
t_{0}
.
So, we also call them
tropical
y
-variables
.

As for the separation formula (
2.59
) for
x
-variables,
the above interpretation of the tropicalization
with respect to
the initial
x
-variables
\mathbf{x}
does not hold
because the
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})
therein is
a polynomial in
\hat{\mathbf{y}}
, not in
\mathbf{x}
.
Nevertheless, in view of the duality structure between the separation formulas
(
2.59
) and (
2.60
),
we regard
the monomial

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{\mathbf{g}_{i;t}}:=}\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{g_{ji;t}}

(2.72)

as
the
tropical part
of the
x
-variable
x_{i;t}
,
in the sense that it is formally obtained by “forgetting
F
-polynomials (= setting

F
-polynomials to
1
)” in (
2.59
).
Correspondingly, we regard the factor
F_{i;t}(\hat{\mathbf{y}})/F_{i;t}{|_{\mathbb{P}}}({\mathbf{y}})
in (
2.59
)
the
nontropical part
of the
x
-variable
x_{i;t}
.

2.6
Advanced results

The results below are the consequences of
Theorems
2.24
and
2.29
.
Both theorems had been important and long-standing conjectures
until they were proved by Gross-Hacking-Keel-Kontsevich
[
GHKK18
]

by the scattering diagram method,
which we explain later in Chapter
8
.
Thus, they are regarded as advanced results.

2.6.1
Sign-coherence of
c
-vectors and duality

We continue considering the situation for Theorem
2.20
.

Definition 2.23
.

We say that a vector
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
is
positive
(resp.
negative
)
if it is a nonzero vector and all nonzero components are positive (resp. negative).

The following theorem was conjectured by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ07
]

and proved by Gross-Hacking-Keel-Kontsevich
[
GHKK18
]

in full generality
after several affirmative partial results
(e.g.,
[
DWZ10
,
Pla11
,
Nag13
]
).

Theorem 2.24

(Sign-coherence of
c
-vectors
[
GHKK18
, Cor. 5.5]
)
.

Every
c
-vector
c_{i;t}
is either positive or negative.

This is one of the cornerstones
of the cluster algebra theory
because many nontrivial and important results follow from it.
Here, we limit ourselves to the ones that we will use in the text.

It was shown by
[
FZ07
]
that the theorem is
equivalent to the following one.
(In fact,
[
GHKK18
]
proved Theorem
2.25
instead of Theorem
2.24
)

Theorem 2.25

(Unit constant property
[
GHKK18
, Cor. 5.5]
)
.

Every
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})
has constant term 1.

Note that the property (
2.71
) is weaker than Theorem
2.25
.
For example, we have
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}(y_{1}+y_{2})=1
.

Thanks to Theorem
2.24
,
the following notion is well-defined.

Definition 2.26

(Tropical sign)
.

To each
c
-vector
c_{i;t}
, we assign the

tropical sign

\varepsilon_{i;t}\in\{1,-1\}
by

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i;t}=\begin{cases}1&\text{$c_{i;t}$ is positive},\\
-1&\text{$c_{i;t}$ is negative}.\end{cases}

(2.73)

We stress that the sign
\varepsilon_{i;t}
depends on
the choice of the initial vertex
t_{0}
.

The following duality between
C
- and
G
-matrices
was shown by Nakanishi-Zelevinsky
[
NZ12
]
under the assumption of Theorem
2.24
.

Theorem 2.27

(
Second duality

[
NZ12
, Thm. 1.2]
)
.

Let
D
be a common skew-symmetrizer of
B_{t}
’s.
Then, the following equality holds:

\displaystyle D^{-1}G_{t}^{T}DC_{t}=I,

(2.74)

or, equivalently,

\displaystyle D^{-1}C_{t}^{T}DG_{t}=I.

(2.75)

We have the following easy consequences of
Theorem
2.27
.

Proposition 2.28
.

(1) (Unimodularity
[
NZ12
, Prop. 4.2]
).

\displaystyle|C_{t}|=|G_{t}|=\pm 1.

(2.76)

(2)

[
NZ12
, Eq. (2.9)]
).
The matrix
B_{t}
is determined by
B_{t_{0}}
and
C_{t}

as follows:

\displaystyle DB_{t}=C_{t}^{T}(DB_{t_{0}})C_{t}.

(2.77)

2.6.2
Laurent positivity, detropicalization, and synchronicity

The following theorem,
which is another cornerstone of the cluster algebra theory,
was also conjectured by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ02
,
FZ07
]

and proved by Gross-Hacking-Keel-Kontsevich
[
GHKK18
]

in full generality
after several affirmative partial results
(e.g.,
[
MSW11
,
KQ14
,
LS15
]
).

Theorem 2.29

(Laurent positivity
[
GHKK18
, Cor. 0.4]
)
.

Every
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}(\mathbf{y})

has no negative coefficient.

In accordance with the
S_{n}
-action on seeds in Definition
2.8
,
we define the
S_{n}
-action on
C
- and
G
-matrices and
F
-polynomials as

\displaystyle C^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu C_{t},

\displaystyle\quad c^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=c_{i\nu^{-1}(j);t},

(2.78)

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu G_{t},

\displaystyle\quad g^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=g_{i\nu^{-1}(j);t},

(2.79)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\nu\mathbf{F}_{t},

\displaystyle\quad F^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=F_{\nu^{-1}(i);t}.

(2.80)

This is compatible with the separation formulas (
2.59
) and (
2.60
).

The following theorem was proved by Cao-Huang-Li
[
CHL20
]

for
\nu=\mathrm{id}
using
Theorems
2.24
,
2.25
,
2.27
,
2.29

and the separation formulas all together.
It was generalized to any permutation
\nu
by Nakanishi

[
Nak21
]
.

Theorem 2.30

(Detropicalization
[
CHL20
, Thm. 2.5]
,

[
Nak21
, Thm. 4.7]
)
.

For any
t,\,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}

and any permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
,
the following facts hold:

\displaystyle G_{t}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}\quad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\mathbf{F}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{F}_{t^{\prime}},\quad\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}},

(2.81)

\displaystyle C_{t}=\nu C_{t^{\prime}}\quad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad\mathbf{F}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{F}_{t^{\prime}},\quad\mathbf{y}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}.

(2.82)

In other words, the
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
are uniquely
determined by their tropical parts
G_{t}
and
C_{t}
, respectively.

One can complete Theorem
2.30

as the following equivalences of periodicities.

Theorem 2.31

(Synchronicity
[
Nak21
, Thm. 5.3]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{\Sigma}
be
a cluster pattern with free coefficients at
t_{0}
.
Then,
for given
t,\,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
and a given permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
,
the following five conditions are equivalent:

(a).

G_{t}=\nu G_{t^{\prime}}
.

(b).

C_{t}=\nu C_{t^{\prime}}
.

(c).

\mathbf{x}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{x}_{t^{\prime}}
.

(d).

\mathbf{y}_{t}=\nu\mathbf{y}_{t^{\prime}}
.

(e).

\Sigma_{t}=\nu\Sigma_{t^{\prime}}
.

Remark 2.32
.

The assumption of
free
coefficients guarantees the implication
from the condition (d) to the other conditions. The rest of the implications hold
for any coefficients.
In particular,
the implication (a)
\Longleftrightarrow
(c) implies that the periodicity of

x
-variables is independent of their coefficients.

Notes

Here is a bibliographic guide for learning the basics of cluster algebra theory.
The foundation of cluster algebra theory was
established by a series of papers “Cluster Algebras I–IV” by Fomin-Zelevinsky together with Berenstein
[
FZ02
,
FZ03a
,
BFZ05
,
FZ07
]
.
Also, the papers on
Y
-systems
[
FZ03b
]

and the quantum cluster algebras
[
BZ05
]

may be included in the series.
Other foundational works on cluster algebras and cluster varieties are given
by Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09a
,
FG06
,
FG07
,
FG09c
]
.
A comprehensive treatment is given in the ongoing writing

[
FWZ16
]
.
Reviews, monographs, and textbooks with different emphases are available
(e.g.,
[
Car06
]
,
[
Kel10
]
,
[
GSV10
]
,
[
Kel13a
]
,
[
Mar13
]
,
[
Wil14
]
,

[
Pla18
]
,
[
GR17
]
,
[
Nak23
]
).

2.13

are conveniently found in the companion monograph

as specified below.

.
