Chapter 3
Dilogarithm identities in
Y
-patterns

We present the dilogarithm identity associated with
any period of a free
Y
-pattern.
This is the
leitmotif
throughout the text,
and we will give several proofs and variations of the identity with
various methods, techniques, and perspectives.
Here, we give the first proof (actually two alternative proofs)
via the constancy condition by Frenkel-Szenes.
We call it the
algebraic method

because it is most directly related to the algebraic structure of
seeds and mutations in a cluster pattern compared with other methods.

3.1
Periodicity

Let us introduce the central idea of the whole text, namely,

periodicity
in a cluster pattern and a
Y
-pattern.
Consider a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with
free coefficients
at
t_{0}
and its
Y
-pattern.
Consider sequences of mutations therein

\displaystyle\Sigma(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \Sigma(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{1}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \Sigma(P),

(3.1)

\displaystyle\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \Upsilon(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{1}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \Upsilon(P),

(3.2)

where

\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k}}}
stands for the mutation in direction
k
.
The seed
\Sigma(0)
is not necessarily the initial seed
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
We set

\Sigma(s)=(\mathbf{x}(s),\mathbf{y}(s),B(s))
,

\Upsilon(s)=(\mathbf{y}(s),B(s))
.

Definition 3.1

(Periodicity)
.

For a permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
,
we say that
sequences of mutations (
3.1
) and (
3.2
) are

\nu
-periodic

if

\displaystyle\Sigma(P)=\nu\Sigma(0),

(3.3)

\displaystyle\Upsilon(P)=\nu\Upsilon(0)

(3.4)

hold, respectively.

Thanks to the synchronicity in Theorem
2.31
,
two conditions (
3.3
) and (
3.4
)
are equivalent.
Thus, we can use the two notions interchangeably.
Below, we focus on the periodicity of (free)
Y
-patterns.
Since there is no confusion with
+
in the ambient field
\mathcal{F}
,
we write the addition
\oplus
in
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
as
+
,
which is indeed the ordinary addition of rational functions by definition.

We give a necessary condition for a permutation
\nu

having
the
\nu
-periodicity.

Definition 3.2
.

We say that a permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
is
compatible with
a diagonal matrix
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})

if

\displaystyle d_{\nu(i)}=d_{i}\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\ n).

(3.5)

Proposition 3.3

(
[
Nak18
, Prop. 4.3]
)
.

Suppose that the sequence (
3.2
) is
\nu
-periodic.
Let
D
be any common skew-symmetrizer for the
exchange matrices
B(s)
of (
3.2
).
Then,
\nu
is compatible with
D
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, one may assume that
B(0)
is decomposed into
a block diagonal form such that each block
is indecomposable.
By (
2.11
),
mutations preserve the block diagonal form of exchange matrices.
Moreover,
by (
2.10
),
mutations do not mix
y
-variables whose indices belong to different blocks.
On the other hand,
due to
the
\nu
-periodicity,
we have

y_{i}(P)=y_{\nu^{-1}(i)}(0)
.
Thus,

\nu
only permutes the indices in the same block of
B(0)
.
Therefore, we may further assume that
B(0)
is indecomposable.
Then, it is easy to see that its skew-symmetrizer
D
is unique up to a positive multiplicative constant.
In particular, there is a unique minimal integer skew-symmetrizer

D_{0}=\mathrm{diag}(d^{\prime}_{1},\dots,d^{\prime}_{n})
.
Due to the
\nu
-periodicity,
we have

B(P)=\nu B(0)
.
It follows that
the matrix
\nu D_{0}=\mathrm{diag}(d^{\prime}_{\nu^{-1}(1)},\dots,d^{\prime}_{\nu^{-1}(n)})

is also the minimal integer skew-symmetrizer of
B(0)
.
Due to the uniqueness
\nu D_{0}=D_{0}
, we have
d^{\prime}_{\nu^{-1}(i)}=d^{\prime}_{i}
for any
i
.
Therefore,
d_{\nu(i)}=d_{i}
holds.
∎

3.2
Tropical limit

Let
\mathbf{y}(s)=(y_{1}(s),\dots,y_{n}(s))
.
Then, each
y
-variable
y_{i}(s)
is
a rational function of the initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})

having a subtraction-free expression.
In particular, if
\mathbf{y}
takes values in

\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
, all
y
-variables
y_{i}(s)
also take values in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.
More formally speaking, for any
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
,
we have a specialization homomorphism

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbf{a}}:\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0},\quad y_{i}\mapsto a_{i}.

(3.6)

Since the function
f(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
does not have any pole
in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}

due to the subtraction-free property,
the map is defined.

Let us write the
C
-matrix and the
F
-polynomials for
\Upsilon(s)

as
C(s)
and
F_{i}(s)
,
where we drop the argument
\mathbf{y}
for the
F
-polynomials for simplicity.
Let
\varepsilon_{i}(s)
denote the tropical sign of
y_{i}(s)

in Definition
2.26
.

The following fact is a consequence of
the results in Section
2.6.1
.

Lemma 3.4
.

In the limit
\mathbf{y}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
,
each
y
-variable
y_{i}(s)
converges to 0
if
\varepsilon_{i}(s)=1

and
diverges to
\infty
if
\varepsilon_{i}(s)=-1

as a function of
\mathbf{y}
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
.

Proof.

In the current notation, the separation formula
(
2.60
) takes the form

\displaystyle y_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=\biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{c_{ji}(s)}\biggr)\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j}(s)^{b_{ji}(s)}.

(3.7)

In the limit
\mathbf{y}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
, the nontropical part
converges to 1 thanks to Theorem
2.25
.
Meanwhile, thanks to Theorem
2.24
,
the tropical part converges to 0
if
\varepsilon_{i}(s)=1

and
diverges to
\infty
if
\varepsilon_{i}(s)=-1
.
∎

It is natural to call the limit
\mathbf{y}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}

the
tropical limit
.

3.3
DI associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern

Let us formulate the DI associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern.
For this purpose,
it is convenient to choose a common skew-symmetrizer
for
B(s)
(
s=0
, …,
P-1
)
of the form

\displaystyle D=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1}^{-1},\dots,\delta_{n}^{-1})\quad(\delta_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(3.8)

Such a skew-symmetrizer always (and not uniquely) exists.
For example, take any (rational positive) skew-symmetrizer
D=\mathrm{diag}(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})
of
B
,
then divide it by the least common multiple of the numerators
of
d_{1}
, …,
d_{n}
.

Let us further introduce notations.
We write
\varepsilon_{s}:=\varepsilon_{k_{s}}(s)

(s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1)
,
where
k_{s}
is the direction of the mutation at time
s
in (
3.2
).
Then, we define integers
N_{+}
and
N_{-}
by

\displaystyle N_{+}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\frac{1+\varepsilon_{s}}{2},\quad N_{-}:=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\frac{1-\varepsilon_{s}}{2},

(3.9)

so that we have

\displaystyle N_{+}+N_{-}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}.

(3.10)

In other words,

N_{\pm}
is the total number of
s\in\{0,\,\dots,\,P-1\}
such that

\varepsilon_{s}=\pm 1
weighted by
\delta_{k_{s}}
.

The following theorem is the
leitmotif
throughout the text.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
Nak11b
, Thms. 6.1, 6.8]
)
.

Suppose that a sequence of mutation (
3.2
)
is
\nu
-periodic
and that the initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}
take values in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
.
Then, the following
DIs hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\tilde{L}(y_{k_{s}}(s))

\displaystyle=N_{-}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6},

(3.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\tilde{L}(y_{k_{s}}(s)^{-1})

\displaystyle=N_{+}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6},

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\varepsilon_{s}\delta_{k_{s}}\tilde{L}(y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})

\displaystyle=0,

(3.13)

where
\tilde{L}
is the modified Rogers dilogarithm in
(
1.24
).
Moreover, the above three identities are equivalent to each other
under Euler’s identity (
1.29
).

They are functional identities with respect to the initial
y
-variables

\mathbf{y}
in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
.
We call them together
the
DI associated with a period of a free
Y
-pattern (or a cluster pattern)
.

Let us closely look at the first DI (
3.11
).
According to the algebraic method in Section
1.2
,
the claim of the identity is separated into two parts,
namely, the
constancy
and the
constant term
.
Assuming the constancy,
the constant term is immediately obtained
by considering the tropical limit
\mathbf{y}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
.
Thanks to (
1.27
) and Lemma
3.4
,
only the terms
in (
3.11
) with
\varepsilon_{s}=-1

contribute to the sum.
This yields the constant term
N_{-}(\pi^{2}/6)
.
The other DIs (
3.12
) and (
3.13
)
are equivalent to (
3.11
)
by Euler’s identity (
1.29
) and (
3.10
).
Thus,
we are left to prove the constancy of the LHS of
(
3.11
).
We will give two alternative proofs in
Sections
3.5
and
3.6
.

Remark 3.6
.

The constancy and the constant values of the above DIs do not depend on the choice of
the initial vertex
t_{0}

because
changing
t_{0}
only causes the change of variables
of the identities.
In other words, even though
each tropical sign
\varepsilon_{s}
depends
on
t_{0}
,
the sums
N_{\pm}
are independent of
t_{0}
.

Let us see some examples of the DIs in Theorem
3.5
.

Example 3.7
.

The involution property of mutations

\displaystyle\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k}}}}\ \Upsilon(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k}}}}\ \Upsilon(2)=\Upsilon(0)

(3.14)

is regarded as the simplest example of periodicity.
The corresponding DIs (
3.11
) and (
3.12
) are both written as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(y_{k}(0))+\tilde{L}(y_{k}(0)^{-1})=\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(3.15)

This is Euler’s identity (
1.29
).
Meanwhile, the last one (
3.13
) is a trivial identity

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(y_{k}(0))-\tilde{L}(y_{k}(0))=0.

(3.16)

Example 3.8
.

Let us consider the pentagon periodicity
(
2.48
)
for the free
Y
-pattern of type
A_{2}

in Example
2.15
.
The sequence of mutations is written as

\displaystyle\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(2)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(3)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(4)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(5)=\tau_{12}\Upsilon(0).

(3.17)

We choose a skew-symmetrizer
D=I
.
We set
\mathbf{y}(0)=\mathbf{y}_{t_{0}}=\mathbf{y}
.
By (
2.43
)–(
2.47
),
the
y
-variables
y_{k_{s}}(s)
concerning the DI (
3.11
) are
given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{1}(0)&=y_{1},\ y_{2}(1)=y_{2}(1+y_{1}),\ y_{1}(2)=y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2}),\\
y_{2}(3)&=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+y_{2}),\ y_{1}(4)=y_{2}^{-1}.\end{split}

(3.18)

Also, by (
2.61
)–(
2.66
),
we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}=1,\quad\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{3}=\varepsilon_{4}=-1,

(3.19)

\displaystyle N_{+}=2,\quad N_{-}=3.

(3.20)

Then,
the corresponding DI (
3.11
)
is explicitly written as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(y_{1}(0))+\tilde{L}(y_{2}(1))+\tilde{L}(y_{1}(2))+\tilde{L}(y_{2}(3))+\tilde{L}(y_{1}(4))

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{2}}{2}.

(3.21)

This is exactly the pentagon identity in the form (
1.34
).
In fact, the variables
Y_{i}
in (
1.30
) are taken from (
3.18
).
Also, the DI without a constant term (
3.13
) is the one (
1.35
).

Example 3.9
.

We give two more examples of rank 2 periodicity.
See
[
Nak23
, §2.3]
for the calculation of the mutations.

(a) Type
B_{2}
. Under the same parametrization of the 2-regular tree
\mathbb{T}_{2}
in Example
2.15
,
we replace
B
in (
2.38
) with the following one and take the following choice of a skew-symmetrizer
D
:

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
2&0\end{pmatrix},\quad D=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1/2\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{1}=1,\ \delta_{2}=2.

(3.22)

The Cartan counterpart
A(B)
is the Cartan matrix of type
B_{2}
.
A long and straightforward (but educational) calculation, which is similar to Example
2.15
,
yields the periodicity of the free
Y
-pattern

\displaystyle\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(2)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(3)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(4)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(5)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(6)=\Upsilon(0).

(3.23)

The
y
-variables
y_{k_{s}}(s)
concerning the DI (
3.11
) are

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{1}(0)&=y_{1},\ y_{2}(1)=y_{2}(1+y_{1}),\ y_{1}(2)=y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2})^{2},\\
y_{2}(3)&=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+2y_{2}+y_{2}^{2}+y_{1}y_{2}^{2}),\ y_{1}(4)=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-2}(1+y_{2})^{2},\\
y_{2}(5)&=y_{2}^{-1}.\end{split}

(3.24)

Also, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}=1,\quad\varepsilon_{2}=\cdots=\varepsilon_{5}=-1,

(3.25)

\displaystyle N_{+}=3,\quad N_{-}=6.

(3.26)

(b) Type
G_{2}
. We replace
B
and
D
in (
3.22
)
with

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
3&0\end{pmatrix},\quad D=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1/3\end{pmatrix},\quad\delta_{1}=1,\ \delta_{2}=3.

(3.27)

The Cartan counterpart
A(B)
is the Cartan matrix of type
G_{2}
.
Then, we have the periodicity of the free
Y
-pattern

\displaystyle\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(2)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(3)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{1}}}}\ \Upsilon(7)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{2}}}}\ \Upsilon(8)=\Upsilon(0).

(3.28)

The
y
-variables
y_{k_{s}}(s)
concerning the DI (
3.11
) are

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{1}(0)&=y_{1},\ y_{2}(1)=y_{2}(1+y_{1}),\ y_{1}(2)=y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2})^{3},\\
y_{2}(3)&=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+3y_{2}+3y_{2}^{2}+y_{2}^{3}+3y_{1}y_{2}^{2}+2y_{1}y_{2}^{3}+y_{1}^{2}y_{2}^{3}),\\
y_{1}(4)&=y_{1}^{-2}y_{2}^{-3}(1+2y_{2}+y_{2}^{2}+y_{1}y_{2}^{2})^{3},\\
y_{2}(5)&=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-2}(1+3y_{2}+3y_{2}^{2}+y_{2}^{3}+y_{1}y_{2}^{3}),\\
y_{1}(6)&=y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-3}(1+y_{2})^{3},\quad y_{2}(7)=y_{2}^{-1}.\end{split}

(3.29)

Also, we have

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{1}=1,\quad\varepsilon_{2}=\cdots=\varepsilon_{7}=-1,

(3.30)

\displaystyle N_{+}=4,\quad N_{-}=12.

(3.31)

3.4
Constancy condition

To prove the constancy of the DI
(
3.11
),
we use the constancy condition given by Frenkel-Szenes
[
FS95
]
,
following the idea of Chapoton
[
Cha05
]
.

Let
G
be any multiplicative abelian group.
Let
G\otimes G
be its tensor product over
\mathbb{Z}
,
that is, the additive abelian group with the generators
f\otimes g
(
f,\,g\in G
) and the relations

\displaystyle(fg)\otimes h=f\otimes h+g\otimes h,\quad f\otimes(gh)=f\otimes g+f\otimes h.

(3.32)

We have

\displaystyle 1\otimes h=h\otimes 1=0,\quad f\otimes g^{-1}=f^{-1}\otimes g=-f\otimes g.

(3.33)

Let
S^{2}G
be the
symmetric subgroup
of

G\otimes G
,
namely, the subgroup generated by all
f\otimes f
(
f\in G)
.

Thus, we have
f\otimes g+g\otimes f\in G
(
f,\,g\in G)
.

The
wedge product
of
G
is defined by

\bigwedge^{2}G=G\otimes G/S^{2}G
.
The symbol
f\wedge g
denote the equivalence class of
f\otimes g
in

\bigwedge^{2}G
as usual.

Let

\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(I,\mathbb{R}_{>0})
be the set of all
differentiable
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
-valued functions on the interval
I=[0,1]
in
\mathbb{R}
.
The set
\mathcal{C}
is equipped with a semifield structure
by the usual multiplication and the addition of functions.
Regarding it as
a multiplicative abelian group, we have
the wedge product

\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{C}
as defined above.

The following sufficient condition for the constancy of the dilogarithm sum
was given by Frenkel-Szenes
[
FS95
]
,
whose idea originated in
the work of Bloch
[
Blo78
]
on the Bloch-Wigner function.

Theorem 3.10

(
[
FS95
, Proposition 1]
)
.

Let
f_{1}(u)
, …,
f_{n}(u)\in\mathcal{C}
.
Suppose that they satisfy the following relation in
\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{C}
:

\displaystyle\text{\emph{(Constancy condition)}}{\@index{\@indexphrase{constancy condition}}}\quad\sum_{t=1}^{r}f_{t}\wedge(1+f_{t})=0.

(3.34)

Then, the sum of the modified Rogers dilogarithm

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{r}\tilde{L}\left(f_{t}(u)\right)

(3.35)

is constant as a function of
u
on
I
.

Proof.

By (
1.25
), for each
t=1
, …,
r
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ 2\frac{d}{du}\tilde{L}\left(f_{t}(u)\right)\\
=&\ \frac{d}{du}\log f_{t}(u)\cdot\log(1+f_{t}(u))-\log f_{t}(u)\cdot\frac{d}{du}\log(1+f_{t}(u)).\end{split}

(3.36)

On the other hand, by the assumption (
3.34
),
there are some
k\geq 0
and
g_{i},\,h_{i}\in\mathcal{C}
(
i=1
, …,
k
) such that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{r}f_{t}\otimes(1+f_{t})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(g_{i}\otimes h_{i}+h_{i}\otimes g_{i}).

(3.37)

For each
u
,
v\in I
, we have an additive group homomorphism

\Psi_{u,v}:\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

f\otimes g\mapsto\log f(u)\cdot\log g(v)
.
Applying it on
(
3.37
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \sum_{t=1}^{r}\log f_{t}(u)\cdot\log(1+f_{t}(v))\\
=&\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(\log g_{i}(u)\cdot\log h_{i}(v)+\log h_{i}(u)\cdot\log g_{i}(v)\big).\end{split}

(3.38)

Then, taking the derivative for
u
and setting
v=u
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \sum_{t=1}^{r}\frac{d}{du}\log f_{t}(u)\cdot\log(1+f_{t}(u))\\
=&\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\frac{d}{du}\log g_{i}(u)\cdot\log h_{i}(u)+\frac{d}{du}\log h_{i}(u)\cdot\log g_{i}(u)\right).\end{split}

(3.39)

Similarly,
taking the derivative for
v
and setting
v=u
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \sum_{t=1}^{r}\log f_{t}(u)\cdot\frac{d}{du}\log(1+f_{t}(u))\\
=&\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\log g_{i}(u)\cdot\frac{d}{du}\log h_{i}(u)+\log h_{i}(u)\cdot\frac{d}{du}\log g_{i}(u)\right).\end{split}

(3.40)

Note that the RHSs of
(
3.39
) and (
3.40
) are identical.
Thus, by (
3.36
),
(
3.39
), and (
3.40
),
we obtain the equality

\displaystyle\frac{d}{du}\sum_{t=1}^{r}\tilde{L}\left(f_{t}(u)\right)=0.

(3.41)

∎

Remark 3.11
.

To be precise, the original condition in
[
FS95
]

was given for the function

\displaystyle g_{t}=\frac{f_{t}}{1+f_{t}},

(3.42)

and it has the form

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{r}g_{t}\wedge(1-g_{t})=0.

(3.43)

It was converted to
the one (
3.34
)
by Chapoton
[
Cha05
]
.

To apply Theorem
3.10

to the constancy
of the LHS of (
3.11
),
we need to make some adjustments.

Firstly,
we introduce a multiplicity
m_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
to each function
f_{t}
above.
Then,
(
3.34
) and (
3.35
)
are written as

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{r}m_{t}f_{t}\wedge(1+f_{t})=0

(3.44)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{r}m_{t}\tilde{L}\left(f_{t}(u)\right).

(3.45)

Secondly, we regard the
y
-variables
y_{k_{s}}(s)

in Theorem
3.5

as
functions of multi-variables
\mathbf{y}
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
,
while
f_{t}
in Theorem
3.10
are
functions of a single variable
u
on
I=[0,1]
.
So, following the argument of
[
FS95
]
,
we recast Theorem
3.10
in a suitable form
as follows.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
y_{k_{s}}(s)\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})

(s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1)
.
Suppose that the following relation holds in
\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}y_{k_{s}}(s)\wedge(1+y_{k_{s}}(s))=0.

(3.46)

Then, the sum of the modified Rogers dilogarithm

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\tilde{L}\left(y_{k_{s}}(s)\right)

(3.47)

is constant as a function of
\mathbf{y}
on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{a}_{0}
and
\mathbf{a}_{1}
be arbitrary points in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
.
We linearly interpolate them as

\mathbf{a}(u)=(1-u)\mathbf{a}_{0}+u\mathbf{a}_{1}
(
u\in[0,1]
),
and apply the specialization homomorphism

\pi_{\mathbf{a}(u)}\colon\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}

in (
3.6
) as

\displaystyle f_{s}(u):=\pi_{\mathbf{a}(u)}(y_{k_{s}}(s))\quad(s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1).

(3.48)

Then, we have functions
f_{s}(u)\in\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}_{>0})
.
Moreover, since
\pi_{\mathbf{a}(u)}
induces a semifield homomorphism

\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
,
the relation (
3.46
) implies the relation

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}f_{s}\wedge(1+f_{s})=0\quad\text{in $\textstyle\bigwedge^{2}\mathcal{C}$}.

(3.49)

Then, by Theorem
3.10

and the modification in (
3.45
),
the sum (
3.47
) coincides for
the specializations at
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{a}_{0}
and
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{a}_{1}
.
This is the desired constancy.
∎

In summary,
to complete the proof of Theorem
3.5
,
it is enough to prove the following claim.

Proposition 3.13

(Constancy
[
Nak12
, Prop. 6.3]
)
.

Suppose that a sequence of mutation (
3.2
)
is
\nu
-periodic.
Then, the following relation holds in
\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
:

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}y_{k_{s}}(s)\wedge(1+y_{k_{s}}(s))=0.

(3.50)

Example 3.14
.

Let us directly check the consistency condition (
3.50
)
for the
y
-variables (
3.18
) in the
Y
-pattern of type
A_{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \sum_{s=0}^{4}y_{k_{s}}(s)\wedge(1+y_{k_{s}}(s))\\
&=y_{1}\wedge(1+y_{1})+y_{2}(1+y_{1})\wedge(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2})\\
&\qquad+y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2})\wedge y_{1}^{-1}(1+y_{1})(1+y_{2})\\
&\qquad+y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+y_{2})\wedge y_{1}^{-1}y_{2}^{-1}(1+y_{2}+y_{1}y_{2})\\
&\qquad+y_{2}^{-1}\wedge y_{2}^{-1}(1+y_{2}).\end{split}

(3.51)

It is easy to see that all terms cancel each other.
This also gives an alternative proof of the pentagon identity in the form (
1.34
).

In the rest of this chapter, we present
two alternative proofs
of Proposition
3.13
, both of which crucially depend on
the results on cluster patterns presented in Chapter
2
.

3.5
First proof of constancy

Here, we give the first proof of the constancy condition
(
3.50
).
The basic idea
is to
express the
y
-variables therein
by the initial
y
-variables
through the separation formula (
2.60
).
Then, we manipulate the resulting expression
by the change of the summation index with the help of the periodicity.
This approach is regarded as
a “brute force” method
(namely, straightforward but less efficient compared with the second one).

In view of Remark
3.6
,
we may set the initial vertex
t_{0}

so that
\Upsilon(0)
coincides with the
initial
Y
-seed
\Upsilon_{t_{0}}
without
loss of generality.
Let us extend the
\nu
-periodic
sequence (
3.2
) in the both directions
as

\displaystyle\cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{-2}}}}}\ \Upsilon(-1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{-1}}}}}\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \Upsilon(P)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P}}}}}\ \Upsilon(P+1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P+1}}}}}\ \cdots

(3.52)

so that
\Upsilon(s+P)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nu}\Upsilon(s)
and
k_{s+P}=k_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nu(s)}
hold for any
s\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The
\nu
-periodicity
\mathbf{y}(P)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nu}\mathbf{y}(0)
implies

\displaystyle F_{i}(P)=F_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nu^{-1}(i)}(0)=1\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\,n)

(3.53)

by Theorems
2.30
and
2.31
.
For each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we define
\lambda_{+}(s)
and
\lambda_{-}(s)
as the minimal positive numbers
such that
k_{s\pm\lambda_{\pm}(s)}=k_{s}
hold, respectively.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle s^{\prime}=s+\lambda_{+}(s)\ \Longleftrightarrow\ s=s^{\prime}-\lambda_{-}(s^{\prime}).

(3.54)

For each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define the set

\displaystyle T(s):=\{t\in\mathbb{Z}\mid s\in(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)\},

(3.55)

where
(a,b)
is the open interval between
a
and
b
.

For simplicity, let
[f]
denote the image
of
f\in\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
under the tropicalization
\pi_{\mathrm{trop}}

in (
2.68
).
For example, by (
2.70
), we have

\displaystyle[y_{i}(s)]=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{c_{ji}(s)},\quad[1+y_{i}(s)]=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{-[-c_{ji}(s)]_{+}}.

(3.56)

By (
2.8
), we also have

\displaystyle\left[\frac{y_{i}(s)}{1+y_{i}(s)}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{[c_{ji}(s)]_{+}},\quad\left[\frac{1}{1+y_{i}(s)}\right]=\prod_{j=1}^{n}y_{j}^{[-c_{ji}(s)]_{+}}.

(3.57)

We use the following formulas.
(The formulas (
3.58
) and (
3.61
)
are only used to prove
(
3.62
).)

Lemma 3.15
.

(a) (Mutations).

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{k_{s}}(s+\lambda_{+}(s))F_{k_{s}}(s)&=\left[\frac{y_{k_{s}}(s)}{1+y_{k_{s}}(s)}\right]\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{[b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)]_{+}}\\
&\qquad+\left[\frac{1}{1+y_{k_{s}}(s)}\right]\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{[-b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)]_{+}},\end{split}

(3.58)

\displaystyle\begin{split}[y_{k_{s}}(s)][y_{k_{s}}(s-\lambda_{-}(s))]&=\prod_{t\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)}\bigl([y_{k_{t}}(t)]^{[b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(t)]_{+}}[1+y_{k_{t}}(t)]^{-b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(t)}\bigr).\end{split}

(3.59)

(b) (Separation formulas).

\displaystyle y_{k_{s}}(s)

\displaystyle=[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{b_{k_{t}{k_{s}}}(s)}

(3.60)

\displaystyle=[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\frac{\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{[b_{k_{t}{k_{s}}}(s)]_{+}}}{\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{[-b_{k_{t}{k_{s}}}(s)]_{+}}},

(3.61)

\displaystyle 1+y_{k_{s}}(s)

\displaystyle=[1+y_{k_{s}}(s)]\frac{F_{k_{s}}(s+\lambda_{+}(s))F_{k_{s}}(s)}{\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{[-b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)]_{+}}}.

(3.62)

(c) (Skew-symmetry).

\displaystyle\delta_{k_{s}}b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)=-\delta_{k_{t}}b_{k_{s}k_{t}}(s).

(3.63)

Proof.

(a).

For each
s\in\mathbb{Z}
,
if
j\neq k_{s}
(
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
) does not appear in
T(s)
,
then
j\neq k_{s^{\prime}}
for any
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
in the sequence (
3.52
).
This implies that, for such
j
, we have
F_{j}(s^{\prime})=F_{j}(0)=1
for any
s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Keeping it in mind,

the formula (
3.58
) is the mutation formula (
2.56
)
rewritten with the expressions (
3.57
) and the set
T(s)
in (
3.55
).
The formula (
3.59
) follows from the mutation formula (
2.10
)
by applying successive mutations at
s-\lambda_{-}(s)
, …,
s-1

and the tropicalization.
(b). The formula (
3.60
) is the separation formula (
2.60
).
The formula (
3.61
) is equivalent to (
3.60
).
The formula (
3.62
) is obtained from (
3.61
) and (
3.58
).
(c). This follows from (
2.5
).
∎

Now, we are ready to prove the constancy condition (
3.50
).
We put the formulas (
3.60
) and (
3.62
)
in the LHS of (
3.50
),
then, separate it into three parts as follows.

The first part involves only tropical parts, namely,

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\wedge[1+y_{k_{s}}(s)].

(3.64)

Recall the sign-coherence in Theorem
2.24
.
If the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{s}
is 1, we have
[1+y_{k_{s}}(s)]=1
.
If
\varepsilon_{s}
is
-1
, we have
[1+y_{k_{s}}(s)]=[y_{k_{s}}(s)]
.
In either case, we have
[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\wedge[1+y_{k_{s}}(s)]=0
.
Therefore, the sum (
3.64
) vanishes.

The second part involves both tropical and nontropical parts.
It consists of the following four terms.

\displaystyle(A)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s),

(3.65)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(B)&=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s+\lambda_{+}(s))\\
&=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}[y_{k_{s}}(s-\lambda_{-}(s))]\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s),\end{split}

(3.66)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(C)&=-\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}[y_{k_{s}}(s)]\wedge\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{[-b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)]_{+}}\Biggr)\\
&=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)}[y_{k_{t}}(t)]^{-[b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(t)]_{+}}\Biggr)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s),\end{split}

(3.67)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(D)&=-\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}[1+y_{k_{s}}(s)]\wedge\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)}\Biggr)\\
&=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)}[1+y_{k_{t}}(t)]^{b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(t)}\Biggr)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s).\end{split}

(3.68)

In the above, we did the following manipulations.

•

For (B), we changed the index
s
of the summation
based on (
3.54
).

The first sum with
s+\lambda_{+}(s)\leq P-1

and the second sum with
s-\lambda_{-}(s)\geq 0
clearly match.
When
s+\lambda_{+}(s)\geq P
in the first sum,
we have
F_{k_{s}}(s+\lambda_{+}(s))=F_{k_{s}}(P)=1
by (
3.53
),
so that it does not contribute to the sum.
Similarly,
when
s-\lambda_{-}(s)<0
in the second sum,
we have
F_{k_{s}}(s)=F_{k_{s}}(0)=1
,
so that it does not contribute to the sum.

•

For (C) and (D), we first exchanged the indices
s
and
t

and also used the skew-symmetry (
3.63
).
(This clarifies the necessity of the factor
\delta_{k_{s}}

in (
3.50
).)
Then, we cut off
s\geq P
, because, if so,
then
F_{k_{s}}(s)=F_{k_{s}}(P)=1
by (
3.53
).
See Figure
3.1
.
We also lifted the condition
t\geq 0
, because,
if
t<0
happens, then
F_{k_{s}}(s)=F_{k_{s}}(0)=1

for any
s\geq 0
such that
t\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)
;
thus, it does not contribute to the sum.

0
P
t
s-\lambda_{-}(s)
s

Figure 3.1:
Schematic diagram for the manipulations
in (
3.67
) and (
3.68
).

Then, thanks to the mutation formula (
3.59
),
the sum
(A)+(B)+(C)+(D)
vanishes.

The third part involves only nontropical parts,
and it requires more elaborated treatment.
It consists of the following three terms.

\displaystyle(E)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)}\Biggr)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s),

(3.69)

\displaystyle(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)}\Biggr)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s+\lambda_{+}(s)),

(3.70)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(G)&=-\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\,\prod_{t\in T(s)}F_{k_{t}}(t)^{b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)}\Biggr)\wedge\Biggl(\,\prod_{r\in T(s)}F_{k_{r}}(r)^{[-b_{k_{r}k_{s}}(s)]_{+}}\Biggr).\end{split}

(3.71)

We do similar manipulations as before.

The first term
(E)
is written as follows.

\displaystyle(E)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\scriptstyle s,t=0\atop\scriptstyle s\in(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s)F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\scriptstyle s,t=0\atop\scriptstyle t\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(t)F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle s,t=0\atop\scriptstyle(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)\cap(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)\neq\emptyset}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(\min(s,t))F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s).

In the first line, we cut off

t\geq P
as before.
In the second line, we exchanged the indices
s
and
t

and also used the skew-symmetry (
3.63
).
The third line is obtained by
averaging the first and second ones
with factor
1/2
.
(The first one contributes to the case
s<t
,
while the second one contributes to the case
t<s
.)

Similarly, the second term
(F)
is written as follows.

\displaystyle(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\scriptstyle s,t=0\atop\scriptstyle s-\lambda_{-}(s)\in(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(s-\lambda_{-}(s))F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle s,t=0\atop\scriptstyle(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)\cap(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)\neq\emptyset}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(\max(s-\lambda_{-}(s),t-\lambda_{-}(t)))

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt\times F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s).

In the second line, we took the average of the first line
and the one obtained from it by exchanging the indices
s
and
t
.

Finally, the third term
(G)
is written as follows.

\displaystyle(G)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{s,t=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\,\sum_{r\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)\cap(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)}b_{k_{t}k_{r}}(r)[b_{k_{r}k_{s}}(r)]_{+}\Biggr)F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s,t=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}\Biggl(\sum_{r\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)\cap(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.0pt\biggl(b_{k_{t}k_{r}}(r)[b_{k_{r}k_{s}}(r)]_{+}+[-b_{k_{t}k_{r}}(r)]_{+}b_{k_{r}k_{s}}(r)\biggr)\Biggr)F_{k_{t}}(t)\wedge F_{k_{s}}(s).

In the first line,
we first exchange variables
s
and
r
,
and also used the skew-symmetry (
3.63
).
Then,
we cut off
s,\,t\geq P
as before.
Also, we lifted the condition
r\geq 0
,
because, if
r<0
happens, then

F_{k_{t}}(t)=F_{k_{s}}(s)=1

for the relevant
t
and
s
as before.
In the second line, we took the average of the first line
and the one obtained from it by exchanging the indices
s
and
t
.

Meanwhile,
the mutation of exchange matrices (
2.11
) yields
the equality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(\min(s,t))+b_{k_{t}k_{s}}(\max(s-\lambda_{-}(s),t-\lambda_{-}(t)))\\
=&\sum_{r\in(s-\lambda_{-}(s),s)\cap(t-\lambda_{-}(t),t)}\biggl(b_{k_{t}k_{r}}(r)[b_{k_{r}k_{s}}(r)]_{+}+[-b_{k_{t}k_{r}}(r)]_{+}b_{k_{r}k_{s}}(r)\biggr).\end{split}

(3.72)

Thus, the sum
(E)+(F)+(G)
vanishes.
This completes the first proof of
Proposition
3.13
.

3.6
Second proof of constancy

Let us give an alternative proof of Proposition
3.13
,
which was inspired by the result of Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09a
,
FG09c
]
.

Here we return to the usual notation for a
Y
-seed
(\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})
and a
F
-polynomial
F_{i;t}
.
To each
t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, we assign the following element in

\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
:

\displaystyle V_{t}:=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}F_{i;t}\wedge[y_{i;t}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\delta_{i}b_{ji;t}F_{i;t}\wedge F_{j;t}.

(3.73)

The following mutation formula holds.

Proposition 3.16

(
[
Nak11b
, Prop. 6.7]
)
.

Let
t,\,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
be
k
-adjacent.
Then, we have

\displaystyle V_{t^{\prime}}-V_{t}=\delta_{k}y_{k;t}\wedge(1+y_{k;t}).

(3.74)

Proof.

We have
the following formulas, which are parallel with the ones
in Lemma
3.15
.
(The relation (
3.75
) is only used to prove
(
3.78
).)

\displaystyle F_{k;t^{\prime}}F_{k;t}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{y_{k;t}}{1+y_{k;t}}\right]\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{[b_{ik;t}]_{+}}+\left[\frac{1}{1+y_{k;t}}\right]\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{[-b_{ik;t}]_{+}},

(3.75)

\displaystyle[y_{i;t^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}[y_{k;t}]^{-1}&i=k,\\
[y_{i;t}][y_{k;t}]^{[b_{ki;t}]_{+}}[1+y_{k;t}]^{-b_{ki;t}},&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(3.76)

\displaystyle y_{k;t}

\displaystyle=[y_{k;t}]\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{b_{ik;t}},

(3.77)

\displaystyle 1+y_{k;t}

\displaystyle=[1+y_{k;t}]\frac{F_{k;t^{\prime}}F_{k;t}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{[-b_{ik;t}]_{+}}},

(3.78)

\displaystyle\delta_{j}b_{ij;t}

\displaystyle=-\delta_{i}b_{ji;t}.

(3.79)

We put the expressions (
3.77
)
and (
3.78
) into the RHS of (
3.74
).
Then, separate it into three parts as before.

The first part consists of a single term

\displaystyle\delta_{k}[y_{k;t}]\wedge[1+y_{k;t}].

(3.80)

This vanishes by the same reason for the one (
3.64
).

The second part consists of four terms

\displaystyle(A)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k}[y_{k;t}]\wedge F_{k;t},

(3.81)

\displaystyle(B)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k}[y_{k;t}]\wedge F_{k;t^{\prime}},

(3.82)

\displaystyle(C)

\displaystyle=-\delta_{k}[y_{k;t}]\wedge\biggl(\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{[-b_{ik;t}]_{+}}\biggr)=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}[b_{ki;t}]_{+}[y_{k;t}]\wedge F_{i;t},

(3.83)

\displaystyle(D)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k}\biggl(\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{b_{ik;t}}\biggr)\wedge[1+y_{k;t}]=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}b_{ki;t}F_{i;t}\wedge[1+y_{k;t}].

(3.84)

Meanwhile, we have

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}F_{i;t^{\prime}}\wedge[y_{i;t^{\prime}}]-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}F_{i;t}\wedge[y_{i;t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k}^{n}\delta_{i}F_{i;t}\wedge[y_{k;t}]^{[b_{ki;t}]_{+}}[1+y_{k;t}]^{-b_{ki;t}}-\delta_{k}F_{k;t^{\prime}}\wedge[y_{k;t}]-\delta_{k}F_{k;t}\wedge[y_{k;t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (A)+(B)+(C)+(D),

where we used the fact
b_{kk}=0
.

The third part consists of three terms

\displaystyle(E)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k}\biggl(\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{b_{ik;t}}\biggr)\wedge F_{k;t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{k}b_{ik;t}F_{i;t}\wedge F_{k;t},

(3.85)

\displaystyle(F)

\displaystyle=\delta_{k}\biggl(\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{b_{ik;t}}\biggr)\wedge F_{k;t^{\prime}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{k}b_{ik;t}F_{i;t}\wedge F_{k;t^{\prime}},

(3.86)

\displaystyle\begin{split}(G)&=-\delta_{k}\biggl(\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}F_{i;t}^{b_{ik;t}}\biggr)\wedge\biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}F_{j;t}^{[-b_{jk;t}]_{+}}\biggr)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\delta_{i}[-b_{jk;t}]_{+}b_{ki;t}F_{i;t}\wedge F_{j;t}.\end{split}

(3.87)

Meanwhile, we have

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\delta_{i}b_{ji;t^{\prime}}F_{i;t^{\prime}}\wedge F_{j;t^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\delta_{i}b_{ji;t}F_{i;t}\wedge F_{j;t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle i,j=1\atop\scriptstyle i,j\neq k}^{n}\delta_{i}\left(b_{jk;t}[b_{ki;t}]_{+}+[-b_{jk;t}]_{+}b_{ki;t}\right)F_{i;t}\wedge F_{j;t}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k}^{n}\delta_{i}(-b_{ki;t})F_{i;t}\wedge F_{k;t^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k}^{n}\delta_{i}b_{ki;t}F_{i;t}\wedge F_{k;t}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle j=1\atop\scriptstyle j\neq k}^{n}\delta_{k}(-b_{jk;t})F_{k;t^{\prime}}\wedge F_{j;t}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\scriptstyle j=1\atop\scriptstyle j\neq k}^{n}\delta_{k}b_{jk;t}F_{k;t}\wedge F_{j;t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (E)+(F)+(G).

Therefore, the equality (
3.74
) holds.
∎

Now let us prove Proposition
3.13

using Proposition
3.16
,
where we do not need to assume that
\Upsilon(0)=\Upsilon_{t_{0}}

for the initial vertex
t_{0}
.
Let us go back to the sequence (
3.2
)
and assume the
\nu
-periodicity.
Let
V(s)
denote the element
V_{t}
in (
3.73
) corresponding to each
Y
-seed

\Upsilon(s)
therein.
Then, thanks to
Theorem
2.31
, (
2.82
), and Proposition
3.3
,
we have the periodicity

\displaystyle V(P)=V(0).

(3.88)

Meanwhile, by Proposition
3.16
, we have

\displaystyle V(P)-V(0)=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}\delta_{k_{s}}y_{k_{s}}(s)\wedge(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)).

(3.89)

This is 0 by (
3.88
).
This completes the second proof of
Proposition
3.13
.

Compared with the first proof, the second proof
is more efficient; moreover, it exhibits the role of the periodicity
in Proposition
3.13
more transparently.
Meanwhile, the intrinsic meaning of the element
V_{t}
is not clear.

In summary, we have proved
Theorem
3.5
via Proposition
3.13

with two alternative proofs.
We call them the
algebraic method

because it is directly related to the algebraic structure of
seeds and mutations in a cluster pattern.
We also note that both proofs
depend on some advanced results on cluster algebras.
Even though the idea of the proofs is clear,
we are not completely satisfied with them because
they do not clarify
any intrinsic reason why
DIs are relevant to cluster algebras
.
Thus, we continue to study Theorem
3.5

in different perspectives in the following part, looking for an answer to this question.

Remark 3.17
.

In
[
FG09a
, Lemma 6.3]
,
for a cluster pattern without coefficients,
the following element was considered
instead of
V_{t}
in (
3.73
):

\displaystyle W_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{i}x_{i;t}\wedge\hat{y}_{i;t}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\delta_{i}b_{ji;t}x_{i;t}\wedge x_{j;t}.

(3.90)

Then,
the mutation formula

\displaystyle W_{t^{\prime}}-W_{t}=\delta_{k}\hat{y}_{k;t}\wedge(1+\hat{y}_{k;t}).

(3.91)

is shown without using the advanced results in
Chapter
2.6
.
Moreover, when the underlying
B
-pattern is nonsingular,
the initial
\hat{y}
-variables are algebraically independent.
Then, the formula (
3.91
) implies Proposition
3.13
.
In the singular case, however, we still need the results in
Chapter
2.6
to resolve the issue of algebraic dependence.

Notes

The constancy condition in Theorem
3.10

was formulated by
[
FS95
]

to prove the DI for the
Y
-system of type
(A_{n},A_{1})
.
It is parallel with the result on the Bloch-Wigner function by
[
Blo78
, Thm. 1.7, Remark 1.11]
,
which is recapitulated in
[
FS95
, Prop. 3]
.

There have been several prototypical examples before Theorem
3.5

was formulated in
[
Nak12
]
.
The relation between the periodicity and
the constancy condition for the
Y
-system of type
(X,X^{\prime})
was
studied in
[
CGT99
]
.
The crucial step was made by

[
Cha05
]
,
where
the DIs for the
Y
-system of type
(X,A_{1})
was proved.
The consistency condition (
3.34
) was proved
using the explicit description of the solution of
the above
Y
-system by
[
FZ03b
]
.
The evaluation of the constant term by taking the tropical limit
also originated in
[
Cha05
]
.
This method was generalized by
[
Nak11a
]

to prove the DI for the
Y
-system of general type
(X,X^{\prime})

in a more cluster algebraic setting,
where all ingredients of the method in this chapter
was essentially introduced.
There are subsequent papers where the same method
was applied
to prove the DIs for various
Y
-systems

[
IIK+13a
,
IIK+13b
,
NT10
]
.
After these works, it was figured out in
[
Nak12
]

that the periodicity
in a cluster pattern is the sole reason for these DIs.

The Bloch-Wigner function was studied in

[
FG09a
,
FG09c
]

by a more abstract approach with algebraic
K
-theory.

Chapter 4
Y
-systems and
Y
-patterns

Now we come back to and prove the DIs for
Y
-systems
in Chapter
1
.
We first formulate these
Y
-systems in terms of
Y
-patterns.
Then, we prove their periodicity using the tropicalization.
It turns out that the Coxeter elements
of root systems are behind the scenes and govern the dynamics.
By applying Theorem
3.5
,
we obtain the DIs for
Y
-systems,
where the counting of the constant term is also done by the tropicalization.

4.1
Quivers and mutations

It is often convenient and useful to represent
a
skew-symmetric
integer matrix by a
quiver
.

Definition 4.1

(Quiver)
.

A (finite)
quiver

is a finite directed graph. Namely, it consists of a finite set of vertices
and a finite set of arrows between the vertices.
For a quiver with
n
vertices, we assume that its vertices are labeled with 1, …,
n

without duplication.
The following arrows are called a
loop

and a
2
-cycle
, respectively:

For a skew-symmetric integer matrix
B
, one can associate a quiver
Q(B)
without loops and 2-cycles by the following rule:

•

If
b_{ij}>0
, then assign
b_{ij}
arrows from the vertex
i
to the vertex
j
.

Since
b_{ii}=0
, there are no loops. Also, there are no 2-cycles,
because
b_{ji}=-b_{ij}<0
if
b_{ij}>0
.
For example,

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-3&2\\
3&0&1\\
-2&-2&0\end{pmatrix},\quad Q(B)=\raisebox{-15.0pt}{
\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 2.31248pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.90521pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 26.4526pt\raise 48.36943pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-2.31248pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.9pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small 1}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 54.59273pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.9pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small 2}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 17.60434pt\raise 48.36943pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.9pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small 3}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.69052pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.69052pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.69052pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.29787pt\raise 42.67891pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.02997pt\raise 42.10986pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.18471pt\raise 43.24794pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}
}

(4.1)

Conversely, one can recover a skew-symmetric integer matrix
B
from a quiver without loops and 2-cycles by applying the above rule in the opposite direction.
It is clear that this correspondence is one-to-one.

For a quiver
Q
, the quiver obtained from
Q
by changing all arrows of
Q
to the
ones in the opposite direction is called the
opposite quiver
of
Q
,
and it is denoted by
Q^{\mathrm{op}}
.
If a skew-symmetric integer matrix
B
corresponds to a quiver
Q
,
then
-B
corresponds to
Q^{\mathrm{op}}
.

One can translate the matrix mutation (
2.11
) into the quiver mutation as follows.

Definition 4.2

(Quiver mutation)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with
n
vertices and without loops and 2-cycles.
For each
k=1
, …,
n
, we define a new quiver

Q^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(Q)
by the following operation:

•

For each pair
i
,
j
(
i\neq j
) such that
i
,
j\neq k
,
if
there are
p>0
arrows from the vertex
i
to the vertex
k
,
and
q>0
arrows from the vertex
k
to the vertex
j
,
then add
pq
arrows from the vertex
i
to the vertex
j
.

•

Remove the resulting 2-cycles as many as possible.

•

Invert all arrows into and out of the vertex
k
.

The quiver
Q^{\prime}
is called the
mutation of
Q
at the vertex
k
.

Under the above identification, a
Y
-seed
(\mathbf{y},B)
with a skew-symmetric matrix
B

is replaced with
(\mathbf{y},Q)
for the corresponding quiver
Q
.
For the mutation
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=\mu_{k}(\mathbf{y},Q)
of a
Y
-seed,
the mutation of
y
-variables (
2.10
)
is translated into the following simple pictorial rule:

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}y_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i},&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.9526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-1.5826pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96925pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$i$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.91269pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.125pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$k$}}$}}}}}}}}}\ \text{(no arrow)},\\
y_{i}(1\oplus y_{k})^{m}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.9526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-1.5826pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96925pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$i$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.91269pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.125pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$k$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.15167pt\raise 5.69052pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.9375pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$m$}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.76208pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ \text{($m$ arrows)},\\
y_{i}(1\oplus y_{k}^{-1})^{-m}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.9526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-1.5826pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96925pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$i$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.91269pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.125pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$k$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 10.15167pt\raise 5.69052pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.9375pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$m$}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.69052pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ \text{($m$ arrows)}.\end{cases}

(4.2)

Example 4.3
.

Let us consider the following quiver
Q
:

Q=\ \lower 12.0pt\hbox{

}

We consider the sequence of mutations at the vertices
2
,
4
,
6
,

1
,
3
,
5
in this order.
In the first half,
the quivers mutate as follows,
where a black vertex is the one at which the mutation is
going to be applied:

\textstyle{\bullet}
1
2
3
4
5
6
2

\textstyle{\bullet}
1
2
3
4
5
6
4

\textstyle{\bullet}
1

1
2

The last quiver is the opposite quiver
Q^{\mathrm{op}}
of
Q
.
It is easy to see that the final result is independent of the order
of the mutations at 2, 4, 6.
Similarly,
in the second half,
the quivers mutate as follows:

\textstyle{\bullet}

\textstyle{\bullet}

The last quiver is the original quiver
Q
.
Thus, we have a periodicity of quivers.
Again, it is easy to see that the final result is independent of the order
of the mutations at 1, 3, 5.
For
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},Q^{\mathrm{op}})=\mu_{6}\mu_{4}\mu_{2}(\mathbf{y},Q)
,
by applying the pictorial rule (
4.2
),
we have

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}y_{i}^{-1}&i=2,\,4,\,6,\\
y_{1}(1\oplus y_{2})(1\oplus y_{4}^{-1})^{-1}&i=1,\\
y_{3}(1\oplus y_{2})(1\oplus y_{6}^{-1})^{-1}&i=3,\\
y_{5}(1\oplus y_{4})(1\oplus y_{6})(1\oplus y_{2}^{-1})^{-1}&i=5.\end{cases}

(4.3)

Similarly,
for
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime},Q)=\mu_{5}\mu_{3}\mu_{1}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},Q^{\mathrm{op}})
,
we have

\displaystyle y^{\prime\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}y^{\prime}_{i}{}^{-1}&i=1,\,3,\,5,\\
y^{\prime}_{2}(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{1})(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{3})(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{5}{}^{-1})^{-1}&i=2.\\
y^{\prime}_{4}(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{5})(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{1}{}^{-1})^{-1}&i=4,\\
y^{\prime}_{6}(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{5})(1\oplus y^{\prime}_{3}{}^{-1})^{-1}&i=6.\end{cases}

(4.4)

4.2
Y
-systems and
Y
-patterns

The readers may already notice
the similarity between the mutation of
y
-variables
(
4.3
), (
4.4
)
and
Y
-systems (
1.38
).
Let us make this observation more precise.

In general, mutations are not commutative,
namely,
\mu_{k}\mu_{\ell}(\Sigma)\neq\mu_{\ell}\mu_{k}(\Sigma)
.
However, they commute under the following condition.

Lemma 4.4

(e.g.,
[
FZ07
, §8]
)
.

For a seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},B)
and
a pair
k
,
\ell
(
k\neq\ell
),
suppose that

\displaystyle b_{k\ell}=b_{\ell k}=0

(4.5)

holds.
Then, for the seed
\Sigma^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime},B^{\prime})=\mu_{k}(\Sigma)
,
we also have

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{k\ell}=b^{\prime}_{\ell k}=0.

(4.6)

Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{k}\mu_{\ell}(\Sigma)=\mu_{\ell}\mu_{k}(\Sigma).

(4.7)

The equality (
4.7
) can be easily verified
by a direct calculation (e.g.,
[
Nak23
, Prop. 2.9]
).

Now we formulate the
Y
-system (
1.38
)
of type
(X,X^{\prime})

in terms of a
Y
-pattern following Keller
[
Kel10
]
.
We divide the formulation into three steps.

Step 1. Quiver
Q(X,X^{\prime})
.

At each vertex
a
of
X
we assign a sign
\kappa_{a}\in\{+,-\}

so that any adjacent vertices in
X
have the opposite sign.
(There are two possible choices, and either will do.)
We conveniently identify the signs
\pm
with
\pm 1
as usual.
We do the same assignment of signs for
X^{\prime}
.
Then, we consider the product graph
T=X\times X^{\prime}
,
where
X
lies horizontally and
X^{\prime}
lies vertically.
For each vertex
v=(a,a^{\prime})
of
T
, we set
a sign
\kappa_{v}=\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}:=\kappa_{a}\kappa_{a^{\prime}}\in\{+,-\}
.
Moreover,
we set an orientation on each edge of
T

by the following rule:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}(++)&\leftarrow&(-+)\\
\downarrow&&\uparrow\\
(+-)&\rightarrow&(--)\end{matrix}

(4.8)

The resulting quiver is denoted by
Q(X,X^{\prime})
.
See Figure
4.1
for examples.
The quiver
Q
in Example
4.3

is also an example for
Q(A_{3},A_{2})
.

\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
+
-
+
-
+
-
+
(a)
Q(A_{4},A_{3})

\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
+
-
+
-
-
+
-
+
(b)
Q(D_{5},A_{3})

Figure 4.1:
Examples of quiver
Q(X,X^{\prime})
.
Black and white vertices belong to
V_{+}
and
V_{-}
, respectively.

Step 2. Composite mutations
\mu_{+}
and
\mu_{-}
.

We consider the
Y
-pattern
\mathbf{\Upsilon}=\{\Upsilon_{t}\}

of a cluster pattern with free coefficients
at
t_{0}
,
where
the initial
Y
-seed is given by
\Upsilon_{t_{0}}=\Upsilon=(\mathbf{y},Q)

with
Q=Q(X,X^{\prime})
.
Let
V_{+}
and
V_{-}
be the sets of vertices
v
of the quiver
Q
with sign

\kappa_{v}
being

+

and
-
, respectively.
In Figure
4.1
, the black and white vertices
belong to
V_{+}
and
V_{-}
, respectively.
Let
p=|V_{+}|
, and let
v_{1}
, …,
v_{p}
be all vertices in
V_{+}
.
Similarly,
let
q=|V_{-}|
, and let
u_{1}
, …,
u_{q}
be all vertices in
V_{-}
.
Then, the following facts hold:

1.

By the construction of
Q
, there is no arrow between
the vertices
v
and
v^{\prime}
with the same sign.
Therefore,
by Lemma
4.4
,
the sequence of mutations

\displaystyle\mu_{+}(\mathbf{y},Q):=\mu_{v_{p}}\cdots\mu_{v_{1}}(\mathbf{y},Q)

(4.9)

is independent of the order of the product.

2.

Let
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},Q^{\prime})=\mu_{+}(\mathbf{y},Q)
.
Then,
Q^{\prime}=Q^{\mathrm{op}}
holds in the same way as the quiver
Q^{\prime}=\mu_{6}\mu_{4}\mu_{2}(Q)

in Example
4.3
.

3.

For the above seed
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},Q^{\mathrm{op}})
, we apply the sequence of mutations

\displaystyle\mu_{-}:=\mu_{u_{q}}\cdots\mu_{u_{1}}.

(4.10)

Again, by Lemma
4.4
,
it is independent of the order of the product.

4.

Let
(\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})=\mu_{-}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime},Q^{\mathrm{op}})
.
Then,
Q^{\prime\prime}=Q
holds
in the same way as the quiver
Q^{\prime\prime}=\mu_{5}\mu_{3}\mu_{1}(Q^{\mathrm{op}})

in Example
4.3
.
Note that this periodicity is only for quivers,
and
\mathbf{y}^{\prime\prime}=\mathbf{y}
does not hold.

In the case
(X,X^{\prime})=(A_{1},A_{1})
,
one of
V_{+}
or
V_{-}
is empty.
If
V_{+}
is empty, for example,
we regard
\mu_{+}=\mathrm{id}
. All formulas below are still applicable.

Step 3. Identification with
Y
-system.

For the above
Y
-pattern,
consider the following sequence of (composite) mutations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}}\ (\mathbf{y}(-1),Q^{\mathrm{op}})\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}}&\ (\mathbf{y}(0),Q)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}}\ (\mathbf{y}(1),Q^{\mathrm{op}})\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}}\ (\mathbf{y}(2),Q)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}}\ \cdots,\end{split}

(4.11)

where
(\mathbf{y}(0),Q)=(\mathbf{y},Q)
is the initial
Y
-seed,
and

\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}
and
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}

represent
\mu_{+}
and
\mu_{-}
, respectively.
Let us write
y
-variables
as
\mathbf{y}(s)=(y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s))_{(a,a^{\prime})\in I\times I^{\prime}}
.
Also, let us write the addition
\oplus
in
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
as
+

as in the previous chapter.
Then, in the same way as (
4.3
) and (
4.4
),
we have,
for even
s
,

\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)=\begin{cases}y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)^{-1}&\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}=+,\\
y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}(1+y_{b,a^{\prime}}(s))}{\displaystyle\prod_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}}(1+y_{a,b^{\prime}}(s)^{-1})}&\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}=-,\\
\end{cases}

(4.12)

and, for odd
s
,

\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}(1+y_{b,a^{\prime}}(s))}{\displaystyle\prod_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}}(1+y_{a,b^{\prime}}(s)^{-1})}&\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}=+,\\
y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)^{-1}&\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}=-,\end{cases}

(4.13)

where the symbol
\sim
denotes the adjacency
in
X
in the numerator
and in
X^{\prime}
in the denominator, respectively.
Now we separate the
y
-variables into the
even
and the
odd sectors

\mathcal{Y}_{+}
,
\mathcal{Y}_{-}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\{y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)\mid\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{s}=\pm 1\}.

(4.14)

Then, by (
4.12
) and (
4.13
),
the variables in
\mathcal{Y}_{+}
and
\mathcal{Y}_{-}
are related as

\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)=y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)^{-1}\quad(y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)\in\mathcal{Y}_{+},\ y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)\in\mathcal{Y}_{-}).

(4.15)

By using this relation,
both relations (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) are
summarized into a single relation

\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s-1)=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}(1+y_{b,a^{\prime}}(s))}{\displaystyle\prod_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}}(1+y_{a,b^{\prime}}(s)^{-1})},

(4.16)

where all
y
-variables are in the even sector
\mathcal{Y}_{+}
.
This is exactly the
Y
-system (
1.36
)
of type
(X,X^{\prime})
.
On the other hand, by (
4.15
) and (
4.16
),
y
-variables
in the odd sector
\mathcal{Y}_{-}
satisfy the “dual” relations

\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s-1)=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}}(1+y_{a,b^{\prime}}(s))}{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}(1+y_{b,a^{\prime}}(s)^{-1})}.

(4.17)

Meanwhile, we observe that the
Y
-system (
1.36
) itself
is decoupled into two sectors.
Namely,
let us separate the variables
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)
therein into the even and the odd sectors

Y_{+}
,
Y_{-}
by

\displaystyle Y_{\pm}

\displaystyle:=\{Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)\mid\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{s}=\pm 1\}.

(4.18)

In contrast to (
4.15
),
there is no relation between the variables in
Y_{+}
and the ones in
Y_{-}

in the
Y
-system (
1.36
).
Therefore,
the
Y
-system can be restricted on each sector.

In summary, the
Y
-system (
1.36
) restricted on the even sector
Y_{+}

is “embedded”
in the ambient
Y
-pattern,
where the variables
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)\in Y_{+}
for the
Y
-system
are identified with the
y
-variables
y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)\in\mathcal{Y}_{+}

in the sequence of mutations (
4.11
).

4.3
DIs for
Y
-systems

For each simply-laced Dynkin diagram
X
,
we introduce the following diagram automorphism
\omega:I\rightarrow I

of
X
,

\displaystyle\text{for $A_{r}$}\quad

\displaystyle\omega:a\leftrightarrow r+1-a,

(4.19)

\displaystyle\text{for $D_{r}$ ($r$: odd)}\quad

\displaystyle\omega:a\mapsto a\ (a\neq r-1,\ r),\ r-1\leftrightarrow r,

\displaystyle\text{for $E_{6}$}\quad

\displaystyle\omega:{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}6\mapsto 6,\ 1\leftrightarrow 5,\ 2\leftrightarrow 4,}

\displaystyle\text{otherwise}\quad

\displaystyle\omega=\mathrm{id},

where we used the labels of
I
in Figure
1.4
.
The intrinsic meaning of
\omega
will be clarified soon in (
4.30
).
Note that
\omega
is an involution for any
X
.

The following periodicity was proved for
(X,X^{\prime})=(X,A_{1})

by Fomin-Zelevinsky
[
FZ03b
,
FZ07
]
,
and in general
by Keller
[
Kel10
,
Kel13b
]
(for full periodicity)
and Inoue-Iyama-Kuniba-Nakanishi-Suzuki
[
IIK+10
]
(for half periodicity).

Theorem 4.5

(
[
FZ03b
, Thm. 1.1]
,
[
Kel10
, Thm. 8.2]
,
[
Kel13b
, Thm. 2.3]
,
[
IIK+10
, Cor. 4.28]
)
.

For the sequence of mutations (
4.11
),
the following periodicities hold:

(half periodicity)

\displaystyle\quad y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+h+h^{\prime})

\displaystyle=y_{\omega(a),\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}(s),

(4.20)

(full periodicity)

\displaystyle\quad y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+2(h+h^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s),

(4.21)

where
h
,
h^{\prime}
are the Coxeter numbers,
and
\omega
,
\omega^{\prime}
are the diagram automorphisms
(
4.19
) of
X
and
X^{\prime}
, respectively.
(When
\omega=\omega^{\prime}=\mathrm{id}
,
the half periodicity (
4.20
) is already a full periodicity.)

The periodicity (
4.21
)
is equivalent to the one for the
Y
-systems
in Conjecture
1.4
under the correspondence in the previous section.
Temporarily assume Theorem
4.5
.
Then,
thanks to Theorem
2.31
,
or by
Q(2(h+h^{\prime}))=Q
more directly,
the periodicity (
4.21
)
is lifted to the periodicity of
Y
-seeds

\displaystyle\Upsilon(s+2(h+h^{\prime}))=\Upsilon(s).

(4.22)

Thus, by Theorem
3.5
,
there is a DI associated with this periodicity.
Note that
y_{k_{s}}(s)
appearing in the DI are
variables in the even sector
\mathcal{Y}_{+}
.
Also, we take
D=I
because the
Y
-pattern is skew-symmetric.
After evaluating the constant term,
we obtain the following DI.

Theorem 4.6

(
[
Nak11a
, Thm. 2.8]
)
.

Suppose that the initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}
take values in
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
.
Then, the following DIs hold.

\displaystyle\sum_{s=0}^{2(h+h^{\prime})-1}\sum_{\scriptstyle(a,a^{\prime})\in I\times I^{\prime}\atop\scriptstyle\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{s}=1}\tilde{L}(y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s))=hrr^{\prime}\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}.

(4.23)

Taking into account the following fine points,
this is indeed the DI (
1.40
) for the
Y
-system:

•

The constant term of (
4.23
) is
half
of
the one of (
1.40
) because
the sum in (
4.23
)
is restricted to the even sector
\mathcal{Y}_{+}
,
which corresponds to the even sector
Y_{+}

of the
Y
-system.

•

By (
4.15
),
the initial
y
-variables
y_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)
are identified with the initial
variables of the
Y
-system in the even sector
Y_{+}
as

\displaystyle y_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)=\begin{cases}Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)&\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}=+,\\
Y_{a,a^{\prime}}(-1)^{-1}&\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}=-.\end{cases}

(4.24)

Corollary 4.7
.

[
Kel10
,
Kel13b
,
Nak11a
]

Conjecture
1.4
is true.

In the rest of the chapter, we present the proofs of
Theorem
4.5
and
the counting of the constant term
in Theorem
4.6
.
The main idea is to use the tropicalization.
By Theorem
2.30
,
to prove the periodicity of
y
-variables
y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)
,
it is enough to prove the periodicity of
tropical
y
-variables

[y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)]
.
Remarkably,
the dynamics of tropical
y
-variables is governed by
the
Coxeter elements
of the root systems.
This mechanism was found for the
Y
-systems of type
(X,A_{1})
in
[
FZ03b
,
FZ07
]
,
and generalized to the
Y
-systems of type
(X,X^{\prime})
in
[
Nak11a
]
.

4.4
Coxeter elements

This section summarizes the facts on the Coxeter elements we will use.
The presentation here is a minimal one.
See
[
Bou02
,
Hum90
]
for further information.

Let
X
be a simply-laced Dynkin diagram of rank
r
.
Let
\Phi=\Phi(X)\subset V
be the root system corresponding to
X
,
where
V
is the ambient real vector space spanned by
\Phi
with an inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)
.
Let
\alpha_{1}
, …,
\alpha_{r}\in\Phi
be the simple roots
parametrized by the index set
I=\{1,\,\dots,\,r\}
as in Figure
1.4
.
We normalize them as
(\alpha_{a},\alpha_{a})=2
.
Then,
they satisfy, for
a\neq b
,

\displaystyle(\alpha_{a},\alpha_{b})=\begin{cases}-1&a\sim b,\\
0&a\not\sim b,\end{cases}

(4.25)

where
\sim
means the adjacency in the diagram
X
as before.
Let
\Phi_{+}
and
\Phi_{-}
be the sets of

positive
and
negative
roots, respectively,
so that we have
\Phi=\Phi_{+}\sqcup\Phi_{-}
.
The explicit description of
\Phi_{+}
is found
in
[
Bou02
]
.

Example 4.8

(
[
Bou02
, Plates]
)
.

(1) Type
A_{r}
.
The positive roots are given by

\displaystyle[i]:=\alpha_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq r),\quad[i,j]:=\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j}\ (1\leq i<j\leq r).

(4.26)

Thus, we have
|\Phi_{+}|=r(r+1)/2
and
|\Phi|=r(r+1)
.

(2) Type
D_{r}
.
The positive roots are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}[i]&:=\alpha_{i}\ (1\leq i\leq r),\\
[i,j]&:=\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j}\ (1\leq i<j\leq r-2),\\
[i;r-1]&:=\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{r-2}+\alpha_{r-1}\ (1\leq i\leq r-2),\\
[i;r]&:=\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{r-2}+\alpha_{r}\ (1\leq i\leq r-2),\\
[i;r-1,r]&:=\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{r-2}+\alpha_{r-1}+\alpha_{r}\ (1\leq i\leq r-2),\\
\{i,j\}&:=\alpha_{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{j-1}+2\alpha_{j}+\cdots+2\alpha_{r-2}+\alpha_{r-1}+\alpha_{r}\\
&\hskip 150.0pt(1\leq i<j\leq r-2),\end{split}

(4.27)

Thus, we have
|\Phi_{+}|=r(r-1)
and
|\Phi|=2r(r-1)
.

(3) Types
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
.
The explicit description of the positive roots is case-by-case.
For example, for type
E_{6}
, besides the ones from subdiagrams of type
A
and
D
,
there are seven positive roots

1
1
1
1
1
1

1
1
2
1
1
1

1
2
2
1
1
1

1
1
2
2
1
1

1
2
2
2
1
1

1
2
3
2
1
1

1
2
3
2
1
2

(4.28)

Here we only record the fact

|\Phi|=72
,
126
,
240
for
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
, respectively.

To each simple root
\alpha_{a}
,
we assign a linear map

s_{a}:V\rightarrow V
called a
simple reflection
by
(under the normalization
(\alpha_{a},\alpha_{a})=2
)

\displaystyle s_{a}(v)=v-(v,\alpha_{a})\alpha_{a}.

(4.29)

The group
W=W(X)
generated by all simple reflections
is caleed the
Weyl group
associated with the root system
\Phi=\Phi(X)
.
We have
W(\Phi)=\Phi
; it is indeed the defining condition of a root system.

Let
w_{0}
be the
longest element
of
W
;
namely, it has a reduced expression of the longest length in
W
.
It is known that
w_{0}
is unique,
and its length is
|\Phi_{+}|

[
Hum90
, §1.8]
.
Explicitly, it is described as follows
[
Bou02
, Plates]
:

\displaystyle w_{0}(\alpha_{a})=-\alpha_{\omega(a)},

(4.30)

where
\omega
is the diagram automorphism defined in (
4.19
).
(This gives an intrinsic meaning of
\omega
.)
We have
w_{0}^{2}=\mathrm{id}
.

Let us introduce some distinguished elements in
W
.

Definition 4.9
.

A
Coxeter element

c\in W
is a product of all simple reflections,
where each reflection appears exactly once and the order of the product is arbitrary.

A Coxeter element
c

does
depend on the order of the product.
Thus, it is not unique.
However, all Coxeter elements are known to be conjugate to each other in
W

[
Hum90
, Prop. 3.16]
.
Therefore, the following definition makes sense.

Definition 4.10
.

The
Coxeter number

h=h(X)
of the Dynkin diagram
X

is the order of any Coxeter element
c
.

The following fact due to Coxeter is well-known and already mentioned in
Conjecture
1.1
.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
Hum90
, Prop. 3.18]
)
.

The following equality holds:

\displaystyle|\Phi|=hr.

(4.31)

Explicitly, the Coxeter number is given
by

h=r+1
for
A_{r}
,
2r-2
for
D_{r}
, and 12, 18, 30 for
E_{6}
,
E_{7}
,
E_{8}
.
In particular,
h
is odd if and only if
X=A_{r}
(
r
: even).

We are especially interested in the following choice of Coxeter elements.
We put the sign
\kappa_{a}
to each vertex
a
of
X
as we did in Section
4.2
,
so that
any adjacent vertices in
X
have opposite signs.
Then, for any
a
and
b
with
\kappa_{a}=\kappa_{b}
,

s_{a}
and
s_{b}
commute.
So, we have the products
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
by

\displaystyle s_{\pm}=\prod_{a\in I;\ \kappa_{a}=\pm 1}s_{a},

(4.32)

where both are independent of the order of the product.
In the special case of
X=A_{1}
, one of
s_{\pm}
is the identity.
Then,
s_{-}s_{+}
and
s_{+}s_{-}
are Coxeter elements.
By (
4.25
) and (
4.29
),
we have

\displaystyle s_{\kappa_{a}}(\alpha_{a})=-\alpha_{a},\quad s_{-{\kappa_{a}}}(\alpha_{a})=\alpha_{a}+\sum_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}\alpha_{b}.

(4.33)

For any positive even integer
k
, the powers
(s_{+}s_{-})^{k/2}

and
(s_{-}s_{+})^{k/2}
are defined in the ordinary way.
For any positive odd integer
k
, we define the powers as

\displaystyle(s_{+}s_{-})^{k/2}:=\underbrace{s_{-}s_{+}\cdots s_{-}}_{\text{$k$ terms}},\quad(s_{-}s_{+})^{k/2}:=\underbrace{s_{+}s_{-}\cdots s_{+}}_{\text{$k$ terms}}.

(4.34)

The following formula is regarded as the “half periodicity” of the Coxeter elements
s_{-}s_{+}

and
s_{+}s_{-}
.

Proposition 4.12

(
[
Bou02
, V. Exercise §6.2]
,
[
Hum90
, §3.19 Exercise 2]
)
.

The following formula holds.

\displaystyle(s_{-}s_{+})^{h/2}=(s_{+}s_{-})^{h/2}=w_{0}.

(4.35)

Moreover, both products are reduced expressions of
w_{0}
.

Indeed, they are reduced expressions because the number of the simple reflections
in each product is
hr/2=|\Phi_{+}|
.

We define a family of roots
\alpha(a;k)\in\Phi
(
a\in I,\ -1\leq k\leq h
) by

\displaystyle\alpha(a;-1)

\displaystyle=-\alpha_{a}.

(4.36)

\displaystyle\alpha(a;k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(s_{+}s_{-})^{k/2}(\alpha_{a})&\kappa_{a}=+,\\
(s_{-}s_{+})^{k/2}(\alpha_{a})&\kappa_{a}=-\end{cases}\quad(0\leq k\leq h).

(4.37)

The following result due to
[
FZ03b
,
FZ07
]
is crucial to our problem.
So, we provide a proof.
(Our definition of
\alpha(a;k)
is slightly different from the one in
[
FZ03b
,
FZ07
]
.)

Proposition 4.13
.

(a)
[
FZ03b
, Prop. 2.5]
.
For
0\leq k\leq h-1
,
\alpha(a;k)
is a positive root.

(b).

\displaystyle\alpha(a;h-1)=\alpha_{\omega(a)},\quad\alpha(a;h)=-\alpha_{\omega(a)}.

(4.38)

(c)

[
FZ07
, Eq. (10.9)]
.
For
0\leq k\leq h-1
, the following relation holds:

\displaystyle\alpha(a;k+1)+\alpha(a;k-1)=\sum_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}\alpha(b;k).

(4.39)

In particular,
\alpha(a;k)

(1\leq k\leq h)
are uniquely determined
by the initial condition

\displaystyle\alpha(a;-1)=-\alpha_{a},\quad\alpha(a;0)=\alpha_{a}.

(4.40)

and the recurrence relation (
4.39
).

Proof.

(a).
Let us concentrate on the case
h
is even and
\kappa_{a}=+
.
(The other cases are similar.)
The following facts are known or checked easily:

(i).

If
h
is even,
\kappa_{\omega(a)}=\kappa_{a}
.
If
h
is odd,
\kappa_{\omega(a)}=-\kappa_{a}
.

(ii).

A positive root
\alpha
is sent to a negative root by
a simple reflection
s_{a}
if and only if
\alpha=\alpha_{a}

[
Hum90
, Prop. 1.4]
.

(iii).

For any reduced expression
w=s_{a_{1}}\cdots s_{a_{m}}
,
there is no pair
1\leq i<j\leq m
such that

\alpha_{a_{i}}=s_{a_{i+1}}\cdots s_{a_{j-1}}(\alpha_{a_{j}})

[
Hum90
, Thm. 1.7]
.

Since we assume
\kappa_{a}=+
, we have
\kappa_{\omega(a)}=+
by (i).
Thus, we have
s_{+}(\alpha_{\omega(a)})=-\alpha_{\omega(a)}
by (
4.33
).
Then, by (
4.30
), the result (
4.35
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle(s_{+}s_{-})^{(h-1)/2}(\alpha_{a})=\alpha_{\omega(a)}.

(4.41)

Let
(s_{+}s_{-})^{(h-1)/2}=\cdots s_{b_{3}}s_{b_{2}}s_{b_{1}}
.
Suppose that the statement (a) fails.
Then,
by (ii), there is some
p
such that

s_{b_{p}}\cdots s_{b_{1}}(\alpha_{a})=\alpha_{b_{p+1}}
.
Since the elements in
s_{+}
is commutative, we may assume that the leftmost element of
s_{+}
is
s_{a}
.
Then,
s_{b_{p}}\cdots s_{b_{1}}s_{a}
is a subproduct of
(s_{-}s_{+})^{h/2}
.
By (iii), this contradicts the non-reducibility of
(s_{-}s_{+})^{h/2}
in Proposition
4.12
.

(b). The second equality is true by Proposition
4.12
.
The first equality follows from the second one.
For example, if
h
is even,

\kappa_{\omega(a)}=\kappa_{a}
. Thus,

\displaystyle\alpha(a;h-1)=s_{\kappa_{a}}(\alpha(a;h))=s_{\kappa_{a}}(-\alpha_{\omega(a)})=\alpha_{\omega(a)}.

(4.42)

(c).
We prove it by the induction on
k
.
The case
k=0
is nothing but the second equality in
(
4.33
).
Suppose that the relation (
4.39
) holds for some
k
(
1\leq k\leq h-1
).
Suppose that
\kappa_{a}=+
and
k
is even. (The other cases are similar.)
Note that
\kappa_{b}=-
for the one in the RHS of (
4.33
).
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}s_{+}(\alpha(a;k+1))&=\alpha(a;k+2),\quad s_{+}(\alpha(a;k-1))=\alpha(a;k),\\
s_{+}(\alpha(b;k))&=\alpha({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b};k+1).\end{split}

(4.43)

Thus, we obtain the relation (
4.39
) for
k+1

from the one for
k
.
∎

Example 4.14
.

Let us give examples to illustrate Proposition
4.13
.
Let
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}
and
\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}

represent
s_{+}
and
s_{-}
, respectively.
We choose the sign
\kappa_{1}=+
for all examples.

(1) Type
A_{5}
.
h=6
.
We have the following sequences.

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[1]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[1,2]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[2,3]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[3,4]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[4,5]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[5],\\
&[2]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[1,3]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[1,4]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[2,5]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[3,5]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[4],\\
&[3]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[2,4]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[1,5]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[1,5]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}[2,4]\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{-}}[3].\end{split}

(4.44)

The sequences for
[4]
and
[5]
are also found in the above
by reading them in reverse.
As an example of the relation (
4.39
), for
a=2
and
k=3
,
we have
[3,5]+[1,4]=[3,4]+[1,5]
.
