&\hskip 137.0pt\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}\cdots\ \mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{+}}\begin{picture}(0.0,4.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(5.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(10.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(15.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(20.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(10.0,8.0){\small 1}\end{picture}\ .\end{split}

(4.48)

As an example of the relation (
4.39
), for
a=3
and
k=3
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{picture}(0.0,4.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(5.0,0.0){\small 2}\put(10.0,0.0){\small 3}\put(15.0,0.0){\small 2}\put(20.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,8.0){\small 1}\end{picture}\ +\begin{picture}(0.0,4.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(5.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,0.0){\small 2}\put(15.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(20.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,8.0){\small 1}\end{picture}\ =\,\begin{picture}(0.0,4.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(5.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,0.0){\small 2}\put(15.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(20.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,8.0){\small 1}\end{picture}\ +\begin{picture}(0.0,4.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(5.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,0.0){\small 2}\put(15.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(20.0,0.0){\small 0}\put(10.0,8.0){\small 1}\end{picture}\ +\begin{picture}(0.0,4.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(5.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(15.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(20.0,0.0){\small 1}\put(10.0,8.0){\small 0}\end{picture}\ .

(4.49)

4.5
Dynamics of tropical
Y
-systems

We prove
Theorems
4.5
and
4.6

simultaneously by the
tropicalization method
.
Here, we continue to use the notation
[y_{a,a^{\prime}}(u)]
for the tropical
y
-variables
in Chapter
3
.

By applying the tropicalization (
2.68
),
the
Y
-system (
4.16
)
has the form

\displaystyle[y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)][y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s-1)]=\frac{\displaystyle\prod_{b\in I:\,b\sim a}[1+y_{b,a^{\prime}}(s)]}{\displaystyle\prod_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}}[1+y_{a,b^{\prime}}(s)^{-1}]},

(4.50)

which we call the
tropical
Y
-system
.
Even though it is in the same form as (
4.16
),
it is drastically simplified, as explained below.
As we see in Proposition
2.22
,
each tropical
y
-variable

[y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)]
is a monomial in the initial
y
-variables

\mathbf{y}
whose powers are given by the corresponding
c
-vector

\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)
. Let us write it simply as

\displaystyle[y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)]=y^{\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)}.

(4.51)

Recall the sign-coherence property of
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)
in Theorem
2.24
.
Let
\varepsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)
be the tropical sign of
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)
.
Then,

\displaystyle[1+y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)]

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\varepsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)=1,\\
y^{\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)}&\varepsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)=-1,\end{cases}

(4.52)

\displaystyle[1+y_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)^{-1}]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}y^{\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)}&\varepsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)=1,\\
1&\varepsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)=-1.\end{cases}

(4.53)

Thus, the tropical
Y
-system (
4.16
) is equivalent to the following

piecewise-linear
system
for
c
-vectors:

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s+1)+\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(s-1)=\sum_{\scriptstyle b\in I:\,b\sim a\atop\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{b,a^{\prime}}(s)=-1}\mathbf{c}_{b,a^{\prime}}(s)+\sum_{\scriptstyle b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}:\,b^{\prime}\sim a^{\prime}\atop\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{a,b^{\prime}}(s)=1}\mathbf{c}_{a,b^{\prime}}(s),

(4.54)

where the sum is zero if it it empty.
It is piecewise-linear because it involves the signs of
c
-vectors.
Do not confuse the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)
with the sign
\kappa_{a,a^{\prime}}

for the graph
X\times X^{\prime}
.
Observe the similarity to the relation (
4.39
).

We
will solve the tropical
Y
-system
(
4.54
)
with the help of Proposition
4.13
.
Recall that we concentrate on
c
-vectors in the even sector.
Let
\mathbf{e}_{a,a^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I\times I^{\prime}}
be the unit vector whose component is 1 only for
the index
(a,a^{\prime})
and 0 otherwise.

Step I:
The initial condition at
s=-1
,
0
.

We have
y_{a,a^{\prime}}(-1)=y_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)^{-1}
for
\kappa_{a,a}=-
.
Thus, the initial conditions for (
4.54
)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(-1)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{e}_{a,a^{\prime}}\quad(\kappa_{a,a}=-),\quad\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)=\mathbf{e}_{a,a^{\prime}}\quad(\kappa_{a,a}=+).

(4.55)

Let us compare it with (
4.40
).

Step II:
The positive region
0\leq s\leq h^{\prime}-1
.

We determine
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(1)
by (
4.54
)
with the initial condition (
4.55
).
Since
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(0)
is positive, the first term
in the RHS of (
4.54
) vanishes.
Then, it has the same form as the recurrence relation (
4.39
)
of
\alpha(a;k)
for the vertical diagram
X^{\prime}
.
Let
\alpha^{\prime}_{a^{\prime}}
and
\alpha^{\prime}(a^{\prime};k)
be the corresponding ones in
X^{\prime}

to
\alpha_{a}
and
\alpha(a;k)
in
X
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(1)=\sum_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}c_{b^{\prime}}\mathbf{e}_{a,b^{\prime}},\quad\text{where $\alpha^{\prime}(a^{\prime};1)=\sum_{b^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}}c_{b^{\prime}}\alpha^{\prime}_{b^{\prime}}$}.

(4.56)

By (
4.33
),
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(1)
is positive.
So, the same formula holds for
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(2)

by replacing
\alpha^{\prime}(a^{\prime};1)
with
\alpha^{\prime}(a^{\prime};2)
.
Moreover, by Proposition
4.13
(a),

\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(2)
is positive if
h^{\prime}\geq 3
.
The same process continues until
s=h^{\prime}-1
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}-1)=\mathbf{e}_{a,\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})},\quad\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime})=-\mathbf{e}_{a,\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}

(4.57)

by (
4.38
).

Step III:
The negative region
h^{\prime}\leq s\leq h+h-1
.

We determine
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}+1)
by (
4.54
)
and with the initial condition (
4.57
).
Since
\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime})
is negative, the second term
in the RHS of (
4.54
) vanishes.
Then, it has the same form as the recurrence relation (
4.39
)
of
\alpha(a;k)
for the horizontal diagram
X
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}+1)=-\sum_{b\in I}c_{b}\mathbf{e}_{b,a^{\prime}},\quad\text{where $\alpha(a;1)=\sum_{b\in I}c_{b}\alpha_{b}$}.

(4.58)

Again, the process continues until
s=h^{\prime}+h-1
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}+h-1)=-\mathbf{e}_{\omega(a),\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})},\quad\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}+h)=\mathbf{e}_{\omega(a),\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}.

(4.59)

Let us collect the desired results obtained in the above process.

•

We have the periodicity

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{a,a^{\prime}}(h^{\prime}+h)=\mathbf{c}_{\omega(a),\omega^{\prime}(a^{\prime})}(0).

(4.60)

•

The number of the positive and negative
c
-vectors

c_{a,a^{\prime}}(s)

(in the even sector)
for
0\leq s\leq h^{\prime}+h-1
are counted as

\displaystyle N_{+}=\frac{1}{2}h^{\prime}rr^{\prime},\quad N_{-}=\frac{1}{2}hrr^{\prime}.

(4.61)

This is clear when
h^{\prime}
and
h
are even.
When
h^{\prime}
is odd, for example, note that
r^{\prime}
is even.
Then, the formula still holds.

This completes the proof of Theorems
4.5
and
4.6
.

.

(X,X^{\prime})=(A_{3},A_{2})

4.3
.

and

Figure 4.2:
Tropical
Y
-system for
(X,X^{\prime})=(A_{3},A_{2})
,
where
h+h^{\prime}=7
.
The framed ones are the
c
-vectors in the even sector.

We take
Q
as the initial quiver for the corresponding
Y
-pattern.
We arrange each
c
-vector in a matrix form with
horizontal and vertical indices in
I\times I^{\prime}
.
For example, the matrix

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}0&0&0\\
0&1&1\end{matrix}

(4.62)

represents the
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{e}_{2,2}+\mathbf{e}_{3,2}
.
Here, we calculate the mutation of
c
-vectors
directly by the mutation rule (
2.49
)
in the ambient
Y
-pattern,
rather than by the tropical
Y
-system
(
4.54
).
The rule (
2.49
) is translated
into the following pictorial rule, which is very efficient:

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{k}

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{c}_{k},

(4.63)

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathbf{c}_{i}+\mathbf{c}_{k}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.9526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-1.5826pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96925pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$i$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.91269pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.125pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$k$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 43.91948pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.4875pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small and $\varepsilon_{k}=-$}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.76208pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}},\\
\mathbf{c}_{i}+\mathbf{c}_{k}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 2.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-2.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\cirbuild@}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.9526pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.22223pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-1.5826pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.96925pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$i$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 25.91269pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.125pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small$k$}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 41.86394pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.4875pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\text{\small and $\varepsilon_{k}=+$}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.69052pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}},\\
\mathbf{c}_{i}&\text{othterwise}\end{cases}\quad\text{($i\neq k$)}.

(4.64)

All
c
-vectors from
s=0
to
s=7
are presented in Figure

4.2
.
The desired periodicity is found at
s=h+h^{\prime}=7
.

As we see in the above example,
and it is also clear from the analysis in the general case,
in the
positive region

0\leq s\leq h^{\prime}-1
,
a nontrivial mutation occurs only “in the vertical direction”
by the coordination of tropical signs and arrows.
(This is indicated in the dotted vertical lines in Figure

4.2
.)
Similarly,
in the
negative region

h^{\prime}\leq s\leq h^{\prime}+h-1
,
a nontrivial mutation occurs only “in the horizontal direction”.
We call this factorization into the vertical and horizontal directions the
factorization property

of the tropical
Y
-system (
4.50
).
Such a simplification does not occur in the nontropical
Y
-system (
4.16
).

In summary,
we use the formulation of the
Y
-systems by
Y
-patterns, together with the tropicalization,
to prove the periodicity and the DIs for
Y
-systems.
The tropicalization reveals that
the Coxeter elements are behind the scene and govern the dynamics of the
Y
-systems.
This is why the Coxeter number is relevant for the periodicity.

Notes

The cluster algebraic structure for the
Y
-systems (
1.36
) of type
(X,A_{1})

was recognized first by
[
FZ03b
]
and formulated by
Y
-patterns by
[
FZ07
]
.
It was generalized to the type
(X,X^{\prime})
by
[
Kel10
]

using the quiver
Q(X,X^{\prime})
.

There is a “twin” system to
Y
-systems
called
T
-systems
, which were also introduced and studied together with
Y
-systems in B.C.
[
KNS94
]
.

T
-systems are satisfied by
x
-variables for the same sequence of mutations (
4.11
)

[
DK09
,
IIK+10
]
.

After the results by
[
GT96
,
FS95
]
in B.C. mentioned in
Chapter
1
,
the periodicity of
Y
-systems was proved by

[
FZ03b
]
for type
(X,A_{1})
by a cluster algebraic method.
The method consists of the tropicalization and the construction of
F
-polynomials (with the assistance of a computer).
The role of the Coxeter elements was also clarified.
The periodicity of
Y
-systems for type
(X,X^{\prime})

was proved by

[
Kel10
,
Kel13b
]

based on the
categorification
of cluster patterns by
triangulated categories.
The refinement to the half periodicity was given
in
[
IIK+10
]
, where the periodicity of
associated
T
-systems was also proved.
Using the ideas and results of
[
CGT99
,
FZ03b
,
FZ07
,
Cha05
,
Kel10
]
,
the DIs for the
Y
-systems
of type
(X,X^{\prime})
were proved by
[
Nak11a
]
.

By further developing the above methods and ideas,
together with the detropicalization by cluster categories by
[
Pla11
]
,
the periodicity and DIs for more complicated
Y
-systems were
proved.
For the
Y
-systems of nonsimply-laced types
they were proved by
[
IIK+13a
,
IIK+13b
]
.
For the sine-Gordon and reduced sine-Gordon
Y
-systems
they were proved
by
[
NT10
]
in a special case and by
[
NS16
]

in full generality.

Meanwhile, the periodicity of
Y
-systems of type
(A_{r},A_{r^{\prime}})

was also proved by
[
Vol07
]
with the determinant solution
and by
[
Sze09
]
using the flatness of a graph connection.
Thus, the type
A
is the special case where several ideas in mathematics intersect
as in Lie theory.

Part II
Classical mechanical method

Chapter 5
Fock-Goncharov decomposition and dilogarithm

We introduce two new ideas for
Y
-patterns.
The first one is the decomposition of a mutation of
Y
-seeds into
tropical and nontropical parts based on the idea of
Fock-Goncharov.
The second one is the mutation-compatible Poisson bracket
introduced by Gekhtman-Shapiro-Vainshtein.
By combining two ideas, the Euler dilogarithm
emerges as the Hamiltonian
for the nontropical part of each mutation.

5.1
Fock-Goncharov decomposition

Let us introduce the following notion.

Definition 5.1

(Skew-symmetric decomposition)
.

For a skew-symmetrizable (integer) matrix
B=(b_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
,
we consider a decomposition

\displaystyle B=\Delta\Omega,

(5.1)

where
\Delta=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n})
is a diagonal matrix with positive integer diagonals
and
\Omega
is a skew-symmetric rational matrix.
We call it a
skew-symmetric decomposition
of
B
.

For a skew-symmetrizer
D
of the form (
3.8
) for
B
,
we set

\displaystyle\Delta=D^{-1},\quad\Omega=DB.

(5.2)

Then, we have a skew-symmetric decomposition of
B
.
Conversely, for a skew-symmetric decomposition
of
B
,
D=\Delta^{-1}
is a skew-symmetrizer of
B

of the form (
3.8
) such that
DB=\Omega
.
Thus, choosing a skew-symmetrizer of the form (
3.8
)
is equivalent to choosing a skew-symmetric decomposition.

We start by observing that there is some flexibility in writing the mutation
(
2.9
)–(
2.11
).
Namely, Let
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
.
Then, by (
2.8
), one can easily check that the RHSs of the following expressions
are independent of
\varepsilon
:

\displaystyle x^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle x_{k}^{-1}\Biggl(\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}^{[-\varepsilon b_{jk}]_{+}}\Biggr)\frac{1+\hat{y}_{k}^{\varepsilon}}{1\oplus y_{k}^{\varepsilon}}&i=k,\\
x_{i}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(5.3)

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k}^{-1}&i=k,\\
y_{i}y_{k}^{[\varepsilon b_{ki}]_{+}}(1\oplus y_{k}^{\varepsilon})^{-b_{ki}}&i\neq k,\end{cases}

(5.4)

\displaystyle b^{\prime}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-b_{ij}&\text{$i=k$ or $j=k$,}\\
b_{ij}+b_{ik}[\varepsilon b_{kj}]_{+}+[-\varepsilon b_{ik}]_{+}b_{kj}&i,j\neq k.\end{cases}

(5.5)

The case
\varepsilon=1
yields
the original mutation formulas.
We call them the
\varepsilon
-expressions

of the mutation.
Similarly, the mutations of
C
- and
G
-matrices
in Section
2.5

have the following
\varepsilon
-expressions:

\displaystyle c_{ij;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-c_{ik;t}&j=k,\\
c_{ij;t}+c_{ik;t}[\varepsilon b_{kj;t}]_{+}+[-\varepsilon c_{ik;t}]_{+}b_{kj;t}&j\neq k,\end{cases}

(5.6)

\displaystyle g_{ij;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle-g_{ik;t}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}g_{i\ell;t}[-\varepsilon b_{\ell k;t}]_{+}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}b_{i\ell;t_{0}}[-\varepsilon c_{\ell k;t}]_{+}&j=k,\\
g_{ij;t}&j\neq k.\end{cases}

(5.7)

To check the
\varepsilon
-independence of (
5.7
),
we also use the formula (
2.54
).

Now, we are back in the situation in Section
3.1
.
Namely,
we consider a cluster pattern
\mathbf{\Sigma}
with
free coefficients
at
t_{0}
.
Again, we concentrate on the
Y
-pattern only,
and we write
\oplus
in
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})
as
+
.
Consider a sequence of mutations therein

\displaystyle\Upsilon=\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \Upsilon(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{1}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \Upsilon(P),

(5.8)

where
\Upsilon(s)=(\mathbf{y}(s),B(s))
.
Here, we especially assume that

the
Y
-seed
\Upsilon(0)
coincides with the initial

Y
-seed
\Upsilon_{t_{0}}=\Upsilon=(\mathbf{y},B)
.
The
\varepsilon
-expression of the mutation
\mu_{k_{s}}
for
y
-variables is given by

\displaystyle y_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k_{s}}(s)^{-1}&i=k_{s},\\
y_{i}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}

(5.9)

Originally, all
y
-variables
y_{i}(s)
(
s=0
, …,
P
) belong to the common semifield
\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y})

for the initial
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}
,
and the formula (
5.9
) gives relations among them.
Here, we introduce an alternative veiwpoint.
For each
s=0
, …,
P
, we regard the
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}(s)=(y_{1}(s),\dots,y_{n}(s))
as
formal variables and consider the universal semifield

\mathbb{P}(s):=\mathbb{Q}_{\mathrm{sf}}(\mathbf{y}(s))
.
Then, we interpret the mutation (
5.9
) as a semifield isomorphism
(
s=0
, …,
P-1
)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mu{(s)}\colon&\mathbb{P}(s+1)&\rightarrow&\mathbb{P}(s)\\
&y_{i}(s+1)&\mapsto&\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k_{s}}(s)^{-1}&i=k_{s},\\
y_{i}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(5.10)

Now taking advantage of the
\varepsilon
-expression,
we set
\varepsilon
therein to the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{s}:=\varepsilon_{k_{s}}(s)

in Section
3.3
.
Accordingly,
we introduce a semifield isomorphism (
s=0
, …,
P-1
)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tau{(s)}\colon&\mathbb{P}(s+1)&\rightarrow&\mathbb{P}(s)\\
&y_{i}(s+1)&\mapsto&\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k_{s}}(s)^{-1}&i=k_{s},\\
y_{i}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}&i\neq k_{s}\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(5.11)

and a semifield automorphism (
s=0
, …,
P-1
)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho{(s)}\colon&\mathbb{P}(s)&\rightarrow&\mathbb{P}(s)\\
&y_{i}(s)&\mapsto&y_{i}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}.\end{matrix}

(5.12)

Then, we have a decomposition of the mutation
\mu{(s)}

\displaystyle\mu{(s)}=\rho{(s)}\circ\tau{(s)}.

(5.13)

We call it the
Fock-Goncharov decomposition

\mu{(s)}
.
Note that this decomposition fully depends on the choice of the initial vertex
t_{0}
,
because the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{s}
depends on
t_{0}
.
Also, it crucially relies on the sign-coherence property in Theorem

2.24
.

Let us clarify the meaning of this decomposition.
Let
C(s)
be the
C
-matrix for
\Upsilon(s)
,
and let
\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)
be its
i
th
c
-vector.
We do the same specialization
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{s}
for the
C
-matrix mutation
(
5.6
).
By the sign-coherence property, we have

\displaystyle[-\varepsilon_{s}c_{\ell k_{s}}(s)]_{+}=0\quad(1\leq\ell\leq n).

(5.14)

Thus, the mutation (
5.6
) is simplified
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)+[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s)&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}

(5.15)

We recognize it as the log-version of (
5.11
).
By this reason, we call
\tau{(s)}
and
\rho{(s)}

the
tropical part
and the
nontropical part
of
\mu{(s)}
,
respectively.

Next,
we introduce the composite mutation

(s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1)

\displaystyle\mu(s;0)

\displaystyle:=\mu(0)\circ\mu(1)\circ\cdots\circ\mu(s)\colon\mathbb{P}(s+1)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(0).

(5.16)

Let us define its Fock-Goncharov decomposition.

Consider a semifield isomorphism (
s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1
)

\displaystyle\tau(s;0)

\displaystyle:=\tau(0)\circ\tau(1)\circ\cdots\circ\tau(s)\colon\mathbb{P}(s+1)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(0).

(5.17)

The map
\tau{(s;0)}
is identified with the
tropical part
of
\mu{(s;0)}

as follows.

Proposition 5.2

(e.g.,
[
Nak23
, Prop. II.4.3]
)
.

The following formula holds:

\displaystyle\tau(s;0)(y_{i}(s+1))=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s+1)}.

(5.18)

Proof.

We prove it by the induction on
s
.
For
s=0
,
by (
5.15
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tau{(0)}:&\mathbb{P}(1)&\rightarrow&\mathbb{P}(0)\\
&y_{i}(1)&\mapsto&\begin{cases}\displaystyle y_{k_{0}}^{-1}=y^{\mathbf{c}_{k_{0}}(1)}&i=k_{0},\\
y_{i}y_{k_{0}}^{[\varepsilon_{0}b_{k_{0}i}(0)]_{+}}=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(1)}&i\neq k_{0}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(5.19)

Similarly, assuming (
5.18
) for
s-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\quad\ \tau{(s;0)}(y_{i}(s+1))=(\tau{(s-1;0)}\circ\tau(s))(y_{i}(s+1))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle y^{-\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s)}&i=k_{s},\\
y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)}y^{\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s)}{}^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}&i\neq k_{s}\end{cases}

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s+1)},

where we used (
5.15
) again in the last equality.
∎

Below, we consider the nontropical part of
\mu{(s;0)}
.
Let us fix a skew-symmetric decomposition

\displaystyle B(s)=\Delta\Omega(s),

(5.20)

where
\Delta=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n})
is common for
B(s)
(
s=0
, …,
P
).
Let
\Omega=\Omega(0)
.
We introduce
a skew-symmetric bilinear form
\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}

on
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
by

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}:=\mathbf{n}^{T}\Omega\mathbf{n}^{\prime}.

(5.21)

Also, for each
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)
and its tropical sign
\varepsilon_{i}(s)
, we set

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i}(s):=\varepsilon_{i}(s)\mathbf{c}_{i}(s).

(5.22)

This is a positive vector, and we call it a
c^{+}
-vector
.
Then, we introduce a semifield automorphism (
s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1
)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{q}{(s)}\colon&\mathbb{P}(0)&\rightarrow&\mathbb{P}(0)\\
&y^{\mathbf{n}}&\mapsto&y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-\{\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s),\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}}.\end{matrix}

(5.23)

Note that
\mathfrak{q}{(0)}=\rho(0)
.

Proposition 5.3

(
[
Nak23
, Prop.II.4.5]
)
.

(a).
The following formulas hold:

\displaystyle\{\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i}(s),\mathbf{c}_{j}(s)\}_{\Omega}=b_{ij}(s),

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}{(s)}(y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)})=y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)}(1+y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}.

(5.25)

(b). The following commutative diagram holds
(s=1,\,\dots,\,P-1)
:

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(s)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(s)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\tau(s-1;0)
\tau(s-1;0)
\rho(s)
\mathfrak{q}(s)

(5.26)

Proof.

(a).
The equality (
5.24
) is written in the matrix form

\displaystyle\Delta C(s)^{T}\Omega C(s)=\Delta\Omega(s).

(5.27)

This is
equivalent to the equality (
2.77
).
The equality (
5.25
) follows from (
5.23
) and (
5.24
).
(b).
By
Proposition
5.2
, (
5.12
), and (
5.25
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}y_{i}(s)&\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\tau(s-1;0)}}y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\mathfrak{q}(s)}}y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)}(1+y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)},\\
y_{i}(s)&\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\rho(s)}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y_{i}(s)}(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\mapsto}\limits^{\tau(s-1;0)}}y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s)}(1+y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}.\end{split}

(5.28)

∎

All ingredients introduced so far are
summarized in a single commutative diagram as follows:

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(P)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(P-1)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(P-1)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(P-2)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(2)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(1)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(1)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}(0)}
\tau(P-1)
\tau(P-2;0)
\tau(P-3;0)
\tau(P-2)
\tau(1)
\tau(0)
\tau(0)
\mu(P-1)
\mu(1)
\mu(0)
\rho(P-1)
\rho(1)
\mathfrak{q}(P-1)
\mathfrak{q}(1)
\mathfrak{q}(0)
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\vdots}
\textstyle{\ddots}

(5.29)

Finally,
we introduce a semifield automorphism

(s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}(s;0)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{q}(0)\circ\mathfrak{q}(1)\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{q}(s)\colon\mathbb{P}(0)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}(0).

(5.30)

From the diagram (
5.29
), we immediately obtain
the decomposition

\displaystyle\mu(s;0)=\mathfrak{q}(s;0)\circ\tau(s;0).

(5.31)

We call it the
Fock-Goncharov decomposition
of the
composite
mutation
\mu(s;0)
,
where
\mathfrak{q}{(s;0)}
is the
nontropical part
of
\mu{(s;0)}
.

5.2
Mutation-compatible Poisson bracket

Let us recall the most basic notion in Poisson geometry
(e.g.,
[
Wei83
]
).

Definition 5.4

(Poisson bracket)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}(M)
be the commutative algebra of all smooth real-valued functions on
a smooth manifold
M
.
A bilinear map
\{\cdot,\cdot\}\colon\mathcal{C}(M)\times\mathcal{C}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(M)
is a
Poisson bracket
on
M
(or
\mathcal{C}(M)
) if the following
conditions are satisfied.

•

skew-symmetry:
\{f,g\}=-\{g,f\}
.

•

Leibniz rule:
\{f,gh\}=\{f,g\}h+g\{f,h\}.

•

Jacobi identity:
\{\{f,g\},h\}+\{\{g,h\},f\}+\{\{h,f\},g\}=0.

Equipped with a Poisson bracket,
the manifold
M
is called a
Poisson manifold
,
and
the algebra
\mathcal{C}(M)
is called a
Poisson algebra
.

For simplicity,
consider a smooth manifold
M=\mathbb{R}^{n}

with global coordinates

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
.
Then,
\mathcal{C}(M)
is identified with the algebra
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y})
of all smooth functions
of
\mathbf{y}
.
The following fact is due to Lie.

Proposition 5.5

(e.g.,
[
Wei83
]
)
.

(a).
Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
be any poisson bracket on
\mathcal{C}(M)
.
Then, the following formula holds:

\displaystyle\{f,g\}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial y_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial y_{j}}\{y_{i},y_{j}\}.

(5.32)

(b).
Conversely, for any data
w_{ij}\in\mathcal{C}(M)

(i,\,j=1,\,\dots,\,r)
satisfying

\displaystyle w_{ij}=-w_{ji},

(5.33)

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\partial w_{ij}}{\partial y_{\ell}}w_{\ell k}+\frac{\partial w_{jk}}{\partial y_{\ell}}w_{\ell i}+\frac{\partial w_{ki}}{\partial y_{\ell}}w_{\ell j}\right)=0,

(5.34)

the bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
defined by
\{y_{i},y_{j}\}:=w_{ij}

and (
5.32
)
is a Poisson bracket on

\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y})
,
Moreover, (
5.34
) is written as

\displaystyle\{\{y_{i},y_{j}\},y_{k}\}+\{\{y_{j},y_{k}\},y_{i}\}+\{\{y_{k},y_{i}\},y_{j}\}=0.

(5.35)

Proof.

For the reader’s convenience, we present an outline of the proof.

(a).
For polynomial functions
f
,
g
in
\mathbf{y}
,
the formula (
5.32
) is deduced from
the Leibniz rule.
Then, it is uniquely extended to smooth functions
by the Weierstrass approximation theorem.

(b).
The only nontrivial condition to be checked is the Jacobi identity.
Let us use the abbreviations

\partial f/\partial y_{i}=f_{i}
,
\partial^{2}f/\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}=f_{ij}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \{\{f,g\},h\}=\biggl\{\sum_{i,j}f_{i}g_{j}w_{ij},h\biggr\}\\
&=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}(f_{i}g_{j})_{k}h_{\ell}w_{ij}w_{k\ell}+\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}f_{i}g_{j}h_{\ell}(w_{ij})_{k}w_{k\ell}\\
&=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}(f_{ik}g_{j}h_{\ell}-g_{ik}h_{j}f_{\ell})w_{ij}w_{k\ell},+\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}f_{i}g_{j}h_{\ell}(w_{ij})_{k}w_{k\ell},\end{split}

(5.36)

where we used the skew-symmetry (
5.33
)
in the last equality.
The Jacobi identity follows
from the last expression and the condition (
5.34
).
∎

We are interested in the following
quadratic
Poisson brackets.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\Omega=(\omega_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}
be any skew-symmetric real matrix.
Define the data
w_{ij}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y})

(i,\,j=1,\,\dots,\,n)
by

\displaystyle w_{ij}=\{y_{i},y_{j}\}:=\omega_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}.

(5.37)

Then, they satisfy the conditions (
5.33
) and (
5.35
).
Thus, they define a Poisson bracket on
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y})
.

Proof.

The condition (
5.33
) is clear.
The condition (
5.35
) is verified by the cyclic expression

\displaystyle\{\{y_{i},y_{j}\},y_{k}\}=(\omega_{ij}\omega_{ik}-\omega_{jk}\omega_{ji})y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}.

(5.38)

∎

The variables
\mathbf{y}
in
the Poisson bracket (
5.37
)
are called
log-canonical variables

by
[
GSV03
]
because of the following property:

\displaystyle\{\log y_{i},\log y_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}.

(5.39)

(The name might be a little confusing because
\log y_{i}
are not canonical in the terminology for classical mechanics.)

Now we come back to the situation in the previous section.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))
be the algebra of all smooth functions
of
\mathbf{y}(s)

defined on
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}

with coordinates
\mathbf{y}(s)
.
By regarding
the semifield isomorphism (
5.10
)–(
5.12
)
as
changes of variables
,
we have
the algebra isomorphisms (denoted by the same symbols)

\displaystyle\mu(s)\colon

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s+1))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s)),

(5.40)

\displaystyle\tau(s)\colon

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s+1))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s)),

(5.41)

\displaystyle\rho(s)\colon

\displaystyle\ \mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s)).

(5.42)

Now,
we consider a smooth manifold
M\simeq\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}

equipped with
P+1
global positive coordinates
\mathbf{y}(s)

(
s=0,\,\dots,\,P
) such that
\mathbf{y}(s+1)
and
\mathbf{y}(s)
are glued
by the tropical transformation
\tau(s)
(
not by the ordinary mutation
\mu(s)
).
The algebra

\mathcal{C}(M)
is identified with
\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))

in the coordinates
\mathbf{y}(s)
for each
s
.

Let
\Omega(s)
be the rational skew-symmetric matrix in (
5.20
).
By (
5.5
),
we have the mutation formula

\displaystyle\omega_{ij}(s+1)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\omega_{ij}(s)&\text{$i=k$ or $j=k$,}\\
\omega_{ij}(s)+[\varepsilon b_{kj}(s)]_{+}\omega_{ik}(s)+[\varepsilon b_{ki}(s)]_{+}\omega_{kj}(s)&i,\,j\neq k.\end{cases}

(5.43)

Following Gekhtman-Shapiro-Vainshtein
[
GSV03
]
,
we define a Poisson bracket on each
\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))
(
s=0
, …,
P-1
) by

\displaystyle\{y_{i}(s),y_{j}(s)\}_{s}:=\omega_{ij}(s)y_{i}(s)y_{j}(s),

(5.44)

so that
y_{i}(s)
are log-canonical variables.
They
are
mutation-compatible
in the following sense.

Theorem 5.7

(
[
GSV03
, Thm. 1.4]
)
.

The maps

\mu(s)
,
\tau(s)
, and
\rho(s)
in
(
5.40
)–(
5.42
) commute
with the Poisson brackets (
5.44
).
Namely, we have

\displaystyle\{\mu(s)(f),\mu(s)(g)\}_{s}

\displaystyle=\mu(s)(\{f,g\}_{s+1})\quad(f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s+1))),

(5.45)

\displaystyle\{\tau(s)(f),\tau(s)(g)\}_{s}

\displaystyle=\tau(s)(\{f,g\}_{s+1})\quad(f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s+1))),

(5.46)

\displaystyle\{\rho(s)(f),\rho(s)(g)\}_{s}

\displaystyle=\rho(s)(\{f,g\}_{s})\quad(f,g\in\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))).

(5.47)

Proof.

Let us first prove (
5.47
).
By Proposition
5.5
(a),
it is enough to prove it for

f(\mathbf{y})=y_{i}(s)
and
g(\mathbf{y})=y_{j}(s)
.
We have

\displaystyle\quad\ \{\rho(s)(y_{i}(s)),\rho(s)(y_{j}(s))\}_{s}

\displaystyle=\{y_{i}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)},y_{j}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}j}(s)}\}_{s}

\displaystyle=(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}j}(s)}\{y_{i}(s),y_{j}(s)\}_{s}

\displaystyle\qquad+y_{i}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}j}(s)}\{(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)},y_{j}(s)\}_{s}

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}y_{j}(s)\{y_{i}(s),(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}j}(s)}\}_{s}

\displaystyle{=}\omega_{ij}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}j}(s)}y_{i}(s)y_{j}(s)

\displaystyle=\omega_{ij}(s)\rho(s)(y_{i}(s))\rho(s)(y_{j}(s))=\rho(s)(\{y_{i}(s),y_{j}(s)\}_{s}).

The equality (
5.46
) is proved similarly.
For example,
for
i,\,j\neq k
,
we have

\displaystyle\quad\ \{\tau(s)(y_{i}(s+1)),\tau(s)(y_{j}(s+1))\}_{s}

\displaystyle=\{y_{i}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}},y_{j}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}\}_{s}

\displaystyle=y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}j}(s)]_{+}}\{y_{i}(s),y_{j}(s)\}_{s}

\displaystyle\qquad+y_{i}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}j}(s)]_{+}}\{y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}},y_{j}(s)\}_{s}

\displaystyle\qquad+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}y_{j}(s)\{y_{i}(s),y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}j}(s)]_{+}}\}_{s}

\displaystyle{=}(\omega_{ij}(s)+[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}\omega_{k_{s}j}(s)+[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}j}(s)]_{+}\omega_{ik_{s}}(s))

\displaystyle\qquad\times y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}y_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}j}(s)]_{+}}y_{i}(s)y_{j}(s)

\displaystyle{=}\omega_{ij}(s+1)\tau(s)(y_{i}(s+1))\tau(s)(y_{j}(s+1))

\displaystyle=\tau(s)(\{y_{i}(s+1),y_{j}(s+1)\}_{s+1}).

Other cases are similar and easier.
Lastly, the equality (
5.45
) follows from
(
5.46
) and (
5.47
).
∎

5.3
Euler dilogarithm as Hamiltonian

Let
\rho(s)
be the automorphism of
\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))
in (
5.42
).
Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09c
, §1.3]

made the following remarkable observation (translated in the present context):

With the Poisson bracket (
5.44
),
the automorphism
\rho(s)
is described by
the time-one flow
for the Hamiltonian given by the Euler dilogarithm.

Below, we explain the meaning of this statement.

In general,
for an algebra of functions
\mathcal{C}(M)
with a Poisson bracket

\{\cdot,\cdot\}
,
let
f_{t}\in\mathcal{C}
(
t\in\mathbb{R}
) be a one-parameter family of functions,
where
t
is the
continuous time
.
We specify
a function
\mathscr{H}\in\mathcal{C}(M)
called the
Hamiltonian

that
governs the time development of
f_{t}
by the following

equation of motion
:

\displaystyle\dot{f}_{t}=\{\mathscr{H},f_{t}\},\quad\dot{f}_{t}:=\frac{d}{dt}f_{t}.

(5.48)

We apply this idea
to our Poisson bracket
(
5.44
)
to describe the automorphism
\rho(s)
.
We choose the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y}(s))

that is
given
by the Euler dilogarithm as follows:

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(s):={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})=-{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\int_{0}^{y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}}\frac{\log(1+y)}{y}\,dy.

(5.49)

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\mathscr{H}(s),y_{i}(s)\}_{s}&={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\{\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}),y_{i}(s)\}_{s}\\
&=-{\delta_{k_{s}}}\frac{\log(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})}{y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}}\omega_{k_{s}i}(s)y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}y_{i}(s)\\
&=y_{i}(s)\log(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}.\end{split}

(5.50)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\{\mathscr{H}(s),y_{k_{s}}(s)\}_{s}=0.

(5.51)

Thus, we have a solution of the equation of motion (
5.48
)

\displaystyle f_{t}=y_{i}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)t}.

(5.52)

By setting
t=0
and
t=1
,
we obtain

\displaystyle f_{0}=y_{i}(s),\quad f_{1}=y_{i}(s)(1+y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}=\rho(s)(y_{i}(s)).

(5.53)

This explains the meaning of “the automorphism
\rho(s)
is described by the time-one flow
of
\mathscr{H}(s)
”.

By the formula (
5.18
)
and the compatibility (
5.46
),
the automorphism
\mathfrak{q}(s)
in (
5.23
) is described by the
time-one flow of the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{0}(s):=\tau(s-1;0)(\mathscr{H}(s))={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})

(5.54)

in the initial
y
-coordinates
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(0)
with the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}=\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{0}
.

More generally,
for any integer vector
\mathbf{c}=(c_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and any real number
a
,
we may consider a Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a}=a\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y^{\mathbf{c}})

(5.55)

for the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}=\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{0}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

The time-one flow of
the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a}
in (
5.55
)
is given by

\displaystyle y^{\mathbf{n}}\mapsto y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{c}})^{-a\{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}},

(5.56)

where
\{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}
is the one in (
5.21
).

Proof.

In the same way as
(
5.50
),
we have

\displaystyle\{\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a},y_{i}\}=-a\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}\omega_{ji}\biggr)y_{i}\log(1+y^{\mathbf{c}}).

(5.57)

Then, by the Leibniz rule, we have

\displaystyle\{\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a},y^{\mathbf{n}}\}=-a\{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}y^{\mathbf{n}}\log(1+y^{\mathbf{c}}).

(5.58)

Thus, we obtain the claim.
∎

In summary,
the Euler dilogarithm is built into cluster algebra theory
as the Hamiltonian
for the nontropical part
of each mutation in a
Y
-pattern.
This gives an
intrinsic connection
between the dilogarithm and cluster algebra theory.

Notes

The
\varepsilon
-expressions and the connection to the tropicalization
are gradually recognized
by
[
BFZ05
,
Nak11b
,
NZ12
,
Nak12
]
.
The Fock-Goncharov decomposition was introduced
by
[
FG09a
,
FG09c
]
for
\varepsilon=1

in studying the cluster varieties and their quantizations.
The signed version first appeared in
[
Nag13
,
Kel11
]
in the study of the Donaldson-Thomas invariants.
The presentation here follows
[
Nak23
]
.
A parallel decomposition
is also available for
x
-variables
[
FG09a
,
Nak23
]
.

The mutation-compatible Poisson bracket was introduced by
[
GSV03
]
.
A more extensive treatment is given in
[
GSV10
]
.
For Poisson geometry, see the reviews
[
Wei83
,
Wei98
]
and references therein.

The appearance of the dilogarithm as the Hamiltonian
naturally guided Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09a
,
FG09c
]

to quantize cluster algebras using the
quantum dilogarithm
,
which we treat in Part IV.

Chapter 6
Classical mechanical method for dilogarithm identities

We reformulate the Hamiltonian system for
mutations with
canonical coordinates
.
The Legendre transformation in classical mechanics
turns the
Hamiltonian
given by the Euler dilogarithm
into the
Lagrangian
given by the modified Rogers dilogarithm.
Based on this perspective, we give an alternative and independent proof of Theorem
3.5
.
This proof provides a more intrinsic understanding of the DIs for
Y
-patterns,
where the constancy of the dilogarithm sum is regarded as a variant of Noether’s theorem.

6.1
Hamiltonian formulation of mutations

In the previous chapter, we observed that the Euler dilogarithm is built into
a
Y
-pattern as the Hamiltonian (
5.49
) for the nontropical part
of each mutation.
We then ask the following natural questions:

Q1.

Is there any relation between this fact and the DIs in
Theorem
3.5
?

Q2.

If so, how and why does
the Euler dilogarithm
turn into
the modified Rogers dilogarithm
in these DIs?

It was shown by Gekhtman-Nakanishi-Rupel
[
GNR17
]

that the above questions are naturally answered
if we reformulate the Hamiltonian system with

canonical coordinates

and transform the Hamiltonian into the
Lagrangian

by the
Legendre transformation
in classical mechanics.

To this end,
let us recall and make it precise
how the sequence of mutations
(
5.8
) is formulated as a Hamiltonian system
with
continuous time

based on the setting and results in Chapter
5
.

1.

We consider a smooth manifold
M\simeq\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
equipped with

P+1
global positive coordinates by
\mathbf{y}(s)
(
s=0,\,\dots,\,P
)
that are glued by the tropical transformations

\tau(s)
in (
5.41
) (not by the ordinary mutation
\mu(s)
).
We call
M
the
phase space
.

2.

As usual, the change of coordinates (variables)

\tau(s):\mathcal{C}_{+}(s+1)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{+}(s)

induces the bijection
\tau^{*}(s):\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
in the
opposite direction
.
Namely,
\tau^{*}(s)
maps a point
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
in the
\mathbf{y}(s)
-coordinates
to the point
(a^{\prime}_{1},\dots,a^{\prime}_{n})
in the
\mathbf{y}(s+1)
-coordinates, where

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle a_{k_{s}}^{-1}&i=k_{s},\\
a_{i}a_{k_{s}}^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}

(6.1)

The same applies to other maps
\mu(s)
,
\rho(s)
, and
\mathfrak{q}(s)

and induces the maps
\mu^{*}(s)
,
\rho^{*}(s)
, and
\mathfrak{q}^{*}(s)

all from
\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
to itself.

3.

The phase space
M
is equipped with the mutation-compatible Poisson
bracket in (
5.44
).

4.

We consider the time span
[0,P]

for the continuous time
t
.
In each time span
[s,s+1]
(
s=0
, …,
P-1
),
the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)
is given in the
\mathbf{y}(s)
-coordinates by

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(s)={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})

(6.2)

as in (
5.49
).
In the initial
\mathbf{y}
-coordinates, the Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{0}(s)={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})

(6.3)

5.

By (
5.52
), in each time span
[s,s+1]
,
the trajectory (the Hamiltonian flow) of a point in the phase space
M

along time
t

is described in the
\mathbf{y}(s)
-coordinates as follows:
Let
\mathbf{a}_{s}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{n}
be the position of the point at
t=s
.
Then, the position
\mathbf{a}_{t}=(a^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,a^{\prime}_{n})
at

t\in[s,s+1]

is given by

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{i}=a_{i}(1+a_{k_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)(t-s)}.

(6.4)

In particular,
a^{\prime}_{k_{s}}=a_{k_{s}}
for any
t\in[s,s+1]
.

6.

For a trajectory in
M
in the full time span
[0,P]
,
if the initial position in the initial coordinates
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(0)
is
\mathbf{a}_{0}
,
the final position in the
\mathbf{y}(P)
-coordinates
is given by
\mu^{*}(P-1;0)(\mathbf{a}_{0})
,
while the final position
in the initial coordinates
\mathbf{y}

is given by
\mathfrak{q}^{*}(P-1;0)(\mathbf{a}_{0})
.

See Figure
6.1
for the summary of the above formulation.

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

P
M
\mathbf{y}
\mathbf{y}(1)
\mathbf{y}(2)
\mathbf{y}(3)
\mathbf{y}(P)
\tau^{*}(0)
\tau^{*}(1)
\tau^{*}(2)
\tau^{*}(P-1)
\mathbf{a}_{0}
\mathfrak{q}^{*}(P-1;0)(\mathbf{a}_{0})
\mu^{*}(P-1;0)(\mathbf{a}_{0})

Figure 6.1:
Schematic diagram of Hamilton formulation of mutations.

6.2
Canonical coordinates

We will “embed” the above Hamiltonian system into the one with
canonical coordinates.
Let us first concentrate on the initial coordinates
\mathbf{y}
, for simplicity.
Let
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
and
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})

be variables.
We regard the pair
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})

as global coordinates of a manifold
\tilde{M}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
Let
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
be the set of all smooth functions
of
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
We consider the following standard Poisson bracket on
\tilde{M}
:

\displaystyle\{f,g\}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{i}}\frac{\partial g}{\partial u_{i}}-\frac{\partial g}{\partial p_{i}}\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_{i}}\right)\quad(f,g\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})).

(6.5)

Thus,
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
are
canonical coordinates (variables)

in classical mechanics (also called
Darboux coordinates
in Poisson geometry).
The following properties hold.

\displaystyle\{p_{i},u_{j}\}=\delta_{ij},\quad\{u_{i},u_{j}\}=\{p_{i},p_{j}\}=0,

(6.6)

\displaystyle\{f,u_{i}\}=\frac{\partial f}{\partial p_{i}},\quad\{f,p_{i}\}=-\frac{\partial f}{\partial u_{i}}\quad(f\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})).

(6.7)

(Here and below, we use both
the
Kronecker delta
\delta_{ij}

and the symbol
\delta_{i}
for a component of
\Delta
.
We ask
the reader to distinguish them carefully.)

We set

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{i}:=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}+w_{i})\right),\quad w_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}u_{j}.

(6.8)

Proposition 6.1

(
[
GNR17
, Prop. 3.3]
)
.

The following formula holds.

\displaystyle\{\tilde{y}_{i},\tilde{y}_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}\tilde{y}_{i}\tilde{y}_{j}.

(6.9)

Proof.

As noted in (
5.39
),
the claim is equivalent to

\displaystyle\{\log\tilde{y}_{i},\log\tilde{y}_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}.

(6.10)

Indeed, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{\log\tilde{y}_{i},\log\tilde{y}_{j}\}&=\frac{1}{2}\{\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i},w_{j}\}+\frac{1}{2}\{\delta^{-1}_{j}p_{j},w_{i}\}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\omega_{ij}-\frac{1}{2}\omega_{ji}=\omega_{ij}.\end{split}

(6.11)

∎

The bracket (
6.9
) coincides with the one (
5.44
) with
s=0
.
Moreover, the functions (variables)
\tilde{y}_{1}
, …,
\tilde{y}_{n}
are algebraically independent.
Thus, we have an embedding of the Poisson algebra

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\eta\colon&\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})\\
&y_{i}&\mapsto&\tilde{y}_{i}.\end{matrix}

(6.12)

Let us concentrate on the time span
[0,1]
.
We consider the Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}={\delta_{k_{0}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})

(6.13)

corresponding to
\mathscr{H}(0)
in (
6.2
).
We have

\displaystyle\{\mathscr{H},\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}\}=0.

(6.14)

Therefore, the function
\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}
is constant along the time development.
The equations of motion for the canonical coordinates are given by

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{i}

\displaystyle=\{\mathscr{H},u_{i}\}=\frac{\partial\mathscr{H}}{\partial p_{i}}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}),

(6.15)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{i}

\displaystyle=\{\mathscr{H},p_{i}\}=-\frac{\partial\mathscr{H}}{\partial u_{i}}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}),

(6.16)

where we used
b_{ik_{0}}\delta_{k_{0}}=-b_{k_{0}i}\delta_{i}

in the second formula.
Thus, for
t\in[0,1]
, the functions
u_{i}
,
p_{i}
,
w_{i}
,
\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}
develop
linearly as

\displaystyle u_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=u_{i}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})\right)t,

(6.17)

\displaystyle p_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=p_{i}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})\right)t,

(6.18)

\displaystyle w_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=w_{i}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})\right)t,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})\right)t,

(6.20)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{y}_{i}(t)}

\displaystyle=\tilde{y}_{i}(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})^{-b_{k_{0}i}t}.

(6.21)

The last one agrees with the result (
5.52
) as expected.
Therefore,
the time-one flow of the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
induces the following automorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho(0)\colon&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})\\
&u_{i}&\mapsto&\displaystyle u_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}),\\
&p_{i}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.0pt}p_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}),\\
&w_{i}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.0pt}w_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}),\\
&\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\rule{0.0pt}{18.0pt}\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{0}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}}),\\
&\tilde{y}_{i}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{i}(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{0}})^{-b_{k_{0}i}}.\end{matrix}

(6.22)

Again, the last one is compatible with the maps
\rho(0)=\mathfrak{q}(0)
in (
5.42
).

Proposition 6.2
.

The map
\rho(0)
preserves the Poisson bracket in (
6.6
).

Proof.

This is a special case of a well-known fact that
the Hamiltonian flow preserves the Poisson bracket (e.g.,
[
AM78
, Pop. 3.3.4]
).
Alternatively,
one can directly check it by (
6.22
).
∎

A drawback of this formulation is that
we have
2n
variables
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
,
which are twice of the original
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}
.
To eliminate this redundancy,
let us introduce the subset
\tilde{M}_{0}
of
\tilde{M}

consisting of the points such that
in the
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
-coordinates the following condition holds:

\displaystyle p_{i}=\delta_{i}w_{i}\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\,n).

(6.23)

Lemma 6.3
.

The subset
\tilde{M}_{0}
is preserved under the time-development by
the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
in (
6.13
).

Proof.

This is clear from (
6.19
) and (
6.20
).
∎

We call
\tilde{M}_{0}
the
small phase space
.
The algebra
\mathcal{C}(\tilde{M}_{0})
of the smooth functions on
\tilde{M}_{0}
is identified with
the quotient

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})_{0}:=\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})/I_{0},

(6.24)

where
I_{0}
is the ideal of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
consisting of the functions
vanishing on
\tilde{M}_{0}
.

This causes, however, a new problem.
Due to the definition (
6.8
) of
\tilde{y}
,
the map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\eta_{0}\colon&\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y})&\rightarrow&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})_{0}\\
&y_{i}&\mapsto&\tilde{y}_{i}.\end{matrix}

(6.25)

is injective (and also bijective) if and only if the matrix
B
is nonsingular.
Equivalently,
\tilde{y}_{i}
’s are algebraically independent
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})_{0}

if and only if the matrix
B
is nonsingular.
For example, in the extreme case
B=0
,

\tilde{y}_{i}=1
for any
i
.
We will come back to this issue later.
At this moment
we do not require that
B
is nonsingular.

6.3
Tropical transformations of canonical coordinates

The canonical coordinates for the time span
[0,1]

in the previous section can be generalized for each time span
[s,s+1]
(
s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1
)
just by repeating the same construction.
So, we quickly summarize the notation and results.

For each
s=0,\,\dots,\,P-1
,
we introduce variables
\mathbf{u}(s)=(u_{i}(s))_{i=1}^{n}
and
\mathbf{p}(s)=(p_{i}(s))_{i=1}^{n}
,
and regard the pair
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
as global coordinates of
\tilde{M}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
The Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{s}
on
\tilde{M}
is defined
as in (
6.5
) by replacing
u_{i}
and
p_{i}
therein with
u_{i}(s)
and
p_{i}(s)
.
We set

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{i}(s):=\exp\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}(s)+w_{i}(s))\right),\quad w_{i}(s):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}(s)u_{j}(s).

(6.26)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\{\tilde{y}_{i}(s),\tilde{y}_{j}(s)\}_{s}=\omega_{ij}(s)\tilde{y}_{i}(s)\tilde{y}_{j}(s),

(6.27)

which agrees with (
5.44
).
So, we have an embedding

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\eta(s)\colon&\mathcal{C}_{+}(\mathbf{y}(s))&\rightarrow&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))\\
&y_{i}(s)&\mapsto&\tilde{y}_{i}(s).\end{matrix}

(6.28)

The Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}(s)={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(6.29)

The equations of motion for the canonical coordinates are given as

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{k_{s}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}),

(6.30)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{i}(s)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(6.31)

The time-one flow of the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)
induces the
automorphism

\displaystyle\rho(s)\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),

\displaystyle\,\mathbf{p}(s))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))

\displaystyle u_{i}(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto\displaystyle u_{i}(s)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{k_{s}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}),

(6.32)

\displaystyle p_{i}(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto\displaystyle p_{i}(s)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}),

(6.33)

\displaystyle w_{i}(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto\displaystyle w_{i}(s)-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})\right),

(6.34)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto\displaystyle\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}(s)-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})\right),

(6.35)

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{i}(s)

\displaystyle\mapsto\tilde{y}_{i}(s)(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-b_{k_{s}i}(s)}.

(6.36)

Moreover, it preserves the Poisson bracket
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{s}
.

Now we glue
the canonical coordinates
(\mathbf{u}(s+1),\mathbf{p}(s+1))
and

(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))

by the following linear transformation,
which is the counterpart of the tropical transformation
\tau(s)
in (
5.41
).

\displaystyle\tau{(s)}\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s+1),

\displaystyle\,\mathbf{p}(s+1)))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s)),

\displaystyle u_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle-u_{k_{s}}(s)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-\varepsilon_{s}b_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}u_{j}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
u_{i}(s)&i\neq k_{s},\end{cases}

(6.37)

\displaystyle p_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle-p_{k_{s}}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
p_{i}(s)+[-\varepsilon_{s}b_{ik_{s}}(s)]_{+}p_{k_{s}}(s)&i\neq k_{s},\end{cases}

(6.38)

\displaystyle w_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle-w_{i}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
w_{i}(s)+[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}w_{k_{s}}(s)&i\neq k_{s},\end{cases}

(6.39)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\delta^{-1}_{k_{s}}p_{k_{s}}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
\delta^{-1}_{i}p_{i}(s)+[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}\delta^{-1}_{k_{s}}p_{k_{s}}(s)&i\neq k_{s},\end{cases}

(6.40)

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{i}(s+1)

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{-1}&i=k_{s},\\
\tilde{y}_{i}(s)\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}}&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}

(6.41)

By (
6.41
),
we have a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y}(s+1))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s+1),\mathbf{p}(s+1))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y}(s))}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))}
\eta(s+1)
\eta(s)
\tau(s)
\tau(s)

(6.42)

Proposition 6.4

(
[
GNR17
, Prop. 3.9]
)
.

(a).
The map
\tau(s)
commutes
with the Poisson brackets.
Namely, we have

\displaystyle\{\tau(s)(f),\tau(s)(g)\}_{s}

\displaystyle=\tau(s)(\{f,g\}_{s+1})\quad(f,\,g\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s+1),\mathbf{p}(s+1))).

(6.43)

(b). The following property holds:

\displaystyle\tau(s)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}(s+1)p_{i}(s+1)\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u_{i}(s)p_{i}(s).

(6.44)

Proof.

(a).
It is enough to check it for
f=p_{i}(s+1)
and
g=u_{j}(s+1)
.
So, let us show

\{\tau(s)(p_{i}(s+1)),\tau(s)(u_{j}(s+1))\}_{s}=\delta_{ij}
.
By (
6.37
) and (
6.38
),
the map
\tau(s)
acts on

\mathbf{u}(s+1)
and
\mathbf{p}(s+1)
separately and linearly.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N}
be their matrix representations
with respect to the bases
\mathbf{u}(s+1)
,
\mathbf{u}(s)
and
\mathbf{p}(s+1)
,
\mathbf{p}(s)
,
respectively.
Then, it is easy to check that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}N^{T}=M=M^{-1}}
holds.
Thus, the equality (
6.43
) holds.
(b). The same proof is applicable.
∎

Define the mutation
\mu(s)
by the composition

\displaystyle\mu(s):=\rho(s)\circ\tau(s)\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s+1),\mathbf{p}(s+1))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s)).

(6.45)

We also define

\mu(s;0)
,
\tau(s;0)
,

\mathfrak{q}(s)
, and
\mathfrak{q}(s;0)

in a parallel way with the ones in Section
5.1
.

Let us turn to the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}
in the previous section.
Let
\tilde{M}_{0}(s)
be set of the points in the phase space
\tilde{M}

such that in the
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
-coordinates the following condition holds:

\displaystyle p_{i}(s)=\delta_{i}w_{i}(s)\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\,n).

(6.46)

Lemma 6.5
.

The subset
\tilde{M}_{0}(s)
is preserved under the time-development by
the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)
.
Moreover,
the set
\tilde{M}_{0}(s)
is independent of
s
, namely,

\tilde{M}_{0}(s)=\tilde{M}_{0}
.

Proof.

The first claim is proved in the same way as Lemma
6.3
.
Also, by (
6.39
) and (
6.40
),
the condition (
6.46
) is preserved by
\tau(s)
.
∎

By Lemma
6.5
,
we can restrict our Hamiltonian system on the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}

through the full time-span
[0,P]
whenever necessary.

Again, we have a map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\eta_{0}(s)\colon&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y}(s))&\rightarrow&\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))_{0}:=\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))/I_{0}(s)\\
&y_{i}(s)&\mapsto&\tilde{y}_{i}(s),\end{matrix}

(6.47)

where
I_{0}(s)
is the ideal of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
consisting of the functions vanishing on
\tilde{M}_{0}
.
It is injective (also bijective) if and only if
B(s)
is nonsingular.

6.4
Modified Rogers dilogarithm as Lagrangian

Let us recall basic facts on the Legendre transformation
and the Lagrangian.
See, for example,
[
AM78
]
for more information.

For simplicity, consider a Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
on the phase space
M\simeq\mathbb{R}^{2n}

with canonical coordinates
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
as global coordinates.
The space
M
is identified with the contangent bundle
\pi:T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}

so that
\pi(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})\mapsto\mathbf{u}
.
Then, the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
induces the following fiber-preserving map:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}F_{\mathscr{H}}\colon&T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}&\rightarrow&T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})&\mapsto&(\mathbf{u},\dot{\mathbf{u}}),&\quad\dot{u}_{i}=\{\mathscr{H},u_{i}\}.\end{matrix}

(6.48)

Definition 6.6
.

A Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
is
regular
(resp.
singular
) if the map
F_{\mathscr{H}}
is a diffeomorphism
(resp. otherwise).

Suppose that a Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
is regular.
The
Lagrangian

\mathscr{L}
for
\mathscr{H}
is defined by the
Legendre transformation

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\mathbf{u},\dot{\mathbf{u}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\dot{u}_{i}p_{i}-\mathscr{H}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p}).

(6.49)

Here and below the symbol
\mathscr{L}
should not be confused with the Rogers dilogarithm
L(x)
.
The RHS of (
6.49
) is a function of
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
.
However, by converting the map (
6.48
), we may regard it as a function
of
(\mathbf{u},\dot{\mathbf{u}})
.
The equations of motion for
\mathscr{H}
are equivalent to
the following equations:

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}}{\partial\dot{u}_{i}},

(6.50)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}}{\partial\dot{u}_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}}{\partial u_{i}}\quad\text{(the \emph{Euler-Lagrange equations}{\@index{\@indexphrase{Euler-Lagrange equations}}})}.

(6.51)

Moreover, the Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}
is regular in the following sense

[
AM78
, §3.6]
.

Definition 6.7
.

A Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}
is
regular
(resp. singular) if the map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}F_{\mathscr{L}}\colon&T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}&\rightarrow&T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\\
&(\mathbf{u},\dot{\mathbf{u}})&\mapsto&(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p}),&\quad\displaystyle p_{i}=\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}}{\partial\dot{u}_{i}}(\mathbf{u},\dot{\mathbf{u}}).\end{matrix}

(6.52)

is a diffeomorphism (resp. otherwise).

Conversely, starting from a regular Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}
,
we obtain a regular Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}
by reversing the procedure.
Moreover, the two systems are equivalent again.

Coming back to the current situation,
for the Hamiltonian

\mathscr{H}(s)
in (
6.29
),
the map (
6.48
) is given by

\displaystyle F_{\mathscr{H}(s)}\colon(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))\mapsto(\mathbf{u}(s),\dot{\mathbf{u}}(s)),

(6.53)

\displaystyle\dot{u}_{i}(s)=\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{k_{s}i}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(6.54)

Unfortunately,
this is not surjective at all. Thus, the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)

is singular.
Nevertheless, let us apply the Legendre transformation to
\mathscr{H}(s)

to see what we get. We have

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(s)=\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s)p_{k_{s}}(s)-{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(6.55)

At this moment, this is a function of
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
.
Observe that
\mathscr{L}(s)
depends on
\mathbf{p}(s)
only through
p_{k_{s}}(s)
,
which is written by

\displaystyle p_{k_{s}}(s)={\delta_{k_{s}}}(\sqrt{2}\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)-w_{k_{s}}(s)).

(6.56)

Also, we convert (
6.54
) as

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}=\exp(-\sqrt{2}\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s))-1.

(6.57)

Thus, the Lagrangian (
6.55
) is written in the form

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(s)={\delta_{k_{s}}}\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s)(\sqrt{2}\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)-w_{k_{s}}(s))-{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}),

(6.58)

\displaystyle w_{i}(s)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}(s)u_{j}(s),\quad\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}=\exp(-\sqrt{2}\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s))-1,

(6.59)

which is a function of
(\mathbf{u}(s),\dot{\mathbf{u}}(s))
.
The Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}(s)
is singular, because

\partial\mathscr{L}(s)/\partial\dot{u}_{i}(s)=0
for
i\neq k_{s}
.

Since both
\mathscr{H}(s)
and
\mathscr{L}(s)
are singular,
we expect that the equations
(
6.50
) and (
6.51
)
for
\mathscr{L}(s)
are
not
equivalent to
the equations of motion for
\mathscr{H}(s)
,
which are (
6.30
)
and (
6.31
).
Indeed, we have the following equations.

Lemma 6.8

(
[
GNR17
, Prop. 4.3]
)
.

For the Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}(s)
in (
6.58
),
the equations (
6.50
) and (
6.51
) are explicitly written as
follows:
For
i=k_{s}
,

\displaystyle p_{k_{s}}(s)={\delta_{k_{s}}}(\sqrt{2}\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)-w_{k_{s}}(s)),\quad\dot{p}_{k_{s}}(s)=0,

(6.60)

and, for
i\neq k_{s}
,

\displaystyle p_{i}(s)=0,\quad(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{b_{k_{s}i}(s)}=1.

(6.61)

Proof.

They follow from the following calculations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(s)}{\partial\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s)}&={\delta_{k_{s}}}(\sqrt{2}\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)-w_{k_{s}}(s))\\
&\qquad-2{\varepsilon_{k_{s}}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\frac{\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s)}{\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)}-\sqrt{2}{\varepsilon_{k_{s}}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\frac{\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})}{\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:yu1}}{}&{=}{\delta_{k_{s}}}(\sqrt{2}\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)-w_{k_{s}}(s)),\end{split}

(6.62)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(s)}{\partial u_{k_{s}}}

\displaystyle=0,

(6.63)

and, for
i\neq k_{s}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(s)}{\partial\dot{u}_{i}(s)}

\displaystyle=0,

(6.64)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(s)}{\partial u_{i}(s)}

\displaystyle=-{\delta_{k_{s}}}b_{ik_{s}}(s)\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s)=-\frac{\delta_{i}}{\sqrt{2}}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(6.65)

∎

We observe that the equations (
6.60
) coincide with
(
6.26
) and
(
6.31
) for
i=k_{s}
.
On the other hand, the equations (
6.61
) impose
unwanted constraints.
Therefore,
they are false
for our dynamical system.

Next, let us restrict the system in the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}

and set
p_{k_{s}}(s)=\delta_{k_{s}}w_{k_{s}}(s)
in (
6.60
).
Then, we have

\displaystyle w_{k_{s}}(s)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s).

(6.66)

By (
6.57
) and (
6.66
),
the function (
6.58
) is rewritten
entirely as a function of
\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)
(therefore, as a function of
\dot{u}_{k_{s}}(s)
), as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathscr{L}(s)&=-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{s}\delta_{k_{s}}\log(1+\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})\log\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}-{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})\\
&={\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\tilde{L}(\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).\end{split}

(6.67)

We see that the modified Rogers dilogarithm emerges as
the (false) Lagrangian.

Having obtained the exact function
appearing in the DIs in Theorem
3.5
,
our strategy for the rest of the chapter is as follows: We
continue to rely on the equations of motion for the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}(s)

for our dynamical system.
On the other hand, we also use the function
\mathscr{L}(s)
and its intrinsic property to
prove Theorem
3.5
.

6.5
Periodicity conditions

Let us return to the sequence (
5.8
) for a
Y
-pattern.
Our next task is to clarify the relation between the periodicity of
y
-variables therein
and the one for the dynamical system with canonical coordinates
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
in the previous section.

Before that, we resolve the issue concerning the nonsingularity of
B
.
For the initial matrix
B
of (
5.8
) with a skew-symmetrizer
D
, we introduce an
2n\times 2n
matrix

\displaystyle\overline{B}=\begin{pmatrix}B&-I\\
I&O\\
\end{pmatrix}

(6.68)

called the
principal extension of
B
.
The matrix
\overline{B}
is skew-symmetrizable
with a skew-symmetrizer
D\oplus D
.
Moreover,
\overline{B}
is nonsingular whether
B
is singular or not.
Starting from the initial extended
Y
-seed
\overline{\Upsilon}(0)=\overline{\Upsilon}(\overline{\mathbf{y}},\overline{B})
with
\overline{\mathbf{y}}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{2n})
,
we have a lift of the sequence (
5.8
) in the extended
Y
-pattern of rank
2n
,

\displaystyle\overline{\Upsilon}(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \overline{\Upsilon}(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{1}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \overline{\Upsilon}(P),

(6.69)

where
k_{s}\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n\}
.

Proposition 6.9

(
[
Nak11b
, Thm. 4.3]
)
.

Let
\nu\in S_{n}
be a permutation of the first
n
indices
1
, …,
n
of
extended
Y
-seeds in (
6.69
).
Then, the sequence (
6.69
) is
\nu
-periodic
if and only if the sequence (
5.8
) is
\nu
-periodic.

Proof.

Let
\overline{C}(s)
be the
C
-matrix for the
extended
Y
-seed
\overline{\Upsilon}(s)
.
Then, the following fact is known (
[
FG19
, Thm. 4.2]
):

\displaystyle\overline{C}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}C(s)&O\\
O&I\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(6.70)

(The proof requires the sign-coherence property in Theorem
2.24
.
Thus, it is considered as an advanced result.)
Thus,
\overline{C}(P)=\nu\overline{C}(0)
holds if and only if

C(P)=\nu C(0)
holds.
Therefore, by Theorem
2.31
,

\overline{\Upsilon}(P)=\nu\overline{\Upsilon}(0)
holds
if and only if
\Upsilon(P)=\nu\Upsilon(0)
holds.
∎

Observe that the DI for the sequence (
5.8
)
coincides with the one for the extended sequence (
6.69
)
due to (
6.70
).
Thus,
thanks to Proposition
6.9
,
to prove the DI for
B
in Theorem
3.5
, we may prove the one for
\overline{B}
.
Therefore,
in proving Theorem
3.5
,
we may assume that
B
is nonsingular
without loss of generality.

Now,
let us formulate the periodicity condition for
the sequence (
5.8
) in the current setting.
In a parallel way with (
5.10
),
for any permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
,
we define a semifield automorphism
(denoted by the same symbol)

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\nu\colon&\mathbb{P}(P)&\rightarrow&\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(0)\\
&y_{i}(P)&\mapsto&\displaystyle y_{\nu^{-1}(i)}.\end{matrix}

(6.71)

Then, the
\nu
-periodicity for the sequence
(
5.8
) is rephrased as the equality

\displaystyle\mu(P-1;0)=\nu\quad\text{for}\ \mathbb{P}(P)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}.

(6.72)

The
\nu
-periodicity of (
5.8
)
also
implies the same periodicity for tropical
y
-variables.
Thus, by (
5.18
), we also have the following equality:

\displaystyle\tau(P-1;0)=\nu\quad\text{for}\ \mathbb{P}(P)\rightarrow\mathbb{P}.

(6.73)

Therefore, by the decomposition (
5.31
),
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0)=\mathrm{id}\quad\text{for}\ \mathbb{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}.

(6.74)

Note that
\nu
disappeared
in this equality.
This further induces the equality

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0)=\mathrm{id}\quad\text{for}\ \mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y}).

(6.75)

In particular, any trajectory
\alpha=(\alpha_{t})_{t\in[0,P]}
in the phase space
M
in the full time span
[0,P]

is periodic. (See Figure
6.1
.). Namely, we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{P}.

(6.76)

Let us formulate a parallel result for the canonical coordinates
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
.
For any permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
, define

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\nu\colon&\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{u}(P),\mathbf{p}(P))&\rightarrow&\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{C}(\mathbf{u}(0),\mathbf{p}(0))\\
&u_{i}(P)&\mapsto&\displaystyle u_{\nu^{-1}(i)},\\
&p_{i}(P)&\mapsto&\displaystyle p_{\nu^{-1}(i)}.\end{matrix}

(6.77)

Remarkably, the same periodicity still holds in the phase space
\tilde{M}
,
which is larger than
M
, if
B
is nonsingular.

Proposition 6.10

(
[
GNR17
, Prop. 5.11]
)
.

Suppose that
the sequence
(
5.8
)
is
\nu
-periodic
and
the initial exchange matrix
B
is nonsingular.
Then, the following periodicities hold:

\displaystyle\mu(P-1;0)

\displaystyle=\nu\quad\text{for}\ \mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(P),\mathbf{p}(P))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p}),

(6.78)

\displaystyle\tau(P-1;0)

\displaystyle=\nu\quad\text{for}\ \mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(P),\mathbf{p}(P))\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p}),

(6.79)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}\quad\text{for}\ \mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p}).

(6.80)

In particular, any trajectory
\alpha=(\alpha_{t})_{t\in[0,P]}
in the phase space
\tilde{M}
in the full time span
[0,P]

is periodic; namely,

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{P}.

(6.81)

Proof.

The variables
y_{i}(s)
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y}(s))
and
\tilde{y}_{i}(s)
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))

obey the same transformations under
\rho(s)
and
\tau(s)
.
Thus, they also obey the same transformations under
\mathfrak{q}(s)
and
\mathfrak{q}(s;0)
.
Therefore, the periodicities (
6.72
)–(
6.75
) imply the periodicities
for
\tilde{y}
-variables

\displaystyle\mu(P-1;0):

\displaystyle\ \tilde{y}_{i}(P)\mapsto\tilde{y}_{\nu^{-1}(i)},

(6.82)

\displaystyle\tau(P-1;0):

\displaystyle\ \tilde{y}_{i}(P)\mapsto\tilde{y}_{\nu^{-1}(i)},

(6.83)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0):

\displaystyle\ \tilde{y}_{i}\mapsto\tilde{y}_{i}.

(6.84)

Let us show that they imply the same periodicities for
\mathbf{u}(s)
and
\mathbf{p}(s)
as well.
We set
z_{i}(s)=\log\tilde{y}_{i}(s)
.
Then,
by (
6.35
), (
6.36
), (
6.40
), (
6.41
),
we see that

w_{i}(s)
,

\sqrt{2}\delta_{i}^{-1}p_{i}(s)
, and
z_{i}(s)
obey the same affine transformations under
\rho(s)
and
\tau(s)
.
It follows that
\mathbf{w}(s)
and
\mathbf{p}(s)
obey the same periodicities
in (
6.82
)–(
6.84
)
for
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}(s)
.
Moreover,
since
B
is nonsingular, one can invert
the relation between
\mathbf{u}(s)
and
\mathbf{w}(s)
in (
6.26
).
Thus,
\mathbf{u}(s)
also obeys the same periodicity.
∎

6.6
Classical mechanical proof of DIs (I): In local picture

Let us give an alternative proof of
Theorem
3.5

based on the classical mechanical formulation presented in this chapter.
Actually, we will give two alternative proofs, and this is the first one.

We prove the DI (
3.13
)
with variables
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
.
However, variables
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
are algebraically independent on
\tilde{M}_{0}

only when the matrix
B
is nonsingular.
So, to restore the DI in (
3.13
)
from the DI with variables
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
,
we need to assume that
B
is nonsingular.
On the other hand, we have already argued in the last section
that this assumption does not lose generality.
So, from now on,
we assume that the initial matrix
B
is nonsingular
.
Then, under this assumption,
the map (
6.47
) is injective
and we may identify
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}(s)
and
\mathbf{y}(s)
.

Suppose that
the mutation sequence (
5.8
)
is
\nu
-periodic.
Let us consider a trajectory
\alpha=(\alpha_{t})_{t\in[0,P]}
in the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}
.
By Proposition
6.10
,

\alpha
is periodic. Namely,

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{P}

(6.85)

holds.
The main idea of the proof of Theorem
3.5
is
to consider the following integral of the Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}
given by (
6.67
)
along the trajectory
\alpha
:

\displaystyle S[\alpha]:=\int_{0}^{P}\mathscr{L}(\alpha_{t})\,dt.

(6.86)

We call (
6.86
) the
action integral
along
\alpha
following the convention in classical mechanics.
Recall that
\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}
is constant in the time span
[s,s+1]
.
Thus,
by (
6.67
),
the action integral is reduced to a sum of the modified Rogers dilogarithm

\displaystyle S[\alpha]=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\tilde{L}(\tilde{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(6.87)

Observe that
this is exactly the LHS of the DI (
3.13
).

Once the constancy of (
6.87
) is shown,
the constant term is 0 by considering the tropical limit

\mathbf{y}\rightarrow\mathbf{0}
as we did when proving Theorem
3.5
.
Thus, we are left to prove the constancy of (
6.87
).
We formulate it as the invariance of the action integral as follows.

Theorem 6.11

(
[
GNR17
, Thm. 6.3]
)
.

Under the periodicity (
6.85
),
the action integral
S[\alpha]
in (
6.86
) is independent of
a trajectory
\alpha
in
\tilde{M}_{0}
.

The rest of the section is devoted to proving Theorem
6.11
.
We prove it by the standard
variational calculus
in classical mechanics.
Consider an infinitesimal variation
\alpha+\delta\alpha

of a trajectory
\alpha
,
where we assume that
\alpha+\delta\alpha
is also a trajectory
in the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}
.
Let us concentrate on the time span
[s,s+1]
.
Let
(\overline{\mathbf{u}}(t),\overline{\mathbf{p}}(t))
(
t\in[s,s+1]
)
be the point corresponding to
\alpha_{t}
in the
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
-coordinates.
Due to the constraint (
6.46
),
we have the variations

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\overline{u}_{i}(t)+\delta\overline{u}_{i}(t),

(6.88)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}_{i}\overline{p}_{i}(t)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\delta^{-1}_{i}\overline{p}_{i}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}(s)\delta\overline{u}_{j}(t).

(6.89)

Let

\displaystyle S[\alpha;s]:=\int_{s}^{s+1}\mathscr{L}(s)(\alpha_{t})\,dt.

(6.90)

The variation of
S[\alpha;s]
is evaluated by the “boundary terms” as follows.

Lemma 6.12

(
[
GNR17
, Lemma. 6.4]
)
.

(a).
In the
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
-coordinates, we have

\displaystyle\delta S[\alpha;s]=R[\alpha;s+1]-R[\alpha;s],

(6.91)

where

\displaystyle R[\alpha;t]:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}_{i}(t)\delta\overline{u}_{i}(t).

(6.92)

(b). The above quantity
R[\alpha;t]
is independent of the choice of
coordinates
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
for
\tilde{M}_{0}
.

Proof.

(a).
We regard
\mathscr{L}(\alpha_{t})
as
a function of
(\overline{\mathbf{u}}(t),\dot{\overline{\mathbf{u}}}(t))

as explained in Section
6.4
.
We first recall that, by (
6.30
),
we have

\displaystyle\dot{\overline{u}}_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=0\quad(i\neq k_{s}).

(6.93)

This implies that,

\displaystyle\delta\dot{\overline{u}}_{i}(t)

\displaystyle=0\quad(i\neq k_{s}).

(6.94)

Thus, we decompose the variation
\delta S[\alpha;s]
as

\displaystyle\delta S[\alpha;s]=\delta S_{1}+\delta S_{2},

(6.95)

where

\displaystyle\delta S_{1}:=

\displaystyle\ \int_{s}^{s+1}\left\{\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(\alpha_{t})}{\partial\overline{u}_{k_{s}}(t)}\delta\overline{u}_{k_{s}}(t)+\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(\alpha_{t})}{\partial\dot{\overline{u}}_{k_{s}}(t)}\delta\dot{\overline{u}}_{k_{s}}(t)\right\}dt,

(6.96)

\displaystyle\delta S_{2}:=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k_{s}}^{n}\int_{s}^{s+1}\left\{\frac{\partial\mathscr{L}(\alpha_{t})}{\partial\overline{u}_{i}(t)}\delta\overline{u}_{i}(t)\right\}dt.

(6.97)

As we see in Lemma
6.8
,
the equations (
6.50
) and (
6.51
)
for
i=k_{s}
are reliable.
By using them, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta S_{1}&=\int_{s}^{s+1}\left\{\dot{\overline{p}}_{k_{s}}(t)\delta\overline{u}_{k_{s}}(t)+\overline{p}_{k_{s}}(t)\delta\dot{\overline{u}}_{k_{s}}(t)\right\}dt\\
&=\int_{s}^{s+1}\frac{d}{dt}\left\{{\overline{p}}_{k_{s}}(t)\delta\overline{u}_{k_{s}}(t)\right\}dt\\
&=\overline{p}_{k_{s}}(s+1)\delta\overline{u}_{k_{s}}(s+1)-\overline{p}_{k_{s}}(s)\delta\overline{u}_{k_{s}}(s).\end{split}

(6.98)

To evaluate
\delta S_{2}
,
we have to be careful because
the equations (
6.50
) and (
6.51
)
for
i\neq k_{s}

are false.
By (
6.65
), we have

\displaystyle\delta S_{2}=\sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k_{s}}^{n}\int_{s}^{s+1}\left\{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\overline{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})\delta\overline{u}_{i}(t)\right\}dt,

(6.99)

where

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{i}(t)=\exp\left(\sqrt{2}\delta_{i}^{-1}\overline{p}_{i}(t)\right)=\exp\left(\sqrt{2}\overline{w}_{i}(t)\right),\quad\overline{w}_{i}(t):=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}(s)\overline{u}_{j}(t).

(6.100)

in
\tilde{M}_{0}
.
By (
6.94
), the integrand is constant on
[s,s+1]
. So, we have

\displaystyle\delta S_{2}=\sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k_{s}}^{n}\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\delta_{i}b_{k_{s}i}(s)\log(1+\overline{y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})\delta\overline{u}_{i}(s)\right).

(6.101)

Moreover,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta S_{2}\overset{\eqref{eq:rhop1}}{}&{=}\sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k_{s}}^{n}\left\{\overline{p}_{i}(s+1)-\overline{p}_{i}(s)\right\}\delta\overline{u}_{i}(s)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:du1}}{}&=\sum_{\scriptstyle i=1\atop\scriptstyle i\neq k_{s}}^{n}\left\{\overline{p}_{i}(s+1)\delta\overline{u}_{i}(s+1)-\overline{p}_{i}(s)\delta\overline{u}_{i}(s)\right\}.\end{split}

(6.102)

By summing up (
6.98
) and (
6.102
),
we obtain (
6.91
).

(b).
Since
\tau^{*}(s)
acts on
\overline{u}_{i}(t)
as a linear transformation,
it also acts on
the variation
\delta\overline{u}_{i}(t)

in the same way.
Then,
the claim is proved in the same way as
Proposition
6.4
(b).
∎

Let us finish the proof of
Theorem
6.11
.
By Lemma
6.12
(a) and (b),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta S[\alpha]&=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}(R[\alpha;s+1]-R[\alpha;s])\\
&=R[\alpha;P]-R[\alpha;0].\end{split}

(6.103)

By Lemma
6.12
(b) again,
we may evaluate both
R[\alpha;P]
and
R[\alpha;0]

in the initial coordinates
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{p})
.
Finally and most importantly, by the periodicity (
6.85
) of
\alpha
,
we have

\displaystyle R[\alpha;P]=R[\alpha;0].

(6.104)

Therefore,
\delta S[\alpha]=0
.
This completes the proof of
Theorem
6.11
and also
Theorem
3.5
.

The method of evaluating
\delta S[\alpha;s]
in the proof of Lemma
6.12
is
essentially the same one for celebrated
Noether’s theorem
(e.g.,
[
Tak08
, Thm. 1.3]
) in classical mechanics.
Thus, we may regard
Theorem
6.11

as a variant of Noether’s theorem.
This gives us a conceptual understanding of the constancy of the DI in Theorem
3.5
.

6.7
Classical mechanical proof of DIs (II): In global picture

The proof of Theorem
3.5
in the previous section
uses the canonical coordinates
(\mathbf{u}(s),\mathbf{p}(s))
for each time span
[s,s+1]
.
Here we present the second proof of Theorem
3.5

based on the same classical mechanical method, but
using only the initial canonical coordinates.
The proof is less intrinsic than the first one.
On the other hand, it is applicable in a more general context.
The main reason to present this proof is that
we use it later
in Chapter
9
.

Again, we assume that
B
is nonsingular,
and we identify
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
and
\mathbf{y}

by the map (
6.25
).
First, we consider a little more general Hamiltonian (see (
5.55
))

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a}=a\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-\tilde{y}^{\mathbf{c}})

(6.105)

for the time span
[0,1]

and a trajectory

\alpha=(\alpha_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
in
\tilde{M}_{0}
by
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a}
,
where
\alpha_{0}=(\overline{\mathbf{u}},\overline{\mathbf{p}})
and

\alpha_{1}=(\overline{\mathbf{u}}^{\prime},\overline{\mathbf{p}}^{\prime})
.
Let
