\displaystyle\overline{y}_{i}:=\exp\left({\sqrt{2}}\delta^{-1}_{i}\overline{p}_{i}\right)=\exp\left(\sqrt{2}\overline{w}_{i}\right),\quad\overline{w}_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}\overline{u}_{j}.

(6.106)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\overline{u}_{i}-\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}c_{i}\delta^{-1}_{i}\log(1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}),

(6.107)

\displaystyle\overline{p}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\overline{p}_{i}+\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}b_{ij}\biggr)\log(1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}),

(6.108)

\displaystyle\log\overline{y}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\log\overline{y}_{i}-a\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{j}\omega_{ji}\biggr)\log(1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}).

(6.109)

Consider the corresponding Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{c},a}=a\tilde{L}(\tilde{y}^{\mathbf{c}}),

(6.110)

and the action integral along
the above trajectory

\displaystyle S_{\mathbf{c},a}[\alpha]:=\int_{0}^{1}\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{c},a}(\alpha_{t})\,dt.

(6.111)

The integrand is constant because

\{\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{c},a},\tilde{y}^{\mathbf{c}}\}=0
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle S_{\mathbf{c},a}[\alpha]=\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{c},a}(\alpha_{0}).

(6.112)

Then, we consider an infinitesimal variation

\alpha+\delta\alpha

of a trajectory
\alpha
in
\tilde{M}_{0}
.

The following result formally coincides with Lemma
6.12
.
However, it is more general than
Lemma
6.12
because
it is applicable to any integer vector
\mathbf{c}
other than the
c
-vectors
\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)
.

Proposition 6.13

(
[
Nak24a
, Lemma 3.5]
)
.

The infinitesimal variation of
S_{\mathbf{c},a}[\alpha]
in (
6.112
) is given by

\displaystyle\delta S_{\mathbf{c},a}[\alpha]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}^{\prime}_{i}\delta\overline{u}^{\prime}_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}_{i}\delta\overline{u}_{i}.

(6.113)

Proof.

Here, we compute and compare both sides explicitly.
Thanks to (
6.106
) and (
1.25
),
the LHS of (
6.113
) is given by

\displaystyle\delta\mathscr{L}_{\mathbf{c},a}(\alpha_{0})=\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}\biggl(\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}c_{j}b_{ij}\delta\overline{u}_{i}\biggr)\biggl(\log(1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}})-\frac{\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}\log\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}}{1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}}\biggr).

(6.114)

Let us calculate the RHS of (
6.113
).
By (
6.107
), we have

\displaystyle\delta\overline{u}_{i}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta\overline{u}_{i}-\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}c_{i}\delta^{-1}_{i}\biggl(\,\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}c_{j}b_{kj}\delta\overline{u}_{k}\biggr)\frac{\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}}{1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}}.

(6.115)

Let us write (
6.108
) and (
6.115
) as

\overline{p}_{i}^{\prime}=\overline{p}_{i}+A_{i}

and

\delta\overline{u}_{i}^{\prime}=\delta\overline{u}_{i}-B_{i}
.
Then, the RHS of (
6.113
) is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\delta\overline{u}_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}_{i}B_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}B_{i}.

(6.116)

They are calculated as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\delta\overline{u}_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}\biggl(\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}c_{j}b_{ij}\delta\overline{u}_{i}\biggr)\log(1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}),

(6.117)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}_{i}B_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}\biggl(\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}c_{j}b_{ij}\delta\overline{u}_{i}\biggr)\frac{\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}\log\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}}{1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}},

(6.118)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}B_{i}

\displaystyle=\frac{a^{2}}{{2}}\{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{c}\}_{\Omega}\biggl(\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}c_{j}b_{ij}\delta\overline{u}_{i}\biggr)\frac{\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}\log(1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}})}{1+\overline{y}^{\mathbf{c}}}=0,

(6.119)

where the last equality is due to the skew-symmetry of
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\Omega}

in (
5.21
).
Then, we see that (
6.116
) coincides with (
6.114
).
∎

The rest of the proof of Theorem
6.11
is the same as before.
Assume that that
B
is nonsingular, and
suppose that
\bar{\mathbf{y}}_{t=P}=\bar{\mathbf{y}}_{t=0}
.
Then, by (
6.107
)–(
6.109
),
we have

\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{t=P}=\bar{\mathbf{u}}_{t=0}
and
\bar{\mathbf{p}}_{t=P}=\bar{\mathbf{p}}_{t=0}
.
Therefore, for the action integral

\displaystyle S[a]:=\int_{0}^{P}\mathscr{L}(\alpha_{t})\,dt=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)}),

(6.120)

we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta S[\alpha]&=\sum_{s=0}^{P-1}(R[\alpha;s]_{+}-R[\alpha;s]_{-})=R[\alpha;P]-R[\alpha;0]=0,\end{split}

(6.121)

where we used Proposition
6.13
for the first equality.

Notes

The content of this section is mostly based on
[
GNR17
]

except for Section
6.7
, which appeared in
[
Nak24a
]
.
The idea of representing
y
-variables with canonical coordinates (variables) originates
in the work of
[
FG09c
,
FG09b
,
KN11
]
,
where the quantum version was considered.
The appearance of the modified Rogers dilogarithm as
the Lagrangian was expected by the semi-classical calculation
of quantum dilogarithm identities by
[
KN11
]
.

Part III
Scattering diagram method

Chapter 7
Algebraic formulation of dilogarithm and mutations

Motivated by the cluster scattering diagram formalism in the next chapter,
we define a certain infinite group for each skew-symmetric matrix.
We focus on certain distinguished elements in the group called the
dilogarithm elements
.
They are regarded as an algebraic version of the dilogarithm and mutations,
and
they satisfy the
pentagon relation
corresponding to the pentagon periodicity
of the
Y
-pattern of type
A_{2}
.
We present several interesting relations generated by the pentagon relation, including infinite ones,

7.1
Lie algebra associated with skew-symmetric form

Let
\Omega=(\omega_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{n}

be any skew-symmetric rational matrix.
Let us fix a lattice
N=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
of rank
n
.
Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\Omega}
be the skew-symmetric rational bilinear form on
N
defined by
(
5.21
).
Let

\displaystyle N^{+}=\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}

(7.1)

be the set of the positive vectors of
N
.
The
degree

\deg(\mathbf{n})
of
\mathbf{n}=(n_{i})\in N^{+}
is defined by

\displaystyle\deg(\mathbf{n}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}n_{i}.

(7.2)

For any integer
\ell>0
,
we write

\displaystyle(N^{+})^{\leq\ell}:=

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}\mid\deg(\mathbf{n})\leq\ell\},

(7.3)

\displaystyle(N^{+})^{>\ell}:=

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}\mid\deg(\mathbf{n})>\ell\}.

(7.4)

We say that
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
is
primitive

if there is no pair
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{>1}

and
\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\in N^{+}

such that
\mathbf{n}=j\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
.
The set of all primitive vectors are denoted by

N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
.

Following Gross-Siebert
[
GS11
]
,
Kontsevich-Soibelman
[
KS08
,
KS14
]
,
and
Gross-Hacking-Keel-Kontsevich (GHKK)
[
GHKK18
]
,
we introduce an
N^{+}
-graded Lie algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{g}_{\Omega}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}}\mathbb{Q}X_{\mathbf{n}}

(7.5)

with generators

X_{\mathbf{n}}
(
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
) and relations

\displaystyle[X_{\mathbf{n}},X_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}]=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}X_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}.

(7.6)

Clearly, the above Lie bracket is skew-symmetric.
The Jacobi identity is also clear from the following cyclic expression:

\displaystyle\begin{split}[X_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},[X_{\mathbf{n}_{2}},X_{\mathbf{n}_{3}}]]&=\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega}[X_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},X_{\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3}}]\\
&=(\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega}\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega}-\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega}\{\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega})X_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3}}.\end{split}

(7.7)

We consider the completion of
\mathfrak{g}
by
\deg
.
Namely, for any integer
\ell>0
,
consider a Lie algebra ideal and its quotient

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{>\ell}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{n}\in(N^{+})^{>\ell}}\mathbb{Q}X_{\mathbf{n}},

(7.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}^{>\ell}.

(7.9)

Note that
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
is nilpotent.
Then, we take the inverse limit

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}=\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\Omega}:=\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}.

(7.10)

Thus, an element of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
is given by a formal infinite sum

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}}c_{\mathbf{n}}X_{\mathbf{n}}\quad(c_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{Q}).

(7.11)

For each
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
,
let
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbf{n}}^{\parallel}

denote the commutative Lie subalgebra of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
spanned by
elements

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}c_{j\mathbf{n}}X_{j\mathbf{n}}
.

7.2
Exponential group

For the Lie algebra
\widehat{g}
in (
7.10
),
we define the associated exponential group

\displaystyle G=G_{\Omega}:=\exp(\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}),

(7.12)

where
\exp
is a formal exponential map.
Namely, as a set,
G
is identified with
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}

with
\exp(X)\leftrightarrow X
,
and its product is defined by the
Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula

(e.g.,
[
Bou89
, Ch.2, §6]
,
[
BF12
]
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \exp(X)\exp(Y)\\
=&\ \exp\biggl(X+Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]+\frac{1}{12}[X,[X,Y]]-\frac{1}{12}[Y,[X,Y]]+\cdots\biggr),\end{split}

(7.13)

where we do not need the explicit form of the higher-order
brackets.
The infinite sum in the RHS converges in
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
due to the nilpotency of
\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell}
for any
\ell
.
Originally, the BCH formula is a product formula
for the exponentials of noncommutative variables
X
and
Y
,
where
[X,Y]:=XY-YX
.
So, the associativity of the product is guaranteed. See
[
BF12
, Thm. 5.42]
for a detailed treatment.

Alternatively,
for the Lie algebra
{g}^{\leq\ell}
in (
7.9
),
we define
the associated exponential group

\displaystyle G^{\leq\ell}=\exp(\mathfrak{g}^{\leq\ell})

(7.14)

in the same way.
Then,
G
is identified with the inverse limit

\displaystyle G=\lim_{\longleftarrow}G^{\leq\ell}.

(7.15)

For the canonical projection

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}:G\rightarrow G^{\leq\ell},

(7.16)

we set
G^{>\ell}:=\mathrm{Ker}\,\pi_{\ell}
.

For each
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
,
let
G_{\mathbf{n}}^{\parallel}
be the exponential group of

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbf{n}}^{\parallel}
.
It is an abelian subgroup of
G
.
We call it the
parallel subgroup
of
\mathbf{n}
.

An
infinite
product in
G

makes sense if it is reduced to a finite product by applying
\pi_{\ell}
for any
\ell
.
For any element
g=\exp(X)
, its rational power
g^{c}
(
c\in\mathbb{Q}
) is ambiguously defined
by
g^{c}:=\exp(cX)
.

7.3
y
-representation

We introduce an important representation of the group
G=G_{\Omega}
in the previous section.
Let
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
be an
n
-tuple of variables
and let
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
be the formal power series ring of
\mathbf{y}

over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Namely, any element of
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]

is written as a formal infinite sum

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{\geq 0}}c_{\mathbf{n}}y^{\mathbf{n}}\quad(c_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathbb{Q}),\quad y^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}=y_{i}.

(7.17)

The algebra
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
is naturally equipped with the Poisson bracket
defined by

\displaystyle\{y^{\mathbf{n}},y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\}=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}y^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}},

(7.18)

where
\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}
is the same skew-symmetric rational bilinear form in (
7.6
).
The Leibniz rule is confirmed by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\{y^{\mathbf{n}},y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\}y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\{y^{\mathbf{n}},y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\}&=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}y^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}_{1}}y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega}y^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}\\
&=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega}y^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}},\end{split}

(7.19)

while the Jacobi identity is verified by the following cyclic expression:

\displaystyle\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}},y^{\mathbf{n}_{3}}\}\}

\displaystyle=(\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega}\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega}-\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega}\{\mathbf{n}_{3},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega})y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3}}.

(7.20)

Compare (
7.20
) with (
7.7
).
As a special case of (
7.18
), we have

\displaystyle\{y_{i},y_{j}\}=\omega_{ij}y_{i}y_{j}.

(7.21)

Thus, the Poisson bracket on
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
here is
essentially the same as the quadratic Poisson bracket (
5.37
) on
\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{y})

under the identification of the matrix
\Omega

in both situations.

We define an endomorphism
\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}
(
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
) of
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}):=\{y^{\mathbf{n}},y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\}=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}y^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}.

(7.22)

This is a
derivation
; namely, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})=\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})

(7.23)

due to (
7.19
).
Moreover, it preserves the Poisson bracket; namely, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})\})=\{\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}),y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\}+\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})\}

(7.24)

due to the Jacobi identity.

Let
\mathrm{Der}(\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]])
be
the
derivation Lie algebra
of
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
,
i.e.,
the Lie algebra consisting of all derivations of
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]

[
Jac79
, §I.2]
.

Proposition 7.1

(Cf.
[
GHKK18
, §1.1]
)
.

The linear map

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}:&\widehat{g}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Der}(\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]])\\
&\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in N_{+}}c_{\mathbf{n}}X_{\mathbf{n}}&\mapsto&\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in N_{+}}c_{\mathbf{n}}\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}\end{matrix}

(7.25)

yields a Lie algebra homomorphism.
Moreover, if
\Omega
is nonsingular, it is injective.

Proof.

Thanks to the Jacobi identity of the Poisson bracket, we have

\displaystyle[\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}_{2}}](y^{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}},y^{\mathbf{n}}\}\}-\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}},\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},y^{\mathbf{n}}\}\}

\displaystyle=\{\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}},y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}\},y^{\mathbf{n}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega}\{y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}},y^{\mathbf{n}}\}

\displaystyle=\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega}\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}(y^{\mathbf{n}}).

Thus, it is a Lie algebra homomorphism.
If
\Omega
is nonsingular,

\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}
(
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
) are linearly independent by (
7.22
).
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}
is injective.
∎

For any
X\in\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
,
let
\tilde{X}:={\rho}_{y}(X)
.
We define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\tilde{X}^{k}\in\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]).

(7.26)

Since
\tilde{X}
is a derivation,
\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X})

is an algebra automorphism of
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]

[
Jac79
, §I.2]
.
Then, we have the following group homomorphism,
where we abuse the symbol
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}
, for simplicity.

Proposition 7.2

(Cf.
[
GHKK18
, §1.1]
)
.

We have a group homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}:&G&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]])\\
&\exp(X)&\mapsto&\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}).\end{matrix}

(7.27)

Moreover, if
\Omega
is nonsingular,
it is injective.

Proof.

The map
\rho_{y}
is a group homomorphism,
because the exponential (
7.26
) also satisfies the
BCH formula.
The injectivity of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}
follows from the one in
Proposition
7.1
.
∎

As we see in the next section,
the representation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}
is
closely related to the mutations of
y
-variables.
So, we call it the
y
-representation
of
G
.

7.4
Dilogarithm elements and mutations

Now we introduce the main player of Part III.

Definition 7.3

(Dilogarithm element)
.

For each
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
,
we define

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}]:=\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}X_{j\mathbf{n}}\Biggr)\in G_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}^{\parallel},

(7.28)

where
\mathbf{n}_{0}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
is the one such that
\mathbf{n}=h\mathbf{n}_{0}
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
.
We call
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]
the
dilogarithm element

for
\mathbf{n}
.

The above definition is closely related to the following formula.

Proposition 7.4

(Cf.
[
GHKK18
, Lemma 1.3]
)
.

Under the
y
-representation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}
,
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]
acts on
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
as

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}](y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}})=y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}})^{\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}\quad(\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}).

(7.29)

Proof.

By (
7.22
), we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}](y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\exp\biggl(\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}y^{j\mathbf{n}}\{j\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}\biggr)

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\exp\biggl(\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}y^{j\mathbf{n}}\biggr)^{\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}})^{\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}.

∎

We may compare the above calculation with the following one (see also (
1.3
)):

\displaystyle\begin{split}x\frac{d}{dx}\biggl(-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-x)\biggr)&=x\frac{d}{dx}\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}}x^{j}\biggr)\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}{\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}}x^{j}=\log(1+x).\end{split}

(7.30)

Thus, the dilogarithm elements are regarded as a certain algebraic formulation
of (the exponential of) the Euler dilogarithm.

Next, let us compare the formula (
7.29
) with
the automorphism

\mathfrak{q}(s)
in (
5.23
).
Under the identification of the matrix
\Omega
in both situations,
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}{(s)}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}(\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)]^{-\varepsilon_{s}\delta_{k_{s}}}),

(7.31)

where we regard
\mathfrak{q}(s)
as acting on

\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
.
More generally,
the time-one flow (
5.56
)
of the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{n},a}=a\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y^{\mathbf{n}})

is identified with the action of
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{-a}
.

In summary,
mutations are described by
the action of dilogarithm elements.
This is also regarded as an algebraic reformulation
of the Hamiltonian picture of mutations,
where the time-one flow of the Hamiltonian is replaced with the
action of dilogarithm elements.
The identification (
7.31
) serves as a key to relate a cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern and a forthcoming
cluster scattering diagram
.

There are several advantages of
this algebraic formulation as follows:

1.

A composition of mutations is expressed
as a product of dilogarithm elements in
G
.

2.

In particular, a period of mutations is expressed as a
relation among dilogarithm elements in the group
G
.

3.

The infinite product of dilogarithm elements makes sense in
G
.
This enables us to go beyond mutations in a cluster pattern,
where only finite compositions of mutations are allowed.

4.

The group
G
controls mutations locally and globally.

5.

The quantum case will be treated in a parallel way.

7.5
DI for dilogarithm elements

The periodicity condition (
3.4
) implies a relation of dilogarithm elements in the group
G
.

Theorem 7.5

(DI for dilogarithm elements)
.

Suppose that
the sequence of mutations
(
3.2
) is
\nu
-periodic
.
Then, the following relation holds in
G
.

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})]^{\varepsilon_{P-1}\delta_{k_{P-1}}}\cdots\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)]^{\varepsilon_{0}\delta_{k_{0}}}=\mathrm{id}.

(7.32)

Proof.

Case 1.
Assume
\Upsilon(0)=\Upsilon
.
Then, we are in the situation of (
5.8
).
By (
6.75
), we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0)=\mathrm{id}.

(7.33)

Thus,
thanks to the identification (
7.31
),

\displaystyle g=\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)]^{-\varepsilon_{0}\delta_{k_{0}}}\cdots\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})]^{-\varepsilon_{P-1}\delta_{k_{P-1}}}

(7.34)

acts trivially on
\mathbb{Z}[[\mathbf{y}]]
.
Assume that
B
is nonsingular.
Then, by Proposition
7.2
,

g
is the identity element in
G
.
Taking its inverse, we obtain (
7.32
).
If
B
is singular,
let
\overline{B}
be the principal extension in (
6.68
).
Accordingly, the matrix
\Omega
is extended as

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}=\begin{pmatrix}\Omega&-\Delta^{-1}\\
\Delta^{-1}&O\\
\end{pmatrix}

(7.35)

By Proposition
6.9
and (
6.70
),
we obtain the relation (
7.32
) in the group
G_{\overline{\Omega}}
.
Since
G=G_{\Omega}
is a subgroup of
G_{\overline{\Omega}}
, we obtain the relation
(
7.32
) in
G
.

Case 2. Assume
\Upsilon(0)\neq\Upsilon
.
By (
2.15
), we extend the sequence (
3.2
)
as

\displaystyle\Upsilon\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k^{\prime}_{0}}}}}\ \Upsilon^{\prime}(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k^{\prime}_{1}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k^{\prime}_{r}}}}}\ \Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \Upsilon(P)=\nu\Upsilon(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{\nu(k^{\prime}_{r})}}}}\cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{\nu(k^{\prime}_{0})}}}}\ \nu\Upsilon.

(7.36)

Then, the extended sequence belongs to Case 1.
Note that the
k_{t}
th
c
-vector of
\Upsilon^{\prime}(t)
coincides with
the
\nu(k_{t})
th
c
-vector of
\nu\Upsilon^{\prime}(t)
.
Moreover, by (
2.51
),
it is the inverse of the
\nu(k_{t})
th
c
-vector of
\nu\Upsilon^{\prime}(t+1)
.
Thus,
the corresponding relation (
7.32
) has the form

\displaystyle h^{-1}(\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})]^{\varepsilon_{P-1}\delta_{k_{P-1}}}\cdots\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)]^{\varepsilon_{0}\delta_{k_{0}}})h=\mathrm{id}

(7.37)

where
h\in G
is the contribution of the mutations from
\Upsilon
to
\Upsilon(0)
.
Therefore, we have (
7.32
).
∎

We regard the relation (
7.32
) as an algebraic version of the dilogarithm identity
(
3.13
).

Example 7.6
.

Let us consider the pentagon periodicity of type
A_{2}
.
We choose a skew-symmetric decomposition of the initial exchange matrix
B
in (
2.38
)
as

\displaystyle\Delta=I,\quad\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(7.38)

The
c
-vectors are found in (
3.18
).
Then, the relation (
7.32
) is written as

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]=\mathrm{id}.

(7.39)

Equivalently, we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}].

(7.40)

We call it the
pentagon relation
.

7.6
Pentagon relation

The pentagon relation in Example
7.6
holds in a more general situation.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\in N^{+}
.
The following relations hold in
G
.

(a) (Commutative relation). If
\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}=0
,
then, for any
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{c_{2}}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{c_{1}}=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{c_{1}}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{c_{2}}.

(7.41)

(b) (Pentagon relation
[
GHKK18
, Ex. 1.14]
,
[
Nak23
, Prop. III.1.14]
).
If
\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}=c\neq 0
,
we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}.

(7.42)

Proof.

(a).
This is clear from (
7.6
).
(b).
Let us consider the rank 2 sublattice
N^{\prime}
of
N
generated by
\mathbf{n}_{1}
and
\mathbf{n}_{2}
.
By the assumption
\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}\neq 0
, the form
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\Omega}
restricted on
N^{\prime}
is nondegenerate.
Let
G^{\prime}
be the subgroup of
G
corresponding to
N^{\prime}
.
Since the relation (
7.42
) involves only elements in
G^{\prime}
,
one can prove it
by the
y
-representation of
G^{\prime}
, which is faithful.
The LHS is given by

\displaystyle\quad\ \Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}))^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}.

The RHS is given by

\displaystyle\quad\ \Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}})

\displaystyle=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c})

\displaystyle=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}

\displaystyle\qquad\times(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c})

\displaystyle=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{-1})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}

\displaystyle\qquad\times(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{-1}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{-1})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}/c}.

Thus, two expressions agree.
∎

Example 7.8
.

For the matrix
\Omega
in (
7.38
),
we have
\{\mathbf{e}_{2},\mathbf{e}_{1}\}_{\Omega}=\omega_{21}=1
.
Then, we recover (
7.40
) by the specialization of the relation (
7.42
).

7.7
Ordering problem

Let us demonstrate that the pentagon relation
(
7.42
) generates
several interesting relations in
G
, including infinite ones.

Concentrate on the rank 2 case,
and let
\Omega
be the one in (
7.38
).
We consider a possibly infinite product of powers of dilogarithm elements
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{c}

(
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
,
c\in\mathbb{Q}
). Such a product is well-defined in
G
if, for each degree
\ell
,
there are only finitely many terms in the product such that
\deg\mathbf{n}\leq\ell
.
We say that
such
a (possibly infinite) product is
ordered
(resp.
anti-ordered
)
if, for any pair
\Psi[\mathbf{n}^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}
and
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{c}
in the product, where the former appears left to the latter,

\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}\leq 0

(resp.
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}\geq 0
)
holds.
For example, in the relation (
7.40
),
the LHS is anti-ordered, while the RHS is ordered.

Then, the following problem arises.

Problem 7.9

(Ordering Problem)
.

Is it possible to order a given anti-ordered product
to an ordered one only by applying the commutative relation (
7.41
) and the
pentagon relation (
7.42
) repeatedly (possibly infinitely many times)?

The answer crucially depends on the exponents in the product.
For example, consider the anti-ordered product
\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{1/2}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]
.
Then, it is easy to see that it cannot be ordered
by the pentagon relation (
7.40
)
due to the fractional power of
\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]
.

The following fact holds.

Proposition 7.10

(
[
Nak23
, Prop. III.5.4]
)
.

For any positive integers
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
,
the anti-ordered product
\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}

can be transformed into a possibly infinite ordered product
only by applying the commutative relation (
7.41
) and the
pentagon relation (
7.42
) possibly infinitely many times.

This is a special case of a more general result in the forthcoming
Proposition
8.24
(Ordering Lemma).
Later,
the integers
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{1}
will be identified with the diagonal elements
of the matrix
\Delta=\mathrm{diag}(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
in the decomposition (
5.1
) of
B
.
Here we present some examples, which reveal a surprisingly rich structure of the ordered product obtained
in Proposition
7.10
.
Below,
for
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})
,
we write
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{c}
as
[n_{1},n_{2}]^{c}
(or column-wise).
By (
7.38
),
we have
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}=n_{1}n^{\prime}_{2}-n_{2}n^{\prime}_{1}
.
Thus,
the condition
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}\leq 0
is equivalent to

n^{\prime}_{1}/n^{\prime}_{2}\geq n_{1}/n_{2}
,
where
n_{1}/n_{2}=\infty
if
n_{2}=0
.

Example 7.11

(Finite type:
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\leq 3
)
.

(a) Type
A_{2}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,1)
.
This is already covered in (
7.40
).
In the current notation, it is written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

(7.43)

Observe that the vectors appearing in the RHS are
identified with the positive roots
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{2}

of the root system of type
A_{2}
.
See Figure
7.1
.

(b) Type
B_{2}
.
Here we consider the case

(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,2)
.
By applying
the pentagon relation (
7.42
) with
c=1
repeatedly for adjacent pairs

\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{n}

with
\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}=1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.\end{split}

(7.44)

This relation coincides with the equality (
7.32
)
for the periodicity of the free
Y
-pattern of type
B_{2}
in (
3.23
).
The vectors appearing in the RHS are
identified with the positive roots
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
,

\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{2}

of the root system of type
B_{2}
.
Moreover, the exponent is 2 if and only if the corresponding root is a short one.

(c) Type
G_{2}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,3)
.
In the same way as above,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\biggl(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\biggr)\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{3}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}.\end{split}

(7.45)

This relation coincides with the equality (
7.32
)
for the periodicity of the free
Y
-pattern of type
G_{2}
in (
3.28
).
The vectors appearing in the RHS are
identified with the positive roots
\alpha_{1}
,
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
,

2\alpha_{1}+3\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}
,
\alpha_{1}+3\alpha_{2}
,

\alpha_{2}

of the root system of type
G_{2}
.
Moreover, the exponent is 3 if and only if the corresponding root is a short one.

\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{2}
\textstyle{A_{2}}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{2}
\textstyle{B_{2}}
\alpha_{1}
\alpha_{1}+3\alpha_{2}
2\alpha_{1}+3\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{1}+2\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}
\alpha_{2}
\textstyle{G_{2}}

Figure 7.1:
Rank 2 root systems of finite type.

Example 7.12

(Affine type:
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}=4
)
.

(a) Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(2,2)
.
By using the pentagon relation (
7.42
)
with
c=1
,
we obtain the equality

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.

(7.46)

The expression in the RHS
is not yet ordered.
To make it ordered, we need to interchange

[1,2]
and
[1,0]
in the middle,
where

\{\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}=2
.
As the lowest approximation, we consider modulo

G^{>3}
.
Then,
[1,2]
and
[1,0]
commute, and we have,
modulo
G^{>3}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.

(7.47)

To proceed to a higher degree, we
now apply the pentagon relation (
7.42
)
with
c=2
to the pair
[1,2]^{1/2}
and
[1,0]^{1/2}

in (
7.46
),
which are obtained by factorizing the elements
[1,2]
and
[1,0]
.
To clarify the structure among fractional powers,
we use the temporal notation
[n_{1},n_{2}]_{2}:=[n_{1},n_{2}]^{1/2}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}.

(7.48)

This is parallel with (
7.46
).
As the next approximation, we consider modulo

G^{>7}
.
Then,
[3,4]
and
[1,0]
commute, and we have,
modulo
G^{>7}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{2}^{2}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}.

(7.49)

Then, we plug it into
(
7.46
)
and apply the pentagon relation with
c=1
again,
and
we have,
modulo
G^{>7}
,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}.

(7.50)

By repeating this procedure modulo
G^{>2^{\ell}-1}
,
the relations converge to

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\cdots\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j}\end{bmatrix}^{2^{2-j}}\cdots\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2},

(7.51)

where an infinite product emerges in the RHS.
Also, fractional powers appear due to the factorization in the procedure.
A complete proof of the relation (
7.51
) by the pentagon relation was given by Matsushita

[
Mat21
]
.

(b) Type
A_{2}^{(2)}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,4)
.
The situation is in parallel with
A_{1}^{(1)}
though a little more complicated.
The resulting relation is as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}5\\
8\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{4}\cdots\\
&\hskip 60.0pt\times\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{6}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j+1}\end{bmatrix}^{2^{2-j}}\cdots\begin{bmatrix}5\\
12\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
8\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{4}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{4}.\end{split}

(7.52)

Again,
a complete proof of the equality (
7.52
) by the pentagon relation was given by
[
Mat21
]
.

Example 7.13

(Non-affine infinite type:
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\geq 5
)
.

The explicit expression of the ordered product is not yet known,
but it is quite intriguing.
There is a program to compute the ordered product
written for SageMath

[
Sag
]

whose source code is available
in
[
Nak23
, III. Appendix A]
.
The program can calculate the ordered product
up to a given degree; however,
in practice, once some exponent exceeds 1,000,
it is difficult to continue calculation due to the limitation of time.
As an example, for
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,5)
,
the product has the following form modulo
G^{>16}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{5}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}&\equiv\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{5}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
5\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{5}\biggl\{\begin{bmatrix}5\\
7\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
6\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}6\\
9\end{bmatrix}^{15}\begin{bmatrix}5\\
8\end{bmatrix}^{85}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{27}\\
&\quad\times\begin{bmatrix}6\\
10\end{bmatrix}^{302}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
7\end{bmatrix}^{85}\begin{bmatrix}5\\
9\end{bmatrix}^{295}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
6\end{bmatrix}^{15}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
8\end{bmatrix}^{45}\begin{bmatrix}5\\
10\end{bmatrix}^{130}\\
&\quad\times\begin{bmatrix}5\\
11\end{bmatrix}^{1095}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
9\end{bmatrix}^{295}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
7\end{bmatrix}^{85}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
5\end{bmatrix}^{27}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
10\end{bmatrix}^{302}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
8\end{bmatrix}^{85}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
11\end{bmatrix}^{295}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}^{10}\\
&\quad\times\begin{bmatrix}2\\
6\end{bmatrix}^{10}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
9\end{bmatrix}^{15}\begin{bmatrix}4\\
12\end{bmatrix}^{45}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
10\end{bmatrix}^{27}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
7\end{bmatrix}^{10}\biggr\}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
11\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}^{5}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
5\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{5}.\end{split}

(7.53)

The behavior of
the terms outside the bracket are completely determined by combining the results by
GHKK
[
GHKK18
]
and Reading

[
Rea14
]
.
Namely,
they are

\displaystyle[\mathbf{c}_{0}][\mathbf{c}_{1}]^{5}[\mathbf{c}_{2}][\mathbf{c}_{3}]^{5}\cdots\{\cdots\}\cdots[\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{3}][\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{2}]^{5}[\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{1}][\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{0}]^{5},

(7.54)

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
1\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}4\\
5\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}3\\
4\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}_{4}=\begin{pmatrix}11\\
15\end{pmatrix},\,\cdots,

(7.55)

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
5\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
4\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}4\\
15\end{pmatrix},\ \mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{4}=\begin{pmatrix}3\\
11\end{pmatrix},\,\cdots.

(7.56)

These vectors are uniquely determined from the recurrence relations

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{i}=\begin{cases}5\mathbf{c}_{i-1}-\mathbf{c}_{i-2}&\text{$i$: even},\\
\mathbf{c}_{i-1}-\mathbf{c}_{i-2}&\text{$i$: odd},\end{cases}\quad\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i-1}-\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i-2}&\text{$i$: even},\\
5\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i-1}-\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i-2}&\text{$i$: odd}.\end{cases}

(7.57)

The ratios of the components of these vectors have the limits

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}n_{1;i}\\
n_{2;i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n_{1;i}}{n_{2;i}}=\frac{2}{5-\sqrt{5}}=0.723\dots,

(7.58)

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}n^{\prime}_{1;i}\\
n^{\prime}_{2;i}\end{pmatrix},\quad\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n^{\prime}_{1;i}}{n^{\prime}_{2;i}}=\frac{2}{5+\sqrt{5}}=0.276\dots.

(7.59)

In contrast, inside the bracket
in (
7.53
),
every
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
with
\deg(\mathbf{n})\leq 16

such that

\displaystyle\frac{2}{5+\sqrt{5}}<\frac{n_{1}}{n_{2}}<\frac{2}{5-\sqrt{5}}

(7.60)

appears.
It is recently shown by Gräfnitz and Luo
[
GL23
]
that this is true for any
\mathbf{n}

satisfying (
7.60
).
However, the explicit description of the exponents is known only partially (e.g.,
[
Aka23
]
).

The meaning of the relations in the above examples will be clarified in the next chapter
in view of cluster scattering diagrams.

Notes

The class of Lie algebras and their exponential groups
in this chapter
were introduced
in
[
GS11
,
KS08
,
KS14
]

in more generality in the study of mirror symmetry and wall-crossing structures
for the Donaldson-Thomas invariants.
The group
G
here appeared in
[
GHKK18
]

in the disguised form of the
x
-representation.
The dilogarithm elements and the pentagon relation
appeared in
[
KS08
,
KS14
]
in a slightly different
setup, and also
in
[
GHKK18
]

in the disguised form of the
x
-representation.
Here we follow
the presentation in the companion monograph
[
Nak23
]
.
Theorem
7.5
is new here, but
the rank 2 examples of DIs appeared in

[
Nak23
]
.
The ordering problem is closely related to the wall-crossing formula in
[
KS08
]
.

Chapter 8
Quick course on cluster scattering diagrams

Following GHKK
[
GHKK18
]
,
we introduce an algebraic and geometrical object called a
cluster scattering diagram
(CSD)
.
For any cluster pattern, the fan constructed from its
G
-matrices are embedded in the corresponding CSD.
Therefore, it is regarded as an extension of
a cluster pattern.
It turns out that the dilogarithm elements and the pentagon relation play fundamental roles in CSDs.
Our presentation is minimal without proofs.
The proofs and details are found in
the companion monograph

[
Nak23
]
.

8.1
Scattering diagrams

Continuing from the previous chapter,
we fix a given skew-symmetric rational matrix
\Omega
.
Let
G=G_{\Omega}
be the group
constructed in Section
7.2
.

Following
[
GHKK18
]
, we introduce several notions related to scattering diagrams.
For the lattice
N=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
,
let

\displaystyle M:=\mathrm{Hom}(N,\mathbb{Z}),\quad M_{\mathbb{R}}:=M\otimes\mathbb{R}.

(8.1)

Let
\langle\mathbf{n},m\rangle
be the canonical paring
N\times M\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

or its extension
N\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, depending on the context.
For each
\mathbf{n}\in N
,
\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
,
let

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}^{\perp}:=\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle\mathbf{n},z\rangle=0\}

(8.2)

be the hyperplane in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
which is orthogonal to
\mathbf{n}
.
For any
m_{1},\,\dots,\,m_{k}\in M
, the positive span

\displaystyle\sigma(m_{1},\dots,m_{k}):=\biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}m_{i}\,\bigg|\,a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\biggr\}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}

(8.3)

is called a
convex rational cone
. We simple call it a
cone
.
We also set
\sigma(\emptyset):=\{0\}
, which is also a cone.
A cone
\sigma
is
strongly convex
if

\sigma\cap(-\sigma)=\{0\}
.

Definition 8.1

(Wall/Scattering diagram)
.

Let
G=G_{\Omega}
be the group as above.

(a). A
wall

\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
is a triplet such that

\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
, a cone
\mathfrak{d}\subset\mathbf{n}^{\perp}
of dimension
n-1
,
and
g\in G_{\mathbf{n}}^{\parallel}
.
We call
\mathbf{n}
,
\mathfrak{d}
,
g
the
normal vector
, the
support
, and the
wall element

of
\mathbf{w}
, respectively.

(b). A
scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}
is a collection of walls

\{\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},g_{\lambda})_{\mathbf{n}_{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}

with a (possibly infinite) index set
\Lambda

satisfying the following
finiteness condition
:

•

For any integer
\ell\geq 1
, there are only finitely many walls such that

\pi_{\ell}(g_{\lambda})\neq\mathrm{id}
, where
\pi_{\ell}:G\rightarrow G^{\leq\ell}
is
the canonical projection (
7.16
).

The group
G
is called the
structure group
of
\mathfrak{D}
.

(c). For each integer
\ell\geq 1
, the subcollection

\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
consisting of the walls with
\pi_{\ell}(g_{\lambda})\neq\mathrm{id}

is called the
reduction
of
\mathfrak{D}
at degree
\ell
.
By the finiteness condition, there are only finitely may walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
.

We give more related notions.

Definition 8.2

(Support/Singular locus)
.

For a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}=\{\mathbf{w}_{\lambda}=(\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},g_{{\lambda}})_{\mathbf{n}_{\lambda}}\}_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
,
the
support
and the
singular locus
of
\mathfrak{D}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda},

(8.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})

\displaystyle:=\bigcup_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\partial\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cup\bigcup_{\scriptstyle\lambda,\,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda\atop\scriptstyle\dim\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\,=\,n-2}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda^{\prime}}.

(8.5)

Definition 8.3

(Admissible curve)
.

A curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
is

admissible
for a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1).

The endpoints of
\gamma
are in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.

(2).

It is a smooth curve and intersects
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
transversally.

(3).

\gamma
does not intersect
\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Definition 8.4

(Path-ordered product)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be any scattering diagram.
For any admissible curve
\gamma
for
\mathfrak{D}
,
we define an element
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\in G
as follows:
For each integer
\ell>0
, let
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
be the reduction of
\mathfrak{D}
at
degree
\ell
.
Suppose that

\gamma=\gamma(t)
crosses walls
\mathbf{w}_{i}=(\mathfrak{d}_{i},g_{i})_{\mathbf{n}_{i}}

(
i=1
, …,
s
)
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}

in this order at
t=t_{i}
with

\displaystyle 0<t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq\cdots\leq t_{s}<1.

(8.6)

Since
\gamma(t_{i})\notin\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
,
when
\gamma
crosses multiple walls at a time,
these walls have a common normal vector.
We define the
intersection sign

\epsilon_{i}
(
i=1
, …,
s
) by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}=\begin{cases}1&\langle n_{i},\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})\rangle<0,\\
-1&\langle n_{i},\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})\rangle>0,\\
\end{cases}

(8.7)

where
\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})
is the velocity vector of
\gamma(t)
at
t_{i}
.
Then, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}:=

\displaystyle\ g_{s}^{\epsilon_{s}}\cdots g_{1}^{\epsilon_{1}},\quad\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}:=\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}}\in G.

(8.8)

We call
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}

the
path-ordered product
(of wall elements in
\mathfrak{D}
)
along
\gamma
.

Note that
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
only depends on the homotopy class of
\gamma

in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Definition 8.5

(Equivalence)
.

Two scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
with a common structure group
G
are

equivalent
if, for any curve
\gamma
that is admissible for both
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
, the equality

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}}
holds.

For a given scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, one can obtain infinitely many scattering diagrams
that are equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}
by joining and splitting the supports
and wall elements and also by adding and removing the walls with the trivial wall element

g=\mathrm{id}
(trivial walls) possibly infinitely many times.

Finally, we introduce
a crucial notion for scattering diagrams.

Definition 8.6

(Consistency)
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is
consistent
if for any admissible curve
\gamma

for
\mathfrak{D}
,
the associated path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
depends
only on
the endpoints of
\gamma
.
In other words,
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent if

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\mathrm{id}

(8.9)

for any admissible loop (i.e., closed admissible curve)
\gamma
for
\mathfrak{D}
.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{C}^{+}&:=\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle\mathbf{e}_{i},z\rangle\geq 0\ \text{for any $i$}\},\\
\mathcal{C}^{-}&:=\{z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle\mathbf{e}_{i},z\rangle\leq 0\ \text{for any $i$}\}.\end{split}

(8.10)

It is easy to see that
the support of a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
intersects
\mathcal{C}^{\pm}

only in the boundary of
\mathcal{C}^{\pm}
.
For a given consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
,
let
\gamma_{+-}
be any admissible path with the initial point
in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
and the final point
in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{-})
.
Then,
thanks to the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}
,
the unique element
g(\mathfrak{D}):=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{+-},\mathfrak{D}}\in G
is assigned
to
\mathfrak{D}
.

The following fact reveals the intrinsic nature of consistent scattering diagrams.

Theorem 8.7

(
[
KS14
, Thm. 2.1.6]
,
[
GHKK18
, Thm. 1.17]
)
.

The assignment
\mathfrak{D}\mapsto g(\mathfrak{D})
gives a one-to-one correspondence between
the consistent scattering diagrams up to equivalence and
the elements in
G
.

Definition 8.8
.

We say that a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is with
minimal support

if its support
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
is the minimal set
among the supports of all equivalent scattering diagrams to
\mathfrak{D}
.

The proof of Theorem
8.7
also ensures the following fact.

Proposition 8.9

(e.g.,
[
Nak23
, Prop. III.2.25]
)
.

For any consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
,
there exists a (not unique) scattering diagram with minimal support
that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}
.

8.2
Cluster scattering diagrams

Next, we will define a unique consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}(B)
(up to equivalence and isomorphism) associated with
a skew-symmetrizable integer matrix
B
called a
cluster scattering diagram
.

First, we choose an arbitrary skew-symmetric decomposition (
5.1
) of
B
.
We then construct the group
G=G_{\Omega}
for the matrix
\Omega
as in Section
7.1
.

Let us introduce an abelian group homomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}p^{*}\colon&N&\rightarrow&M_{\mathbb{R}}\\
&\mathbf{n}&\mapsto&\{\cdot,\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}.\end{matrix}

(8.11)

In particular,

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{n},p^{*}(\mathbf{n})\rangle=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}=0.

(8.12)

Thus,

\displaystyle p^{*}(\mathbf{n})\in\mathbf{n}^{\perp}

(8.13)

holds for any
\mathbf{n}\neq\mathbf{0}
.

Definition 8.10
.

A wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
is said to be
incoming
(resp.
outgoing
)
if
p^{*}(\mathbf{n})\in\mathfrak{d}
(resp. otherwise).
See figure
8.1
.

\bullet
0
n^{\perp}
\bullet
p^{*}(n)
incoming wall
\small\bullet
\bullet
outgoing wall

Figure 8.1:
Incoming and outgoing walls.

For example, a wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{n}^{\perp},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
is incoming for any
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}

by (
8.13
).

So far, we only use the data
\Omega
in the decomposition
(
5.1
).
The following definition involves
\Delta
and
\Omega
together.

Definition 8.11
.

For a skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
with a skew-symmetric decomposition
(
5.1
),
a
cluster scattering diagram
(CSD) for
B

is a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

satisfying the following properties:

•

The structure group of

\mathfrak{D}
is
G_{\Omega}
.

•

The incoming walls
\mathbf{w}_{i}:=(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
(
i=1,\dots,n
) belong to
\mathfrak{D}
,
where
\delta_{i}
is the
i
th diagonal entry of
\Delta
.

•

All other walls of
\mathfrak{D}
are outgoing.

Now we present the first fundamental result on CSDs.

Theorem 8.12

(
[
GHKK18
, Thm. 1.21]
)
.

For any skew-symmetrizable matrix
B
with a skew-symmetric decomposition
(
5.1
),
there exists a unique CSD
\mathfrak{D}(B)
up to equivalence.

The decomposition (
5.1
) is not unique at all.
For example, one may rescale
\Delta^{\prime}=\lambda^{-1}\Delta
and
\Omega^{\prime}=\lambda\Omega

such that the entries of
\Delta^{\prime}
are integers.
However,
G_{\Omega}
and
G_{\Omega^{\prime}}
are isomorphic by the correspondence

g\mapsto g^{\prime}=g^{\lambda}
.
So, the CSDs for the two decompositions are naturally identified under this isomorphism.
Moreover, this is generalized to any decomposition of
B

[
Nak23
, Prop. III.1.23]
.
Therefore, a CSD essentially depends only on
B
.

The matrix
\Delta
also determines the data

\displaystyle N^{\circ}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}\delta_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i},\quad M^{\circ}:=\mathrm{Hom}(N^{\circ},\mathbb{Z}),

(8.14)

so that
N^{\circ}\subset N
and
M\subset M^{\circ}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
For the standard basis
\mathbf{e}_{1}
, …,
\mathbf{e}_{n}
of
N
,
let
e^{*}_{1}
, …,
e^{*}_{n}
be the dual basis of
M
.
Then, we have a basis of
\delta_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}
, …,
\delta_{n}\mathbf{e}_{n}
of
N^{\circ}

and its dual basis
f_{1}=e^{*}_{1}/\delta_{1}
, …,
f_{n}=e^{*}_{n}/\delta_{n}
of
M^{\circ}
.
We identify
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
f_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}
, not with
e^{*}_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
Under the identification,
the canonical pairing

\langle\mathbf{n},z\rangle\colon N\times M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

is given
by

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{z}\rangle=\mathbf{n}^{T}\Delta^{-1}\mathbf{z}.

(8.15)

We also have

\displaystyle p^{*}(\mathbf{e}_{j})(\delta_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i})=\{\delta_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{\Omega}=\delta_{i}\omega_{ij}=b_{ij}.

(8.16)

This means that the representation matrix of the map

p^{*}
in (
8.11
) with respect the basis

\mathbf{e}_{1}
, …,
\mathbf{e}_{n}\in N
and
f_{1}
, …,
f_{n}\in M^{\circ}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
is given by
B
.
Thus,
p^{*}(\mathbf{n})\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
is represented by the vector
B\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

8.3
Rank 2 CSDs: finite type

The proof of Theorem
8.12

in
[
GHKK18
]

is based on certain abstract construction.
For the rank 2 case, however, it is possible to describe CSDs more explicitly,
thanks to the results in Section
7.7
.
Here we concentrate on the finite type to illustrate the idea of a CSD.

\textstyle{\gamma_{1}}
\textstyle{\gamma_{2}}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}^{\delta_{1}}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{\delta_{2}}

Figure 8.2:
CSD of type
A_{1}\times A_{1}
.

Let us first consider the simplest case.

Example 8.13

(Type
A_{1}\times A_{1}
)
.

When
B=O
, we have a decomposition
(
5.1
) with
\Omega=O
and arbitrary
\Delta
.
By the commutativity (
7.41
), we have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}=\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}}.

(8.17)

This relation is naturally interpreted as
the (unique) consistency condition for a scattering diagram of rank 2 in Figure
8.2
.
Namely, it consists of two incoming walls

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{\delta_{1}})_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{\delta_{2}})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}}.

(8.18)

The LHS of the relation (
8.17
)
is the path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}}

along
\gamma_{1}
,
while the RHS is the one
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{2},\mathfrak{D}}
along
\gamma_{2}
.
Thus, the relation (
8.17
) is exactly the consistency relation

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{1},\mathfrak{D}}
.
Thus, this is a CSD for
B
.

Next, let us assume

\displaystyle B=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\delta_{1}\\
\delta_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}

(8.19)

for some positive integers
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
.
We consider a skew-symmetric decomposition of
B
with

\displaystyle\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}&0\\
0&\delta_{2}\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(8.20)

The advantage of this decomposition is that one can
work with the common structure group
G_{\Omega}

for any matrix
B
in (
8.19
).
Note that the matrix
\Omega
is the same one
in (
7.38
).
So, we can use the results in Section
7.7
.
Also, by (
8.19
),

B\mathbf{n}
(
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
) is in
the closure of the
second

quadrant excluding the origin
\mathbf{0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Thus, a wall
(\mathfrak{d},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
is incoming if and only if
the support
\mathfrak{d}
intersects with
the closure of the second
quadrant excluding the origin
\mathbf{0}
.

(a)
A_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}

(b)
B_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}

(c)
G_{2}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}^{3}
\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}^{3}
\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}

Figure 8.3:
Rank 2 CSDs of finite type.

Example 8.14

(Finite type:
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\leq 3
)
.

We use the results in Example
7.11
.

(a) Type
A_{2}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,1)
.
The relation (
7.43
) is interpreted as
the consistency relation for
the scattering diagram in Figure
8.3
(a).
It consists of three walls

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}])_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[(1,1)])_{(1,1)},

(8.21)

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1)\subset(1,1)^{\perp}

(8.22)

by (
8.15
).
This ray is contained in the closure of the fourth quadrant.
Thus, it is outgoing.
Therefore, this is a CSD for
B
.

(b) Type
B_{2}
.
Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,2)
.
The relation (
7.44
) is interpreted as
the consistency relation for
the scattering diagram in Figure
8.3
(b),
which consists of four walls

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},\\
&(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2),\Psi[(1,1)]^{2})_{(1,1)},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[(1,2)])_{(1,2)}.\end{split}

(8.23)

This is a CSD for
B
.

(c) Type
G_{2}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,3)
.
The relation (
7.45
) is interpreted as
the consistency relation for
the scattering diagram in Figure
8.3
(c),
which consists of six walls

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{3})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},\\
&(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-3),\Psi[(1,1)]^{3})_{(1,1)},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2),\Psi[(2,3)])_{(2,3)},\\
&(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(2,-3),\Psi[(1,2)]^{3})_{(1,2)},\quad(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[(1,3)])_{(1,3)}.\end{split}

(8.24)

This is a CSD for
B
.

We see that
the above CSDs are constructed only with dilogarithm elements and the pentagon relation.

8.4
G
-fan and CSD

Let us explain the relation between
a CSD
\mathfrak{D}(B)
and
a cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern
with
the initial exchange matrix
B
.

Recall that the
G
-pattern

\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}^{t_{0}}=\{G_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
for
the initial exchange matrix
B

in Definition
2.17

encodes all essential information of the corresponding
cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern
by Theorem
2.31
.
One can geometrically represent each
G
-matrix
G_{t}
by
a
G
-cone
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

defined by

\displaystyle\sigma(G_{t}):=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{1;t},\dots,\mathbf{g}_{n;t}).

(8.25)

Due to the unimodularity (
2.76
),
the dimension of
\sigma(G_{t})
is
n
.
Note that the cone
\sigma(G_{t})
is invariant under the
action of a permutation
\nu
on a
G
-matrix
G_{t}

in (
2.79
).
Each cone generated by a subset of

\mathbf{g}_{1;t}
, …,

\mathbf{g}_{n;t}

is called a
face
of
\sigma(G_{t})
,
including the one
\sigma(\emptyset)=\{\mathbf{0}\}
.
In particular,
let

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(G_{t}):=\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{1;t},\dots,\check{\mathbf{g}}_{i;t}\dots,\mathbf{g}_{n;t}),

(8.26)

which is a face of dimension
n-1
.
Then,
by (
2.53
),
if
t
,
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
are
k
-adjacent, the
G
-cones

\sigma(G_{t})
and
\sigma(G_{t^{\prime}})
intersect each other in their common face

\sigma_{k}(G_{t})=\sigma_{k}(G_{t^{\prime}})
.

Definition 8.15
.

A set of cones
\mathcal{F}
is a
fan

if the intersection
\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}
of any pair
\sigma
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}

belongs to
\mathcal{F}
.

The following property is a consequence
of the sign-coherence in Theorem
2.24
.

Proposition 8.16
.

[
Rea14
, Thm. 8.7]

The set
\mathcal{G}(B)
consisting of all
G
-cones and their faces
is a fan in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We call the above fan the
G
-fan
for a skew-symmetrizable matrix
B

or the corresponding cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern.
The
support
of the
G
-fan
\mathcal{G}(B)
is defined by

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}(B)|:=\bigcup_{t\in\mathbb{T}^{n}}\sigma(G_{t}).

(8.27)

We say that the
G
-fan
\mathcal{G}(B)

is
complete
if
|\mathcal{G}(B)|=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The following fact is known.

Proposition 8.17

(
[
Rea14
, Thm. 10.6]
,
[
Nak24b
, Thm. 4.2]
)
.

The
G
-fan
\mathcal{G}(B)
is complete if and only if the
corresponding cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern is of finite type.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{n-1}(B)
be the set
of all cones in
\mathcal{G}(B)
of dimension
n-1
.
Let
|\mathcal{G}_{n-1}(B)|
be the union of the supports of all cones in
\mathcal{G}_{n-1}(B)
.

Here is the second fundamental result on CSDs.

Theorem 8.18

(
[
GHKK18
, Construction 1.30]
,
[
Rea20
, Cor. 4.4]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}(B)
be a CSD for
B
with minimal support.
Let
\mathfrak{S}(B)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be
the image of the
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}(B))\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}

under the identification
M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
f_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
Then, we have the inclusion

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{n-1}(B)|\subset\mathfrak{S}(B).

(8.28)

Also, the interior
\mathrm{Int}(\sigma(G_{t}))
of each
G
-cone
dones not intersect
\mathfrak{S}(B)
.
Moreover,
for a cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern of finite type,
we have the equality

\displaystyle|\mathcal{G}_{n-1}(B)|=\mathfrak{S}(B).

(8.29)

Therefore,
all essential information of a cluster pattern/
Y
-pattern
is contained in the corresponding CSD.

Example 8.19
.

Let us check the equality (
8.29
)
in Examples
8.13
and
8.14
.
For type
A_{1}\times A_{1}
, there are four
G
-matrices

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

(8.30)

Comparing it with
Figure
8.2
,
we see that the equality (
8.29
) holds.
For type
A_{2}
, as we see in Example
2.21
,
there are five
G
-matrices (up to the action of the permutation
\tau_{12}
)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
-1&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(8.31)

Comparing it with
Figure
8.3
(a),
we see that the equality (
8.29
) holds.
Other cases are similar
and left as exercises for the reader.

8.5
Rank 2 CSDs: infinite type

Let us present examples of rank 2 CSDs of
infinite type

using the results in Section
7.7
.
It turns out that the infinite type is more interesting because
CSDs contain some additional information
outside the
G
-fan.

Example 8.20

(Affine type:
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}=4
)
.

(a). Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(2,2)
.
The relation (
7.51
)
is translated into the following consistent scattering diagram:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{2})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},

(8.32)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p,-p-1),\Psi[(p+1,p)]^{2})_{(p+1,p)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(8.33)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p+1,-p),\Psi[(p,p+1)]^{2})_{(p,p+1)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(8.34)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\Psi[2^{j}(1,1)]^{2^{2-j}})_{(1,1)}.

(8.35)

Equivalently, one can split the wall in (
8.35
)
into infinitely many walls

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-1),\Psi[2^{j}(1,1)]^{2^{2-j}})_{(1,1)}\quad(j=0,\,1,\,\dots).

(8.36)

See Figure
8.4
(a).
All walls except for the ones in (
8.32
)
are outgoing.
Thus, it is a CSD for
B
.
The equality (
8.29
) does not hold anymore.
Indeed, the complement of
|\mathcal{G}(B)|
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

is given by the ray
\mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-1)
, which is depicted as
a thick ray in the figure.
As we see in (
8.36
), there is some rich structure of walls in the ray.

(b). Type
A_{2}^{(2)}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,4)
.
The relation (
7.52
)
is translated into the following consistent scattering diagram:

\displaystyle(\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}])_{\mathbf{e}_{1}},\quad(\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{4})_{\mathbf{e}_{2}},

(8.37)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p,-2p-1),\Psi[(2p+1,4p)])_{(2p+1,4p)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(8.38)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(2p-1,-4p),\Psi[(p,2p-1)]^{4})_{(p,2p-1)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(8.39)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(p,-2p+1),\Psi[(2p-1,4p)])_{(2p-1,4p)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(8.40)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(2p+1,-4p),\Psi[(p,2p+1)]^{4})_{(p,2p+1)}\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}),

(8.41)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ (\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,-2),\Psi[(1,2)]^{6}\prod_{j=1}^{\infty}\Psi[2^{j}(1,2)]^{2^{2-j}})_{(1,2)}.\end{split}

(8.42)

See Figure
8.4
(b).
Again, it is a CSD for
B
.
The complement of
|\mathcal{G}(B)|
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

is given by the ray
\mathbb{R}_{>0}(1,-2)
.

(a)
A_{1}^{(1)}
(b,c)=(5,1)
(b)
A_{2}^{(2)}
(a,b)=(5,1)
(c) Non-affine infinite type
(a,b)=(5,1)

Figure 8.4:
Rank 2 CSDs of infinite type.
The shaded area in (c) is called the Badlands.

The remaining case is most intriguing,
as already seen in Example
7.13
,
though the entire structure of a CSD is not known yet.

Example 8.21

(Non-affine infinite type:
\delta_{1}\delta_{2}>4
)
.

We generalize and translate the results and facts in Example
7.13

into the CSD language.
Let

\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})
be the irrational
cone spanned
by two
vectors

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{\pm}=\begin{pmatrix}\delta_{1}\delta_{2}\pm\sqrt{\delta_{1}\delta_{2}(\delta_{1}\delta_{2}-4)}\\
-2\delta_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(8.43)

The complement of
|\mathcal{G}(B)|
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}

is
\sigma(\mathbf{v}_{+},\mathbf{v}_{-})\setminus\{0\}
,
which is
informally called the
Badlands
.
For example, in the case
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,5)
,
the cone
\sigma(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
is depicted as a shaded region in Figure
8.4
(c).
As in Example
7.13
,
every rational ray
in the cone
\sigma(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}^{\prime})
appears
as a nontrivial wall of
\mathfrak{D}(B)

[
GL23
]
. However, their wall elements are not known yet
except for the limited case.

8.6
Positive realization of CSDs and pentagon relation

To conclude this quick course,
let us explain that any CSD of any rank can be
constructed only by the dilogarithm elements and the pentagon relation.

Recall that, for a given skew-symmetric decomposition (
5.1
) of
B
,
a sublattice
N^{\circ}
of
N
is defined by (
8.14
).

Definition 8.22
.

For each
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
, the
normalization factor

\delta(\mathbf{n})
of
\mathbf{n}

is the smallest positive rational number such that

\delta(\mathbf{n})\mathbf{n}\in N^{\circ}
.

For example, for
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{e}_{i}
, we have
\delta(\mathbf{e}_{i})=\delta_{i}
.
Also, for any
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
, we have

\delta(h\mathbf{n})=\delta(\mathbf{n})/h
.

Now we present the third fundamental result on CSDs.

Theorem 8.23

(
[
GHKK18
, Theorem 1.13]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}(B)
be a CSD for
B
.
Then, there is a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}

that is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}(B)

such that
the wall element of any wall
of
\mathfrak{D}

has the following form

\displaystyle g=\Psi[h\mathbf{n}]^{s\delta(h\mathbf{n})}\quad(\mathbf{n}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+};\,s,\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(8.44)

We call a CSD with the property (
8.44
) a
positive realization

due to the positivity of the exponents
s
.
Such a CSD is always with minimal support due to the positivity of
s
.

Below we present the construction of the positive realization of a CSD by the pentagon relation.
Let us temporarily concentrate on the rank 2 case.
The following is a more general answer to Problem
7.9

than Proposition
7.10
.

Proposition 8.24

(Ordering Lemma

[
Nak23
, Prop. III.5.4]
)
.

For the rank 2 case,
let

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{in}}=\Psi[h^{\prime}_{j}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{j}]^{s^{\prime}_{j}\delta(h^{\prime}_{j}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{j})}\cdots\Psi[h^{\prime}_{1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1}]^{s^{\prime}_{1}\delta(h^{\prime}_{1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1})}\quad(\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+};\,\ s^{\prime}_{i},\,h^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0})

(8.45)

be any finite anti-ordered product.
Then,
by applying the relations in Proposition
7.7

possibly infinitely many times,

C^{\mathrm{in}}

is transformed into a (possibly infinite) ordered product
C^{\mathrm{out}}
of
factors of the same form

\displaystyle\Psi[h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}]^{s_{i}\delta(h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i})}\quad(\mathbf{n}_{i}\in N_{\mathrm{pr}}^{+};\,\ s_{i},\,h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}).

(8.46)

Note the positivity of exponents
s_{i}
.
The proof of
Proposition
8.24
is based on an explicit algorithm (Ordering Algorithm)

[
Nak23
, Algorithm III.5.7]
,
which generalizes the procedure in the examples in
Section
7.7
.
The positivity is guaranteed because the pentagon relation involves only positive
exponents throughout.

Now we go back to the general rank case.
We need the following notion from
[
GHKK18
]
.

Definition 8.25

(Joint)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram.

(a).
For any pair of walls
\mathbf{w}_{i}=(\mathfrak{d}_{i},g_{i})_{\mathbf{n}_{i}}
(
i=1,2
), the intersection
of their supports
\mathfrak{j}=\mathfrak{d}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{d}_{2}

is called a
joint
of
\mathfrak{D}
if
\mathfrak{j}

is a cone of dimension
n-2
.

(b) For each joint
\mathfrak{j}
, let

\displaystyle N_{\mathfrak{j}}:=\{\mathbf{n}\in N\mid\langle\mathbf{n},z\rangle=0\ (z\in\mathfrak{j})\},

(8.47)

which is a rank 2 sublattice of
N
.
Then,
a joint
\mathfrak{j}
is
parallel

(resp.
perpendicular
) if

\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}=0

(resp. otherwise).

By the topological reason, the consistency condition (
8.9
)
is reduced to the one for loops around joints.

The construction of a positive realization in Theorem
8.23
is given as follows. (Here we present the modified version in
[
Nak23
]
with the help of Proposition
8.24
.)

Construction 8.26

(
[
GHKK18
, Construction C.1, Appendix C.3]
,
[
Nak23
, Construction III.5.14]
)
.

The construction proceeds inductively on
the degree
\ell
.

1.

We start with the
scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
consisting of
only incoming walls

\mathbf{w}_{i}:=(\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp},\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}})_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}
(
i=1
, …,
n
).

2.

For each perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}:=\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\perp}
(
i\neq j
)
of
\mathfrak{D}_{1}

with
c=\{\mathbf{e}_{j},\mathbf{e}_{i}\}>0
, we apply Proposition
8.24
for

\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{i}]^{\delta_{i}}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{j}]^{\delta_{j}}
.
In the ordered product, we have the degree 2 element

\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}]^{c\delta_{i}\delta_{j}}
.
Accordingly, we add a new outgoing wall

\displaystyle(\sigma(\mathfrak{j},-p^{*}(\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})),\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}]^{c\delta_{i}\delta_{j}})_{\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}},

(8.48)

where
\sigma(\mathfrak{j},-p^{*}(\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j}))
is the cone spanned by

\mathfrak{j}
and
-p^{*}(\mathbf{e}_{i}+\mathbf{e}_{j})
.

3.

Suppose that the procedure is completed up to degree
\ell

and we have the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
.
By splitting walls if necessary,
we may assume that any pair of distinct joints of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
intersect each other only in their boundaries.
For each perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}
, we set

N^{+}_{\mathfrak{j}}=N_{\mathfrak{j}}\cap N^{+}
and

N^{+}_{\mathfrak{j},\mathrm{pr}}=N_{\mathfrak{j}}\cap N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}}
.
Then, there is a unique
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1},\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2}\in N^{+}_{\mathfrak{j},\mathrm{pr}}

such that

\displaystyle N^{+}_{\mathfrak{j}}\subset\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1}+\mathbb{Q}_{\geq 0}\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\quad\{\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2},\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1}\}>0.

(8.49)

There are walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
containing
\mathfrak{j}
and
intersecting the “second quadrant” with respect to
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{e}}_{2}
.
We take
the anti-ordered product of wall elements of
those walls
and
apply Proposition
8.24
.
Then, for each element
\Psi[h\mathbf{n}]^{s\delta(h\mathbf{n})}

with
\deg(h\mathbf{n})=\ell+1

in the ordered product,
we add a new outgoing wall

\displaystyle(\sigma(\mathfrak{j},-p^{*}(\mathbf{n})),\Psi[h\mathbf{n}]^{s\delta(h\mathbf{n})})_{\mathbf{n}}

(8.50)

in the “fourth quadrant”.

4.

The desired positive realization for
\mathfrak{D}(B)

in Theorem
8.23

is obtained as the limit of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}
.

The consistency around each perpendicular joint
is satisfied by the construction. On the other hand, the consistency around each parallel joint
is not obvious; however, it
is guaranteed
thanks to Theorem
8.12
.

By construction, the consistency relation around each perpendicular joint
is reduced to a trivial one (i.e.,
g=g
) by applying the pentagon relation
in the reverse way to the ordereing.
On the other hand, the one around each parallel joint
is reduced to a trivial one by applying the commutative relation
(
7.41
).
Thus, as a by-product,
the above construction
also reveals the fourth fundamental fact
on the structure of a CSD.

Theorem 8.27

(
[
Nak23
, Thm. III.5.17]

)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}(B)
be a CSD for
B
.
For any admissible loop
\gamma
for
\mathfrak{D}(B)
,
the consistency relation
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}(B)}=\mathrm{id}
is reduced to a trivial one
by applying the relations in Proposition
7.7

possibly infinitely many times.

Notes

Scattering diagrams were introduced by
[
KS06
]
and
[
GS11
]

in the study of homological mirror symmetry,
and developed further in
[
KS08
,
GPS10
,
CPS
,
Gro11
,
KS14
]
.
The connection to cluster algebras was established
by
[
GHKK18
]
,
where CSDs were introduced.
With the scattering diagram method,
several important conjectures on cluster algebras
were proved,
including Theorems
2.24
and
2.29
.
Here, we follow
the presentation of CSDs
in the companion monograph
[
Nak23
]
,
where the roles of the dilogarithm elements and the pentagon relation are highlighted.
Proofs of all results in this chapter
are conveniently found in
[
Nak23
]
as specified below. (We omit the ones already cited in the text.)

•

Theorem
8.7
:
See
[
Nak23
, Thm. III.2.20]
.

•

Theorem
8.12
:
See
[
Nak23
, Thm. III.3.12]
.

•

Proposition
8.16
:
See
[
Nak23
, Thm. II.2.17]
.

•

Theorem
8.18
:
See
[
Nak23
, Thm. II.3.3 (a)]
.

•

Theorem
8.23
:
See
[
Nak23
, Thm. III.5.2]
.

Chapter 9
Dilogarithm identities in cluster scattering diagrams

We derive the DI associated with a loop in a CSD
by combining the results on CSDs and the classical dynamical method in Part II.
This generalizes the DI associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern
to the ones with infinite sum.
Thanks to the structure theorem for CSDs, such a DI
is always reduced to the trivial one by applying the pentagon identity
possibly infinitely many times.
Thus, it is
infinitely reducible
.
Moreover, this method provides yet another proof of the DIs in Theorem
3.5
.

9.1
y
-variables in CSD

To formulate DIs in a CSD, we first need to extend the notion of
y
-variables
in a
Y
-pattern to the corresponding CSD.
From now on, we assume that a CSD
\mathfrak{D}(B)
is a positive realization in
Theorem
8.23
. In particular,
\mathfrak{D}(B)
is with minimal support.

We continue to identify

M_{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with
f_{i}=e^{*}_{i}/\delta_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{i}

as in Section
8.4
.
Note that, under the identification,
the lattice
M^{\circ}\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}
corresponds to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Through this identification, we regard a
c
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
and a
g
-vector
\mathbf{g}_{i;t}
as
elements of
N
and
M^{\circ}
, respectively.
Then,
the canonical pairing of them is given by

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{c}_{i;t},\mathbf{g}_{j;t}\rangle=(\mathbf{c}_{i;t})^{T}\Delta^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{j;t}

(9.1)

by (
8.15
).
Let

\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}=\varepsilon_{i;t}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
be the
c^{+}
-vector in (
5.22
).
Also, let

\sigma_{i}(G_{t})

be the cone of dimension
n-1
in (
8.26
).

Lemma 9.1

(
[
Nak23
, §II.5.2]
)
.

The following facts hold.

(a).
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}
is normal to

\sigma_{i}(G_{t})

with respect to the canonical pairing (
9.1
).

(b).
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}
is primitive.

(c).

\delta(\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+})=\delta_{i}
.

Proof.

(a).
By the duality (
2.75
),
the canonical pairing (
9.1
)
is written as

\displaystyle\langle\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i;t},\mathbf{g}_{j;t}\rangle=\delta_{ij}.

(9.2)

(b).
Since the
C
-matrix
C_{t}
is unimodular by (
2.76
),

\mathbf{c}_{1;t}
, …,
\mathbf{c}_{n;t}
are a basis of
N
.
Thus,
\mathbf{c}_{i;t}
is primitive.

(c).
Since the
G
-matrix
G_{t}
is unimodular by (
2.76
),

\mathbf{g}_{1;t}
, …,
\mathbf{g}_{n;t}
are a basis of
M^{\circ}
.
Then,
by the duality (
9.2
) again, we see that
\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}\in N^{\circ}
.
Moreover, it also tells that
\delta_{i}
is the minimal one
such that
\delta_{i}\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}\in N^{\circ}
.
Therefore,
\delta(\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+})=\delta_{i}
.
∎

Let
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
be the cone in (
8.26
).
Recall that it is contained in
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}(B))

by
Theorem
8.18
.
This means that,
if
z\in\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
,
then there is a wall
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
of
\mathfrak{D}(B)

such that
z\in\mathfrak{d}
.

Lemma 9.2

(
[
Nak23
, Prop. III.6.24]
)
.

Let
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
be a point in
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})

with
z\not\in\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}(B))
.
Suppose that
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
is a wall of
\mathfrak{D}(B)

such that
z\in\mathfrak{d}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+},\quad g=\Psi[\mathbf{c}_{i;t}^{+}]^{\delta_{i}}.

(9.3)
