Moreover, there is no other wall
\mathbf{w}^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{d}^{\prime},g^{\prime})_{\mathbf{n}}
of
\mathfrak{D}(B)

such that
z\in\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
.

We omit the proof.
See
[
Nak23
, §III.6.6]
for details.

In Definition
8.3
of an admissible curve
\gamma
,
we weaken Condition (1) so that
only the ending point
\gamma(1)
is prohibited from being in
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.
Namely, the starting point
\gamma(0)
is now allowed to be in
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})

(but not allowed to be in
\mathrm{Sing(\mathfrak{D})}
by Condition (3)).
Such a curve
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
is said to be

weakly admissible
for a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
.
The definition of the path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
in
Definition
8.4
is applied to a weakly admissible curve
\gamma
.
In particular, the contribution of the wall at the starting point
\gamma(0)
is
not
counted in
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
.

Let us extend the notion of
y
-variables
and
c^{+}
-vectors in (
5.22
)
for a CSD.

Definition 9.3

(
y
-variable/
c^{+}
-vector for CSD)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}=(\mathfrak{d},g)_{\mathbf{n}}
be any wall of
\mathfrak{D}(B)

with
g=\Psi[h\mathbf{n}]^{s\delta(h\mathbf{n})}
as in (
8.44
).
Let
z\in\mathfrak{d}

and
z\not\in\mathrm{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}(B))
.
Let
\mathcal{C}^{+}
be the positive orthant in (
8.10
).
Let
\gamma_{z}
be any weakly admissible curve in
\mathfrak{D}(B)

from
z
to any point in
\mathrm{Int}(\mathcal{C}^{+})
.
Then, we define a
y
-variable
y_{z}[h\mathbf{n}]
at
z
with the
c^{+}
-vector

h\mathbf{n}
by

\displaystyle y_{z}[h\mathbf{n}]:=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{z},\mathfrak{D}(B)}(y^{h\mathbf{n}})\in\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]],

(9.4)

where the path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{z},\mathfrak{D}(B)}\in G
acts on
y^{h\mathbf{n}}

under the
y
-representation
\rho_{y}
in Proposition
7.2
.
(Even though
y_{z}[h\mathbf{n}]
only depends on
z
as we see below, to represent its
c^{+}
-vector
h\mathbf{n}
explicitly is useful in our application.)

Remark 9.4
.

Let
\gamma_{z}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{z}
be weakly admissible curves.
Then, the equality
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{z},\mathfrak{D}(B)}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma^{\prime}_{z},\mathfrak{D}(B)}

does
not
hold in general because the contribution of the wall element

\Psi[h\mathbf{n}]^{s\delta(h\mathbf{n})}
at
z
is not counted.
Nevertheless, the RHS of (
9.4
) does not depend on the choice of
\gamma_{z}

because
\Psi[h\mathbf{n}]^{s\delta(h\mathbf{n})}
acts on
y^{h\mathbf{n}}
trivially.

The following fact justifies the above definition.

Proposition 9.5

(
[
Nak24a
, Remark 3.2 (a)]
)
.

In (
9.4
),
if the
point
z

lies in
\sigma_{i}(G_{t})
,
then we have

\displaystyle y_{z}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}]=y_{i;t}^{\varepsilon_{i;t}},

(9.5)

where the RHS is regarded as an element of
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]
by the mutations.

Proof.

For clarity, the proof is separated into three steps.

(i).
Let
t_{0}
,
t_{1}
, …,
t_{P}=t
be a sequence in
\mathbb{T}_{n}

such that
t_{0}
is the initial vertex of
\mathbb{T}_{n}
and
t_{s}
and
t_{s+1}
are adjacent.
For each
s=0
, …,
P-1
,
let
k_{s}\in\{1,\,\dots,\,n\}
be the one such that

t_{s}
and
t_{s+1}
are
k_{s}
-adjacent.
We take a weakly admissible curve
\gamma_{z}
in (
9.4
)
so that, starting form
z\in\sigma_{i}(G_{t_{P}})
,
it passes through the
G
-cones

\sigma(G_{t_{P}})
,
\sigma(G_{t_{P-1}})
, …,
\sigma(G_{t_{0}})=\mathcal{C}^{+}
in this order.
(To be precise, if
k_{P-1}=i
,
the curve
\gamma_{z}
may stay in
\sigma(G_{t_{P}})
only at the starting point
\gamma_{z}(0)

depending on the choice of
\gamma_{z}
;
otherwise, it stays in
\sigma(G_{t_{P}})
for a while.
See Figure
9.1
.)
Let us take into account Lemma
9.2
.
Also, if
k_{P-1}=i
, then
\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1};t_{P-1}}=\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}
.
Thus,
\Psi[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1};t_{P-1}}]
acts on
y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}}
trivially.
Then, in any situation, we have

\displaystyle y_{z}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}]=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{z},\mathfrak{D}(B)}(y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}})=\Psi[\mathbf{c}_{k_{0};t_{0}}^{+}]^{\epsilon_{0}\delta_{k_{0}}}\cdots\Psi[\mathbf{c}_{k_{P-1};t_{P-1}}^{+}]^{\epsilon_{P-1}\delta_{k_{P-1}}}(y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}}),

(9.6)

where
\epsilon_{s}
is the intersection sign (
8.7
)
of
\gamma_{z}
at the wall
\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{s})=\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{s+1})
.

\sigma(G_{t(P-1)})
\sigma(G_{t(P)})
\mathcal{C}^{+}
\gamma
z
(a)
k_{P-1}=i
\sigma(G_{t(P-1)})
\sigma(G_{t(P)})
\mathcal{C}^{+}
\gamma
z
(b)
k_{P-1}\neq i

Figure 9.1:
Admissible curve
\gamma_{z}
.
The shaded region represents
\sigma(G_{t(P)})
.

(ii).
Consider the the mutation sequence (
5.8
) corresponding to the above sequence
k_{0}
, …,
k_{P-1}
.
Let us find the relation between the intersection sign
\epsilon_{s}
and the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{k_{s};t_{s}}
.
The duality (
9.2
)
implies that,
when the curve
\gamma_{z}
crosses
the wall
\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{s})=\sigma_{k_{s}}(G_{s+1})

from
\sigma(G_{s+1})
to
\sigma(G_{s})
,
its derivative
\gamma^{\prime}_{z}
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{c}_{k_{s};t_{s}},\gamma^{\prime}_{z}\rangle>0.

(9.7)

Since the vector
\mathbf{n}_{i}
in (
8.7
)
is given by
\mathbf{c}_{k_{s};t_{s}}^{+}:=\varepsilon_{k_{s};t_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s};t_{s}}
,
the sign of

\langle\mathbf{n}_{i},\gamma^{\prime}_{z}\rangle

coincides with the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{k_{s};t_{s}}
.
Thus, by (
8.7
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{s}=-\varepsilon_{k_{s};t_{s}}.

(9.8)

Putting it into (
9.6
), we have

\displaystyle y_{z}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}]=\Psi[\mathbf{c}_{k_{0};t_{0}}^{+}]^{-\varepsilon_{k_{0};t_{0}}\delta_{k_{0}}}\cdots\Psi[\mathbf{c}_{k_{P-1};t_{P-1}}^{+}]^{-\varepsilon_{k_{P-1};t_{P-1}}\delta_{k_{P-1}}}(y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i;t}}).

(9.9)

(iii).
Let us identify
y_{i;t_{s}}
with
y_{i}(s)
in the sequence of mutations in (
5.8
)
and switch to the notation therein.
In the formalism of (
5.10
),
the equality (
9.9
) is written as

\displaystyle y_{z}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i}(P)]

\displaystyle{=}(\mathfrak{q}(0)\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{q}(P-1))(y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i}(P)})

\displaystyle{=}\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0)(y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{i}(P)})

\displaystyle{=}(\mathfrak{q}(P-1;0)\circ\tau(P-1;0))(y_{i}(P)^{\varepsilon_{i}(P)})\quad

\displaystyle{=}\mu(P-1;0)(y_{i}(P)^{\varepsilon_{i}(P)})).

This is the desired result.
∎

The above proof also tells
that a sequence of mutations of
y
-variables in a
Y
-pattern
is expressed by a path-ordered product in the corresponding CSD.
By the same token,
the periodicity condition of a
Y
-pattern
is expressed by the consistency relation along a loop in the CSD.

Example 9.6
.

Suppose that the sequence of mutations of
Y
-seeds in (

) is
\nu
-periodic; namely,
\Upsilon(P)=\nu\Upsilon(0)
.
Here we do not assume
\Upsilon(0)
is the initial
Y
-seed
\Upsilon
.
By Theorem
2.31
, we have
G(P)=\nu G(0)
;
hence,
\sigma(G(P))=\sigma(G(0))
.
Correspondingly, we consider an admissible loop
\gamma
with the base point
w=\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)
in
\mathfrak{D}(B)
such that

•

it is entirely contained in the support of the
G
-fan
\mathcal{G}(B)
,

•

the base point
w
is in the internal of
\sigma(G(0))=\sigma(G(P))
,

•

it crosses the
G
-cones
\sigma(G(P-1))
, …,
\sigma(G(0))=\sigma(G(P))

in this order.

See Figure
9.2
.
We have the consistency relation along the loop
\gamma

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}(B)}=\mathrm{id}.

(9.10)

This relation coincides with the relation (
7.32
).

w
\gamma

-pattern.

.

9.2
DI associated with a loop in a CSD

Now we extend our leitmotif, Theorem
3.5
, for an arbitrary loop in a CSD.
Since such a DI involves an infinite sum in general,
it is natural to regard it as an identity for formal power series in the initial variables
\mathbf{y}
.
However, as an additional issue,
the modified Rogers dilogarithm

\tilde{L}(x)
in (
1.26
) is not analytic at
x=0
. Instead, it has the following
expansion
around
x=0
with
\log
factor,

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j^{2}}x^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\log x\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}x^{j}.

(9.11)

Accordingly,
for variables
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
,
we consider the following formal sum

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{y})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\log y_{i})g_{i}(\mathbf{y})\quad(f(\mathbf{y}),g_{i}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]),

(9.12)

and call it a
formal Puiseux series in
\mathbf{y}
with
\log
factor
.
For each integer
\ell\geq 1
,
let
\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}
be the set of all formal power series
f(\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]

such that all coefficients of
f(\mathbf{y})
vanish up to the total degree
\ell
.
Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}_{\log}:=\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\log y_{i})\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}.

(9.13)

Let
\gamma
be any admissible loop in
\mathfrak{D}(B)

with the base point
w
.
Let us fix
\ell\geq 1
.
Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}(B)
the reduction of
\mathfrak{D}(B)
at
degree
\ell
.
Suppose that
\gamma
intersects walls of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}(B)

at points
z_{0}
, …,
z_{P-1}
in this order.
There might be multiple walls with a common normal vector intersected by
\gamma
at a time.
We distinguish them by allowing the multiplicity
z_{a}=z_{a+1}=\cdots=z_{a+k}
.
Then, the walls crossed at
z_{0}
, …,
z_{P-1}
are parametrized as
\mathbf{w}_{0}
, …,
\mathbf{w}_{P-1}
.
By our assumption,
each wall
\mathbf{w}_{a}
has the form

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{a}=(\mathfrak{d}_{a},\Psi[h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}]^{s_{a}\delta(h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a})})_{\mathbf{n}_{a}}.

(9.14)

By the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}(B)
,
we have
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}(B)}=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}(B)}&=\Psi[h_{P-1}\mathbf{n}_{P-1}]^{\epsilon_{P}s_{P-1}\delta(h_{P-1}\mathbf{n}_{P-1})}\cdots\Psi[h_{0}\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{\epsilon_{0}s_{0}\delta(h_{0}\mathbf{n}_{0})}\equiv\mathrm{id}\\
&\hskip 200.0pt\mod G^{>\ell},\end{split}

(9.15)

where
\epsilon_{a}
is the intersection sign.
Let us turn it into an identity for the modified Rogers dilogarithm
\tilde{L}

that is parallel with the one in Theorem
3.5
.

Theorem 9.7

(
[
Nak24a
]
)
.

In the situation (
9.15
),
the following identity holds
as
a formal Puiseux series in
\mathbf{y}
with
\log
factor:

\displaystyle\sum_{a=0}^{P-1}\epsilon_{a}s_{a}\delta(h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a})\tilde{L}(y_{z_{a}}[h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}])\equiv 0\mod\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}_{\log}.

(9.16)

Proof.

We give a proof based on the classical mechanical method
in Chapter
6
(especially, Proposition
6.13
).
Let us consider an admissible curve
\gamma_{w}
from the base point
w
of
\gamma

to a point in
\mathrm{Int}({\mathcal{C}}^{+})
.
Then, we concatenate
\gamma_{w}^{-1}
,
\gamma
, and
\gamma_{w}
in this order
to have an admissible loop
\gamma^{\prime}
with the base point in
\mathrm{Int}({\mathcal{C}}^{+})
.
Then, the contribution to the LHS of (
9.16
) from
\gamma_{w}^{-1}
and the one from
\gamma_{w}
cancel because the intersection signs therein are opposite.
Therefore, we assume that the base point
w
of
\gamma

is in
\mathrm{Int}({\mathcal{C}}^{+})
without loss of generality.
We separate the proof into two cases.

(a). Assume that
B
is nonsingular.
We use a parallel notation in Section
6.7
.
We consider the Hamiltonian system on the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}

for the time span
[0,P]
.
The Hamiltonian for the time span
[a,a+1]
(
a=0
, …,
P-1
)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}_{a}=\epsilon_{a}s_{a}\delta(h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a})\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y^{h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}}).

(9.17)

Accordingly,
for a trajectory
\alpha=(\overline{\mathbf{u}}(t),\overline{\mathbf{p}}(t))_{t\in[0,P]}

in the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}
,
we consider the quantity

\displaystyle S[\alpha]=\sum_{a=0}^{P-1}\mathscr{L}_{a},\quad\mathscr{L}_{a}=\epsilon_{a}s_{a}\delta(h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a})\tilde{L}(\overline{y}(a)^{h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}}),

(9.18)

\displaystyle\overline{y}_{i}(a)=\exp\left(\sqrt{2}\delta^{-1}_{i}\overline{p}_{i}(a)\right)=\exp\biggl(\sqrt{2}\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}\overline{u}_{j}(a)\biggr),

(9.19)

which is the counterpart of the “action integral” in (
6.87
).
Recall that
the time-one flow of the Hamiltonian
\mathscr{H}_{\mathbf{n},a}=a\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y^{\mathbf{n}})

coincides with the action of
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{-a}
on the function space as explained in Section
7.4
.
For the
i
th coordinate function
{y}_{i}
at time 0,
let
{y}^{\prime}_{i}
be the one at time
P

after the above time development.
Then, it is described as a function of
\mathbf{y}
as

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma^{-1},\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}(B)}(y_{i}),

(9.20)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma^{-1},\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}(B)}

\displaystyle=\Psi[h_{0}\mathbf{n}_{0}]^{-\epsilon_{0}s_{0}\delta(h_{0}\mathbf{n}_{0})}\cdots\Psi[h_{P-1}\mathbf{n}_{P-1}]^{-\epsilon_{P-1}s_{P-1}\delta(h_{P-1}\mathbf{n}_{P-1})}.

(9.21)

Note that the dilogarithm elements act in the opposite order
along the time development on
the coordinate functions.

For simplicity, suppose that the equality
(
9.15
) is the exact one

without
modulo
G^{>\ell}
.
So, by (
9.20
),
we have

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}=y_{i}.

(9.22)

Then, we repeat the argument in Section
6.7
.
Namely, by (
6.107
)–(
6.109
),
we have
u^{\prime}_{i}=u_{i}
and
p^{\prime}_{i}=p_{i}
.
It follows from Proposition
6.13
that

\displaystyle\delta S[\alpha]=\sum_{a=0}^{P-1}\delta\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{c},a}(\alpha_{a})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}^{\prime}_{i}\delta\overline{u}^{\prime}_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{p}_{i}\delta\overline{u}_{i}=0

(9.23)

under any infinitesimal deformation
\alpha+\delta\alpha
in
\tilde{M}_{0}
.
This implies that
S[\alpha]
is constant.
Moreover,
the constant term is evaluated as zero in the limit
\overline{\mathbf{y}}=\overline{\mathbf{y}}(0)\rightarrow\mathbf{0}

because, in (
9.18
),

\overline{y}_{i}(a)\rightarrow 0
and
h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}
is positive.
By replacing
\overline{y}(a)^{h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}}
with
y_{z_{a}}[h_{a}\mathbf{n}_{a}]
,

S[\alpha]
can be viewed as a
a formal Puiseux series in
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(0)
with
\log
factor.
Thus, we obtain the desired formula (
9.16
).

Now we consider the general case in (
9.15
).
The equality (
9.22
) is replaced with

\displaystyle y^{\prime}_{i}=y_{i}(1+h_{i}(\mathbf{y}))

(9.24)

for some
h_{i}(\mathbf{y})\in\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}
due to the action of elements in
G^{>\ell}
.
Then, again
by Proposition
6.13
,
under any infinitesimal variation
\alpha+\delta\alpha
,
we have

\displaystyle\delta S[\alpha]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\overline{p}^{\prime}_{i}\delta\overline{u}^{\prime}_{i}-\overline{p}_{i}\delta\overline{u}_{i}),

(9.25)

\displaystyle\delta^{-1}_{i}\overline{p}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}\overline{u}_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\log\overline{y}_{i},\quad\delta^{-1}_{i}\overline{p}^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{ji}\overline{u}^{\prime}_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\log\overline{y}^{\prime}_{i},

(9.26)

where we set
\overline{p}_{i}=\overline{p}_{i}(0)
and
\overline{p}^{\prime}_{i}=\overline{p}_{i}(P)
, etc.
From now on, we view
\overline{p}_{i}
,
\overline{p}^{\prime}_{i}
,
\overline{u}_{i}
,
\overline{u}^{\prime}_{i}

as formal Puiseux series in
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{y}(0)
with
\log
factor,
and we omit bars.
By (
9.24
) and (
9.26
), we can write them as

\displaystyle p^{\prime}_{i}-p_{i}=f_{i}\quad u^{\prime}_{i}-u_{i}=g_{i}\quad(f_{i},\,g_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}).

(9.27)

Thus,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\quad\ \delta S[\alpha]&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl((p_{i}+f_{i})\biggl(\delta u_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial g_{i}}{\partial y_{j}}\frac{\partial y_{j}}{\partial u_{i}}\delta u_{i}\biggr)-p_{i}\delta u_{i}\biggr)\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}((\log y_{i})\tilde{f}_{i}+\tilde{g}_{i})\delta u_{i}\quad(\tilde{f}_{i},\,\tilde{g}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{>\ell})\\
&=\sum_{i=1}^{n}((\log y_{i})\hat{f}_{i}+\hat{g}_{i})\delta y_{i}\quad(\hat{f}_{i},\,\hat{g}_{i}\in\mathcal{F}^{>\ell-1}).\end{split}

It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S[\alpha]}{\partial y_{i}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\log}^{>\ell-1}.

(9.28)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle S[\alpha]\in\mathcal{F}_{\log}^{>\ell},

(9.29)

where the constant term is evaluated as
0
as before.
This is the desired formula (
9.16
).

(b). When
B
is singular,
as discussed in Section
6.6
, the problem is that
those
\mathbf{y}
represented in
(
6.8
)

are not algebraically independent on the small phase space
\tilde{M}_{0}
.
So,
let us take
the principal extension
\overline{B}
in (
6.68
), which is nonsingular.
Let
\overline{\Omega}
be the one in (
7.35
).
Meanwhile, let
G=G_{\Omega}
be unchanged,
so that the CSD
\mathfrak{D}(B)
is also unchanged.
We extend
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
to
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{2n})
.
Then, we extend the
y
-representation of
G
on
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]

in (
7.22
) to
the
principal
y
-representation
on
\mathbb{Q}[[\tilde{\mathbf{y}}]]
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(y^{(\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})}):=\{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{0}),(\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})\}_{\overline{\Omega}}\,y^{(\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime},\mathbf{m}^{\prime})}.

(9.30)

Also, we introduce canonical variables

\tilde{\mathbf{u}}=(u_{1},\dots,u_{2n})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{2n})

and represent
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}
in the same way as (
6.8
),
where the matrix
B
is replaced with
\overline{B}
.
Then,
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
are now algebraically independent
on the small phase space for
(\tilde{\mathbf{u}},\tilde{\mathbf{p}})
.
After this, the proof of (a) is applicable.
∎

Now we take the limit
\ell\rightarrow\infty
.
We parametrize the intersection of
\gamma
and
the walls in
\mathfrak{D}(B)
as
z_{i}
(
i\in J
) by a countable and totally ordered set
J

so that
\gamma
crosses
z_{i}
earlier than
z_{j}
only if
i<j
.
Then, the consistent relation along
\gamma
is presented by an infinite product
in the increasing order in
J
from right to left as follows:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}(B)}=\prod_{i\in J}^{\leftarrow}\Psi[h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}]^{\epsilon_{i}s_{i}\delta(h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i})}=\mathrm{id}.

(9.31)

Theorem 9.8

(
[
Nak24a
]
)
.

For any admissible loop
\gamma
with the consistent relation
(
9.31
),
the following identity holds
as a formal Puiseux series in
\mathbf{y}
with
\log
factor:

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}\epsilon_{i}s_{i}\delta(h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i})\tilde{L}(y_{z_{i}}[h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}])=0.

(9.32)

Proof.

This is obtained by taking the limit
\ell\rightarrow\infty

of (
9.16
).
∎

We call the identity (
9.32
), together with its reduction (
9.16
) at degree
\ell
,
the
DI associated with a loop
\gamma
in
\mathfrak{D}(B)
.
When the loop
\gamma
is contained in the support of
G
-fan
\mathcal{G}(B)

as in Example
9.6
,
it is regarded
as a
\nu
-period of a
Y
-pattern.
Then, the sum in the identity is finite, and the identity coincides with the one
in Theorem
3.5
.

Example 9.9

(Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
)
.

For the consistency relation (
7.51
),
the associated DI (
3.11
) is written as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{L}(y_{2}(1+y_{1})^{2})+\tilde{L}(y_{1})=\tilde{L}(y_{1}(1+y_{2}(1+y_{1})^{2})^{-2})+\Lambda+\tilde{L}(y_{2}),

(9.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Lambda=\tilde{L}(y[(2,1)])+\tilde{L}(y[(3,2)])+\cdots\\
&\qquad+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{1-j}\tilde{L}(y[2^{j}(1,1)])+\cdots+\tilde{L}(y[(2,3)])+\tilde{L}(y[(1,2)]).\end{split}

(9.34)

In the sum
\Lambda
,
y_{z_{i}}[h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}]
in (
9.16
) is unambiguously parametrized by
h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}
, so that
z_{i}
is omitted.
Also, the common factor 2 is omitted.
Since the LHS is a finite sum, the RHS converges
as a function of
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
.
Unfortunately, we do not know the explicit expressions of
y[\mathbf{n}]
in the RHS in general.

9.3
Alternative proof of DIs and infinite reducibility

Let us show that
the reduction of the consistency relations in
Theorem
8.27
implies a parallel reduction of the corresponding DIs in Theorem
9.8

(including the ones in Theorem
3.5
).
This also provides an alternative proof of Theorem
9.8
and
also Theomem
3.5
.

First, we reformulate the pentagon identity
(
1.35
)
into the form that is closer to
the pentagon relation (
7.42
).
We continue to work on the situation of the previous section.

Lemma 9.10

(Pentagon identity
[
Nak24a
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{n}_{1}
,
\mathbf{n}_{2}\in N^{+}
.
If
\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}=c\neq 0
,
the following identity as functions of
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{n}
holds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \frac{1}{c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{-1/c}(\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}))+\frac{1}{c}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})\\
&=\frac{1}{c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}(\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}))+\frac{1}{c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}(\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}))+\frac{1}{c}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}).\end{split}

(9.35)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{-1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}),

\displaystyle\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})

\displaystyle=y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1},

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})&=\Psi[\mathbf{n}_{2}]^{-1/c}(y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1})\\
&=y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1})^{-1}\\
&=y^{\mathbf{n}}(1+y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}+y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1}.\end{split}

Then, the pentagon identity (
1.35
)
is written in the form
(
9.35
)
by setting
y_{1}=y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}
and
y_{2}=y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}
.
∎

The overall factor
1/c
was put so that the identity (
9.35
) exactly matches (the log form of) the
pentagon relation (
7.42
).
Based on the correspondence between (
9.35
) and (
7.42
),
we obtain a parallel result with Theorem
8.27
.

Theorem 9.11

(
[
Nak24a
]
)
.

For any admissible loop
\gamma
for
\mathfrak{D}(B)
,
the associated DI in (
9.32
)
is constructed from and
reduced to a trivial one
by applying the pentagon identity in (
1.35
)
possibly infinitely many times.

\mathcal{C}^{+}
z^{\prime}_{1}
z^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}
z_{1}
z_{j}
w
\vdots
\vdots
\mathfrak{j}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}
\textstyle{\bullet}

Figure 9.3:
Configuration for the consistency relation in
(
9.36
).

Proof.

Fix
\ell>0
, and consider the reduction
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell}(B)
at
\ell
.
We may concentrate on a sufficiently small loop
\gamma
around a joint
\mathfrak{j}

with the base point
w
.
If the joint
\mathfrak{j}
is parallel,
the consistency relation is reduced to a trivial one
by applying the commutative relation (
7.41
).
Accordingly, the corresponding DI is also trivial.
So, let us assume that the joint
\mathfrak{j}
is perpendicular.
The consistency relation around
\mathfrak{j}
has the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \Psi[h^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}]^{s^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\delta(h^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{j^{\prime}})}\cdots\Psi[h^{\prime}_{1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1}]^{s^{\prime}_{1}\delta(h^{\prime}_{1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1})}\\
&\equiv\Psi[h_{j}\mathbf{n}_{j}]^{s_{j}\delta(h_{j}\mathbf{n}_{j})}\cdots\Psi[h_{1}\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{s_{1}\delta(h_{1}\mathbf{n}_{1})}\mod G^{>{\ell}},\end{split}

(9.36)

where the LHS and RHS are the anti-ordered and ordered products
in Construction
8.26
(3), respectively.
The corresponding DI (
9.32
) has the form (see Figure
9.3
)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{j^{\prime}}s^{\prime}_{i}\delta(h^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i})\tilde{L}(y_{z^{\prime}_{i}}[h^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i}])\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{j}s_{i}\delta(h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i})\tilde{L}(y_{z_{i}}[h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}])\mod\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}_{\log}.

(9.37)

In view of Figure
9.3
,
this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\ \sum_{i=1}^{j^{\prime}}s^{\prime}_{i}\delta(h^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i})\Psi[h^{\prime}_{1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1}]^{-s^{\prime}_{1}\delta(h^{\prime}_{1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{1})}\cdots\Psi[h^{\prime}_{i-1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i-1}]^{-s^{\prime}_{i-1}\delta(h^{\prime}_{i-1}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i-1})}(\tilde{L}(y^{h^{\prime}_{i}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}_{i}}))\\
&\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{j}s_{i}\delta(h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i})\Psi[h_{1}\mathbf{n}_{1}]^{-s_{1}\delta(h_{1}\mathbf{n}_{1})}\cdots\Psi[h_{i-1}\mathbf{n}_{i-1}]^{-s_{i-1}\delta(h_{i-1}\mathbf{n}_{i-1})}(\tilde{L}(y^{h_{i}\mathbf{n}_{i}}))\\
&\hskip 240.0pt\mod\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}_{\log}.\end{split}

(9.38)

Along the procedure, say,
\mathcal{P}
of obtaining (
8.46
) from (
8.45
) by
Proposition
8.24

(more precisely, by
[
Nak23
, Algorithm III.5.7]
),
we do the following procedure:

1.

As the initial input, we set
S(y)
to be the LHS of (
9.38
).

2.

If the pentagon relation (
7.42
) is applied to the adjacent pair

\Psi[h^{\prime}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}]^{1/c}
and
\Psi[t\mathbf{n}]^{1/c}

in the procedure
\mathcal{P}
,
we apply the pentagon identity (
9.35
)
to the corresponding terms in
S(y)
.
Simultaneously, we apply the pentagon relation (
7.42
) itself
to the corresponding pair

\Psi[h^{\prime}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}]^{1/c}
and
\Psi[t\mathbf{n}]^{1/c}

in
S(y)
.
(This does not change
S(y)
as a formal Puiseux series in
\mathbf{y}
with
\log
factor.)

3.

If the commutative relation modulo
G^{>\ell}

is applied
to the adjacent pair

\Psi[h^{\prime}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}
and
\Psi[t\mathbf{n}]^{c}

in the procedure
\mathcal{P}
,
we also apply it
to the corresponding pair

\Psi[h^{\prime}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}]^{c^{\prime}}
and
\Psi[t\mathbf{n}]^{c}

in
S(y)
.
(This occurs for the relation (
7.41
)
or the truncation of the relation (
7.42
) modulo
G^{>\ell}
.
In the former case, this does not change
S(y)
,
while in the latter case the result equals to
S(y)
modulo
\mathcal{F}^{>\ell}_{\log}
.)

Then, thanks to the correspondence between
(
9.35
)
and
(
7.42
),
the final result is the RHS of (
9.38
).
Thus,
the identity
(
9.38
) is obtained from a trivial one
S(y)=S(y)
.
Also, by reversing the procedure,
the equality (
9.38
) is reduced to a trivial one.
∎

Note that the above proof of Theorem
9.11
is completely formal and relies only on
the results on the CSDs in Section
8.6

and the correspondence between the pentagon identity
(
9.35
)
and the pentagon relation
(
7.42
).
Therefore, it provides an alternative proof of Theorem
9.8
,
which is
independent of the classical mechanical method in the previous section.
In particular, it gives yet another proof of Theorem
3.5
.

Example 9.12

(Type
B_{2}
)
.

Let us demonstrate how the procedure in the proof of Theorem
9.7
works in practice.
Consider the consistency relation (
7.44
) for type
B_{2}
.
Here, we present again all steps of ordering by the pentagon relation therein,
where the pentagon relation is applied to pairs in the parentheses.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right)&=\left(\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right)\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\left(\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\right)\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\\
&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}.\end{split}

Accordingly, we apply the procedure in the proof of Theorem
9.11
as follows:

\displaystyle\ \uwave{\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})+\uline{\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})}+\uline{\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{1}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\uline{\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})}+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\uline{\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{1}})}

\displaystyle\quad+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{(1,1)})+\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\uwave{\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}}\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle\quad+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\uwave{\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}}\tilde{L}(y^{(1,1)})

\displaystyle\quad+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\uline{\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})}+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\uline{\tilde{L}(y^{(1,1)})}+\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[(1,2)]^{-1}\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{1}})

\displaystyle\quad+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[(1,2)]^{-1}\Psi[(1,1)]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{(1,1)})

\displaystyle\quad+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[(1,2)]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{(1,1)})

\displaystyle\quad+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{(1,2)})+\Psi[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}})+\tilde{L}(y^{\mathbf{e}_{2}}).

Here, the pentagon identity
(
9.35
) is applied to the pairs of terms with underline,
while the pentagon relation (
7.42
) is applied to
the terms with wavy underline.
Thus, we obtain the associated DI
in the form (
9.38
).

Let us specialize Theorem
9.7
to the case of a period of a
Y
-pattern.

Corollary 9.13

(
[
Nak24a
]
)
.

For any
\nu
-period of a
Y
-pattern,
the following facts hold.

(a).
The corresponding consistency relation
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}(B)}=\mathrm{id}

in the CSD
\mathfrak{D}(B)

is reduced to a trivial one
by applying the commutative and pentagon relations in Proposition
7.7

possibly infinitely many times.

(b).
The associated DI
in Theorem
3.5

is
reduced to a trivial one by applying the pentagon identity (
1.35
)
possibly infinitely many times.

Now we come back to the
reduction problem in Section
1.5

for the DI associated with a loop in a CSD.
(All DIs in this text fall into this class.)
As shown in Theorem
9.7
,
it is reduced to the trivial one by applying the pentagon identity possibly infinitely many times.
We call this property
infinite reducibility
.
Of course, infinitely many operations are necessary for DIs with an infinite product.
However, our method only proves the infinite reducibility even for DIs with a finite product.
This is a somewhat unexpected answer to the problem.

Remark 9.14
.

(1)
Corollary
9.13
(b) does not deny the finite reducibility
for a DI with a finite sum
as we see in Example
9.12
.
Also,
a finite reduction may occur differently from the procedure in Theorem
9.11
.
So, the finite reducibility problem for the above DIs with a finite product is still open.

(2)
Corollary
9.13
(a) does not imply that
any
\nu
-period of a
Y
-pattern is reduced to the trivial one
by the square and pentagon periodicities
in the
Y
-pattern itself
.

Here, the square periodicity is the relation
\mu_{i}\mu_{j}\mu_{i}\mu_{j}=\mathrm{id}
for
commuative mutations
\mu_{i}
and
\mu_{j}
.

Again, Example
9.12
is such a case because there is
only the hexagon periodicity
in the
Y
-pattern.
Therefore, the reducibility of a period in a
Y
-pattern and the one in a CSD are
different
problems.
Some other examples of the periodicity that are not reducible in a
Y
-pattern are known
[
FST08
,
KY20
]
.

Notes

The whole content of this chapter is due to
[
Nak24a
]
.

Part IV
Quantum dilogarithm identities

Chapter 10
Quantum dilogarithm

A natural
q
-deformation of the dilogarithm
called the
quantum dilogarithm

was introduced by Faddeev, Kashaev, Volkov
[
FV93
,
FK94
]

and modified by Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09a
]
.
The function satisfies the pentagon identity for
q
-commutative variables,
which is regarded as the counterpart of the pentagon identity for the dilogarithm.

10.1
q
-exponential

Let
q
and
x
be variables.
Let
j
be a nonnegative integer.
We introduce the
q
-Pochhammer symbol

\displaystyle(x;q)_{\infty}=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-xq^{k}),\quad(q)_{j}=\prod_{k=1}^{j}(1-q^{k}),\quad(q)_{0}=1,

(10.1)

and the
q
-number
(
a\in\mathbb{Z}
)

\displaystyle[a]_{q}=\frac{q^{a}-q^{-a}}{q-q^{-1}},\quad\lim_{q\rightarrow 1}[a]_{q}=a.

(10.2)

We define
the
q
-exponential

e_{q}(x)
,
first as a formal power series in
x
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
,
by the following three equivalent expressions:

\displaystyle e_{q}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(q)_{j}}x^{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(1-q)\cdots(1-q^{j})}x^{j}

(10.3)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(x;q)_{\infty}}=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{k}x)^{-1}

(10.4)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j(1-q^{j})}x^{j}\biggr).

(10.5)

Here, we follow the notation in
[
Kir95
]
.
Observe the similarity between the third expression (
10.5
)
and (the exponential of) the Euler dilogarithm
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)
in (
1.1
).

The above three expressions are equivalent due to the following characterization of
e_{q}(x)
.

Proposition 10.1
.

(a).
The properties

\displaystyle e_{q}(0)

\displaystyle=1\quad\text{(initial condition)},

(10.6)

\displaystyle e_{q}(qx)

\displaystyle=(1-x)e_{q}(x)\quad\text{($q$-difference relation)}

(10.7)

uniquely determine a formal power series
e_{q}(x)
in
x
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
.

(b). All expressions (
10.3
), (
10.4
),
and (
10.5
) satisfy
the conditions (
10.6
) and (
10.7
).

Proof.

(a).
We set

\displaystyle e_{q}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{n}(q)x^{n}\quad(f_{n}(q)\in\mathbb{Q}(q)),

(10.8)

where
f_{0}(q)=1
by (
10.6
).
Then, by (
10.7
), we have the recurrence relation

\displaystyle q^{n}f_{n}(q)=f_{n}(q)-f_{n-1}(q)\quad(n\geq 1).

(10.9)

This determines
f_{n}(q)
uniquely as

\displaystyle f_{n}(q)=\frac{1}{1-q^{n}}f_{n-1}(q)=\frac{1}{(1-q)\cdots(1-q^{n})}.

(10.10)

(b).
We have already seen in (
10.10
)
that the expression (
10.3
)
satisfies the conditions in (a).
Thus,
it is enough to check the relation (
10.7
)
for the expressions (
10.4
) and (
10.5
).
For (
10.4
), we see it as

\displaystyle\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{k}qx)^{-1}=(1-x)\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1-q^{k}x)^{-1}.

(10.11)

For (
10.5
), we see it as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exp\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j(1-q^{j})}q^{j}x^{j}\biggr)&=\exp\biggl(-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j}x^{j}\biggr)\exp\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j(1-q^{j})}x^{j}\biggr)\\
&=(1-x)\exp\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j(1-q^{j})}x^{j}\biggr).\end{split}

(10.12)

∎

Next, we specialize
q\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such that
q
is not a root of 1.
Then, every expression for
e_{q}(x)
in (
10.3
)–(
10.5
) makes sense
as a formal power series in
x
with coefficients in
\mathbb{C}
.
Furthermore, suppose that
|q|<1
.
Then, its convergence radius is 1 by (
10.3
).
Thus, we may also regard
e_{q}(x)
as a complex function of
x\in\mathbb{C}
with
|x|<1
;
which is analytically continued to the meromorphic function in (
10.4
).

We may consider the complex parameter
q
(
|q|<1
) as a “quantization
parameter” so that the limit
q\rightarrow 1^{-}
is the
classical limit
.
Equivalently,
we introduce the
Planck constant

\hbar\in\mathbb{C}
with
\mathrm{Im}\,\hbar>0
by

\displaystyle q=\exp(i\hbar),\quad i=\sqrt{-1}.

(10.13)

Then, the limit
\hbar\rightarrow 0^{+i}
is the classical limit,
where
0^{+i}
indicates that
\hbar
approaches to
0
with
\mathrm{Im}\,\hbar>0

in the complex plane.

Proposition 10.2

(
[
FK94
, Eq. (1.5)]
,
[
Kir95
, Cor. 10]
)
.

The following asymptotic expansion holds in the limit
\hbar\rightarrow 0^{+i}
:

\displaystyle e_{q}(x)=(1-x)^{-1/2}\exp\biggl(\frac{-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)}{i\hbar}\biggr)(1+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)).

(10.14)

Proof.

Let
q^{1/2}=\exp(i\hbar/2)
.
By the expression (
10.5
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log e_{q}(x)&=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j(1-q^{j})}x^{j}\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{j}x^{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{j/2}}{j(q^{j/2}-q^{-j/2})}x^{j}\\
&=-\log(1-x)-\frac{1}{q^{1/2}-q^{-1/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{j/2}}{j[j]_{q^{1/2}}}x^{j}.\end{split}

The following expansion formulas hold:

\displaystyle q^{j/2}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{j}{2}i\hbar+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2}),

(10.15)

\displaystyle[j]_{q^{1/2}}

\displaystyle=j+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2}),

(10.16)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q^{1/2}-q^{-1/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i\hbar+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})}=\frac{1}{i\hbar}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

(10.17)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log e_{q}(x)&=-\log(1-x)-\frac{1}{i\hbar}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-x)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).\end{split}

(10.18)

This gives the desired result (
10.14
).
∎

The function
1/e_{q}(x)
was called the
quantum dilogarithm

in
[
FK94
]
.
Here, we reserve the name for its variant introduced in the next section.

10.2
Quantum dilogarithm

Following Fock-Goncharov
[
FG09a
]
,
we define
the
quantum dilogarithm

\Psi_{q}(x)
by

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(x)=e_{q^{2}}(-qx).

(10.19)

Thus, it is essentially the
q
-exponential in the previous section with a slightly different parametrization.
As we will see, this function fits our approach to the quantum dilogarithm identities.

In parallel with (
10.3
)–(
10.5
), we have the following
three equivalent expressions:

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-q)^{j}}{(q^{2})_{j}}x^{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(q-q^{-1})^{j}}\frac{1}{[1]_{q}\cdots[n]_{q}}x^{j},

(10.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(-qx;q^{2})_{\infty}}=\prod_{k=0}^{\infty}(1+q^{2k+1}x)^{-1}

(10.21)

\displaystyle=\exp\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j(q^{j}-q^{-j})}x^{j}\biggr)=\exp\biggl(\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j[j]_{q}}x^{j}\biggr).

(10.22)

As we see, the expressions (
10.20
) and (
10.22
)
are
more “balanced” than the
q
-exponential
e_{q}(x)
under the symmetry
q\leftrightarrow q^{-1}
.
Again, we may view
\Psi_{q}(x)
as a formal power series in
x

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
,
or a meromorphic function of
x
for a given
0<|q|<1
,
depending on the context.

Proposition
10.1
is restated for
\Psi_{q}(x)
as follows.
(The second relation in (
10.24
) is
redundant. However, it is included here for later convenience.)

Proposition 10.3
.

As a formal power series in
x

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
,
the function
\Psi_{q}(x)

is characterized by the conditions

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(0)=1,

(10.23)

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(q^{2}x)=(1+qx)\Psi_{q}(x),\quad\Psi_{q}(q^{-2}x)=(1+q^{-1}x)^{-1}\Psi_{q}(x).

(10.24)

Proof.

The first relation in (
10.24
) is obtained from (
10.7
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{q}(q^{2}x)&=e_{q^{2}}(-q^{3}x)=e_{q^{2}}(q^{2}(-qx))=(1+q^{2}x)e_{q^{2}}(-qx)\\
&=(1+q^{2}x)\Psi_{q}(x).\end{split}

(10.25)

The second relation in (
10.24
) is equivalent to the first one,
and it is obtained from the first one by setting
x
to
q^{-2}x
therein.
∎

The asymptotic property of
\Psi_{q}(x)
is simpler than (
10.14
),
and more directly related with the Euler dilogarithm.

Proposition 10.4

(
[
FG09a
, Eq. (54)]
)
.

The following asymptotic expansion holds in the limit
\hbar\rightarrow 0^{+i}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(x)=\exp\biggl(\frac{-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-x)}{2i\hbar}\biggr)(1+\mathcal{O}(\hbar)).

(10.26)

Proof.

By (
10.22
),

\displaystyle\log\Psi_{q}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j[j]_{q}}x^{j}

(10.27)

The following expansion formulas hold:

\displaystyle[j]_{q}

\displaystyle=j+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{2}),

(10.28)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{q-q^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i\hbar+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{3})}=\frac{1}{2i\hbar}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).

(10.29)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log\Psi_{q}(x)&=-\frac{1}{2i\hbar}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-x)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar).\end{split}

(10.30)

This gives the desired result (
10.26
).
∎

10.3
Pentagon relation

Let us introduce the
q
-binomial coefficient
for
n,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
(
n\geq k
),

\displaystyle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k}_{q}=\frac{(q)_{n}}{(q)_{k}(q)_{n-k}}.

(10.31)

For example,

\displaystyle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{2}{1}_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-q)(1-q^{2})}{1-q}=1+q,

(10.32)

\displaystyle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{3}{1}_{q}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-q)(1-q^{2})(1-q^{3})}{(1-q)(1-q)(1-q^{2})}=1+q+q^{2}.

(10.33)

Lemma 10.5
.

The following recurrence relation holds (
1\leq k\leq n-1
):

\displaystyle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k}_{q}+q^{n-k}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k-1}_{q}=\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k}_{q}.

(10.34)

In particular,
\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{1}{n}{k}_{q}
is a polynomial in
q
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle(\text{LHS})=\frac{(q)_{n-1}}{(q)_{k}(q)_{n-k}}\{(1-q^{n-k})+q^{n-k}(1-q^{k})\}=(\text{RHS}).

(10.35)

∎

For a pair of noncommutative variables
a
,
b

and a commutative (scalar) variable
q
,
we consider the relation (the
q
-commutative relation
)

\displaystyle ab=qba.

(10.36)

We have a
q
-analog of the binomial theorem.

Lemma 10.6

(
q
-binomial theorem
[
Sch53
]
)
.

Under the relation (
10.36
),
we have

\displaystyle(a+b)^{n}=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-k}.

(10.37)

Proof.

We show it by the induction on
n
as follows.

\displaystyle(a+b)^{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-1-k}(a+b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-k}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}q^{n-1-k}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k}_{q}b^{k+1}a^{n-1-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-k}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}q^{n-k}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n-1}{k-1}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-k}

\displaystyle{=}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-k}.

∎

We regard
e_{q}(x)
as a formal power series in
x
as in (
10.3
).
Then,
e_{q}(a)
and
e_{q}(b)
make sense in a formal (noncommutative) power series in
a
and
b

with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
.
By (
10.36
), we have

\displaystyle e_{q}(b)e_{q}(a)\neq e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b).

(10.38)

The product
e_{q}(b)e_{q}(a)
is a “normal” one.

Lemma 10.7

(
[
Sch53
]
)
.

Under the relation (
10.36
),
we have

\displaystyle e_{q}(b)e_{q}(a)=e_{q}(a+b).

(10.39)

Proof.

(RHS)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(q)_{n}}(a+b)^{n}

\displaystyle{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(q)_{n}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{0}{n}{k}_{q}b^{k}a^{n-k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{1}{(q)_{k}}\frac{1}{(q)_{n-k}}b^{k}a^{n-k}=\text{(LHS)}.

∎

It turns out the opposite product

e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b)

contains a hidden rich structure,
which was discovered by
[
FV93
,
FK94
]
.
Here, we follow the presentation by
[
Kir95
]
.

Lemma 10.8

(
[
Kir95
, Lemma 9]
)
.

Under the relation (
10.36
),
we have

\displaystyle e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b)

\displaystyle=e_{q}(b-ba)e_{q}(a).

(10.40)

Proof.

We regard both sides as a formal power series
\varphi(b)
in
b

and check that both sides satisfy the same initial condition
\varphi(0)=e_{q}(a)

and the
q
-difference relation

\displaystyle\varphi(qb)=(1-b+ba)\varphi(b),

(10.41)

which determine
\varphi(b)
uniquely.
First, observe that

\displaystyle e_{q}(qb)e_{q}(b)^{-1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:qerec1}}{=}1-b,

(10.42)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}e_{q}(a)be_{q}(a)^{-1}\overset{\eqref{eq:qcom1}}{}&{=}be_{q}(qa)e_{q}(a)^{-1}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:qerec1}}{}&{=}b(1-a).\end{split}

(10.43)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}e_{q}(a)e_{q}(qb)e_{q}(b)^{-1}e_{q}(a)^{-1}\overset{\eqref{eq:eee1}}{}&{=}e_{q}(a)(1-b)e_{q}(a)^{-1}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:eee2}}{}&{=}1-b+ba.\end{split}

(10.44)

Meanwhile, we also have

\displaystyle\begin{split}e_{q}(q(b-ba))e_{q}(a)e_{q}(a)^{-1}e_{q}(b-ba)^{-1}&=e_{q}(q(b-ba))e_{q}(b-ba)^{-1}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:eee1}}{}&{=}1-b+ba.\end{split}

(10.45)

Therefore, the equality (
10.40
) holds.
∎

By combining Lemmas
10.7

and
10.8
, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 10.9

(
[
FV93
, §3]
,
[
FK94
, §2]
,
[
Kir95
, Lemma 9]
)
.

Under the relation (
10.36
),
we have

\displaystyle e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b)

\displaystyle=e_{q}(b)e_{q}(-ba)e_{q}(a)\quad\text{(pentagon relation){\@index{\@indexphrase{pentagon}\@indexphrase{relation (quantum)}}}}

(10.46)

\displaystyle=e_{q}(b-ba+a).

(10.47)

Proof.

We note the following
q
-commutative relations

\displaystyle a(b-ba)

\displaystyle=q(b-ba)a,\quad a(-ba)=q(-ba)a,\quad(-ba)b=qb(-ba).

(10.48)

Then, we have

\displaystyle e_{q}(a)e_{q}(b)\overset{\eqref{eq:qeid1}}{=}e_{q}(b-ba)e_{q}(a)\overset{\eqref{eq:qee1}}{=}e_{q}(b-ba+a).

(10.49)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle e_{q}(b)e_{q}(-ba)e_{q}(a)\overset{\eqref{eq:qee1}}{=}e_{q}(b)e_{q}(-ba+a)\overset{\eqref{eq:qee1}}{=}e_{q}(b-ba+a).

(10.50)

Thus, we obtain the desired relations.
∎

The relation (
10.46
) is due to
[
FK94
, §2]
,
while the relation (
10.47
) is due to
[
FV93
, §3]
.

For a pair of noncommutative variables
u
,
v

and a commutative (scalar) variable
q
,
we consider the
q
-commutative relation

\displaystyle uv=q^{2}vu.

(10.51)

Then, the pentagon relation (
10.46
) is translated to the ones
for
\Psi_{q}(x)
as follows.

Theorem 10.10

(Pentagon relation
[
FV93
, §3]
,
[
FK94
, §2]
)
.

Under the relation (
10.51
),
we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(u)\Psi_{q}(v)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{q}(v)\Psi_{q}(qvu)\Psi_{q}(u).

(10.52)

Proof.

For
u
and
v
satisfying (
10.51
),
we set
a=-qu
and
b=-qv
, so that they satisfy the
q
-commutative relation

\displaystyle ab=q^{2}ba

(10.53)

instead of (
10.36
).
Then, (
10.46
) reads

\displaystyle e_{q^{2}}(-qu)e_{q^{2}}(-qv)

\displaystyle=e_{q^{2}}(-qv)e_{q^{2}}(-q^{2}vu)e_{q^{2}}(-qu).

(10.54)

By (
10.19
), it is written as (
10.52
).
∎

We will give an alternative proof of the identity in Example
12.6
.

Notes

The
q
-exponential
e_{q}(x)
and its functional relations naturally appeared
in the study of the lattice Virasoro algebra in
[
FV93
]
.
Subsequently, the pentagon identity (
10.46
) was discovered by
[
FK94
]
.
Moreover, the pentagon identity of the classical dilogarithm
was recovered in the limit
q\rightarrow 1
of (
10.46
).
This is why the function
1/e_{q}(x)
was regarded as the quantum dilogarithm.
The version
\Psi_{q}(x)
was introduced in studying the quantization of cluster algebras by
[
FG09a
]
.
Here, we mainly follow the presentation in
[
Kir95
]
.

Chapter 11
Quantum mutations and quantum dilogarithm

Cluster patterns and
Y
-patterns admit natural
quantizations
[
BFZ05
,
FG09c
]
.
The quantum mutations and the quantum dilogarithm are related in the same way
as the classical mutations and the dilogarithm.
We also have a natural quantization of the dilogarithm elements and
the pentagon relations.

11.1
Quantization of
Y
-patterns

The notion of
quantum cluster algebras
was developed in two directions:

•

The quantization of
x
-variables of geometric type

[
BZ05
,
Tra11
]
.

•

The quantization of free
y
-variables

[
FG09a
,
FG09c
]
.

What is common in them is that
x
- and
y
-variables are replaced with

q
-commutative
variables.
They were introduced and studied more or less independently.
Nevertheless, they are closely related to each other naturally.

Let us start with the quantization of
y
-variables, which are simpler and
more directly related to quantum dilogarithm identities.
For a given free
Y
-pattern
\mathbf{\Upsilon}=\{(\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}
,
let

\displaystyle B_{t}=\Delta\Omega_{t}

(11.1)

be a skew-symmetric decomposition in (
5.1
),
where
\Delta=D^{-1}
is a common positive integer diagonal matrix for all
B_{t}
.
Let
q
be a variable, and let

\displaystyle q_{i}:=q^{1/\delta_{i}}=q^{d_{i}}.

(11.2)

The quantization of
\mathbf{\Upsilon}
consists
of the following two modifications
[
FG09a
]
:

•

Quantum
y
-variables
.
For each
Y
-seed
(\mathbf{y}_{t},B_{t})
,
replace
the
y
-variables
\mathbf{y}_{t}=(y_{1;t},\dots,y_{n;t})
with noncommutative variables

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=(Y_{1;t},\dots,Y_{n;t})
.
In particular, the initial variables

\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{Y}_{t_{0}}=(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n})

obey the
q
-commutative relation

\displaystyle Y_{i}Y_{j}=q^{2\omega_{ij}}Y_{j}Y_{i}=q_{i}^{2b_{ij}}Y_{j}Y_{i},\quad\Omega=\Omega_{t_{0}},\quad B=B_{t_{0}}.

(11.3)

•

Quantum mutation
.
For
t,\,t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
which are
k
-adjacent,
replace the mutation (
2.10
) by

\displaystyle Y_{i;t^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\displaystyle Y_{k;t}^{-1}&i=k,\\
\displaystyle q^{\omega_{ki;t}[b_{ki;t}]_{+}}Y_{i;t}Y_{k;t}^{[b_{ki;t}]_{+}}\\
\quad\times\prod_{u=1}^{|b_{ki;t}|}(1+q_{k}^{\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki;t})(2u-1)}Y_{k;t})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki;t})}&i\neq k.\end{cases}

(11.4)

We call
the resulting pattern

\Upsilon_{q}=\{(\mathbf{Y}_{t},B_{t})\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}

a
quantum
Y
-pattern
.
By the specialization
q=1
, it is reduced to the (classical)
Y
-pattern
\Upsilon
.

The following basic example demonstrates the legitimacy
of the above definition.

Example 11.1

(Type
A_{2}
)
.

We use the same convention in Example
2.21
.
We choose
\Delta=I
so that

\displaystyle B=\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(11.5)

Thus, we have the relation

\displaystyle Y_{1}Y_{2}=q^{-2}Y_{2}Y_{1}.

(11.6)

It is equivalent to the relations

\displaystyle Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}=q^{2}Y_{2}Y_{1}^{-1},\quad Y_{1}Y_{2}^{-1}=q^{2}Y_{2}^{-1}Y_{1},\quad Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-1}=q^{-2}Y_{2}^{-1}Y_{1}^{-1}.

(11.7)

The mutations of quantum
Y
-variables,
which are parallel with (
2.43
)–(
2.47
),
are calculated as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y_{1;1}=Y_{1}^{-1},\\
Y_{2;1}=Y_{2}(1+q^{-1}Y_{1}),\end{cases}

(11.8)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y_{1;2}=Y_{1}^{-1}(1+q^{-1}Y_{2}+Y_{1}Y_{2}),\\
Y_{2;2}=Y_{2}^{-1}(1+qY_{1})^{-1},\end{cases}

(11.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y_{1;3}=Y_{1}(1+qY_{2}+q^{2}Y_{1}Y_{2})^{-1},\\
Y_{2;3}=qY_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-1}(1+q^{-1}Y_{2}),\end{cases}

(11.10)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y_{1;4}=Y_{2}^{-1},\\
Y_{2;4}=qY_{1}Y_{2}(1+qY_{2})^{-1},\end{cases}

(11.11)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Y_{1;5}=Y_{2},\\
Y_{2;5}=Y_{1}.\end{cases}

(11.12)

In particular, the pentagon periodicity is preserved.

11.2
Fock-Goncharov decomposition

Let us exhibit the structure of the quantum mutation (
11.4
)
more transparently.
This is done by the Fock-Goncharov decomposition in parallel with the classical case in Section
5.1
.
From now on, we switch to the picture in a sequence of quantum mutations
starting from the initial seed
\Upsilon_{q}=(\mathbf{Y},B)
,

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{q}=\Upsilon_{q}(0)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{0}}}}}\ \Upsilon_{q}(1)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{1}}}}}\ \cdots\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{{k_{P-1}}}}}\ \Upsilon_{q}(P),

(11.13)

where
\Upsilon_{q}(s)=(\mathbf{Y}(s),B(s))
.
Let

\displaystyle B(s)=\Delta\Omega(s)

(11.14)

be the decomposition corresponding to (
11.1
).

First, we present the counterpart of the
\varepsilon
-expression (
5.9
)
for the quantum mutation,
where the case
\varepsilon=1
corresponds to
the second case of (
11.4
).

Lemma 11.2

(
[
Kel11
, Lemma 4.9]
)
.

The following expression is independent of
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&q^{\omega_{ki}(s)[\varepsilon b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}Y_{i}(s)Y_{k}(s)^{[\varepsilon b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}\\
&\quad\times\prod_{u=1}^{|b_{ki}(s)|}(1+q_{k}^{\varepsilon\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))(2u-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))}.\end{split}

(11.15)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ q^{\omega_{ki}(s)[b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}Y_{i}(s)Y_{k}(s)^{[b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}\\
&\quad\times\prod_{u=1}^{|b_{ki}(s)|}(1+q_{k}^{\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))(2u-1)}Y_{k}(s))^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))}\\
=&\ q^{\omega_{ki}(s)[b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}Y_{i}(s)Y_{k}(s)^{[b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}q_{k}^{-b_{ki}(s)b_{ki}(s)}Y_{k}(s)^{-b_{ki}(s)}\\
&\quad\times\prod_{u=1}^{|b_{ki}(s)|}(q_{k}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))(2u-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{-1}+1)^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))}\\
=&\ q^{\omega_{ki}(s)[-b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}Y_{i}(s)Y_{k}(s)^{[-b_{ki}(s)]_{+}}\\
&\quad\times\prod_{u=1}^{|b_{ki}(s)|}(1+q_{k}^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))(2u-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{-1})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{ki}(s))},\end{split}

where in the last equality
we used (
2.8
) and (
11.2
).
∎

In the spirit of the classical case in Section
5.1
,
for each
s=0
, …,
P
,
we regard the
Y
-variables
Y_{i}(s)

(
i=1
, …,
n
) as noncommutative variables
obeying the
q
-commutative relation

\displaystyle Y_{i}(s)Y_{j}(s)=q^{2\omega_{ij}(s)}Y_{j}(s)Y_{i}(s).

(11.16)

Let
\delta_{0}
be the least common multiple of

\delta_{1}
, …,
\delta_{n}
.
Let
N=\mathbb{Z}^{n}
as in Section
7.1
.
For each
s=0
, …,
P
,
let

\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}

be the skew-symmetric bilinear form on
N

defined in the same way as (
5.21
).
Note that

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}\in(1/\delta_{0})\mathbb{Z}.

(11.17)

We introduce the noncommutative algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\Omega(s)}

with generators
Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}
(
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
)
over
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/\delta_{0}})

obeying the relations

\displaystyle Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}=q^{\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}=q^{2\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}.

(11.18)

The product (
11.18
) is associative because

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ (Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{2}})Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{3}}=Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{3}})\\
=&\ q^{\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{2}\}_{\Omega(s)}+\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega(s)}+\{\mathbf{n}_{1},\mathbf{n}_{3}\}_{\Omega(s)}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}+\mathbf{n}_{3}}.\end{split}

(11.19)

By setting

Y(s)^{\mathbf{e}_{i}}=Y_{i}(s)
, we recover the relation (
11.16
).
Moreover,
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega(s)}
is generated
by
Y_{i}(s)
(
i=1
, …,
n
).

We say that a (noncommutative) domain
R
, i.e., an associative ring
with unit having no zero divisor other than 0, is an
Ore domain

if it satisfies the (left) Ore condition:

\displaystyle aR\cap bR\neq\{0\}\quad(a,b\in R\setminus\{0\}).

(11.20)

Any Ore domain is embedded into the
skew-field of all (right) fractions
ab^{-1}
with

a,\,b\in R
, and
b\neq 0

(e.g.,
[
BZ05
, §11]
).

Lemma 11.3

(e.g.,
[
BZ05
, §11]
)
.

The algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega(s)}
is an Ore domain.

Proof.

Let
R=\mathcal{A}_{\Omega(s)}
. Let
R_{m}
be the subspace
spanned by
Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}
with
\mathbf{n}=(n_{i})
such that

0\leq n_{i}\leq m
.
The dimension of

R_{m}
is
(m+1)^{n}
,
which is a polynomial in
m
.
Suppose that
aR\cap bR=\{0\}
for some
a,\,b\in R\setminus\{0\}
.
Let
p
be an integer such that
a,\,b\in R_{p}
.
Since
aR_{m}\cap bR_{m}=\{0\}
,
we have

\displaystyle\dim R_{m+p}\geq\dim aR_{m}+\dim bR_{m}\geq 2\dim R_{m}.

(11.21)

Thus,
\dim R_{m+kp}\geq 2^{k}\dim R_{m}
.
This contradicts the above linear growth property of
R_{m}
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}
be the
skew-field of all fractions
of
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega(s)}
.
In parallel with the mutation
\mu(s)
in (
5.10
),
we formulate the quantum mutation as a skew-field
isomorphism

\displaystyle\mu_{q}{(s)}\colon\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s+1)}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}

(11.22)

\displaystyle Y_{i}(s+1)\mapsto\begin{cases}\displaystyle Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{-1}&i=k_{s},\\
\displaystyle Y(s)^{e_{i}(s)+[\varepsilon b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}e_{k_{s}}(s)}\\
\displaystyle\times\prod_{u=1}^{|b_{k_{s}i}(s)|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon\mathrm{sgn}(b_{k_{s}i}(s))(2u-1)}Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(b_{k_{s}i}(s))}&i\neq k_{s},\end{cases}

where the isomorphism property will be clarified below.
The expression is independent of
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}

thanks to Lemma
11.2
.

First, we consider the tropical part corresponding to the map
\tau{(s)}
in
(
5.11
).
We set
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{s}
in (
11.22
),
where
\varepsilon_{s}:=\varepsilon_{k_{s}}(s)
is the tropical sign.
In parallel with (
5.11
),
we introduce a skew-field isomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tau_{q}{(s)}\colon&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s+1)}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}\\
&Y(s+1)^{\mathbf{n}}&\mapsto&Y(s)^{T(s)(\mathbf{n})},\end{matrix}

(11.23)

where
T(s)
is a linear isomorphism defined by

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}T{(s)}\colon&\mathbb{Z}^{n}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{Z}^{n}\\
&\mathbf{e}_{i}&\mapsto&\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}}&i=k_{s},\\
\mathbf{e}_{i}+[\varepsilon_{s}b_{k_{s}i}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{k}&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(11.24)

Under the specialization
q=1
, the map
\tau_{q}{(s)}

is reduced to the (classical) tropical
transformation
\tau(s)
.
Moreover, both
\tau_{q}{(s)}
and
\tau{(s)}
are essentially
the exponential form of
the common linear map
T{(s)}
.

Lemma 11.4
.

The map
\tau_{q}(s)
is a skew-field isomorphism.

Proof.

By the linearity of
T(s)
,
the desired condition is equivalent to the equality

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{\Omega(s+1)}=\{T(s)(\mathbf{e}_{i}),T(s)(\mathbf{e}_{j})\}_{\Omega(s)}.

(11.25)

This is the mutation formula of
\Omega(s)
in (
5.43
).
∎

Next,
in parallel with (
5.12
), we introduce
a skew-field automorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho_{q}{(s)}\colon&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}\\
&Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}&\mapsto&\displaystyle Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2u-1)}Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)},\end{matrix}

(11.26)

where
\alpha=\{\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega(s)}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that, for
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{e}_{i}
, we have
\alpha=b_{k_{s}i}(s)
.

Lemma 11.5
.

The map
\rho_{q}{(s)}
is
a skew-field automorphism.

Proof.

Let us check that

\displaystyle\rho_{q}{(s)}(Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}})\rho_{q}{(s)}(Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}})=q^{\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}}\rho_{q}{(s)}(Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}).

(11.27)

Let
\alpha=\{\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}},\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega(s)}
and

\alpha^{\prime}=\{\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}
.
Then,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \rho_{q}{(s)}(Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}})\rho_{q}{(s)}(Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}})\\
=&\ Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2u-1)}Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)}\\
&\ \quad\times Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha^{\prime}|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha^{\prime})(2u-1)}Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha^{\prime})},\\
=&\ q^{\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2u-1)}q_{k_{s}}^{2\varepsilon_{s}\alpha^{\prime}}Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)}\\
&\ \quad\times\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha^{\prime}|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha^{\prime})(2u-1)}Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha^{\prime})}.\end{split}

The last expression is equal to the RHS of (
11.27
).
∎

By Lemma
11.2
, we have the
Fock-Goncharov decomposition
of the quantum mutation
\mu_{q}{(s)}

\displaystyle\mu_{q}{(s)}=\rho_{q}{(s)}\circ\tau_{q}{(s)}.

(11.28)

In particular,
\mu_{q}{(s)}
is a skew-field isomorphism.

In parallel with (
5.17
), we introduce the composition

\displaystyle\tau_{q}(s;0)

\displaystyle:=\tau_{q}(0)\circ\tau_{q}(1)\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{q}(s):\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s+1)}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(0)}.

(11.29)

We have an analog of Proposition
5.2
.

Proposition 11.6
.

The following formula holds:

\displaystyle\tau_{q}(s;0)(Y_{i}(s+1))=Y^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s+1)}.

(11.30)

Proof.

The proof of Proposition
5.2
is applicable.
∎

Finally,
in parallel with (
5.23
),
we introduce a skew-field automorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathfrak{q}_{q}{(s)}\colon&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}\\
&Y^{\mathbf{n}}&\mapsto&\displaystyle Y^{\mathbf{n}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2u-1)}Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)},\end{matrix}

(11.31)

where
\alpha=\{\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s),\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that
\mathfrak{q}_{q}{(0)}=\rho_{q}(0)
.

We have an analog of
Proposition
5.3
.

Proposition 11.7
.

The following commutative diagram holds:

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}}
\tau_{q}(s-1;0)
\tau_{q}(s-1;0)
\rho_{q}(s)
\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)

(11.32)

In particular,
\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)
is a skew-field automorphism.

Proof.

The proof is the same as Proposition
5.3
.
∎

We introduce the composite mutations

\displaystyle\mu_{q}(s;0)

\displaystyle:=\mu_{q}(0)\circ\mu_{q}(1)\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{q}(s)\colon\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s+1)}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Omega},

(11.33)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s;0)

\displaystyle:=\mathfrak{q}_{q}(0)\circ\mathfrak{q}(1)\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)\colon\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}.

(11.34)

In parallel with (
5.31
), we have
the
Fock-Goncharov decomposition
of
\mu(s;0)
:

\displaystyle\mu_{q}(s;0)=\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s;0)\circ\tau_{q}(s;0).

(11.35)

Let
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
be the completion of
the noncommutative algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\Omega}

with respect to
\deg{\mathbf{n}}
.
We see from (
11.31
) that

\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)
acts also on
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
.
Thus, one can
regard
\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)
as an automorphism of
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
, whenever preferred.

11.3
Quantum mutations and quantum dilogarithm

As first clarified by Fock and Goncharov
[
FG09a
]
,
the quantum mutation
\rho_{q}(s)
is closely related
to the quantum dilogarithm
\Psi_{q}(x)
in (
10.19
).
For
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
,
the
adjoint action
of
\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})
on
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}

is defined by

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})]:&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}\\
&Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}&\mapsto&\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{-1}.\end{matrix}

(11.36)

Even though
\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})
itself does not belong to
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}
,
the map is well defined as shown below;
therefore, it yields a skew-field automorphism of
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}
.

Lemma 11.8

(
[
FG09a
, Lemma 3.4]
)
.

For any
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}](Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}})=Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k}^{\varepsilon\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2u-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)},\end{split}

(11.37)

where
\alpha=\{\delta_{k}e_{k}(s),\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega(s)}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

By (
11.18
), we have

\displaystyle Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}=q_{k}^{2\varepsilon\alpha}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon}.

(11.38)

It follows that

\displaystyle\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}=Y(s)^{\mathbf{n}}\Psi_{q_{k}}(q_{k}^{2\varepsilon\alpha}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}.

(11.39)

Here, we temporarily consider the product in the completion of
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega(s)}

with respect to
Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon}
.
Meanwhile,
by repeatedly applying the difference relation (
10.24
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \Psi_{q_{k}}(q_{k}^{2\varepsilon\alpha}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}\\
=&\ (1+q_{k}^{\varepsilon\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2|\alpha|-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)}\Psi_{q_{k}}(q_{k}^{2\varepsilon\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(|\alpha|-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}\\
=&\ \biggl(\,\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k}^{\varepsilon\mathrm{sgn}(a)(2u-1)}Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\mathrm{sgn}(a)}\biggr)\Psi_{q_{k}}(Y_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}.\end{split}

(11.40)

By (
11.39
) and (
11.40
),
we obtain the equality (
11.37
).
∎

We have the following identification of automorphisms.

Proposition 11.9

(
[
FG09a
, Lemma 3.4]
)
.

We have

\displaystyle\rho_{q}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\varepsilon_{s}}],

(11.41)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-\varepsilon_{s}}].

(11.42)

Proof.

By setting
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{s}
and
k=k_{s}

in
(
11.37
)
and comparing it with (
11.26
),
we obtain the equality (
11.41
).
The equality (
11.42
) follows from
(
11.41
) and Proposition
11.6
.
∎

By combining (
11.28
) and (
11.41
), we have

\displaystyle\mu_{q}{(s)}=\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\varepsilon_{s}}]\circ\tau_{q}{(s)}.

(11.43)

In fact, this is the original definition of the quantum mutation by
[
FG09a
]

(in our convention),
where
\varepsilon_{s}
was set to 1 therein.

Let us summarize the correspondence between the classical and quantum mutations.

1.

The Poisson bracket (
5.44
) is equivalently written as

\displaystyle\{y(s)^{\mathbf{n}},y(s)^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\}_{s}=\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega(s)}y(s)^{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}.

(11.44)

This is replaced with
the noncommutative product (
11.18
).

2.

The time-one flow
of the Hamiltonian

-{\varepsilon_{s}}{\delta_{k_{s}}}(-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}))

in (
5.49
)
is replaced with
the map
\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(Y_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\varepsilon_{s}}]
.
See (
10.26
)
for the account of the minus signs for
-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(-x)
in the above.

11.4
Algebraic formulation of quantum mutations

As given in (
7.31
),
the description of the mutations
\mathfrak{q}(s)
by
the Poisson bracket and the Hamiltonian
was further replaced with
the action of the dilogarithm elements in the group
G=G_{\Omega}
.
It is possible to do it in the quantum case
\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)
as well;
moreover, it provides a more intrinsic formulation of the quantum mutations
as in the classical case.
Throughout the section, we set

\displaystyle\Omega=\Omega(0).

(11.45)

11.4.1
Exponential group

We first construct the exponential group
G_{q}=G_{q,\Omega}
corresponding to
G=G_{\Omega}
in
(
7.12
).
This is straightforward because the group
G_{q}
is still in the same class of groups
constructed in
[
KS14
]
.
Namely, in the construction of
G
in Sections
7.1
and
7.2
,
we only need to replace
the Lie bracket in (
7.6
) with

\displaystyle[X_{\mathbf{n}},X_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}]=[\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}]_{q}X_{\mathbf{n}+\mathbf{n}^{\prime}},

(11.46)

where
[a]_{q}
is the
q
-number in (
10.2
)
extended to any rational number
a
.
By (
11.17
),
[\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}]_{q}\in\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/\delta_{0}})
.
Accordingly, we set the ground field
of the corresponding Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}_{q}
as
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/\delta_{0}})
.

11.4.2
y
-representation

We consider an analog of the
y
-representation
\rho_{y}
in
Section
7.3
.
We first replace the representation space
\mathbb{Q}[[\mathbf{y}]]

to the noncommutative algebra
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
,
which was
introduced in the end of Section
11.2
.
Then, in parallel with (
7.22
), we define the action of
X_{\mathbf{n}}\in\mathfrak{g}_{q}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{\mathbf{n}}(Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}):=[\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}]_{q}Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}+\mathbf{n}}=\frac{q^{2\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}-1}{q-q^{-1}}Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}Y^{\mathbf{n}}.

(11.47)

It is easy to check that this is an action of
\mathfrak{g}_{q}

and also a derivation on
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
.
Thus, we have a group homomorphism
[
Nak22
]

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}\colon&G_{q}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{Aut}(\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega})\\
&\exp(X)&\mapsto&\mathrm{Exp}(\tilde{X}),\end{matrix}

(11.48)

which is parallel with the one in (
7.27
).
We call it the
y
-representation
of
G_{q}
.
It is injective if
\Omega
is nonsingular.

11.4.3
Quantum dilogarithm elements

Following
[
Nak22
]
,
we introduce
an analog of the dilogarithm elements in
(
7.28
)
with some extra parameters related to
q
.

Definition 11.10

(Quantum Dilogarithm element)
.

For any
\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}
,

a\in(1/\delta_{0})\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, and

b\in(1/\delta_{0})\mathbb{Z}
,
we define

\displaystyle\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]:=\exp\Biggl(\,\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j[ja]_{q}}q^{jb}X_{j\mathbf{n}}\Biggr)\in G_{q},

(11.49)

We call
\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]
the
quantum dilogarithm element
for
\mathbf{n}

with
quantum data

a
,
b
.
We also write
\Psi_{a,0}[\mathbf{n}]
as
\Psi_{a}[\mathbf{n}]
, for simplicity.

Roughly speaking, the parameters
a
and
b

control the
interval
and the
shift

of the powers of
q

in (
11.49
), respectively.
In contrast to the asymptotic behavior (
10.26
) of the quantum dilogarithm
\Psi_{q}(x)
,
the element

\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]\in G_{q}
is simply reduced to a power of the classical one
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]\in G

in the limit
q\rightarrow 1
as

\displaystyle\lim_{q\rightarrow 1}\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]=\Psi[\mathbf{n}]^{1/a}.

(11.50)

Thus, the parameter
a
is regarded as the “inverse exponent”,
though the above factorization does not occur when
q\neq 1
.

By (
11.47
),
the element
\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]
acts on
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}

under the
y
-representation as

\displaystyle\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}](Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}})=Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\exp\biggl(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2j\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}-1}{q^{2ja}-1}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{j}q^{ja}q^{jb}Y^{j\mathbf{n}}\biggr).

(11.51)

In particular, we have the following formula.

Proposition 11.11

(
[
Nak22
, §2.3]
)
.

Under the
y
-representation of
G_{q}
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{1/\delta_{k_{s}}}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)]^{\varepsilon_{s}}(Y^{\mathbf{n}})=Y^{\mathbf{n}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)(2u-1)}Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{\mathrm{sgn}(\alpha)},

(11.52)

where
\alpha=\{\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s),\mathbf{n}\}_{\Omega}\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We set
a=1/\delta_{k_{s}}
,
b=0
,
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)
, and
\mathbf{n}^{\prime}=\mathbf{n}

in (
11.51
).
We have

\displaystyle\frac{q^{2j\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}-1}{q^{2ja}-1}=\frac{q^{2j\varepsilon_{s}\alpha/\delta_{k_{s}}}-1}{q^{2j/\delta_{k_{s}}}-1}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sum_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}q^{2j\varepsilon_{s}(u-1)/\delta_{k_{s}}}&\varepsilon_{s}\alpha>0,\\
0&\alpha=0,\\
\displaystyle-\sum_{u=1}^{|\alpha|}q^{-2j\varepsilon_{s}u/\delta_{k_{s}}}&\varepsilon_{s}\alpha<0.\end{cases}

(11.53)

Thus, we obtain the equality (
11.52
).
∎

The adjoint action of the quantum dilogarithm in (
11.42
) is
replaced with the action of the quantum dilogarithm elements.

Proposition 11.12
.

As an automorphism of
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{q}(s)={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{y}}(\Psi_{1/\delta_{k_{s}}}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)]^{-\varepsilon_{s}}).

(11.54)

Proof.

This is immediately obtained
by comparing (
11.31
) and (
11.52
).
∎

11.4.4
Pentagon relation

Let us present the pentagon relation among
quantum dilogarithm elements
\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]
.

We first note a useful formula.
In (
11.51
), suppose that
|\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}|=a
,
where
a\in(1/\delta_{0})\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
is the one for
\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]
therein.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\frac{q^{2j\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}}-1}{q^{2ja}-1}=\begin{cases}1&\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}=a,\\
-q^{-2ja}&\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}=-a.\end{cases}

(11.55)

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}](Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}})=\begin{cases}Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}(1+q^{a+b}Y^{\mathbf{n}})&\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}=a,\\
Y^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}(1+q^{-a+b}Y^{\mathbf{n}})^{-1}&\{\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}^{\prime}\}_{\Omega}=-a.\end{cases}

(11.56)

We have an analog of Proposition
7.7
.

Proposition 11.13

(
[
Nak22
, Theorem 2.6]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{n}_{1},\,\mathbf{n}_{2}\in N^{+}
.
The following relations hold in
G_{q}
.

(a) (Commutative relation). If
\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}=0
,
then, for any
a_{i}
and
b_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{a_{2},b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}]\Psi_{a_{1},b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}]=\Psi_{a_{1},b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}]\Psi_{a_{2},b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}].

(11.57)

(b) (Pentagon relation).
If
\{\mathbf{n}_{2},\mathbf{n}_{1}\}_{\Omega}=c

(c\in(1/\delta_{0})\mathbb{Z}_{>0})
,
then, for any
b_{i}
,
we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{c,b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}]\Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}]=\Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}]\Psi_{c,b_{1}+b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]\Psi_{c,b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}].

(11.58)

Proof.

(a). This is clear by (
11.46
).
(b).
By the same argument in the proof of Proposition

7.7
,
it is enough to prove the equality
for the action on
Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}
and
Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}

under the
y
-representation.
Let us consider the former case.
Below, we only use (
11.56
)
and the relation
Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}=q^{-2c}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{c,b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}]\Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}](Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})&=\Psi_{c,b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}](Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})\\
&=Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+q^{c+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}).\end{split}

(11.59)

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\ \Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}]\Psi_{c,b_{1}+b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}]\Psi_{c,b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{2}](Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}]\Psi_{c,b_{1}+b_{2}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}](Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+q^{c+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}](Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+q^{c+b_{1}+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})

\displaystyle\quad\times(1+q^{c+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+q^{-c+b_{1}+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}})^{-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi_{c,b_{1}}[\mathbf{n}_{1}](Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+q^{c+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}+q^{c+b_{1}+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ Y^{n_{1}}(1+q^{c+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+q^{-c+b_{1}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad+q^{c+b_{1}+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}+\mathbf{n}_{2}}(1+q^{-c+b_{1}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}})^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ Y^{\mathbf{n}_{1}}(1+q^{c+b_{2}}Y^{\mathbf{n}_{2}}).

Thus, they coincide. The other case is similar.
∎

11.5
Quantization of principally extended cluster patterns

Even though our targets are quantum dilogarithm identities,
where only quantum
y
-variables are involved,
we also need to work with quantum
x
-variables
for the proof of Theorem
12.2
.
The reader can safely skip the content of this section by accepting Theorem
12.2
.

11.5.1
Quantum mutations of principally extended
x
-variables

For the mutation sequence (
3.1
),
let
C(s)
and
G(s)
(
s=0,\,\dots,\,P
)
be the corresponding
C
- and
G
-matrices, respectively.
Let
\Lambda(s)

(s=0,\,\dots,\,P)
be the
2n\times 2n
skew-symmetric matrix defined by

\displaystyle\Lambda(s)=\begin{pmatrix}O&-G(s)^{T}D\\
DG(s)&-\Omega\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega=\Omega(0),\quad D=\Delta^{-1}.

(11.60)

Also, let
\tilde{B}(s)

(s=0,\,\dots,\,P)
and
\tilde{D}
be the
2n\times n
matrices defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{B}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}B(s)\\
C(s)\end{pmatrix},\quad\tilde{D}=\begin{pmatrix}D\\
O\end{pmatrix}.

(11.61)

By the dualities (
2.54
) and (
2.74
), they satisfy the matrix relation

\displaystyle-\Lambda(s)\tilde{B}(s)=\tilde{D}.

(11.62)

Such a pair
(\Lambda(s),\tilde{B}(s))

is called a
compatible pair
in
[
BZ05
]
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\tilde{B}(s)^{T}\Lambda(s)\tilde{B}(s)=\Omega(s).

(11.63)

Also, by (
2.53
),
we have the mutation formula

\displaystyle\Lambda(s+1)=\tilde{Q}(s)^{T}\Lambda(s)\tilde{Q}(s),

(11.64)

where
\tilde{Q}(s)=(q_{ij}(s))
is the
2n\times 2n
unimodular matrix defined by

\displaystyle q_{ij}(s)=\begin{cases}\delta_{ij}&j\neq k_{s},\\
-1&i=j=k_{s},\\
[-\varepsilon_{s}b_{ik_{s}}(s)]_{+}&i\neq j=k_{s}.\end{cases}

(11.65)

For the above
\Lambda(s)=(\lambda_{ij}(s))
,
let
\delta^{\prime}_{0}
be the smallest positive integer such that

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{2n}\mathbb{Z}\lambda_{ij}(s)=(1/\delta^{\prime}_{0})\mathbb{Z}.

(11.66)

Due to the condition (
11.62
),

\delta^{\prime}_{0}
is a multiple of
\delta_{0}
in (
11.17
).
Also, it is independent of
s
, thanks to (
11.64
) and the unimodularity of
\tilde{Q}(s)
.

For each
s=0
, …,
P
, we introduce the
principally extended
x
-variables

\tilde{\mathbf{X}}(s)=(X_{1}(s),\dots,X_{2n}(s))
as noncommutative variables obeying the
q
-commutative
relation

\displaystyle X_{i}(s)X_{j}(s)=q^{2\lambda_{ij}(s)}X_{j}(s)X_{i}(s),

(11.67)

where
X_{i}(s)
(
i=n+1
, …,
2n
) are
frozen variables
; namely,
they are never mutated.
Meanwhile, the unfrozen variables
X_{i}(s)
(
i=1
, …,
n
) are actually commutative with each other
by (
11.60
).
For
\tilde{\mathbf{m}}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{2n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
,
we define

\displaystyle X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}=q^{\sum_{i<j}\lambda_{ij}(s)m_{i}m_{j}}X_{1}(s)^{m_{1}}\cdots X_{2n}(s)^{m_{2n}}.

(11.68)

Then, the quantum mutation
in
[
BZ05
, Eq. (4.22)]

is written as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}X_{i}(s+1)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X(s)^{-\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}}\\
\quad+X(s)^{-\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}}&i=k_{s},\\
\displaystyle X_{i}(s)&i\neq k_{s},\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(11.69)

where
1\leq k_{s}\leq n
and
\varepsilon_{s}=\varepsilon_{k_{s}}(s)
is the tropical sign as usual.
In Section
11.5.2
, we will show that the relation (
11.67
)
is comptible with (
11.69
).
Due to the sign-coherence of the
C
-matrices, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j},

(11.70)

but this is not true for the opposite sign
-\varepsilon_{s}
.

For each
s=0
, …,
P
,
the noncommutative variables

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{i}(s)=X(s)^{\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\tilde{b}_{ji}(s)\mathbf{e}_{j}}\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\,n)

(11.71)

are identified with the quantum analogs of the
the
\hat{y}
-variables
\hat{y}_{i}(s)
with principal coefficients
in Section
2.5.2
.
By (
11.62
)
and (
11.63
),
we have relations

\displaystyle X_{i}(s)\hat{Y}_{j}(s)

\displaystyle=q_{j}^{-2\delta_{ij}}\hat{Y}_{j}(s)X_{i}(s)\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\,2n;\,j=1,\,\dots,\,n),

(11.72)

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{i}(s)\hat{Y}_{j}(s)

\displaystyle=q^{2\omega_{ij}(s)}\hat{Y}_{j}(s)\hat{Y}_{i}(s)\quad(i,\,j=1,\,\dots,\,n).

(11.73)

Note that the relation (
11.73
) is
the same as the one (
11.3
) for
Y_{i}(s)
’s.
By (
11.72
), the first case of
(
11.69
) is also written as

\displaystyle X_{k_{s}}(s+1)=X(s)^{-\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{-\varepsilon_{s}}\hat{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}).

(11.74)

This is an analog of the first case of (
5.3
).

Example 11.14

(Type
A_{2}
)
.

We use the same convention in Example
11.1
.
Let
X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}=X_{t_{0}}^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}
.
Then, we have the following result.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}X_{1;1}=X^{(-1,0,0,0)}+X^{(-1,1,1,0)},\\
X_{2;1}=X^{(0,1,0,0)},\end{cases}

(11.75)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}X_{1;2}=X^{(-1,0,0,0)}+X^{(-1,1,1,0)},\\
X_{2;2}=X^{(0,-1,0,0)}+X^{(-1,-1,0,1)}+X^{(-1,0,1,1)},\end{cases}

(11.76)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}X_{1;3}=X^{(1,-1,0,0)}+X^{(0,-1,0,1)},\\
X_{2;3}=X^{(0,-1,0,0)}+X^{(-1,-1,0,1)}+X^{(-1,0,1,1)},\end{cases}

(11.77)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}X_{1;4}=X^{(1,-1,0,0)}+X^{(0,-1,0,1)},\\
X_{2;4}=X^{(1,0,0,0)},\end{cases}

(11.78)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}X_{1;5}=X^{(0,1,0,0)}=X_{2},\\
X_{2;5}=X^{(1,0,0,0)}=X_{1}.\end{cases}

(11.79)
