The pentagon periodicity is preserved again.
In this example, all coefficients of monomials
X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}
are 1.
However, they are polynomials in
q^{1/\delta^{\prime}_{0}}
, in general.

The following is
the most fundamental result on the quantization of
x
-variables.

Theorem 11.15
.

(Quantum Laurent phenomenon,
[
BZ05
, Cor. 5.2]
).
Each element
X_{i}(s)
is expressed as a (noncommutative) Laurent polynomial
M_{i}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}})
in
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{2n})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/{\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}]
.

Moreover, the following property holds.

Proposition 11.16

(Bar-invariance,
[
BZ05
, Proof of Prop. 6.2]
)
.

In the Laurent polynomial
M_{i}(\tilde{\mathbf{X}})
in Theorem
11.15
each monomial is written in the form

\displaystyle cX^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}\quad(c\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}],\ \tilde{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}),

(11.80)

where
c
is symmetric with respect to
q
and
q^{-1}
.

We skip the proofs of Theorem
11.15
and Proposition
11.16
.

11.5.2
Fock-Goncharov decomposition

The above quantum mutation of
x
-variables
also admits the Fock-Goncharov decomposition
in parallel with the
y
-variables.
We do not use the results in this subsection in the rest of the text.
Nevertheless, we present them to show the consistency of the whole picture.

Let
\{\cdot,\cdot\}_{\Lambda(s)}
be the skew-symmetric bilinear
form on
\mathbb{Z}^{2n}

defined in the same way as (
5.21
).
We introduce the noncommutative algebra

\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda(s)}

with generators
X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}
(
\tilde{\mathbf{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2n}
)
over
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}})

obeying the relations

\displaystyle X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}=q^{\{\tilde{\mathbf{m}},\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\}_{\Lambda(s)}}X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}+\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}=q^{2\{\tilde{\mathbf{m}},\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}\}_{\Lambda(s)}}X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}^{\prime}}X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}.

(11.81)

By setting

X(s)^{\mathbf{e}_{i}(s)}=X_{i}(s)
(
i=1,\,\dots,\,2n
), we recover the relation (
11.67
).

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)}
be the
skew-field of all fractions of
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda(s)}
.
We formulate the quantum mutation
in (
11.69
) (see also (
11.74
)) as
the following skew-field isomorphism

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu_{q}^{x}{(s)}}\colon&\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s+1)}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)},\\
&X_{i}(s+1)&\mapsto&\begin{cases}\displaystyle X(s)^{-\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}}\\
\quad\times(1+q_{k_{s}}^{-\varepsilon_{s}}\hat{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})&i=k_{s},\\
X_{i}(s)&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(11.82)

The map
\mu_{q}^{x}(s)
has the

Fock-Goncharov decomposition

\displaystyle\mu_{q}^{x}(s)=\rho_{q}^{x}(s)\circ\tau_{q}^{x}(s).

(11.83)

Here,
\tau_{q}^{x}(s)
is a skew-field isomorphism
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\tau_{q}^{x}{(s)}:&\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s+1)}&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)}\\
&X(s+1)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}&\mapsto&X(s)^{T^{x}(s)(\tilde{\mathbf{m}})},\end{matrix}

(11.84)

where
T^{x}(s)
is a linear isomorphism defined by

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}T^{x}{(s)}\colon&\mathbb{Z}^{2n}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{Z}^{2n}\\
&\mathbf{e}_{i}&\mapsto&\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\mathbf{e}_{k_{s}}+\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{e}_{j}&i=k_{s}.\\
\mathbf{e}_{i}&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}\end{matrix}

(11.85)

By (
11.70
), the sum in the first case
can be restricted to
1\leq j\leq n
.
Also,

\rho_{q}^{x}(s)
is a skew-field automorphism
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{matrix}\rho_{q}^{x}{(s)}:&\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)}\!\!\!&\rightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)}\\
&X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}\!\!\!&\mapsto&\displaystyle X(s)^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}\prod_{u=1}^{|m_{k_{s}}|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(m_{k_{s}})(2u-1)}\hat{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(m_{k_{s}})}.\end{matrix}

(11.86)

Lemma 11.17
.

(a). The map
\tau_{q}^{x}(s)
is a skew-field isomorphism.

(b). The map
\rho_{q}^{x}(s)
is a skew-field automorphism.

Proof.

(a).
The desired condition is equivalent to the equality

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{e}_{i},\mathbf{e}_{j}\}_{\Lambda(s+1)}=\{T^{x}(s)(\mathbf{e}_{i}),T^{x}(s)(\mathbf{e}_{j})\}_{\Lambda(s)}.

(11.87)

This is nontrivial only for (i)
i\leq n
and
j\geq n+1
,
or (ii)
j\leq n
and
i\geq n+1
.
In both cases, the equality follows from
the specialization of (
5.7
) with the tropical sign
\varepsilon_{s}
,
namely,

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}_{i}(s+1)=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{g}_{i}(s)+\sum_{j=1}^{n}[-\varepsilon_{s}b_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\mathbf{g}_{j}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
\mathbf{g}_{i}(s)&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}

(11.88)

(b). This is proved in the same way as
Lemma
11.5
.
∎

By Lemma
11.17
, the map
\mu_{q}^{x}(s)
is a skew-field isomorphism.

Let
\mathbf{g}_{i}(s)
be the
i
th
g
-vector of the
G
-matrix
G(s)
.
We define
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2n}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)=\begin{cases}(\mathbf{g}_{i}(s),\mathbf{0})&i=1,\,\dots,\,n,\\
\mathbf{e}_{i}&i=n+1,\,\dots,\,2n.\end{cases}

(11.89)

Also, let
\overline{G}(s)
be the
2n\times 2n
matrix defined by

\displaystyle\overline{G}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}G(s)&O\\
O&I\end{pmatrix}.

(11.90)

Then, the first duality (
2.54
) is rewritten as

\displaystyle\overline{G}(s)\tilde{B}(s)=\tilde{B}(0)C(s).

(11.91)

We define the composition

\tau_{q}^{x}(s;0)
in paralel to (
11.29
).

Proposition 11.18
.

The following formulas hold:

\displaystyle\tau_{q}^{x}(s;0)(X_{i}(s+1))=

\displaystyle X^{\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s+1)}\quad(i=1\,\dots,\,2n),

(11.92)

\displaystyle\tau_{q}^{x}(s;0)(\hat{Y}_{i}(s+1))=

\displaystyle\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{c}_{i}(s+1)}:=X^{\sum_{j=1}^{n}c_{ji}(s+1)\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{j}}\quad(i=1\,\dots,\,n),

(11.93)

where
\tilde{\mathbf{b}}_{j}
is the
j
th column vector of the matrix
\tilde{B}=\tilde{B}(0)

in (
11.61
).

Proof.

The formula (
11.88
) is extended as

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s+1)=\begin{cases}-\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)+\sum_{j=1}^{2n}[-\varepsilon_{s}\tilde{b}_{jk_{s}}(s)]_{+}\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{j}(s)&i=k_{s},\\
\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)&i\neq k_{s}.\end{cases}

(11.94)

Then, in the same way as Proposition
5.2
, we have
(
11.92
).
The formula (
11.93
) is obtained from (
11.92
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\mathrm{LHS})\overset{\eqref{eq:qhaty1}}{}&{=}\tau_{q}^{x}(s;0)(X(s+1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\tilde{b}_{ji}(s+1)\mathbf{e}_{j}})\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:qxtaus11}}{}&{=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X}^{\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\tilde{b}_{ji}(s+1)\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{j}(s+1)}\overset{\eqref{2eq:tdual0}}{=}(\mathrm{RHS}).\end{split}

(11.95)

∎

For
\Lambda=\Lambda(0)
,
we define the automorphism
\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s)\colon\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}

by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s)(X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}})=X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}}\prod_{u=1}^{|\beta|}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}\mathrm{sgn}(\beta)(2u-1)}\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-\mathrm{sgn}(\beta)},

(11.96)

where
\beta=\langle\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{m}}\rangle:=\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s)^{T}\tilde{D}^{T}\tilde{\mathbf{m}}
.

Proposition 11.19
.

The following commutative diagram holds:

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda(s)}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}_{\Lambda}}
\tau_{q}^{x}(s-1;0)
\tau_{q}^{x}(s-1;0)
\rho_{q}^{x}(s)
\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s)

(11.97)

Proof.

Let us specialize

\tilde{\mathbf{m}}=\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)
in (
11.96
).
By the duality (
2.74
),
we have

\displaystyle\beta=\langle\delta_{k_{s}}\mathbf{c}_{k_{s}}(s),\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)\rangle=\delta_{k_{s}i}.

(11.98)

Thus,
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s)(X^{\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)})=X^{\tilde{\mathbf{g}}_{i}(s)}(1+q_{k_{s}}^{\varepsilon_{s}}\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-\delta_{k_{s}i}}.

(11.99)

Then, by (
11.86
) and Proposition
11.18
, we have
(
11.97
).
∎

We define
\mu_{q}^{x}(s;0)
and
\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s;0)
in the same way as (
11.33
) and (
11.34
).
Then, we have the
Fock-Goncharov decomposition
for
\mu_{q}^{x}(s;0)
,

\displaystyle\mu_{q}^{x}(s;0)=\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s;0)\circ\tau_{q}^{x}(s;0).

(11.100)

Also, we have a parallel result with Proposition
11.9
.

Proposition 11.20
.

\displaystyle\rho^{x}_{q}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(\hat{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{-\varepsilon_{s}}],

(11.101)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}(s)})^{-\varepsilon_{s}}].

(11.102)

Proof.

By (
11.72
),
for any
\varepsilon\in\{1,-1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{Y}_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon}X_{i}(s)=q_{k}^{2{\varepsilon}\delta_{ki}}X_{i}(s)\hat{Y}_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon}.

(11.103)

Then, as in the proof of Lemma
11.8
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k}}(\hat{Y}_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\varepsilon}](X_{i}(s))=X_{i}(s)(1+q_{k}^{\varepsilon}\hat{Y}_{k}(s)^{\varepsilon})^{\delta_{ki}}.\end{split}

(11.104)

Comparing it with (
11.86
), we have the equality (
11.101
).
The equality (
11.102
) follows from (
11.101
)
and Proposition
11.19
.
∎

In summary, even though the quantization of
x
-variables requires a little more complicated formulation
(i.e., the principal extension and the compatible pair) than
y
-variables,
they can be treated in a unified way.
In particular,
the quantum dilogarithm is responsible for the nontropical part
\rho_{q}^{x}(s)

of the quantum mutation.
The picture is further extended to the
algebraic approach by the group
G_{q}
.
Namely,
the automorphism

\mathfrak{q}_{q}^{x}(s)
in (
11.96
)
is also identified with the action of the same dilogarithm element
in Proposition
11.12
but for a different representation of
G_{q}

called the
principal
x
-representation

[
Nak22
]
.

Notes

The quantization of
y
-variables was introduced in
[
FG09c
]

together with the Fock-Goncharov decomposition (with
\varepsilon=1
)
and also its relevance to the quantum dilogarithm.
The Fock-Goncharov decomposition with the tropical sign
was appeared in
[
Kel11
,
KN11
]
.
The quantization of
x
-variables of geometric type was introduced in
[
BZ05
]

and further studied by
[
Tra11
]
.
Here, we concentrated on the one with principal coefficients,
where the compatibility pair is conveniently given by (
11.60
) and (
11.61
).
Both quantizations are treated in a unified way
by the structure group
G_{q}

[
Nak22
]

based on the results in
[
KS14
,
GHKK18
,
Man21
,
DM21
]
.
The pentagon relation appeared in a closely related but different setup and context in
[
KS08
]
.
The pentagon relation for quantum dilogarithm elements
in the form of Proposition
11.13

is due to
[
Nak22
]
.

Chapter 12
Quantum dilogarithm identities

Remarkably any period of a classical
Y
-pattern is lifted to a period of the corresponding
quantum
Y
-pattern.
Moreover, there is an associated quantum dilogarithm identity (QDI)
for the quantum dilogarithm.
To be precise, there are two forms of QDIs, that is,
a QDI in tropical form and a QDI in universal form.
Examples of the QDI associated with a loop in a quantum cluster scattering diagram (QCSDs)
are also presented.

12.1
Quantum synchronicity

For the sequences of mutations in (
3.1
) and (
3.2
)
we consider their quantum counterparts.
Let
\Upsilon_{q}(s)=(\mathbf{Y}(s),B(s))
be the quantum
Y
-seed in (
11.13
),
while
\Sigma_{q}(s)=(\tilde{\mathbf{X}}(s),B(s))
be the principally extended quantum seed in
Section
11.5
.
For a permutation
\nu\in S_{n}
,
the
\nu
-periodicity condition is given by

\displaystyle\Sigma_{q}(P)=\nu\Sigma_{q}(0),

(12.1)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{q}(P)=\nu\Upsilon_{q}(0),

(12.2)

where in (
12.1
) the permutation
\nu
acts on the unfrozen variables
X_{1}
, …,
X_{n}
among
the principally extended variables
\tilde{\mathbf{X}}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{2n})
.
If the condition (
12.1
) (resp. (
12.2
)) holds,
then the condition (
3.3
) (resp. (
3.4
)) holds
in the limit
q\rightarrow 1
.
Remarkably, the opposite indication also holds.

We start from the
x
-variable case,
which is due to Berenstein-Zelevinsky
[
BZ05
]
.

Theorem 12.1

(Quantum synchronicity
[
BZ05
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

The condition (
3.3
) implies the
condition (
12.1
).

Proof.

Assume that the condition (
3.3
) holds.
Then, the condition (
12.1
) is equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle\mu_{q}^{x}(P-1;0)(X_{i}(P))=X_{\nu^{-1}(i)}\quad(i=1,\,\dots,\,n).

(12.3)

Since the mutation (
11.74
) and the
q
-commutative relation
(
11.67
) do not involve any subtraction,
one can represent it as

\displaystyle\mu_{q}^{x}(P-1;0)(X_{i}(P))=K_{i}(\mathbf{X})L_{i}(\mathbf{X})^{-1},

(12.4)

where
K_{i}(\mathbf{X})
and
L_{i}(\mathbf{X})
are (noncommutative) polynomials in
\mathbf{X}

with subtraction-free coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}})
.
By Theorem
11.15
, this equals to
a Laurent polynomial
M_{i}(\mathbf{X})
in
\mathbf{X}
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}]
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle K_{i}(\mathbf{X})=M_{i}(\mathbf{X})L_{i}(\mathbf{X}).

(12.5)

Let
\mathrm{NP}(M_{i}(\mathbf{X}))
be the
Newton polytope
of
M_{i}(\mathbf{X})
, that is,
the convex hull of all exponents of
\mathbf{X}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
(This is well defined since
\mathbf{X}
are
q
-commutative.)
Let
a_{1}=c_{1}X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{1}}
be a Laurent monomial belonging to
M_{i}(\mathbf{X})
such that

\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{1}
is at a vertex of
\mathrm{NP}(M_{i}(\mathbf{X}))
.
By (
12.5
),
there are some
vertices
{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{2}}
and
{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{3}}
of
\mathrm{NP}(L_{i}(\mathbf{X}))
and
\mathrm{NP}(K_{i}(\mathbf{X}))
,
respectively, such that,
for the monomials
a_{2}=c_{2}X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{2}}
and

a_{3}=c_{3}X^{\tilde{\mathbf{m}}_{3}}
belonging to
L_{i}(\mathbf{X})
and
K_{i}(\mathbf{X})
, respectively,
the equality
a_{3}=a_{1}a_{2}
holds.
Thus,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{1}=c_{3}/c_{2}}
has a subtraction-free expression in
\mathbb{Q}(q^{1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}})
.
In particular,
\lim_{q\rightarrow 1}c_{1}\neq 0
.
This means that the Newton polytope
\mathrm{NP}(M_{i}(\mathbf{X}))
does not shrink in the limit
q\rightarrow 1
.
On the other hand,
by the assumption (
3.3
), we have
\lim_{q\rightarrow 1}M_{i}(\mathbf{X})=x_{\nu^{-1}(i)}
.
Therefore,
M_{i}(\mathbf{X})=cX_{\nu^{-1}(i)}
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}]
.
One can also apply the same argument to the inverse mutation sequence.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\mu_{q}^{x}(P-1;0)^{-1}(X_{\nu^{-1}(i)})=c^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}X_{i}(P)}

(12.6)

for some
c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}]
.
Since
cc^{\prime}=1
,
c
is invertible in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}]
.
Thus,
c=q^{a/{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta^{\prime}_{0}}}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Finally, by Proposition
11.16
, we have
c=1
.
∎

Next, we consider the
y
-variable case,
which is the basis of our approach to quantum dilogarithm identities.

Theorem 12.2

(Quantum synchronicity
[
FG09c
, Lemma 2.22]
,
[
KN11
, Prop. 3.4]
)
.

The condition (
3.4
) implies the
condition (
12.2
).

Proof.

Assume that the condition (
3.4
) holds.
By the classical synchronicity (Theorem
2.31
),
the condition (
3.3
) holds.
Thus, by Theorem
12.1
,
the condition (
12.1
) holds.
It follows that the periodicity of the quantum
\hat{y}
-variables

\hat{\mathbf{Y}}(P)=\nu\hat{\mathbf{Y}}
holds.
On the other hand,

\hat{\mathbf{Y}}
and
\mathbf{Y}
satisfy the same
q
-commutative relation
and
\hat{\mathbf{Y}}(s)
and
\mathbf{Y}(s)
obey the same mutation.
Moreover, if
\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{n}}=\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}
holds,
then
\tilde{B}\mathbf{n}=\tilde{B}\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
holds.
Since
\tilde{B}
has rank
n
(as a matrix),
we have
\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{n}^{\prime}
.
This means that
\hat{Y}^{\mathbf{n}}
satisfies only relations
generated by (
11.73
).
Therefore, we have
\mathbf{Y}(P)=\nu\mathbf{Y}
.
∎

12.2
QDI associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern

In this section, we present several forms of quantum dilogarithm identities
associated with a period of a
Y
-pattern.

12.2.1
QDI for quantum dilogarithm elements

We start from
the quantum analogue of Theorem
7.5
for the quantum dilogarithm elements

\Psi_{a}[\mathbf{n}]=\Psi_{a,0}[\mathbf{n}]
in (
11.49
).

Theorem 12.3

(QDI for quantum dilogarithm elements)
.

Suppose that
the sequence of mutations
(
3.2
) is
\nu
-periodic
.
Then, the following relation holds in
G_{q}
.

\displaystyle\Psi_{1/\delta_{k_{P-1}}}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})]^{\varepsilon_{P-1}}\cdots\Psi_{1/\delta_{k_{0}}}[\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)]^{\varepsilon_{0}}=\mathrm{id}.

(12.7)

Proof.

By Theorem
12.2
,
the periodicity (
12.2
) holds.

Namely, we have
\mu_{q}(P-1;0)=\mathrm{id}
.
Also, by Proposition
11.6
, we have
\tau_{q}(P-1;0)=\mathrm{id}
.
Therefore, the equality
\mathfrak{q}_{q}(P-1;0)=\mathrm{id}
holds.

Then, we repeat the proof of Theorem
7.5

by using Proposition
11.12
.

∎

By (
11.50
),
in the limit
q\rightarrow 1
, the classical identity (
7.32
) is recovered.

Example 12.4

(Continued from Example
11.14
)
.

For the pentagon periodicity of type
A_{2}
,
the relation (
12.7
) is written as

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}]^{-1}\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{1}]^{-1}\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{1}]=\mathrm{id},

(12.8)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{1}]=\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{1}]\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}]\Psi_{1}[\mathbf{e}_{2}].

(12.9)

This is a special case of the pentagon relation (
11.58
).

12.2.2
QDI in tropical form

We turn the relation (
12.7
)
into an identity for the quantum dilogarithm
\Psi_{q}(x)
.
The derivation is more direct than the classical case.

Theorem 12.5

(QDI in tropical form
[
Kel11
, Theorem 5.11]
,
[
KN11
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{Y}
be the initial quantum
Y
-variables
obeying the relation (
11.16
) with
s=0
.
Suppose that
the sequence of mutations
(
3.2
) is
\nu
-periodic
.
Then, the following identity holds in
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{q_{k_{P-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})})^{\varepsilon_{P-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}}=1.

(12.10)

Proof.

Again, we repeat the proof of Theorem
7.5

by using (
11.42
)
.
In the proof, the following fact is essential:
Suppose that
B
is nonsingular.
Then, the equality

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{P-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})})^{\varepsilon_{P-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}}]=\mathrm{id}.\end{split}

(12.11)

implies the equality

\displaystyle\Psi_{q_{k_{P-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{P-1}}({P-1})})^{\varepsilon_{P-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}}=1.

(12.12)

To see it,
let
g=1+\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}}c_{\mathbf{n}}Y^{\mathbf{n}}
be the LHS of (
12.12
).
Then, the condition (
12.11
) implies that
each term
c_{\mathbf{n}}Y^{\mathbf{n}}
commutes with
Y_{1}
, …,
Y_{n}
.
Since
\Omega
is nonsingular, this implies
c_{\mathbf{n}}=0
.
∎

Two identities (
12.7
) and (
12.10
)
can be directly translated to each other, so that one
may regard them as alternative expressions of the same identity.

Example 12.6

(Continued from Example
12.4
)
.

The corresponding identity to (
12.10
) is written as

\displaystyle\Psi_{q}(Y_{2})\Psi_{q}(Y_{1})=\Psi_{q}(Y_{1})\Psi_{q}(Y^{\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}})\Psi_{q}(Y_{2}).

(12.13)

Note that
Y^{\mathbf{e}_{1}+\mathbf{e}_{2}}=qY_{1}Y_{2}
.
Thus,
it coincides with the pentagon identity in (
10.52
),
where
u=Y_{2}
and
v=Y_{1}
.
In particular, it gives an alternative proof of
the pentagon identity (
10.52
).

12.2.3
QDI in universal form

There is another form of QDIs first discovered by Volkov
[
Vol11
]

for the pentagon identity.
The idea is shuffling the product (
12.10
)
in the opposite order.
Below we set

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{k_{s}}(s):={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu_{q}(s-1;0)}(Y_{k_{s}}(s))

(12.14)

and regard
\tilde{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}
and

\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})
as elements of
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
.

Lemma 12.7

(Shuffle formula,
[
KN11
, Lemma 3.6]
)
.

The following equality holds in
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}({s})})^{\varepsilon_{s}}\Psi_{q_{k_{s-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s-1}}({s-1})})^{\varepsilon_{s-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}}\\
=&\ \Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{0}}(0)^{\varepsilon_{0}})^{\varepsilon_{0}}\Psi_{q_{k_{1}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{1}}(1)^{\varepsilon_{1}})^{\varepsilon_{1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{\varepsilon_{s}}.\end{split}

(12.15)

Proof.

By the Fock-Goncharov decomposition
(
11.35
)
and the equality (
11.42
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}}=\mathrm{Ad}[\Psi_{q_{k_{s-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s-1}}({s-1})})^{\varepsilon_{s-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}}]^{-1}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}({s})}).

(12.16)

Thus, we have the equality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s}}({s})})^{\varepsilon_{s}}(\Psi_{q_{k_{s-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s-1}}({s-1})})^{\varepsilon_{s-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}})\\
=&\ (\Psi_{q_{k_{s-1}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{s-1}}({s-1})})^{\varepsilon_{s-1}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(Y^{\mathbf{c}^{+}_{k_{0}}(0)})^{\varepsilon_{0}})\Psi_{q_{k_{s}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{s}}(s)^{\varepsilon_{s}})^{\varepsilon_{s}}.\end{split}

(12.17)

By repeating it, we obtain the equality (
12.15
).
∎

Theorem 12.8

(QDI in universal form
[
Vol11
, §3]
,
[
KN11
, Cor. 3.7]
)
.

Suppose that
the sequence of mutations
(
3.2
) is
\nu
-periodic
.
Then, the following identity holds in
\overline{\mathcal{A}}_{\Omega}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{q_{k_{0}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{0}}(0)^{\varepsilon_{0}})^{\varepsilon_{0}}\cdots\Psi_{q_{k_{P-1}}}(\tilde{Y}_{k_{P-1}}(P-1)^{\varepsilon_{P-1}})^{\varepsilon_{P-1}}=1.

(12.18)

Proof.

This is obtained from (
12.10
) and (
12.15
).
∎

Example 12.9

(Continued from Example
12.6
)
.

By the result in Example
11.1
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Psi_{q}(Y_{1})\Psi_{q}(Y_{2}(1+q^{-1}Y_{1}))\Psi_{q}((1+q^{-1}Y_{2}+Y_{1}Y_{2})^{-1}Y_{1})^{-1}\\
&\quad\times\Psi_{q}(q^{-1}(1+q^{-1}Y_{2})^{-1}Y_{2}Y_{1})^{-1}\Psi_{q}(Y_{2})^{-1}=1.\end{split}

(12.19)

12.3
More examples of QDIs

Let us consider the QDIs for the periodicities of
types
B_{2}
and
G_{2}
in Example
3.9
,
which involve nontrivial factors
\delta_{i}
.

(a) Type
B_{2}
.
We set

\displaystyle\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&2\end{pmatrix}.

(12.20)

We use the notation

[n_{1},n_{2}]_{a,b}
and
[n_{1},n_{2}]_{a}
(or column-wise)
for quantum dilogarithm elements
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]_{a,b}
and
\Psi[\mathbf{n}]_{a}

as in the classical case in Section
7.7
.
From the data in Example
3.9
,
we read the following QDI for quantum dilogarithm elements:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}.

(12.21)

Accordingly,
the QDI in the tropical from is given by

\displaystyle\Psi_{q^{1/2}}(Y_{2})\Psi_{q}(Y_{1})=\Psi_{q}(Y_{1})\Psi_{q^{1/2}}(Y^{(1,1)})\Psi_{q}(Y^{(1,2)})\Psi_{q^{1/2}}(Y_{2}).

(12.22)

The QDI in the universal form is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(0))\Psi_{q^{1/2}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(1))&=\Psi_{q^{1/2}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(5)^{-1})\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(4)^{-1})\\
&\qquad\times\Psi_{q^{1/2}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(3)^{-1})\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(2)^{-1}),\end{split}

(12.23)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{Y}_{1}(0)&=Y_{1},\\
\tilde{Y}_{2}(1)&=Y_{2}(1+q^{-1}Y_{1}),\\
\tilde{Y}_{1}(2)&=Y_{1}^{-1}(1+q^{-1/2}Y_{2}+q^{1/2}Y_{1}Y_{2})(1+q^{-3/2}Y_{2}+q^{-1/2}Y_{1}Y_{2}),\\
\tilde{Y}_{2}(3)&=qY_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-1}(1+(q^{-1/2}+q^{-3/2})Y_{2}+q^{-2}Y_{2}^{2}+qY_{1}Y_{2}^{2}),\\
\tilde{Y}_{1}(4)&=q^{2}Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-2}(1+q^{-1/2}Y_{2})(1+q^{-3/2}Y_{2}),\\
\tilde{Y}_{2}(5)&=Y_{2}^{-1}.\end{split}

As an additional perspective,
let us demonstrate that
the relation (
12.21
) is derived only from the pentagon relation (
11.58
)
[
Nak22
]
.
We use the following
fission-fusion formula

[
Nak22
, Prop. 2.8]
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{a,b}[n]=\prod_{t=1}^{p}\Psi_{pa,b+(2t-p-1)a}[n]\quad(p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}).

(12.24)

Applying the pentagon relation (
11.58
) repeatedly, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}=&\ \begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,0}\\
=&\ \begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,0}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\\
=&\ \begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,0}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\\
=&\ \begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,0}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,0}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1,-\frac{1}{2}}\\
=&\ \begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}},\end{split}

(12.25)

which is in parallel with the classical one (
7.44
).
In other words, the QDI (
12.21
) is
finitely reducible

by the pentagon relation (
11.58
).

(b) Type
G_{2}
.
We set

\displaystyle\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&3\end{pmatrix}.

(12.26)

From the data in Example
3.9
,
we read off the following QDI for the dilogarithm elements:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{3}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{3}}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{3}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\\
\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{3}}.

(12.27)

Accordingly,
the QDI in the tropical form is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ \Psi_{q^{1/3}}(Y_{2})\Psi_{q}(Y_{1})\\
=&\ \Psi_{q}(Y_{1})\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(Y^{(1,1)})\Psi_{q}(Y^{(2,3)})\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(Y^{(1,2)})\Psi_{q}(Y^{(1,3)})\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(Y_{2}).\end{split}

(12.28)

The QDI in the universal form is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(0))\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(1))&=\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(7)^{-1})\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(6)^{-1})\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(5)^{-1})\\
&\qquad\times\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(4)^{-1}),\Psi_{q^{1/3}}(\tilde{Y}_{2}(3)^{-1})\Psi_{q}(\tilde{Y}_{1}(2)^{-1}),\end{split}

(12.29)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=Y_{1},

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{2}(1)

\displaystyle=Y_{2}(1+q^{-1}Y_{1}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{1}(2)

\displaystyle=Y_{1}^{-1}(1+q^{-1/3}Y_{2}+q^{2/3}Y_{1}Y_{2})(1+q^{-1}Y_{2}+Y_{1}Y_{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times(1+q^{-5/2}Y_{2}+q^{-2/3}Y_{1}Y_{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{2}(3)

\displaystyle=qY_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-1}(1+(q^{-4/3}+q^{-2}+q^{-8/3})(qY_{2}+Y_{2}^{2}+q^{3}Y_{1}Y_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+q^{-3}Y_{2}^{3}+(q^{2}+1)Y_{1}Y_{2}^{3}+q^{5}Y_{1}^{2}Y_{2}^{3}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{1}(4)

\displaystyle=q^{6}Y_{1}^{-2}Y_{2}^{-3}(1+(q^{-7/3}+q^{-3})Y_{2}+q^{-16/3}Y_{2}^{2}+q^{5/3}Y_{1}Y_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times(1+(q^{-1/3}+q^{-11/3})Y_{2}+q^{-4}Y_{2}^{2}+qY_{1}Y_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\times(1+(q^{-1}+q^{-5/3})Y_{2}+q^{-8/3}Y_{2}^{2}+q^{1/3}Y_{1}Y_{2}^{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{2}(5)

\displaystyle=q^{2}Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-2}(1+(q^{-1/3}+q^{-1}+q^{-5/3})Y_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times(q^{-4/3}+q^{-2}+q^{-8/3})Y_{2}^{2}+q^{-3}Y_{2}^{3}+q^{2}Y_{1}Y_{2}^{3}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{1}(6)

\displaystyle=q^{3}Y_{1}^{-1}Y_{2}^{-3}(1+q^{-1/3}Y_{2})(1+q^{-1}Y_{2})(1+q^{-5/3}Y_{2}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Y}_{2}(7)

\displaystyle=Y_{2}^{-1}.

Again, the relation (
12.27
) is
finitely reducible by the pentagon relation.

12.4
QDI associated with a loop in a QCSD

As in the classical case,
it is possible to extend QDIs for
quantum cluster scattering diagrams (QCSDs)
.

A QCSD
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{s}}^{q}
is defined in parallel with a CSD
[
Man21
,
DM21
]

by replacing the structure group
G
with
G_{q}
and
the incoming walls
\mathbf{w}_{i}
in Definition
8.11
with

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{i}:=(e_{i}^{\perp},\Psi_{1/\delta_{i}}[e_{i}])_{e_{i}}.

(12.30)

By applying the results and methods in
[
KS14
,
GHKK18
]
,
one can establish the existence and the uniqueness (up to equivalence)
of a QCSD for each skew-symmetrizable integer matrix
B
.
However, not much is known about the structure of QCSDs.

Here, we list some open problems.

Problem 12.10
.

(1)
Positivity and nonpositivity.

It is known
[
Nak22
]
that,
any QCSD has a realization that
every wall element has the form

\displaystyle\Psi_{1/\delta(h\mathbf{n}),b}{[h\mathbf{n}]^{s}}\quad(\mathbf{n}\in N^{+}_{\mathrm{pr}};\,b\in(1/\delta_{0})\mathbb{Z};\,h\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1};\,s\in\mathbb{Z}).

(12.31)

The analog of the positivity property in Theorem
8.23

claims that the only positive powers
s
appear in (
12.31
).
This is known to be true by
[
DM21
, Theorem 2.15]

when
B
is skew-symmetric,
Also, this is true for the types
B_{2}
and
G_{2}
,
which are nonskew-symmetric,
as presented in Section
12.3
.
On the other hand, several counter-examples are known in the nonskew-symmetric case
[
LLRZ14
,
CFMM24
,
Nak22
]
.
The exact condition for the positivity is not known.

(2)
Preservation of the support.

The quantum synchronicity in Section
12.1
suggests that
the support of a QCSD remains unchanged from the classical counterpart.
This is not obvious due to a possible
cancellation of walls
of a QCSD in the classical limit
such as
\lim_{q\rightarrow 1}\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}]\Psi_{a,b^{\prime}}[\mathbf{n}]^{-1}=1
.
If the positivity in (a) holds, this does not happen; therefore, the support is preserved
[
Nak22
]
.
However, in general, this is yet to be proved.

(3)

Infinite reducibility.

An analog of the ordering property in Proposition
8.24

is not known yet.
Therefore, the infinite reducibility of QDIs by the pentagon relation
as in Theorem
8.27
is not clear.
On the other hand,
there are examples with infinitely or finitely reducibility, including the ones in (
12.22
) and (
12.28
).

Despite the above problems,
the QDI associated with a loop
\gamma
in a QCSD is formulated just as in the classical case.
We simply replace the dilogarithm elements in (
9.31
) with the quantum ones
that appear on the walls crossed by
\gamma
.
Below we present examples,
which are “well-behaved” in the sense that
the positivity holds and the properties
(2) and (3) are satisfied.

Example 12.11

(Affine type of rank 2
[
Nak22
]
)
.

Let us consider the affine type of rank 2.
We use the same convention in Example
8.20
.

(a)
Type
A_{1}^{(1)}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(2,2)
. This is a skew-symmetric case.
The support of
a QCSD
\mathfrak{D}^{q}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with minimal support is the same as
the classical one in (
8.32
)–(
8.35
).
The QDI for quantum dilogarithm elements along a loop around the origin is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\cdots\Biggl(\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j}\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j-1},-2^{j-1}}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j}\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j-1},2^{j-1}}\Biggr)\\
&\qquad\times\cdots\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}.\end{split}

(12.32)

This is the simplest example of a QDI
involving an infinite product.
One can prove the infinite reducibility as in the classical case
[
Nak22
]
.
The quantum dilogarithm version of the formula coincides with the identity in
[
DGS11
, Eq. (1.3)]

that describes the wall crossing in
N=2
super Yang-Mills theory.

(b) Type
A_{2}^{(2)}
. Let
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})=(1,4)
.
This is a nonskew-symmetric case.
The support of
a QCSD
\mathfrak{D}^{q}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with minimal support is the same as
the classical one in (
8.37
)–(
8.42
).
The QDI for quantum dilogarithm elements along a loop around the origin is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
3\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}5\\
8\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
5\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}\cdots\\
&\qquad\times\Biggl(\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{2}}\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j+1}\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j-1},-2^{j-1}}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j+1}\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j-1},2^{j-1}}\Biggr)\\
&\qquad\times\cdots\begin{bmatrix}5\\
12\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
5\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
8\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
3\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
4\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{\frac{1}{4}}.\end{split}

(12.33)

One can prove the infinite reducibility as in the classical case
[
Nak22
]
.

Example 12.12

(Type
A_{2}^{(1)}
)
.

As a little more nontrivial example, we consider the
type
A_{2}^{(1)}
case,
which is of rank 3 affine type.
We take

\displaystyle B=\Omega=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&-1\\
1&0&-1\\
1&1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\Delta=I.

(12.34)

The support of
a QCSD
\mathfrak{D}^{q}_{\mathfrak{s}}
with minimal support is the same as
the classical one, and it is depicted in Figure
12.1
.
The diagram is the projection of the support of
\mathfrak{D}^{q}_{\mathfrak{s}}

on the unit sphere
S^{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Furthermore, it is projected on the tangent plane at
(1/\sqrt{3})(1,1,1)
by the stereographic projection.
For example,
the triangle in the center represents the positive orthant in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
In this example,
all joints
are of type
A_{2}
or
A^{(1)}_{1}
.
In particular, every QDI
is infinitely reducible.

Figure 12.1:
The support of a QCSD of type
A_{2}^{(1)}
.
The three-digit number
101
, for example, represents the normal vector
(1,0,1)

of a wall.
There are actually infinitely many walls converging to the thick wall
whose normal vector is
(1,1,1)
, so that
the thick wall is unreachable by cluster mutations.

Let us give one specific example of a QDI.
We choose a loop
\gamma
depicted in Figure
12.1
,
where
\gamma
starts and ends in the positive orthant
and it is counterclockwise.
The loop
\gamma
is
not
admissible because it passes through the common
joints of walls with three normal vectors

\displaystyle\mathbf{n}_{1}=(0,1,0),\quad\mathbf{n}_{2}=(1,0,1),\quad\mathbf{n}_{3}=(1,1,1).

(12.35)

However, we have

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{n}_{i},\mathbf{n}_{j}\}=0.

(12.36)

So, this is a parallel joint.
Therefore, the quantum dilogarithm elements
\Psi_{a,b}[\mathbf{n}_{i}]
(
i=1,\,2,\,3)
commute
with each other, so that the path-ordered product along
\gamma
is well-defined.
The intersections of the loop
\gamma
with walls are separated
in four steps.

1.

First,
the loop
\gamma
crosses the incoming walls
\mathbf{e}_{1}^{\perp}
,

\mathbf{e}_{2}^{\perp}
,
\mathbf{e}_{3}^{\perp}
in this order with positive intersection sign.
(The intersection sign is read from the convexity of the walls at the intersection.
It is positive if and only if the path goes from the convex side to the non-convex side.)

2.

Next,
\gamma
crosses infinitely many walls
with negative intersection sign,
where the normal vectors of walls are

\displaystyle(1,0,0),\ (1,1,0),\ (2,1,1),\ (2,2,1),\ (3,2,2),\ (3,3,2),\ \cdots.

(12.37)

3.

Then,
\gamma
crosses walls
with negative intersection sign,
where the normal vectors of walls are
\mathbf{n}_{1}
,
\mathbf{n}_{2}
,
\mathbf{n}_{3}

in (
12.35
).
The wall with normal vector
\mathbf{n}_{3}
is presented in the thick curve
in Figure
12.1
. Its wall element has
the same structure as the one with normal vector
(1,1)
in Example
12.11
.

4.

Finally,

\gamma
crosses infinitely many walls
with negative intersection sign,
where the normal vectors of walls are

\displaystyle\cdots,(2,3,3),\ (2,2,3),\ (1,2,2),\ (1,1,2),\ (0,1,1),\ (0,0,1),\

(12.38)

and returns to the initial point.

Thus, we obtain the following QDI along
\gamma
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}&=\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
2\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\cdots\\
&\qquad\times\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j}\\
2^{j}\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j},-2^{j}}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j}\\
2^{j}\\
2^{j}\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j},2^{j}}\right)\\
&\qquad\times\cdots\begin{bmatrix}1\\
2\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\\
2\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}.\end{split}

(12.39)

There is one interesting application of this QDI.
Let

\displaystyle P=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&1\\
1&0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\Omega^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

(12.40)

Note that
\mathrm{Ker}\,\Omega=\mathrm{Ker}\,P=\langle(1,-1,1)\rangle
.
Also, we have

\displaystyle P^{T}\Omega^{\prime}P=\Omega.

(12.41)

So, one may reduce the set of positive vectors
N^{+}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{3}
for the structure group
G_{q}

with
N^{\prime}{}^{+}=P(N^{+})\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
which is spanned by
(0,1)
and
(1,-1)
.
Then, applying the projection
P
to the QDI in
(
12.39
), we obtain a QDI in the reduced structured group
G^{\prime}_{q}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}&=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\cdots\\
&\qquad\times\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{1}\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\infty}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j+1}\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j},-2^{j}}\begin{bmatrix}2^{j+1}\\
0\end{bmatrix}_{2^{j},2^{j}}\right)\\
&\qquad\times\cdots\begin{bmatrix}4\\
-1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}3\\
-1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}2\\
-1\end{bmatrix}_{1}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
-1\end{bmatrix}_{1}.\end{split}

(12.42)

The quantum dilogarithm version of the formula is identified with the first identity in
[
DGS11
, Eq. (1.5)]
.
It was proved by
[
Sug23
]
with a very different method in view of the quantum affine algebras.

Notes

The quantum synchronicity for
x
-variables is due to
[
BZ05
]
.
The quantum synchronicity for
y
-variables is due to
[
FG09c
,
KN11
]
.
The QDIs for quantum dilogarithm elements are studied in
[
Nak22
]
.
The QDIs in tropical form were given by
[
Kel11
]
with the categorification method and by
[
KN11
]
in a more direct approach as presented here.
The QDIs in universal form were given by
[
Vol11
]
for the pentagon identity and by
[
KN11
]
in general.
The QCSDs were given by
[
Man21
,
DM21
]
based on the results by
[
KS14
,
GHKK18
]
.
The QDIs for QCSDs are regarded as special cases of
wall-crossing formulas
studied in a more broad context by
[
KS08
]
.
The QDIs are also studied in different approaches in
[
Rei09
,
DGS11
,
GMN13
,
Sug23
]
.
The QDIs in Section
12.3
and Example
12.11
are taken from
[
Nak22
]
, while Example
12.12
is new here.
